# Microarchitecture of the Apple M6 Neural Engine: An Empirical Study Based on Compiled Artifacts, Execution Traces, and Power Measurements

## Abstract

The Apple M6 chip integrates two Apple Neural Engines (ANEs) on a single die, but their program format, on-chip storage, clocks, and power have not been publicly documented. Under the constraints that models are executed only through the public Core ML interfaces and that no modified program is executed, this report characterizes the microarchitecture of the M6 ANE along the lifecycle of a single inference call. The characterization combines analysis of compiled artifacts, firmware timestamps, performance counters, SMC power-rail measurements, and read-only analysis of the compiler, driver, and firmware. The entire report is organized around one thesis: the practical performance of the ANE is determined jointly by the static arrangements made by the compiler, the resources shared by the two engines, and the dynamic management performed by the host. The results show that each engine contains 16 NEs, that each NE completes 256 FP16 or 512 INT8 multiply-accumulate operations per cycle, that the full-load clock is 2.58 GHz, and that the measured FP16 throughput of a single engine is 20.6 TFLOPS. The weight-read bandwidth is about 150 GB/s and is shared by the two engines. FP16 multiply-accumulate operations use a Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator. Compressed and pruned weights are read in compressed format, and the compute array skips zero weights but not zero activations. The compiler generates both single-ANE and dual-ANE programs for each model. When computation is the limiting factor, the dual-engine speedup is 1.92×, but when weight reading is the limiting factor, it is only about 1.4×, and row-wise splitting yields no speedup. The DVFS state of the ANE is determined by the CLPC in the host kernel, which maintains the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77% when calls are spaced closely together. Host overhead therefore directly affects ANE speed. This report also finds that the power rail that can be used to measure ANE power also supplies the CPU, and it gives a subtraction method and a power decomposition. On practical tasks, the M6 ANE is 1.4–2.7× faster than the M4 ANE. On this basis, the report summarizes deployment principles and compiles 14 suspected defects and 10 undocumented behaviors. All experimental tools, raw data, and analysis scripts of this report are publicly available at <https://github.com/751K/Apple-Neural-Engine-Microarchitecture-Study-M6>.

## 1. Introduction

### 1.1 Research Background

Since the A11 chip in 2017, Apple has integrated a dedicated neural network accelerator, the Apple Neural Engine (ANE), into its SoCs. The original A11 had only two cores and was not open to third-party developers. Starting with the A12, the ANE was expanded to eight cores and became accessible through Core ML [8]. On the Mac, the ANE has been a standard component of every chip generation since the M1. The M1 ANE has 16 cores, with an advertised throughput of 11 TOPS, and by the M4 the core count remains 16 while the advertised throughput has risen to 38 TOPS [8]. On chips such as the M1 Ultra, which is assembled from two dies, the ANE core count doubles with the die count to 32 [8]. When the Core ML framework loads a model, it assigns suitable models, or parts of their operations, to the ANE for execution.

As generative models increasingly migrate to on-device execution, the importance of the ANE grows further. Compared with the CPU and GPU, the ANE has a clear advantage in energy efficiency, which makes it suitable for running tasks such as large language models and image generation continuously on battery-powered devices. However, these workloads place different demands on hardware than conventional convolutional networks do. In the decoding phase of a large language model, every generated token requires reading all of the weights, while the amount of computation is small. Performance therefore depends mainly on the bandwidth for reading weights and on the compressed format of the weights. On-device interactive inference usually proceeds intermittently, and the idle periods between calls in turn affect the DVFS state of the hardware and the energy consumed by each inference. Consequently, to deploy such models efficiently on the ANE, developers need to understand more than the peak compute of the ANE. They also need to understand its storage hierarchy, data paths, and data formats, as well as its scheduling and frequency-scaling behavior.

For developers, however, the ANE has long remained opaque and can be invoked only as a black box. The information Apple publishes is limited to the number of cores in each ANE generation and its peak compute, and since the M5 even the peak compute has no longer been published. The program format, on-chip storage, data paths, clocks, and power of the ANE are all undisclosed, and Apple provides no interface for programming the ANE directly. Developers can use the ANE only indirectly, through Core ML. The `MLComputePlan` interface of Core ML can query which devices each operation supports and which device it prefers. However, developers cannot confirm whether each call actually executes on the ANE, and they cannot analyze how the time of one inference is distributed among scheduling, computation, and data movement. This opacity makes it difficult for developers to optimize models for the ANE and makes it difficult for researchers to evaluate its design.

The generational evolution of the ANE aggravates this problem further. The clock, bandwidth, supported data formats, and compiler strategy of each ANE generation change, so knowledge gained on one chip generation may not apply to the next, and these changes are likewise undocumented. The M6 introduces the largest structural change visible to software since the M1: it integrates two complete ANEs on a single die (Section 4.3). The compiler generates both single-ANE and dual-ANE programs for each model, and the driver must distribute requests between the two engines (Chapters 5 and 6). Unlike Ultra-series chips, which have a set of ANEs on each of two dies, the two engines of the M6 are located on the same die and can jointly execute the same model. Apple has not explained how the two engines cooperate, when a model can use both engines at once, how much speedup the second engine can bring, or what limits that speedup. Existing studies have not yet made measurements on the M6.

### 1.2 Prior Work and Its Limitations

Few public resources can be drawn on to answer these questions. Apple's own deployment guide gives several recommendations for deploying Transformers on the ANE, such as using channel-first tensor layouts and replacing fully connected layers with convolutions [24], but it does not explain the hardware reasons behind these recommendations. Community-maintained documentation records mainly Core ML-level behavior, such as which layers can run on the ANE [8]. Only two systematic studies examine the ANE internals in depth: maderix's series of articles on the M4 [1–4], and Bryngelson's survey covering the A11 through the M5 [5]. The former modifies compiled artifacts and executes them on the device, reconstructing the task descriptor (TD), on-chip storage, and data formats of the M4. The latter combines decompilation of the compiler with measurements on the M1 and M5, and gives the architecture and performance characteristics of several ANE generations. Chapter 2 describes these works in detail.

Measured against the requirements stated in Section 1.1, these studies leave gaps in three respects.

First, the hardware parameters of the ANE remain incomplete. Even for the M4, the most thoroughly studied generation, maderix did not measure the width or clock frequency of the MAC array. The author did not determine how the MAC array, the planar engine (PE), and DMA overlap, and did not determine the DMA topology [4]. The measurements in Bryngelson's survey were conducted mainly on the M1 and M5, and most of its descriptions of newer generations derive from decompilation or prediction. Its description of the H18 generation does not agree with the M6 [5]. For the weight-read bandwidth, activation bandwidth, and data formats that developers care about most, existing sources provide only scattered numbers.

Second, cooperation between the two engines has not been studied at all. maderix estimated the peak performance of the M6 only from public information, and listed as open questions whether the two engines share on-chip storage and whether a single graph can execute across both engines [4]. On the basis of the kernel driver of an earlier product, Bryngelson analyzed how requests are allocated in multi-die chips. The analysis found that requests are dispatched as whole submissions to idle dies and that dies do not exchange data [5]. However, this describes request-level allocation. Whether the two engines of the M6 can cooperate at the program level to execute the same model, and what the cost and benefit of such cooperation are, remain unanswered.

Third, the host-side effects on the ANE have been overlooked. Existing studies all measure the ANE as an independent accelerator and treat the overhead of each call as a fixed figure [2, 4, 5]. None of them systematically studies how the host driver allocates requests, how the state of the CPU affects call overhead, or how power management selects the DVFS state of the ANE. In intermittent on-device workloads, however, precisely these factors determine which DVFS state the ANE actually runs in and how much energy each inference consumes. In terms of power measurement, this report also finds that the SMC power rail that can be used to measure ANE power also supplies the CPU (Chapter 13). Existing power data must therefore be interpreted with caution.

### 1.3 Research Objectives and Questions

To address the three gaps described above, the objective of this report is to characterize the microarchitecture of the M6 ANE completely, using only publicly available execution paths. This includes how its two engines cooperate and how host-side software affects its performance and energy consumption.

The core thesis of this report is that the practical performance of the ANE is not determined by its compute array alone. It is jointly determined by three aspects. The first is the static arrangements made by the compiler at compile time: how a program is partitioned, how weights are read in, and whether both engines are used. The second is the resources shared by the two engines: the weight-read bandwidth, the shared cluster clock, and shared power. The third is the dynamic management performed by the host at run time: how requests are submitted and dispatched, and how the DVFS state is selected. Therefore, explaining why an inference is fast or slow, and why it consumes more or less energy, requires analyzing these three aspects together rather than examining the ANE in isolation. The subsequent chapters present measurements of the program format, compute and storage bandwidth, dual-engine scaling, frequency scaling, and power, and these measurements serve as evidence for this thesis. Section 15.1 returns to the thesis on the basis of all results.

To achieve this objective, this report organizes the study along the complete lifecycle of a single inference call. This main line is adopted because the practical performance and energy consumption of the ANE are the joint result of each stage in this process. A call first passes from the program generated by the compiler, through the driver and firmware, to the hardware. It then executes in the compute array and storage paths, and the speed of execution is determined by the DVFS state and ultimately manifests as power consumption. Only by measuring stage by stage along this process can time and energy be attributed to specific stages, and only then can the ANE be studied without separating it from the host. Following this main line, the research questions of this report fall into three groups. These groups correspond to the three gaps of Section 1.2 in an overlapping rather than one-to-one manner (see below). The lifecycle determines only the order in which the questions are developed. The three aspects of the core thesis are used for a comprehensive interpretation based on all results, and the two do not correspond chapter by chapter, because the results of a single chapter often involve several of these aspects at once.

The first group of questions concerns how a program reaches the hardware. It asks what program the compiler translates a model into, and how a program on a dual-engine chip differs from one on a single-engine chip (Chapter 5). It also asks how an inference call passes through the host driver and the ANE firmware to the hardware, where the time is spent, and how multiple requests run concurrently (Chapter 6). This group also addresses the second and third gaps, because the generation of dual-engine programs and the allocation of requests both take place in this stage.

The second group of questions concerns how the hardware computes and moves data internally. It asks how many multiply-accumulate operations the compute array completes per cycle, and which shapes and precisions can exploit its capability (Chapter 7). It asks how the numerical behavior of the ANE differs from that of the CPU (Chapter 8), how large the on-chip storage is, and what the bandwidths of the weight and activation paths are (Chapter 9). It also asks how the two engines cooperate, how much speedup the second engine brings, and what limits that speedup (Chapter 10). This group mainly addresses the first and second gaps.

The third group of questions concerns what determines speed and energy consumption. It asks which clock domains the ANE has, and at what frequency it runs at full load (Chapter 11). It asks by whom, and according to what rules, the DVFS state is determined (Chapter 12), and how much power the ANE consumes and where that power goes (Chapter 13). This group mainly addresses the third gap.

After answering these three groups of questions, this report also tests the conclusions on practical application workloads. It examines how the microarchitectural characteristics obtained above manifest on large language models, image classification, and text recognition tasks. It identifies the tasks on which the M6 ANE is markedly faster than the M4 and the tasks on which its speedup is limited, and it asks whether the causes can be explained by the conclusions of the earlier chapters (Chapter 14). To this end, Chapter 14 first derives observable expectations from the conclusions of the earlier chapters, then tests them group by group, and identifies the factors that application benchmarks cannot separate. Relating the conclusions of microbenchmarks to practical applications is also something that existing studies lack.

### 1.4 Methods and Constraints

This report combines four classes of methods to answer the questions above. First, it calls the compilation interface within its own process, compiling without executing, comparing the artifacts of each compilation target byte for byte, and performing read-only disassembly of the compiler. Second, it executes models only through the public interfaces of Core ML and observes execution with timing, IOReport counters, SMC power rails, and kdebug firmware events. Third, it performs read-only static analysis of the kernelcache and the ANE firmware. Fourth, it runs application benchmarks with real models on two machines, the M6 and the M4. It monitors the busy fraction, memory read bandwidth, and power of the ANE using anemon [27], an ANE monitoring program that we developed, together with `powermetrics` and SMC power rails. This report observes the following constraints. It does not use private execution interfaces, and it does not load or execute any modified compiled artifacts. System observation is performed in read-only mode, and firmware and kernelcache are subjected only to read-only analysis and are not redistributed. This report also does not modify any system security settings. The details of the methods and the definitions of the evidence categories are given in Chapter 3, and the limitations arising from these constraints are discussed in Chapter 16.

### 1.5 Main Contributions

The main findings of this report comprise nine items. Most of them each support the thesis above from one of three aspects: the compiler, shared resources, and the host. The numerical behavior in item five is a relatively independent finding. Item eight tests the thesis on practical applications, and its results are consistent with it. Item nine summarizes the questions raised by these findings, together with the reusable tools and data.

First, for hardware parameters, this report establishes by measurement that each neural engine core (NE) completes 256 FP16 multiply-accumulate operations or 512 INT8 multiply-accumulate operations per cycle. It back-calculates a full-load clock of 2.58 GHz from firmware timestamps. It also measures a weight-read bandwidth of about 150 GB/s and an activation bandwidth of about 35–40 GB/s per ANE. For on-chip storage, this report determines the 64 KiB resident-weight limit that the compiler sets for each NE, and the L2 that the compiler uses for each ANE with a 2 MiB budget. The physical capacities of these structures cannot be read directly (Chapters 7, 9, and 11). The multiply-accumulate width and the clock frequency are quantities that maderix did not measure on the M4.

Second, for weight compression and sparsity, this report establishes by measurement that W8, 2–6-bit palettized, and pruned weights are all read in compressed format. The execution time of weight-read-bound layers therefore shortens approximately in proportion to the number of bytes, at 0.32× the FP16 time for 4-bit palettization and 0.16× for 90% pruning. It finds that the compute arrays of both the M6 and the M4 skip multiply-accumulate operations on zero weights. At 90% pruning, this corresponds to 53.6 TFLOPS when normalized to the dense multiply-accumulate count. This behavior does not require structured sparsity, but zero activations are not skipped. The report also indicates that block-wise int4 quantization and vector palettization cannot be compiled for the ANE with the generation parameters used in this report (Chapter 7).

Third, for dual-engine cooperation, this report finds that each compiled artifact of the M6 contains both single-ANE and dual-ANE programs. It reverse-engineers the cost model that the compiler uses to decide whether to split a layer, and it measures a dual-engine speedup of 1.92× when computation is the limiting factor, corresponding to a scaling efficiency of about 96%. It identifies two causes that limit scaling: the two engines share the weight-read bandwidth, and work partitioning across tiles is uneven. The report also finds that asynchronous requests to the same model from the same process can be dispatched to both engines, with throughput reaching about 2.8× that of synchronous calls when weights are resident on chip. Synchronous calls issued from multiple threads are serialized (Chapters 5, 6, and 10).

Fourth, for the program format and compiler parameters, this report determines that a TD is a sequence of register-write packets. It decodes the encodings of the header word, the address packets, and the base address register (BAR) slots, and it determines the correspondence between the TD layout versions of each generation. It finds that the compiler parameter table used by the M6 is identical to that of the next-generation H19. It infers, from layouts in the compiler that have not yet been named, the data formats that future ANEs will support. The report also reproduces the segmentation rules that Core ML uses to decide which operations are assigned to the ANE. From the ahead-of-time compilation driver, it infers that the M6 inherits the cost model of the M4 (Chapters 5 and 15).

Fifth, for numerical behavior, this report finds that the FP16 multiply-accumulate path uses a Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator, and that the bias is the initial value of the accumulator. It determines the specific behavior of overflow, rounding, subnormal products, and the lookup tables for activation functions (Chapter 8).

Sixth, for host-side scheduling and frequency scaling, this report analyzes the three-layer scheduling structure of the driver and firmware, and finds that the completion-side overhead of each call depends on the idle depth of the host CPU. It shows by measurement that the closed-loop performance controller (CLPC) in the host kernel stabilizes the ANE busy fraction at about 77% when inter-call intervals are short. It also gives an approximate model describing the behavior of the CLPC during long idle periods (Chapters 6 and 12).

Seventh, for power, this report finds that the SMC power rail PP0b supplies both the ANE and the S-cores and P-cores of the CPU. It proposes a measurement method that subtracts the power of the CPU clusters. On this basis, it decomposes the shared overhead of the two engines, the energy per multiply-accumulate and per byte, and the effect of the DVFS state on the energy consumed by each inference (Chapter 13).

Eighth, for practical applications, this report compares the M4 and M6 ANEs on three classes of practical tasks: large language models, image classification, and text recognition. It finds that the M6 is 1.8–2.7× faster on compute-dense tasks, whereas tasks with a small amount of work per call achieve only about a 1.4× speedup. The directions of these differences are consistent with the expectations derived from dual-engine scaling, weight-read bandwidth, and fixed call overhead. A custom model that reduces the number of calls is likewise consistent with the deployment principles of this report. At the same time, we identify factors that application benchmarks cannot separate (Chapter 14).

Ninth, this report compiles 14 suspected defects or contested designs and 10 undocumented behaviors, and it summarizes deployment principles for developers (Chapter 15). Apart from the application benchmark data in Chapter 14, all experimental tools, raw data, and analysis scripts are released with this report (Appendices A and B).

### 1.6 Organization of This Report

Chapter 2 reviews related work. Chapter 3 describes the experimental platform, the observation methods, and the evidence categories. Chapter 4 presents the overall structure of the M6 ANE as a reference for the subsequent chapters. Chapters 5–13 follow the lifecycle of a single inference call. Chapter 5 studies the compiler and the program format. Chapter 6 studies scheduling and execution. Chapters 7–9 study the compute array, numerical behavior, and storage hierarchy. Chapter 10 studies dual-engine cooperation. Chapters 11–13 study clock domains, dynamic frequency scaling, and power. Chapters 5, 6, and 10 all involve the two engines, and the division of labor among them is as follows. Chapter 5 explains how the compiler decides what each of the two engines does. Chapter 6 explains how the runtime submits, schedules, and completes requests. Chapter 10 tests how much speedup these arrangements actually deliver and why the ideal effect is not reached. Chapter 14 tests the conclusions of the preceding chapters with practical applications such as large language models, image classification, and text recognition. On this basis, Chapter 15 comprehensively discusses the design characteristics of the M6 ANE, deployment recommendations, comparison with earlier products, and the defects identified. Chapter 16 discusses the limitations and unresolved issues of this report, and Chapter 17 summarizes the report. The appendices provide the tools and reproduction methods, a data index, a corrections log, a defect list, and a glossary.

## 2. Related Work

Public research on the ANE can be broadly divided into five categories: two systematic studies of the ANE internals, by maderix and by Bryngelson; community-maintained documentation and open-source drivers; application studies aimed at deploying or training models on the ANE; Apple's own deployment guides; and studies of NPUs from other vendors, which provide comparison references and methodological inspiration for this report. This chapter introduces these works in sequence. It focuses on the conclusions each work reached, the methods it used, and the questions it left open, and it concludes by explaining how this report relates to them.

### 2.1 The maderix M4 Series

The series *Inside the M4 Apple Neural Engine* by maderix [1–4] uses the M4 Mac mini as its platform. It examines the ANE in four parts, progressing from shallow to deep, and it is the most thorough analysis of a single ANE generation to date. The author published the code used in the first three parts. The parameter-table reading tool and the program-generation tool used in the fourth part were not published.

#### 2.1.1 Software Stack and Private Interfaces

Part 1 [1] reviews the software stack of the ANE. By listing the Objective-C classes in private frameworks and intercepting Core ML's calls to private interfaces through method replacement, the author determined that below Core ML, the `_ANEClient` in the AppleNeuralEngine framework is responsible for compiling, loading, and executing models. The author reproduced the complete workflow, from establishing a connection, through compiling and loading (which returns a program handle with a queue depth of 127), to creating IOSurface inputs and outputs and submitting requests. The author analyzed the MIL intermediate representation of Core ML. The author found that the compiled binaries of a 1024×1024 matrix multiplication and a 128×128 matrix multiplication are almost the same size (2688 and 2680 bytes), and inferred from this that the compiled artifacts store configuration parameters rather than instructions. The author also found a private interface for compiling MIL directly in memory, recorded several pitfalls in its use, and noted that compilation results are cached in the application's cache directory. The first compilation takes about 20–40 ms. Part 1 concludes by listing interfaces that had not yet been explored, including request chaining, shared events, and performance statistics.

#### 2.1.2 Performance Measurements

Part 2 [2] measures the performance of the ANE by calling private interfaces directly. In a scan over matrix multiplication sizes, a 256×256 matrix multiplication takes about 0.101 ms, of which about 0.095 ms is scheduling overhead. At 2048×2048 the throughput reaches 5.7 TFLOPS, and at 4096×4096 it falls to 4.0 TFLOPS. The author attributed this decline to the total size of the three matrices (96 MB) exceeding the on-chip storage, and on this basis inferred that the SRAM is about 32 MB. The author also found that writing a matrix multiplication as a 1×1 convolution is about 3× faster. A single matrix multiplication can use only about 30% of peak, and only deep networks of 16–64 layers approach peak. For small operators, Core ML is 2–4× slower than direct calls to the private interface. This part also reports that INT8 shows no speedup over FP16, and that the energy efficiency of the ANE is about 6.6 TFLOPS/W, computed at 2.8 W. Both of these conclusions were later corrected in Part 4.

#### 2.1.3 On-Device Training

Part 3 [3] implements training of a small language model on the ANE. Because the ANE does not support branching, indexing by runtime values, or in-place modification, the author split attention into three steps: computing QKᵀ on the ANE, computing the mask and softmax on the CPU, and multiplying by V back on the ANE. Weight gradients and the optimizer are computed on the CPU. The author compared three schemes. In the first, the weights are hard-coded into the program, which requires 72 recompilations per batch, and a process fails after about 119 compilations because of resource leaks. In the second, moving more operations onto the ANE actually increased the number of compilations. In the third, passing the weights as IOSurface inputs requires only one compilation at startup. Ultimately, the author completed 50,000 steps of training of the Stories110M model on the ANE, and reported that the ANE draws about 2.8 W during training, while CPU training requires 15–20 W. This part also measures about 160 µs per XPC dispatch, and it finds that merging 12 layers into a single program is about 3× faster than splitting them into 24 calls.

#### 2.1.4 Hardware Structure

Part 4 [4] is the core of the series. The author proceeded from MIL-level experiments to the compiled artifacts and to the hardware itself. The author adopted two complementary paths. One is "MIL → compiler → ANE", which is used to observe operator support, whole-graph performance, and compiler strategy. The other is "compiled artifact → loader → ANE", which modifies fields in compiled artifacts and then executes them on the device, and confirms the meaning of a field by whether the output or timing changes as expected. The author used a debugger in a running compiler process to read out a 2360-byte hardware parameter table, and ultimately generated from scratch a program that is byte-for-byte identical to the compiler's output, reaching 98.8% of the measured peak.

Based on these methods, Part 4 presents the following main conclusions. In terms of storage, the compiler compares each operand with the 2 MiB threshold in the parameter table. Operands that do not exceed the threshold reside on chip in their entirety, while larger operands are tiled and streamed with double buffering. On-chip storage consists of 64 banks interleaved at 16 bytes, and the compiler chooses a row stride that is coprime with 64 to avoid conflicts. Weights have a 64 KiB resident space, and up to 16 MiB can be streamed in 16 blocks. This conclusion corrects the "SRAM of about 32 MB" inference of Part 2. In terms of compute, the author concluded from the parameter table that the ANE consists of eight compute groups of eight accumulators each, that it assigns work by output channel, and the author gave a method for computing the output-channel-group size. In terms of operator support, the author counted about 50 hardware layer types. Elementwise operations are controlled by a single opcode byte. Activation functions such as sigmoid are implemented by 33 FP16 sample points stored in the weight segment, with linear interpolation between them. The author verified this mechanism by replacing the sample points with x³, after which the hardware gave correct results. In terms of precision, the author found that W8A8 (weights and activations both quantized to 8-bit integers) is a property of the whole chain. The mode word and DMA word of intermediate blocks must change together, and quantizing only the weights to INT8 yields no speedup. The throughput of W8A8 is 1.95× that of FP16, and its power is 2.7×. In terms of performance and power, the author measured that 64-layer 1×1 convolutions reach 18.77 TFLOPS and that W8A8 reaches 36.01 TOPS. FP16 full-load power is 4.57 W, W8A8 is 12.25 W, and idle power is 0. In terms of scheduling, the author measured an overhead of about 90 µs per call, and submitting two requests simultaneously saves only about 16% of the time. In terms of limits, the author found that 383 blocks of 128 KiB per tensor can be compiled and executed, but at 384 blocks the compiler service disconnects. The compiled artifact contains only four record types, namely DMA, compute, synchronization, and termination, and it contains no branches. The FP8 (E4M3) capability bit of the M4 is 0.

Part 4 also discusses the M6 specifically. Based on the public information that the M6 has two 16-core engines and a memory bandwidth of 170 GB/s, the author estimated its peak at about 38 TFLOPS (FP16) and 76 TOPS (W8A8). The author listed open questions, including whether the two engines share SRAM, whether one inference is scheduled once or twice, and whether one graph can execute across both engines. The author explicitly states that no M6 was available for testing.

#### 2.1.5 Corrections and Open Issues across the Parts

The series corrected several early conclusions in subsequent articles, so citations should follow Part 4. Table 2-1 lists these corrections.

*Table 2-1. Conclusions corrected in the maderix series*

| Item | Earlier claim | Part 4 claim |
| --- | --- | --- |
| INT8 | No speedup (Part 2) | W8A8 about 1.95×; no speedup when only the weights are quantized |
| Power | About 2.8 W (Parts 2 and 3) | FP16 4.57 W, W8A8 12.25 W; 2.8 W is the reading in low-power mode |
| Energy efficiency | 6.6 TFLOPS/W | 4.1 TFLOPS/W |
| Scheduling overhead | 95 µs (Part 2); 160 µs (Part 3) | About 90 µs |
| On-chip storage | About 32 MB (inferred from performance decline) | Replaced by a description based on the 2 MiB single-operand threshold and the bank structure; no total capacity is given |

The closing section of Part 4 lists problems that the author did not resolve. These include the maximum length of a program (383 blocks is only a compiler limit); how the MAC array, the planar engine, and DMA overlap within the same task (the independent branches the author measured save only about 1.1%, and all of these are whole-graph timings); the number of DMA engines and their queue depth; the number of channels and the clock frequency of the MAC array; the scope of effect of sync records; and the dual-engine design of the M6 [4]. Chapters 5, 6, 7, 9, 10, and 11 of this report answer most of these questions.

### 2.2 Bryngelson's Multi-Generation Survey and ANEForge

#### 2.2.1 Scope and Method

Bryngelson's *Apple Neural Engine: Architecture, Programming, and Performance* [5] is a 302-page survey covering multiple generations of products, from the A11 to the A18 and from the M1 to the M5. It is currently the broadest literature on the ANE. The method of the book has two notable features. First, the compiler has a built-in constructor for each architecture generation, so the hardware parameter table of any generation can be obtained on any machine. The author used this to obtain the parameter tables of 28 targets and compare them byte by byte, and then determined the meaning of the fields by decompiling the read functions that take the parameter table as an argument. Second, every conclusion in the book is labeled as measured, decompiled, or predicted. Measurements were made only on the M1 and M5 platforms, with a small number of measurements on the M2, and the conclusions for intermediate generations derive from decompilation or prediction.

#### 2.2.2 Main Conclusions

Table 2-2 summarizes, by object of study, the main conclusions of the book that are relevant to this report.

*Table 2-2. Principal conclusions of Bryngelson [5]*

| Subject | Conclusion |
| --- | --- |
| Software stack | Below Core ML and the Espresso runtime lie the AppleNeuralEngine framework and the daemon aned. The kernel contains the ANE driver, a multi-die load balancer, and clock and power management. The interior of the ANE is driven by a control core running RTKit. The host only writes to mailboxes and rings the doorbell, and does not access compute registers directly. |
| Program | A program is a static sequence of task descriptors, with no program counter, microcode, or data-dependent branches (decompilation). There are 14 versions of the task descriptor, and the M5 uses v20. The driver executes only one command at a time. On the M1, two threads submitting simultaneously are only 1.04× faster (measured). |
| Compute | Core counts come in two conventions, marketing and architectural. The architectural count is 4 for the M1 and 16 for the M5. Each core outputs 4 FP16 channels per cycle, or 8 when INT8 is packed (decompilation). The first level of the reduction tree is radix-4 FP16 partial sums, and the second level is an FP32-class accumulator (measured on the M1). The accumulator file has depth 8. On the M1, when the output channels increase from 192 to 256, the time jumps from 20 µs to 61 µs (measured). Each layer has a fixed overhead of about 44 cycles. The MAC array and the planar engine can issue at the same time. Winograd supports two variants, F(2×2,3×3) and F(4×4,3×3). |
| Clock | The M1 runs at about 1.14 GHz and the M5 at about 1.89 GHz (measured). The ANE itself has no DVFS table, and its frequency is determined by SoC power management. The M1 does not reduce its frequency during 210 s of continuous operation. |
| Storage | The single-operand limit is 2 MB, the same across generations. The threshold measured by timing is 2.28–2.34 MB on the M1 and 4.72 MB on the M5. The L2 has 64 banks with 16-byte interleaving. Resident weights are 64 KB, and streamed weights are up to 16 MB. On-chip storage is managed by the compiler and is not a cache. |
| Bandwidth | On the M1, the DRAM ceiling is 85 GB/s, the weight stream is 51 GB/s, and the activation stream is 24 GB/s. On the M5, the weight stream is about 145 GB/s and the activation stream is about 24 GB/s (measured). |
| Scheduling overhead | On the M1, each call takes about 190 µs. This comprises about 25 µs in user space and the runtime, about 16 µs for building the firmware request, 2–3 µs for ringing the doorbell, about 130 µs for the firmware round trip, and about 10 µs for kernel completion processing. Each call involves about two interrupts (measured). |
| Power | The entire block is powered off when idle. The power domains consist of an always-on base domain and several compute clusters that power up on demand. On the M1, the first call after 5 s of idleness takes about 260 ms. The firmware contains no temperature or throttling logic. |
| Numerics | The accumulator is FP32-class. Output rounding is round-half-to-even, and input NaNs become +inf. On the M1, the accumulation stage flushes subnormal numbers to zero, whereas the M5 preserves them. The M1 and the A14 saturate at 32768, whereas the M5 does not saturate. Activation functions are 33-point piecewise-linear lookup tables. |
| Program format | The compiled artifact is a Mach-O file containing segments for the task-descriptor stream, the weights and lookup tables, and the input and output declarations. The version information records the full set of compilation parameters. |

The book also infers the dataflow of the ANE from the names of the 24 hardware performance counters of each task descriptor. Data enters L2 or the weight storage from the fabric, is computed by the NEs and written back to L2, and then, after processing by the planar engine, is written back to DRAM. These counters cannot be read in user space.

#### 2.2.3 Multi-Die and Multi-Engine Designs

The book's analysis of multi-die products is the most relevant to the M6. At the kernel level, the load balancer supports up to four dies and dispatches requests to the most idle die as whole submissions, with no tensor exchange between dies (decompilation). At the firmware level, the firmware first attempts the endpoint of the second engine and falls back to the first engine on failure. At the compiler level, there is an intermediate representation for collective communication, including `all_reduce` and `all_gather`, and cross-die transfers go through a dedicated DMA. However, the enable bit for this function is 0 on all 28 targets, and the corresponding setter functions are assertions. That is, the function exists but is not enabled.

#### 2.2.4 Differences from the M6 and Inconsistencies in the Book

The book contains no data for the M6, and its description of the H18 generation does not agree with the observations of this report on the M6. The book holds that H18 is a single-die product with four cores per engine, that its FP8 (E4M3) capability bit is 1, and that its multi-die conflict-tracking and remote-dependency capability bits are 0. By contrast, each engine of the M6 has 16 NEs, and the two engines can cooperate to execute the same program (Chapters 4 and 10). The book's conclusions about the accumulator and rounding also differ from the measurements of this report on the M4 and M6 (Section 8.6). In addition, the book contains several internal inconsistencies. The size of the parameter table is given as both 0x938 and 0x348. The generation in which the MAC saturation problem was fixed is given as A15 in one place and M5 in another. The maximum width of FP16 convolution kernels is given as 16 starting from A14 in one place and as 15 starting from A16 in another. The base model of the M5 is described inconsistently. The measured slope of about 12 TFLOPS on the M1 also exceeds what the expression "4 cores × 4 output channels per cycle × 1.14 GHz" can account for. Section 5.4 of this report also finds that the parameter-table layout of the compiler version used here differs from the book's. The offsets in the book must be increased by displacements ranging from 0x8 to 0xb8 in different sections.

#### 2.2.5 ANEForge

The same author's ANEForge [6] is a Python tool that computes directly on the ANE. It compiles a lazily evaluated tensor compute graph into a single ANE program, covering 58 fused operators and 19 native bridging operators. It supports training, native fused attention, and Int8, Int4, and sparse weights. Programs are submitted through the private `e5rt` interface of Espresso to aned and the kernel driver for execution. On the M5 Pro, the author measured a scheduling floor of about 70 µs per call. ResNet-18 takes 0.33 ms per inference on the ANE, compared with 2.0 ms on the GPU and 6.0 ms on the CPU. Under sustained load, the ANE power rail is about 4.5 W. The author also points out the limitations of the ANE. It exposes only one hardware channel, so requests execute in sequence. Moreover, FP16's range prevents the sampling loop of diffusion models from converging correctly [6].

### 2.3 Community Documentation and Open-Source Drivers

The neural-engine documentation [8] maintained by Matthijs Hollemans since 2020 is the earliest and most frequently cited community resource on the ANE. It summarizes the ANE core counts of product generations from the A11 to the M5, and the official compute figures up to the M4. It lists known Core ML layer types that cannot run on the ANE, such as custom layers, recurrent networks, gather, dilated convolutions, pooling with kernels larger than 13 or strides larger than 2, and upsampling by more than 2×. It also introduces methods for determining whether a model executes on the ANE, such as observing the `H11ANEServicesThread` thread, setting a breakpoint on `-[_ANEModel program]`, or using the time profiler in Instruments. The documentation holds that the ANE uses FP16 throughout internally, and it found no evidence that convolutions execute natively with quantized weights. Its author states that no one outside Apple truly understands the internal workings of the ANE, and that some conclusions about unsupported layers are speculative.

Eileen Yoon's ANE driver for Asahi Linux [9] is the only public implementation that can drive the ANE under Linux. It supports the M1, M1 Pro, M1 Max, and M1 Ultra, and its last commit was in 2023. It consists of three parts: a kernel module based on the DRM accelerator framework, a user-space library, and Python bindings with matrix multiplication and super-resolution tests. Its source code reveals the hardware submission interface of the ANE on the M1. Buffers are mapped through DART, and the address window begins at 0x4000. A submission request carries the task size and the number and size of the task descriptors, and it fills in addresses for up to 32 buffer slots. The program and the weights are placed in a single buffer aligned to 16 bytes. The task manager and the task queues are located at fixed offsets of the engine. There are eight task queues in total, with priority values from 1 to 6 and two reserved values. The driver waits for the engine to become idle by polling, and it suspends automatically after one second of idleness. It is worth noting that this driver writes the task-manager registers directly and does not go through the ANE firmware. The eight job queues and six priority levels that this report observes in the M6 firmware (Section 6.4) are consistent with this driver, whereas on macOS these queues are managed by the RTKit firmware.

There are two smaller reverse-engineering efforts as well. geohot's ANE experiments in the tinygrad project [26] parse the format of compiled artifacts and the register fields in them. They describe the ANE as "a convolution-centric DMA engine" and record constraints, such as that the stride must be a multiple of 0x40 and that UInt8, Int8, and FP16 are supported. Full execution requires authorization or modification of the kernel. The `ane` repository by mdaiter [23] analyzed, in early 2026, the layer-description structures, compiler passes, and daemons in ANECompiler, Espresso, and the AppleNeuralEngine framework. It found that when the corresponding authorization is missing, compilation and loading calls silently return empty values. Neither of these works involves hardware-level measurement.

### 2.4 Application Studies on Deployment and Training

Kumaresan's Orion [7] takes the M4 Max as its platform and bypasses Core ML. It calls the private `_ANEClient` and `_ANECompiler` interfaces directly, compiles MIL text into ANE programs, and is used for training and inference of large language models. The author compiled 20 usage constraints on the ANE. Of these, 14 are reported for the first time, such as inputs and outputs being ordered alphabetically by name, IOSurface buffers having a minimum size of about 49 KB, the size rules for buffers with multiple inputs and outputs, and the problem that output variables may disappear after optimization. The other six agree with earlier reports, such as a process being able to compile about 119 times, SDPA silently ignoring the causal mask, and 1×1 convolution being about 3× faster than matrix multiplication. The author implemented a compiler with five passes, namely dead-code elimination, identity elimination, cast fusion, SRAM annotation, and constraint checking. By patching weights rather than recompiling, the author reduced the per-step recompilation time during training from 4.2 s to 0.49 s. The author reports a decoding speed for GPT-2 124M of more than 170 tokens per second, a measured FP16 peak of about 19 TFLOPS, and a per-dispatch overhead of about 0.095 ms.

Some constraints reported in Orion, such as "gelu is not supported" and "convolution bias is not supported", differ from the results obtained through Core ML compilation in this report. In our experiments, gelu is implemented by a 33-point lookup table (Section 8.5), and convolution bias participates in the computation as the initial value of the accumulator (Section 8.2.3). We speculate that this difference arises from the two different paths by which the models enter the compiler, and that the constraints listed by Orion belong to its direct-call path 〔inferred〕. The constraints of Orion arise mainly at the software and compiler level, and they do not cover microarchitecture, clocks, frequency scaling, or power.

### 2.5 Apple's Deployment Guides

Apple's article *Deploying Transformers on the Apple Neural Engine* [24], published in 2022, and the accompanying ml-ane-transformers code repository [25] are among the earliest public materials Apple released on ANE deployment. In 2024, Apple published *Deploying Attention-Based Vision Transformers to Apple Neural Engine* [29], which discusses the deployment of vision Transformers on the ANE. For Transformers, the articles give a concrete set of recommendations. They recommend changing the tensor layout from (B, S, C) to a channel-first four-dimensional layout (B, C, 1, S), and replacing fully connected layers with two-dimensional convolutions. They recommend making the last dimension contiguous and aligned to 64 bytes, because otherwise padding can increase memory usage by 32–64×. They recommend splitting the Q, K, and V of attention by head, so that they remain in L2 and use more cores, and avoiding reshapes and transposes. Finally, they recommend treating this class of workloads as bandwidth-limited, and alleviating the limit by increasing the batch size or by quantization and pruning. After rewriting according to these recommendations, distilbert's speed increased by up to about 10×, and its peak memory decreased to about 1/14. On an iPhone 13, with sequence length 128 and batch size 1, each inference takes about 3.47 ms at a power of about 0.454 W [24].

These recommendations are corroborated by several findings of this report. The hardware assigns a layer to the NEs by output channel (Section 5.6). The 1×1 convolution is the most direct use of the MAC array (Chapter 7). The compiler pads the strides of tensors to avoid bank conflicts (Section 9.2.3). Whether intermediate results can remain in L2 has a large effect on performance, and the bandwidth for reading weights and activations is the bottleneck of many workloads (Chapter 9). However, the guide gives only practices. It does not explain their hardware rationale, and it does not address multiple engines, clocks, or power.

### 2.6 Studies of Other NPUs and Methodological Inspiration

Among NPUs from other vendors, public research mainly appears in papers published by chip designers. Google's TPU papers [12, 13] describe a data-center accelerator centered on a systolic array, and report measured power. Mobile NPU papers by Samsung and MediaTek, published at ISSCC and ISCA [14–16], describe phone NPUs that exploit techniques such as sparsity and reconfigurable dataflow, and they report chip-level energy efficiency. Eyeriss [21] proposes the row-stationary dataflow and is representative of research on dataflow accelerators. These works are written by the designers and can directly report internal structure and per-component power. This report, by contrast, is an empirical study conducted from outside on a commercial accelerator, so the methods and the available information differ between the two. Section 13.7 uses the data from these papers to compare the energy efficiency of the M6 ANE.

Methodologically, reverse-engineering studies of undisclosed processors provide inspiration for this report. Dougall Johnson's work on the Apple G13 GPU instruction set [10] recovered the instruction set and execution model of an undisclosed GPU by systematically compiling test programs, comparing the generated binaries, and measuring execution results. The Asahi Linux ANE driver [9] shows that the ANE hardware interface can be understood from the driver level. This report adopts the approach of compiling, comparing, and measuring. However, because of the constraints of the study, it does not execute any modified program.

### 2.7 Relationship between This Report and Prior Work

Table 2-3 compares the coverage of maderix [4], Bryngelson [5], and this report, by object of study.

*Table 2-3. Comparison of this report with the two main prior works*

| Object of study | maderix [4] | Bryngelson [5] | This report |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Platform | M4 | A11–M5 (M1 and M5 measured) | M6 (partial control experiments on the M4) |
| Execution path | Private interfaces; executes modified compiled artifacts | Private interfaces and decompilation | Executes only through Core ML; compiled artifacts are compiled but not executed |
| Program format | Segment structure, W8A8 configuration word, field-modification experiments | TD versions, register groups | Encoding of register-write packets and BARs; correspondence of TD layout versions; dual-ANE programs |
| Scheduling | About 90 µs per call; two concurrent requests save about 16% | Breakdown of scheduling overhead; a single pending execution queue | Three-layer structure of driver and firmware; time breakdown by stage; influence of host CPU idle depth; concurrency across processes and programs |
| Compute | Peak throughput; output-channel grouping; W8A8 | Output-channel grouping; core allocation; Winograd | Multiply-accumulate operations per NE per cycle; convolution-kernel shapes and rewriting; hardware Winograd; PE throughput |
| Numerics | 33-point lookup table | FP32-class accumulator; rounding; saturation | Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator; bias; exponent rules for subnormal products; INT8 accumulator; lookup-table structure |
| Storage | 2 MiB rule; 64 banks | On-chip thresholds; bandwidth | L2 capacity and tiling rules; bank padding; bandwidth of weight and activation paths; DRAM structure |
| Dual engine | Speculative (not measured) | Request allocation across multiple dies (decompilation) | Split cost model; scaling efficiency; shared bandwidth and uneven work partitioning |
| Clocks and frequency scaling | Not measured | Frequencies of the M1 and M5 | Back-calculation of full-load frequency; shared cluster clock; CLPC utilization control and approximate model |
| Power | Full-load power of the M4 | Not systematically measured | Measurement method that subtracts the CPU cluster; power decomposition; DVFS state and energy |
| Applications | On-device training (Part 3); comparison with GPU and CPU | ResNet-18 and attention in ANEForge | Comparison of large language models, image classification, and text recognition on the M4 and M6, interpreted with microarchitectural conclusions |

Compared with existing work, this report has four distinctive features. First, it is the first systematic study of the M6 and its dual-engine structure. Second, under the constraint of not executing any modified program, it combines compiled artifacts, firmware timestamps, and power-rail measurements to measure the multiply-accumulate width and clock frequency that maderix could not measure. Third, it extends the scope of study from the ANE itself to the host side. Driver scheduling, CPU idle depth, and the CLPC frequency control jointly determine the practical performance and energy consumption of the ANE, and existing work has not systematically studied this part. Fourth, it relates conclusions from microbenchmarks to practical workloads such as large language models, image classification, and text recognition, and it uses the former to explain phenomena in the latter.

## 3. Platform and Methodology

This chapter describes the experimental platforms, research constraints, observation methods, and measurement protocols of this report, and it defines the evidence categories used throughout the report. The evidence category annotated for each conclusion in Chapters 4–13, and the measurement protocol followed by each experiment, are defined in this chapter.

### 3.1 Experimental Platform

All microbenchmark measurements in this report were performed on one M6, with one M4 serving as a control. Table 3-1 lists the configurations of the two machines. The application benchmarks in Chapter 14 were run on two Mac mini systems of the same model and system version, one M6 and one M4, each with 16 GB of memory and 256 GB of storage.

*Table 3-1. Experimental platforms*

| Item    | M6 (subject of study)                                                                                                                  | M4 (control)                   |
| ------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| SoC     | T8152                                                                                                                                  | T8132                          |
| Process | 2 nm                                                                                                                                   | 3 nm                           |
| ANE architecture | h18g, 2 engines, 16 NEs each                                                                                                  | h16g, 1 engine, 16 NEs         |
| CPU     | 2 S cores, 4 P cores, 6 E cores                                                                                                        | 4 P cores, 6 E cores           |
| Memory  | 16 GB; the system reports the type as LPDDR5 (inferred to be an LPDDR5X-class part from the 10656 MT/s rate), 8 channels, single rank | 16 GB, 8 channels, about 7467 MT/s |
| Operating system | macOS 27.0.1 (26A434)                                                                                                         | macOS 27.0.1 (26A434)          |
| ANE compiler | `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`                                                                                                        | `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`    |
| Model generation | coremltools 9.0, PyTorch 2.7                                                                                                  | coremltools 9.0, PyTorch 2.7   |

The two machines serve different purposes. The M4 is used to write tools, analyze data, and write this report. The M6 is used to generate models and to compile and run all experiments. The experimental tools are maintained on the M4, synchronized to the M6 for compilation and execution, and the results are copied back to the M4 for analysis. Experiments that require a control on the M4, such as the numerical tests of Chapter 8, the power-rail checks of Chapter 13, and the graph-depth tests of Section 5.10, are repeated on the M4 with the same programs. The zero-activation power measurement on the M4 in Section 13.3 is an exception. The control M4 carried out routine work during the measurement period, which disturbed the SMC readings too strongly, so this set of measurements was performed instead on a second, idle M4 MacBook Air (Mac16,12) that uses the same SoC. The Core ML operator-dispatch experiment of Section 5.2 was also performed on this M4 laptop, and its results for the M6 target were cross-checked against the ahead-of-time compilation driver built into Espresso. In addition, compiler-debugging experiments that involve compilation but not execution (Sections 5.7.8, 5.8.3, 5.9, 7.8, and 15.3) were performed on the M4 with the M6 target h18g. The two machines run the same compiler version, and the artifacts we checked matched the results on the M6. For example, the size of the HWX in Section 5.9 is exactly identical to that on the M6.

### 3.2 Research Constraints

This report adheres to the following four constraints. First, models are executed only through the public interfaces of Core ML, and private execution interfaces such as `_ANEClient` are not used. Second, the compilation interface `ANECCompile` is used only for compilation, and the resulting compiled artifacts are only read and compared; no modified compiled artifact is loaded or executed. Third, system observation is read-only: IOReport, SMC, and IORegistry are only read, and kdebug only records events. Fourth, the ANE firmware and kernelcache are subjected only to static, read-only analysis. Apart from static analysis, the compiler is observed only through debugger observation within this report's own compilation process (see below). The firmware and kernelcache themselves, as well as the compiler's disassembly, are not redistributed with this report.

Under these constraints, three special cases require explanation. First, in the microbenchmark experiments, the steps that require root privileges fall into only five categories: starting a kdebug tracing session, reading the kernel log, terminating the relevant system processes after a compilation service crashes or runs out of control, extracting from the cache directory of aned the Core ML models that are handed to the compiler (Section 5.9), and debugging this report's own compilation process read-only with `sudo lldb` to determine the output switch of the performance-model CSV (see the third case below). The application benchmarks in Chapter 14 additionally run `powermetrics` as root. All of these steps are executed with `sudo`, and the tracing scripts run the workloads internally as an ordinary user.

Second, to obtain the compiler's per-TD estimates of the performance model (Sections 7.1 and 7.8), we used a dynamic library to treat the compiler's internal-build checks as true within this report's own compilation process. To obtain the Core ML segmenter's estimates for other chips (Section 5.2), we ran, within this report's own process, the ahead-of-time compilation driver built into Espresso in a mode that performs only segmentation and does not invoke the ANE compiler. We used a dynamic library to intercept the estimates that it writes to the system log. To make the segmenter output these estimates, we temporarily wrote into the user's global defaults domain a key that enables cost logging. The key was effective for all processes of that user during the experiments and was deleted when the experiments ended. Apart from this temporary defaults key, these measures act only on this report's own processes and do not modify any system file or system security setting. The resulting compiled artifacts were likewise only read and not executed.

Third, we used lldb to debug this report's own compilation process on the M4, targeting h18g, for four kinds of analysis: recording the parameters with which the compiler writes TD fields (Section 5.7.8), verifying the rules of the dual-ANE split (Section 5.8.3), tracing the file and memory-mapping calls made during compilation (Section 5.9), and determining the causal relationship between a field of the HAL parameter table and a compilation decision (Section 15.3). For the last of these, the individual fields in the process's memory were changed to the values of a parameter table from another generation before the compiler copied the table. To this end, we added a debugging-permitting signature (`get-task-allow`) to the `anecc` we compiled ourselves, which requires neither root privileges nor developer mode. These operations likewise act only on that process, and the resulting compiled artifacts are read only and not executed. In addition, to determine the output switch of the performance-model CSV (Section 7.1), we once debugged this report's own compilation process with `sudo lldb` on the M6. We only set breakpoints and read registers and memory, and we rewrote nothing (Appendix A).

These constraints serve two purposes. The first is to ensure that the research relies only on public, stable execution paths, so that its conclusions can be reproduced by others using the same methods. The second is to avoid the system instability that executing modified programs could cause. The limitations they introduce are discussed in Section 16.1.

### 3.3 Observation Methods

Under the above constraints, this report observes the ANE at both the software and hardware levels. Table 3-2 summarizes the observation methods and their purposes.

*Table 3-2. Observation methods*

| Method | Observed quantity | Root required | Main tools |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core ML timing | Per-call latency; per-layer execution time obtained from the slope of latency against layer count | No | [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py), [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m), [runall.sh](../tools/common/runall.sh) |
| IOReport | Per-ANE interrupt counts (identifying which ANE is used and whether execution falls back to the CPU); DRAM link bandwidth; CPU cluster power histograms; clock-on time; voltage-domain states; throttling events | No | [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m), [anewho.c](../tools/common/anewho.c), [iorlist.c](../tools/common/iorlist.c), [pclus.c](../tools/common/pclus.c) |
| kdebug firmware events | Timestamps of ANE task start and end, driver submission, firmware messages, interrupts, driver completion, and return to the user thread | Yes | `*_ktrace.sh` scripts under `tools/*/` and the corresponding analysis scripts |
| SMC power rails | PP0b (the ANE and the `PACC0` cluster of the CPU, that is, the S and P cores), memory power rail, whole-system power | No | [smcpower.c](../tools/common/smcpower.c) |
| Device tree and IORegistry | ANE device nodes, frequency tables, power domains, memory channels | No | `ioreg` |
| Read-only compiler calls | Hardware parameter tables and SoC parameters; compiled artifacts obtained by direct compilation | No | [dump2.c](../tools/re/dump2.c), [socfreq.c](../tools/re/socfreq.c), [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m) |
| Core ML segmentation analysis | Per-backend latency estimated by the segmenter for each operation, the selected backend, and the segmentation results | No | [opd_ana.sh](../tools/03_compile/opd_ana.sh), [computeplan.swift](../tools/common/computeplan.swift), [aotdrv.cpp](../tools/common/aotdrv.cpp), [oslogtap.c](../tools/common/oslogtap.c) |
| Compiled-artifact parsing | Segment structure, TD sequences, register-write packets, convolution configuration words, weight splits | No | [hwx_parse.py](../tools/lib/hwx_parse.py), [tdpkt.py](../tools/lib/tdpkt.py), [tdwalk.py](../tools/lib/tdwalk.py), [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py) |
| Static reverse engineering | Machine code, call relations, address references, and jump tables of compiler functions; readers of parameter-table fields; strings and functions in the kernelcache and firmware | No | [tools/re/](../tools/re/) (including [immscan2.c](../tools/re/immscan2.c)) |
| Compiler debugger observation | Parameters of the TD field-write functions, and file and memory-mapping calls, in this report's own compilation process; changes to individual fields of the in-process HAL parameter table to observe changes in compilation decisions (performed on the M4; for the output switch of the performance-model CSV, a single read-only debugging session with `sudo lldb` was run on the M6) | No (except the one run on the M6) | [tdtrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace_lldb.py), [swaptrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/swaptrace_lldb.py), [halpatch_lldb.py](../tools/re/halpatch_lldb.py) |
| Numerical comparison | Constructing inputs and weights and comparing the outputs of the ANE and the CPU | No | [tools/06_numerics/](../tools/06_numerics/) |
| GPU comparison | GPU memory bandwidth, used in experiments in which the ANE and the GPU share DRAM | No | [membw.swift](../tools/common/membw.swift) |
| Application benchmark monitoring | ANE busy fraction and memory read bandwidth during actual application runs; power of each unit and of the whole system (Chapter 14) | Partially | anemon [27], `powermetrics`, SMC |

These methods complement each other in temporal resolution and in the level of observation. Core ML timing is the easiest to obtain and requires no privileges, but it measures the duration of the entire call, which includes host-side overhead. The firmware events from kdebug, given at the resolution of a 24 MHz timer, provide the start and end times of ANE tasks and the times of the host's stages, so that the execution time of the ANE itself can be separated from the call. However, they require root privileges, and in some experiments the task-start events of single-ANE programs were either not recorded or largely absent (Section 6.1). IOReport and SMC provide counters and power at second-level resolution and are suited to observing average steady-state behavior. Device trees, compiler parameters, and compiled artifacts provide static structural information that is used to explain the measured results. A conclusion that is supported by both static information and measured results is the most credible, and Section 7.1 gives a specific illustration of this.

The experiments in this report are grouped by number, corresponding to the records in the laboratory notes. Group A covers static structure, Group B the compute array, Group C storage and bandwidth, Group D scheduling, Group E power, Group F numerics, Group G summaries, and Group H supplementary experiments outside the original plan. The tools, data, and root requirements of each experiment group are listed in Appendix A.

### 3.4 Measurement Protocol

All experiments follow the measurement protocol below. The protocol was developed step by step in response to specific disturbances encountered during the experiments.

For model generation, test models are constructed by scripts in MIL or PyTorch and converted to Core ML models with coremltools (primary tool: [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py)). During conversion, the default loading step of coremltools is disabled. Otherwise, generating a model would compile the ANE program one additional time, and the cache of that compilation would not be reused by later loads. Each model is generated in a separate process. To separate weight reads from computation, models of the same shape are usually built in two versions: one in which all layers share a single set of weights, and one in which each layer has different weights.

For confirmation of execution, every experiment reads the interrupt counts of each of the two ANEs from IOReport, in order to confirm that the model is actually executed on the ANE and to distinguish which ANE is used. This step is necessary because Core ML silently executes small models, standalone softmax or relu operations, and some sampling-type operations on the CPU without giving any indication (Section 5.2 gives the segmenter's decision rules). For numerical experiments, we add to the model, after the test operation, a convolutional branch unrelated to the test, so that the entire model is assigned to the ANE. We execute the same model in `CPU_ONLY` mode as a control (Section 8.1).

For timing, throughput and per-layer time are always obtained with the slope method. The shape of the layers is fixed, only the number of layers is varied, and the slope of time against layer count is taken as the marginal time per layer. This subtracts the fixed overhead of each call, which consists of about 30 µs of submission-side overhead and 130–325 µs of completion-side overhead (Sections 6.5 and 7.1). Before each measurement, a baseline of idle time is recorded. The model is then called continuously for at least 1 s as warm-up, and no idle period is inserted between the warm-up and the measurement. This is because the NE clock typically takes 100–200 ms to rise from the lowest state to above 2448 MHz; after an idle period, recovering the steady state takes about 0.25–0.36 s; the clock domain of the PE needs about 0.5–0.8 s; and idle time between calls causes the CLPC to lower the state (Chapters 11 and 12). Each point is measured in at least two rounds, and the 10th percentile and the median are checked for agreement. The difference between two rounds is usually less than 1%. For any measurement that needs to exclude host overhead, the ANE task duration obtained from kdebug is used instead.

For power, ANE power is obtained by subtracting the power of the CPU `PACC0` cluster from PP0b, and then subtracting the same difference measured with the ANE powered off (Section 13.1). Because SMC refreshes roughly once per second, each workload runs continuously for about 12 s, and the average from 4 s after the workload starts until just before it ends is taken.

The application benchmarks in Chapter 14 adopt the application-benchmark protocol. Both machines are plugged into power, other applications and iCloud sync are turned off, and the same model file, verified by SHA256, is used. Each test is run formally once. Repeats built into the tool are recorded as usual, and when an anomaly occurs, the test is rerun and the cause is recorded. Speed tests run without any monitoring enabled. When the busy fraction and power are needed, a separate run with monitoring is performed, so speeds under monitoring are reported for reference only. When energy efficiency is compared, the whole-system power minus the idle power is used as the primary basis (Section 14.1).

On system state, the M6 has only 16 GB of memory, so large-scale experiments are not run in parallel, and sufficient free storage is kept available. The compiler service occasionally crashes when compiling very deep graphs, after which all subsequent model loads are blocked (defect A2). When this happens, the aned process must be terminated before the experiment is restarted.

### 3.5 Evidence Categories

At the end of each conclusion, this report indicates its evidence category in tortoise-shell brackets 〔〕; for example, " 〔HWX, HAL〕". Here HWX refers to the compiled artifacts emitted by the ANE compiler (file extension .hwx), and HAL refers to the hardware parameter tables built into the compiler. The compiler names these parameter tables HAL, which we speculate is an abbreviation of hardware abstraction layer. A conclusion may have several kinds of evidence. Table 3-3 defines the evidence categories used throughout this report.

*Table 3-3. Evidence categories*

| Label | Evidence source | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| timing | Call timing through the public Core ML interfaces | Usually corrected for the fixed overhead with the slope method |
| kdebug | ANE firmware and driver event timestamps in kdebug | Requires root; records events only |
| IOReport | Counters and statistics from IOReport | Interrupt counts, bandwidth, power histograms, voltage and frequency states, and similar; no root required |
| SMC | Power readings of the SMC power rails | ANE power has the CPU cluster subtracted |
| HWX | Contents of compiled artifacts | Compiled but not executed; includes byte-wise comparison of artifacts for different models or targets |
| HAL | Hardware parameter tables built into the compiler | Field meanings are inferred from the functions that read them |
| compiler | Disassembly and internal parameters of the ANE compiler, and the output of the Core ML segmenter | Includes the performance model's estimates, SoC parameters, the effects of compilation options, and the segmenter's per-operation estimates and segmentation results |
| firmware | Static analysis of the ANE firmware | Strings, assertions, and a small amount of disassembly |
| kernel | Static analysis of the driver and CLPC in the kernelcache, and the kernel log | Same as above |
| ioreg | Properties in the device tree and IORegistry | Read-only |
| numerical | Constructing inputs and comparing the outputs of the ANE and the CPU | See Section 8.1 |
| inferred | Deduced from the evidence above but not directly measured | The reasoning is explained in the text |

Conclusions drawn from the literature are labeled with reference numbers and receive no separate evidence category. For conclusions supported by multiple kinds of evidence, we list the direct evidence first. Any conclusion labeled "inferred" should be understood as an explanation that awaits further verification rather than as an established fact.

### 3.6 Data and Tools

All experimental tools, raw data, and analysis scripts of this report are stored in the code repository that is published together with it. The `data/` and `tools/` directories of the repository are each divided into subdirectories by research topic. The subdirectory numbers differ from the chapter numbers. For example, `05_compute` corresponds to the compute-array experiments of Chapter 7, and `10_clpc` corresponds to the frequency-scaling experiments of Chapter 12. In addition, `tools/common/` holds the runners and samplers shared across chapters, `tools/lib/` holds the parsing libraries for compiled artifacts, and `tools/re/` holds read-only reverse-engineering tools. The data and tools cited in the text are all given as relative links. Large raw files, such as compiled artifacts and raw kdebug traces, are published separately as compressed archives. The compiler's disassembly, raw exports of the parameter tables, and the firmware and kernelcache are derived from Apple binaries and are not distributed with the repository. Readers can regenerate them on their own machines with the tools in `tools/re/`. The data from the application benchmarks in Chapter 14 are currently kept in our benchmark records and have not yet been organized into the code repository. Appendix A lists the tools and reproduction commands for each experiment by section, and Appendix B gives an index of the data directories.

## 4. Architecture Overview

This chapter presents the overall structure of the M6 ANE as a reference for the chapters that follow. The arguments in this chapter rely mainly on device-tree (IORegistry) information, the hardware parameter tables built into the compiler, and compiled artifacts. The detailed measurements and analyses are developed in Chapters 5–13. In summary, the M6 integrates two complete ANEs, each containing 16 NEs. The two ANEs are located on the same die and each has its own power gating, but in the observations of this report the two are always powered on and off together (Section 12.5). The compiler generates two programs for every model, one single-ANE and one dual-ANE, and the runtime selects one of them for execution. The DVFS state of the ANE is adjusted by the CLPC in the host kernel according to utilization. We have found no evidence that the ANE itself or its firmware participates in selecting the state (Section 6.4).

### 4.1 Principal Parameters

Table 4-1 summarizes the principal parameters measured or derived in this report, and it indicates the evidence category and the section in which each is discussed in detail. The row "theoretical peak" is obtained by multiplying the number of multiply-accumulate operations per NE per cycle, the number of NEs, and the clock frequency at full load. The row "measured marginal throughput" is obtained from the slope of time against layer count for a fixed shape with varying layer count, and it does not include the fixed overhead of each call.

*Table 4-1. Principal parameters of the M6 ANE*

| Item | Value | Evidence | Section |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Number of engines | 2 (`ane`, `ane1`), on the same die, each with its own power gating | ioreg | 4.3 |
| NEs per engine | 16 | ioreg, HAL, HWX | 4.3, 5.6 |
| Multiply-accumulate operations per NE per cycle | FP16: 256; INT8: 512 | timing, kdebug, compiler | 7 |
| NE clock | 32 DVFS states, with an effective range of 852–2580 MHz | ioreg, kdebug | 11 |
| Theoretical peak (direct convolution) | FP16: 21.1 TFLOPS per ANE, 42.3 TFLOPS for two ANEs; INT8: 42.3 TOPS per ANE, 84.5 TOPS for two ANEs | Derived from the data above | 7 |
| Measured marginal throughput (FP16) | 512-channel 1×1 convolution chain with shared weights: 20.6 TFLOPS for one ANE and 39.5 TFLOPS for two ANEs, a ratio of 1.92; a 32×32 chain with different weights in each layer reaches about 40.9 TFLOPS (Section 7.7) | timing | 7, 10 |
| On-chip storage | The compiler sets a 64 KiB resident-weight limit per NE and uses 2 MiB of each ANE's L2, which is divided into 64 banks; the physical capacity is unknown | HAL, HWX, timing | 7, 9 |
| Data movement | Each TD reads at most two and writes at most one DRAM tensor; activation movement is about 35–40 GB/s per ANE; weight reads are about 150 GB/s (145–159 GB/s measured across experiments) | kdebug, timing | 7, 9 |
| Memory | 8 channels, 10656 MT/s, theoretical bandwidth 170.5 GB/s | ioreg, IOReport, compiler | 9 |
| Accumulator | 32-bit fixed-point (Q15.16) on the FP16 path; 32-bit integer on the INT8 path | numerical | 8 |
| Frequency scaling | The CLPC scales frequency according to utilization. When the idle time between calls is at most about 1 ms, the busy fraction settles at about 77%.
