Rising from the lowest state to above 2448 MHz typically takes 100–200 ms, and falling from the top state to the lowest takes about 140–170 ms, so the two proceed at comparable speeds | kdebug, kernel | 12 |
| Power at full load | Single ANE 6.2 W; dual-ANE 10.8 W. About 0.58 pJ per FP16 multiply-accumulate after subtracting shared overhead (0.72–0.79 pJ without subtraction) | SMC, IOReport | 13 |
| Idle power | 0 W; the ANE as a whole is powered off about 5.7 s after the last inference ends | SMC, IOReport, kernel | 12 |

### 4.2 Naming Hierarchy

The ANE of the M6 appears under different names at different levels of the system. These names belong to six levels: product, SoC, ANE architecture, compiler parameter set, kernel driver, and instruction format. The names are easily confused. Table 4-2 lists the meaning of each name and where it appears.

*Table 4-2. Identifiers associated with the M6 ANE*

| Identifier | Level | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| M6 | Product name | — |
| T8152 | SoC model | Kernel version `RELEASE_ARM64_T8152`; power-management and CLPC drivers `AppleT8152PMGR` and `AppleT8152CLPC` |
| h18g | Architecture name of the ANE, also the compilation target name | ioreg property `ArchitectureTypeStr`; compiler target class `TargetH18g` |
| 2026BaseLine | Parameter set used by the compiler for h18g; its hardware parameter table has the same contents as that of H19 (Section 5.4) | Hardware parameter table `ZinIrHal2026BaseLine`; SoC parameters (frequency tables, number of DRAM channels, and similar) `ZinIrSocVariantParams::Soc2026BaseLine` |
| H18 | Single-engine target of the generation before h18g (subtype 10, TD v20), and its parameter set | Compiler target class `TargetH18`, parameter table `ZinIrHalH18`, SoC parameters `ZinIrSocVariantParams::H18` |
| H11ANE, AppleH16ANEInterface | Device class name and driver bundle name in the kernel | `H11ANE` and `H11ANE1` in ioreg; driver `com.apple.driver.AppleH16ANEInterface` |
| TD v24 | Register-layout version of the task descriptor | Result of the lookup in the compiler function `ZinCpuSubtypeToArchValue` (Section 5.7.7) |

Two points need to be noted. First, although h18g and H18 have similar names, they are two distinct targets in the compiler. The constructor of `TargetH18g` calls `Soc2026BaseLine` rather than the SoC parameters of `H18`, and its hardware parameter table is `ZinIrHal2026BaseLine` rather than `ZinIrHalH18`. By the compiler's `ArchCompareValue`, H18 (subtype 10, TD v20) has the value 9, whereas h18g and h19 (subtype 11, TD v24) have the value 10. The H18 parameter set therefore describes the single-engine target of the previous generation, not a single-engine version of the M6 generation 〔compiler〕. Second, the "H11" and "H16" in the driver class names are inherited from earlier products and do not indicate the hardware generation. The hardware generation should be determined from `ArchitectureTypeStr` (h18g) and the TD layout version (v24) 〔ioreg, compiler〕.

### 4.3 Dual-Engine Design

In the IORegistry device tree, the M6 has two parallel ANE device nodes. Under each node, there are, in sequence, an ASC wrapper layer (`AppleASCWrapV8ANE`), a firmware coprocessor proxy running RTKit (`RTBuddy(ANE)` and `RTBuddy(ANE1)`), and an ANE device object (`H11ANE` and `H11ANE1`, both of class `H11ANEIn`). Both devices are managed by the same kernel driver bundle. The two share one address translation unit node, `dart-ane@19800000`, which has nine mappers in total: `mapper-ane`, `mapper-ane-iso1` through `mapper-ane-iso7`, and `mapper-ane-mpm`. Table 4-3 compares the main properties of the two devices (data: [m6_ioreg_ane_20261002.md](../data/02_overview/m6_ioreg_ane_20261002.md)) 〔ioreg〕.

*Table 4-3. Properties of the two ANE devices*

| Property | ANE0 | ANE1 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Device node | `ane@19600000` | `ane1@81600000` |
| `ArchitectureTypeStr` | h18g | h18g |
| `NumANECores` | 16 | 16 |
| `ANEVersion` / `ANEVersionMinor` | 240 / 17 | 240 / 17 |
| `ANECPUSubType` | 11 | 11 |
| `HWBoardType` / `HWBoardSubType` | 784 / 0 | 784 / 1 |
| `ane-id` / `die-id` / `die-ane-id` | 0 / 0 / 0 | 1 / 0 / 1 |
| `ane-type` / `ane-subtype` | 0x310 / 0 | 0x310 / 1 |
| `power-gates` | 0x1e, 0x1f | 0x20, 0x21 |
| `clock-ids` | 0x149, 0x148, 0x177 | 0x149, 0x148, 0x177 |
| `BondedModeSupported` | 1 | 1 |
| `SurfaceSharedEventAgentMask` | 128 | 256 |

Four conclusions follow from Table 4-3. First, the `die-id` of both ANEs is 0, and their `die-ane-id` values are 0 and 1, which indicates that they are on the same die rather than stitched together from two dies. Second, their `power-gates` differ, which indicates that their power can be gated separately. In our experiments, when only one ANE executes tasks, the other is also briefly powered on, and the power-off timing of the two is synchronized. The basis for this is the firmware-processor states and kernel driver logs of both ANEs recorded during idle periods (Section 12.5). Third, their `clock-ids` are identical, which indicates that their clocks have the same source. However, in the CLPC, each ANE has an independent performance domain (8 for ANE0 and 29 for ANE1), and the corresponding frequency tables `voltage-states8` and `voltage-states29` in the device tree each have 32 states with identical contents. Fourth, `ANECPUSubType` is 11 for both, which is the same as the Mach-O CPU subtype of h18g compiled artifacts (Section 5.5). This is consistent with the M6 executing h18g-format programs 〔inferred〕. The values of `SurfaceSharedEventAgentMask` (128 and 256) each occupy one bit on the two devices. We speculate that they distinguish the originator of each shared event 〔inferred〕.

Each ANE contains 16 NE units, which is shown in three respects. First, the ioreg property `NumANECores` has the value 16. Second, the field at offset 0x00c in the compiler parameter table is read by the functions `DetermineNumNesToUse`, `ComputeOcgSize`, and `ComputeNumActiveCoeffDMA`, and it gives the number of NEs per ANE. This field is 2 on the compiler target M9 (the A11 phone chip), 8 on H11 and H12, and 16 from H13 onward. Third, in the compiled artifact, the weights of each layer are split into exactly 16 parts, one per NE (Section 5.6) 〔ioreg, HAL, HWX〕. maderix [4] concluded from the "8 compute groups" in the parameter table that the ANE of the M4 consists of 8 compute units. In this version of the compiler's parameter table, the field with the value 8 (at offset 0x014) is read by `ComputeMaxOcgSize` and `HasAccDoubleBuffering`. It indicates the accumulator depth of each NE rather than the number of NEs. The relationship between this field and output-channel grouping is discussed in Chapter 7 〔HAL〕.

### 4.4 Platform Parameters in the Compiler

The compiler has a built-in set of SoC parameters for each target, which are used by the performance model and by memory allocation. Table 4-4 lists the `Soc2026BaseLine` parameters used for the M6 and compares them with those of the M4 (H16g) and with H18, the single-engine target of the previous generation (subtype 10, TD v20) (data: `socfreq*.txt` under [data/09_clock/](../data/09_clock/); tool: [socfreq.c](../tools/re/socfreq.c)) 〔compiler〕.

*Table 4-4. SoC parameters built into the compiler*

| Parameter set | Target | MCache (i.e., SLC) | DRAM channels | NE frequency table | Top DRAM state | DMA frequency table |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| H16g | M4 | 16 MiB | 8 | 26 states, 504–2364 MHz | 3.733 GHz | 534 / 712 / 1068 MHz |
| H18 | Single-engine h18 | 16 MiB | 4 | 30 states, 396–2172 MHz | 3.75 GHz | 450 / 600 / 900 / 1150 MHz |
| Soc2026BaseLine | M6 (h18g) | 16 MiB | 8 | 32 states, 804–2508 MHz | 5.328 GHz | 534 / 712 / 1068 / 1150 / 1308 MHz |

The top DRAM state of the M6 is 5.328 GHz, which corresponds to about 10656 MT/s at double data rate. The theoretical bandwidth of eight 16-bit channels is about 170.5 GB/s, which is consistent with the record of eight DCS (DRAM channel) entries in the device tree (Chapter 9). The first 24 states of the compiler's NE frequency table are identical to the frequency table in the device tree, but the last eight differ. The top state of the compiler is 2508 MHz, while that of the device tree is 2580 MHz. Section 11.2.2 fits the full-load frequency to the steps in kdebug task duration during frequency ramp-up. The result agrees with the device-tree top state of 2580 MHz and not with the compiler's top state of 2508 MHz, from which we infer that the compiler's frequency table is used only for its performance model 〔inferred〕. The "DMA frequency table" in the table is the compiler's internal name (function `GetValidDMAFrequencies`). Section 11.4 infers, from the fact that the frequency table of the Fabric DVFM module in the CLPC driver contains exactly the same five values, that the table actually describes the five frequency states of the SoC fabric.

### 4.5 Structural Diagrams

Figure 4-1 and Figure 4-2 are structural diagrams drawn from all results of this report. Figure 4-1 shows the hardware organization and data paths, and Figure 4-2 shows how an inference request travels from the application through system services, the kernel driver, and ANE firmware to the hardware. The values and positions of the components in the diagrams are determined to different degrees. The multiply-accumulate width, bandwidth, and clock states come from measurement. The 2 MiB of L2 and the 64 KiB per NE are the amounts used by the compiler, not physical capacities. The placement of L2 in the clock domain of the shared cluster is inferred, and the figures note this. Below, Figure 4-1 is explained first in four aspects (computation, data movement, dual-engine cooperation, and clocks and power), and the control path is then described with reference to Figure 4-2.

![Hardware structure of the M6 ANE](../figs/en/light/fig4-1_hardware.png)

*Figure 4-1. Hardware structure of the M6 ANE. ANE0 is drawn in expanded form, and ANE1, shown behind it, has the same structure. The lower part shows the DRAM and system-level cache shared by the two ANEs. Red lines denote the DRAM read and write paths; orange labels mark clock domains, and dashed labels indicate speculation. The 16×16 grid in the enlarged box only illustrates 256 FP16 multiply-accumulate operations per NE per cycle and does not represent the physical arrangement of the multiply-accumulate units. Purple denotes computation, blue denotes on-chip storage, green denotes data movement, and red denotes off-chip storage.*

In terms of computation, the 16 NEs form a multiply-accumulate array, and each NE completes 256 FP16 multiply-accumulate operations or 512 INT8 multiply-accumulate operations per cycle. The compiler sets a resident-weight limit of 64 KiB per NE. In FP16 mode, each NE computes at most 16 output channels per pass, so the 16 NEs compute at most 256 per pass. When the number of output channels increases from 256 to 272, an additional pass is required, and the latency shows a clear step (Section 7.3.2). Elementwise operations are performed by the separate PE. In the programs tested in this report, no overlap was observed between PE operations and multiply-accumulate operations when they belonged to different TDs (Chapter 6). The activation function is implemented by a 33-point lookup table with linear interpolation. The accumulator of the FP16 path is in fact a 32-bit fixed-point number (Q15.16), and the accumulator of the INT8 path is a 32-bit integer. This determines several numerical behaviors of the ANE. For example, the accumulation becomes infinite once its absolute value reaches 32768 (Chapter 8).

In terms of data movement, the Tile DMA moves activations. Each TD has at most two input paths and one output path, and the two input paths share a bandwidth of about 35–40 GB/s. The Kernel DMA moves weights; each NE has its own set, and the weight-read bandwidth is about 150 GB/s. Intermediate results between layers are stored in the L2 of each ANE, which the compiler uses as 2 MiB. The L2 is divided into 64 banks, and the compiler pads the row stride of tensors to avoid bank conflicts. Below the L2 are the system-level cache (SLC) and an 8-channel DRAM. On the default Core ML path, weight reads do not use the SLC (Section 9.3.3).

In terms of dual-engine cooperation, the compiler generates both a single-ANE (nonbonded) and a dual-ANE (bonded) program for each model. A cost model decides whether to split subgraphs across the two ANEs, and the split is either spatial (by rows, or by columns when the height is 1) or by output channels. When executing on two ANEs, the two ANEs of a row split each read a complete copy of the weights, whereas in an output-channel split each reads half of them, and the two share the DRAM bandwidth. As a result, compute-bound networks obtain a speedup of about 1.92×, whereas weight-read-bound networks gain little: about 1.4× for convolution chains with different weights in each layer, and no speedup with row splitting (Chapter 10).

In terms of clocks and power, the NE clock of each ANE has 32 states, with an effective working range of 852–2580 MHz, and the state is selected by the CLPC. The PE clock is scheduled independently of the NE clock. The CLPC contains a further "shared cluster" that is shared by the two ANEs, and we speculate that the PE and L2 are located in it. The absolute frequency of this clock cannot be read. It is known to ramp up slowly and to be pulled down when the GPU is busy. The SoC fabric has five frequency states (Chapter 11). At full load, the power of a single ANE is 6.2 W and that of the dual-ANE configuration is 10.8 W. At idle, the power is 0 (Chapter 13).

![From software invocation to hardware execution](../figs/en/light/fig4-2_software.png)

*Figure 4-2. From software invocation to hardware execution. The left side shows the loading path, which occurs once per model; within it, the E5 segmenter of Espresso first decides which operations are assigned to the ANE. The right side shows the execution path of each inference. Below, the time composition of a synchronous call is shown: the idle period of about 384 µs between two adjacent ANE tasks includes the completion-side overhead and the submission overhead of the next call.*

On the control path (Figure 4-2), a model first passes through Espresso, the Core ML runtime framework. At load time, the E5 module of Espresso uses the segmenter to select an ANE or CPU backend for each operation, and only the ANE segments are handed to the compiler service of the daemon aned for compilation. The compilation results are cached by process name (Section 5.9). At inference time, requests are submitted through Espresso and the AppleNeuralEngine framework to the kernel driver. According to the observation of maderix [4] on the M4, they do not pass through aned (Section 6.2). The ANE compiler translates each layer of the network into one or more TDs. Each TD is a sequence of register writes with a header, and the program of each ANE is a TD sequence whose order is scheduled by the compiler (Chapter 5). In the programs tested in this report, no overlap was observed between mutually independent TDs either (Chapter 6).

After an inference request reaches the kernel driver, the driver maps the priority of the request, selects which ANE to use and whether to use a single-ANE or dual-ANE program, and throttles the request when necessary. The request is then passed to the firmware in the ANE. The firmware queues requests in six priority levels and places tasks into the hardware task queues, each of which has two slots. Based on firmware strings, we infer that a high-priority task can preempt a low-priority task that is executing. The hardware fetches TDs from the queue, decodes them, and writes the register values into the configuration of each functional unit. Each call has a fixed host-side overhead: submission takes about 30 µs, and completion notification takes about 130–325 µs. Under the synchronous call mode, the ANE is idle for about 384 µs between two consecutive calls (Chapter 6). The CLPC, which determines the ANE's DVFS state, is not inside the ANE but in the host kernel (Chapter 12).

### 4.6 Comparison with the M4

Table 4-5 compares the ANEs of the M4 and the M6. The data in the M4 column are taken from maderix [4] and from this report's control experiments on the M4.

*Table 4-5. Comparison of the ANE on the M4 and the M6*

| Item | M4 | M6 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Architecture | h16g (Mach-O subtype 7) | h18g (Mach-O subtype 11) |
| Number of engines | 1 | 2, on the same die |
| NEs per engine | 16 (Apple's count); maderix counts 8 compute groups | 16 (consistent across ioreg, the parameter table, and the weight split) |
| Compiled artifacts | One program set | Single-ANE and dual-ANE program sets |
| TD layout version | v17 | v24 |
| NE frequency table (compiler) | 26 states, top 2364 MHz | 32 states, top 2508 MHz (actual hardware top 2580 MHz) |
| DRAM | 8 channels, about 7467 MT/s | 8 channels, about 10656 MT/s |
| FP16 throughput | About 18.5 TFLOPS (48 layers, 1024 channels, 64×64, including call overhead) [4] | Comparable basis (512 channels, 32×32), 48 layers: 25.6 TFLOPS (Table 7-12); slope method: 20.6 TFLOPS for one ANE and 39.5 TFLOPS for two ANEs |

The largest change in the M6 relative to the M4 is the addition of a complete second engine, and the resulting compilation scheme of "one model, two programs." The remaining aspects, including the TD mechanism, 16 NEs per engine, and the fixed-point accumulator, are inherited from the M4. We repeated the basic numerical tests of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path from Chapter 8 on the M4, and the results were the same as on the M6. A complete comparison across product generations is given in Chapter 15.

### 4.7 Summary

The ANE of the M6 consists of two engines with identical structure, each with its own power gating. Each engine contains 16 NEs. The two sit on the same die and share one address translation unit. The compiler describes this platform with the `2026BaseLine` parameter set, and its NE frequency table is inconsistent with the actual hardware frequency table in the top eight states.

This chapter gives only an outline of the structure. Two points recur in later chapters and merit noting at the outset. First, two engines do not mean twice the speed. Whether a single request can use the second engine is decided by the compiler (Chapter 5), how multiple requests are distributed is decided by the driver (Chapter 6), and the actual speedup obtained depends on whether the workload is limited by resources shared by the two engines (Chapter 10). Second, the running speed of the ANE depends to a large extent on the host side. The DVFS state is selected by the CLPC in the host kernel, and the more sparsely the host submits requests, the lower the state to which the ANE is assigned (Chapter 12). All measurements in this report were performed on one M6 with 16 GB of memory and one macOS version. Under other memory configurations or system versions, the DRAM states and compiler policies may differ. The usage recommendations derived from these structural characteristics are given in Section 15.2.

## 5. Compiler and Program Format

This chapter follows the order in which a model is compiled. First, the Core ML segmenter decides which operations are assigned to the ANE. Next, the ANE compiler translates these operations into programs that can execute on the hardware, including the split of work between the two engines. Last, the chapter examines the cost of compilation. We read the analysis files written by the segmenter, and we obtain the compiled artifacts for each compilation target by compiling without executing. We determine the format of the programs by comparing the artifacts byte for byte and by disassembling the compiler. The main conclusions of this chapter are four. First, each ANE segment incurs a fixed cost of 0.25–0.375 ms, so small models and small segments are placed on the CPU by the segmenter, and int32 operations, some mathematical functions, and sequence-type operations cannot be placed on the ANE. Second, each compiled artifact contains both a single-ANE program and a dual-ANE program. Each program consists of a sequence of task descriptors, and each task descriptor is a series of register-write packets that contain no instructions or branches. Third, whether a subgraph is split across the two ANEs is determined by a latency model that assumes the two engines do not interfere with each other and does not account for tiles of unequal size; the splitting rule leaves the two engines with unequal workloads for some models. Fourth, the step that generates dual-ANE programs makes compilation time grow superlinearly with the number of elementwise layers. On the 1×1 convolution chains we measured, it causes the compiler to crash from 387 layers onward, and it makes the disk footprint of large models proportional to the number of layers.

### 5.1 Method

In this chapter, this report calls the compilation interface `ANECCompile` directly ([anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m)), supplying an MIL file, a target name, and compilation options, to generate compiled artifacts in HWX format and analyze them. This interface only compiles and does not access the hardware. It therefore allows the same model to be compiled into five targets, h16g (M4), h17s (H17 generation), h18, h18g (M6), and h19 (A20), and the resulting artifacts to be compared byte for byte. The name h17s is not the compilation target actually used by the M5. On a MacBook Air equipped with the M5, the compilation target read from the device properties of the ANE driver service in the IORegistry is h17, and the measured target on the A18 Pro (iPhone 16 Pro) is also h17 〔ioreg〕, whereas Bryngelson [5] labels M5 as H17s. This report selects h17s only to represent the compiler parameters of the H17 generation. Accordingly, the results for h17s in this chapter (Tables 5-4 and 5-17) and in Section 7.4.1 cannot be taken directly as the behavior of the M5, and this report has not systematically compared h17 and h17s. The compilation options are passed as a dictionary. They include several switches that control dual-ANE behavior, such as `EnableForcedMaximalBondedSplit` and `ForceAllToANE0OnBondedProcedure`, which this chapter uses to construct controlled experiments.

All models in this chapter are executed through Core ML, and no modified HWX is loaded or executed. This constraint distinguishes the method of this report from that of maderix [4]. maderix modified fields in the HWX and executed the result on the hardware, confirming the meaning of each field from changes in output and latency. This report can only observe which words of an artifact change when one property of the model is altered, and it combines these observations with the compiler's disassembly to infer the meanings of the fields. The disassembly of the compiler was obtained as follows. On the M6, we loaded the ANECompiler framework into our own process, located the unexported functions through the symbol table, exported their machine code, and disassembled it with system tools (tools: [syms.c](../tools/re/syms.c), [fnbytes.c](../tools/re/fnbytes.c), [xref.c](../tools/re/xref.c)). Consequently, the conclusions in this chapter about field meanings are labeled 〔HWX〕 or 〔compiler〕, and none has been directly verified through execution.

The baseline model used in this chapter is a three-layer, 64-channel 1×1 convolutional network. Each convolution is followed by relu as the activation function. The input tensor has shape [1, 64, 64, 64] and data type FP16 (MIL source: [model.mil](../data/03_compile/hwx/model.mil); generation script: [mk.py](../data/03_compile/hwx/mk.py)). The compiled artifacts for each target are stored in [data/03_compile/hwx/](../data/03_compile/hwx/), and the parser is [hwx_parse.py](../tools/lib/hwx_parse.py). Several specially constructed models are used in the remaining sections of this chapter; each is described where it is used.

### 5.2 Operator Dispatch in Core ML

When a model is loaded through Core ML, the runtime framework Espresso is responsible for assigning the complete operators. Within Espresso, the compilation and execution module E5 first uses a segmenter to choose a backend for each operation: the ANE, BNNS (the CPU neural network implementation in the Accelerate framework), or the classical CPU implementation. It then partitions the graph into several segments, and only the segments assigned to the ANE are passed to the ANE compiler. This step determines the extent to which a model actually uses the ANE, and it also determines which operations never enter the compilation path described below.

For each operation under test, we generated two models: one containing only that operation, and another in which the operation is preceded and followed by two layers of 3×3 convolution or linear layers. A separate set of boundary-scan models was also generated. Each model was loaded once in an independent cache directory, and we read the analysis file `analytics.mil` that the segmenter writes to the E5 cache. This file records, for each operation, the available backends, the estimated latency of each backend, and the selected backend. It agrees operation by operation with the preferred device reported by the public interface `MLComputePlan`, with no discrepancies. Apart from a few models that could not be parsed or were folded, analysis files were obtained for 482 models (tools: [opdispatch_gen.py](../tools/03_compile/opdispatch_gen.py), [opd_all.sh](../tools/03_compile/opd_all.sh), [opd_ana.sh](../tools/03_compile/opd_ana.sh), [computeplan.swift](../tools/common/computeplan.swift); data: [opdispatch/](../data/03_compile/opdispatch/)) 〔compiler〕. These experiments were performed on the M4 (Mac16,12) through Core ML, and their applicability to the M6 is discussed at the end of this section.

The segmenter's decisions can be reproduced with a very simple model. Each operation takes the estimated latency that the segmenter itself gives for each backend. A single ANE segment adds a fixed startup cost of 0.125 ms; when two adjacent segments use different backends, a switching cost of 0.125 ms is added; and when the output of the graph lies on the ANE, a further 0.125 ms is added. Under this model, the segmentation with the lowest cost is computed and then compared with the actual selection. If the BNNS and classical CPU implementations are merged into a single CPU class, and only whether each operation lies on the ANE or on the CPU is compared, 440 of the 482 models agree exactly with the actual selection, 41 are ties of equal cost, and only 1 disagrees. If the three backends are distinguished, only 261 models agree completely, the number of ties rises to 220, and the number of disagreements remains 1. The additional 179 ties all involve a choice between the two CPU backends, where the actual selection differs from the model even though the computed cost is the same (tool: [opdispatch_fit.py](../tools/03_compile/opdispatch_fit.py)) 〔compiler〕. Therefore, this model reproduces the dispatch of operations between the ANE and the CPU, not the choice between the two CPU backends. In addition, the model treats the operations in the analysis file, in the order of their appearance, as a single chain and computes the shortest path; for graphs with multiple inputs or branches, this is only an approximation 〔inferred〕. The startup and switching constants are recorded in the function header of the analysis file, and they are 2⁻³ ms in all models. The graph-output term has no corresponding field in the function header; it was added during fitting, and its value equals the switching cost 〔compiler, inferred〕. It follows that an ANE segment at the head or tail of the graph must save at least 0.25 ms of estimated latency relative to the CPU in order to be selected, and an ANE segment in the middle of the graph must save at least 0.375 ms 〔inferred〕. Table 5-1 lists the estimated latencies of several operations.

*Table 5-1. Estimated latency of individual operations from the segmenter (M4, µs)*

| Operation | ANE | CPU (BNNS) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 3×3 convolution, 64 → 64 channels, 32×32 | 4.67 | 100.06 |
| 1×1 convolution, same | 1.17 | 21.62 |
| Matrix multiplication, 1×128×256 with 256×128 | 2.24 | 11.12 |
| Bilinear upsampling ×2 | 3.31 | 499.64 |
| Concatenation along channels | 2.62 | 22.79 |
| Scaled dot-product attention, 1×8×128×64 | 7.18 | 8.62 |
| softmax, layer_norm (64×32×32) | 3.59 | 4.31 |
| relu | 2.62 | 2.62 |

Compared with these estimates, the fixed cost of 0.25–0.375 ms is considerable, so small models and small segments within networks are almost always placed on the CPU. For example, a single 3×3 convolution with 64 channels at 32×32 saves only about 0.095 ms, so the layer is placed on the CPU. Four such layers save about 0.38 ms, which exceeds 0.25 ms, and only then are they placed on the ANE. When two convolution layers are separated by an operation that can execute only on the CPU, the segmenter must choose between switching twice and placing the whole graph on the CPU; for small networks, the entire graph remains on the CPU 〔compiler, inferred〕. Operations dominated by memory access differ by only about 17% in estimated cost between the two backends, and when they appear alone, they always remain in a CPU segment. Therefore, operations placed on the CPU in a model with context are not all operations that cannot execute on the ANE.

The operations that the ANE does not support fall into four main categories 〔compiler〕. The first category comprises any operation on int32 tensors, including int32 addition, multiplication, and matrix multiplication; type conversions to and from int32; argmax and argsort with int32 output; and the complete index preprocessing required by gather and scatter with dynamic indices. The second category is a subset of mathematical functions: asin, acos, tan, sinh, cosh, atanh, mod, reduce_prod, and cumsum. The third category comprises sequence and structural operations: lstm, gru, rnn, band_part, one_hot, non_zero, range_1d, random_normal, sliding_windows, crop_resize, and slices whose starting index is dynamic. The fourth category consists of size limits, as shown in Table 5-2. These operations offer no ANE option at the segmentation step and are placed on the CPU; the remaining operations are then segmented according to the cost rules described above. The segmenter does not call all 50 layer-validation functions exported by the ANE compiler directly. Espresso imports only 23 of them (convolution, matrix multiplication, pooling, softmax, reduction, gather, topk, and so on). Scaled dot-product attention, layer_norm, elementwise operations, and activation functions have no corresponding imported validation function, yet they can also be placed on the ANE, and their eligibility is determined through a different path, which this report did not trace 〔compiler〕. Common CNN and Transformer operations can be placed on the ANE. These include convolutions (one-dimensional, three-dimensional, grouped, dilated, transposed, and with dynamic weights); matrix multiplication (including batched, broadcast, and dynamic inputs); scaled dot-product attention; layer_norm; instance_norm; softmax; three variants of GELU; topk; gather with static indices; reductions other than reduce_prod; various upsampling and scaling operations; various padding modes; pixel rearrangement; transposition; and concatenation. FP32 inputs are placed on the ANE after a type conversion is inserted 〔compiler〕.

*Table 5-2. Size limits of the ANE (as judged by Core ML on the M4; direct compilation for h16g and h18g agrees with these, but only the 16×16 square kernel remained unconfirmed, because its compilation exceeded 8 GB of memory)*

| Item | Supported | Not supported |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Square kernel K×K | ≤ 16 (16 by the segmenter's judgment only) | 17 |
| Horizontal kernel 1×K | ≤ 15 | 16 |
| Vertical kernel K×1 | ≤ 31 | 32 |
| max_pool kernel K×K | ≤ 8 | 9 |
| Inner dimension of matrix multiplication | ≤ 32768 | 40000 and above |
| Single dimension of a tensor | ≤ 65536 | 65537 |

These limits originate in the ANE compiler itself. We compiled 62 boundary and control models separately to h16g and h18g with `ANECCompile`. Apart from topk, the success or failure of each model is the same for both targets, and the outcomes for h16g agree with Core ML's judgment on the M4. The 16×16 square kernel was aborted on both targets because its compilation exceeded 8 GB of memory, so whether it can be compiled rests only on the segmenter's judgment. The limit of 15 for horizontal kernel width agrees with the result obtained from the configuration word in Section 7.4.1 (tool: [opd_arch.sh](../tools/03_compile/opd_arch.sh); data: [arch_h16g_h18g.tsv](../data/03_compile/opdispatch/arch_h16g_h18g.tsv)) 〔compiler〕. Querying the dispatch itself can also be expensive. When `MLComputePlan` is called for a single 16×16 convolution (64 channels, 32×32), the query triggers a full ANE compilation, which took 212–229 s, and the combined peak memory of the compiler-service processes was about 11.4 GiB. A 13×13 convolution required only 2–23 s 〔timing〕.

topk is the only exception. When a single topk is compiled directly, h16g fails the RCAS validation, whereas h18g compiles successfully; this is consistent with the observation in Section 5.7.8 that RCAS fields appear only in topk 〔compiler〕. Core ML on the M4 nevertheless still places topk in an ANE segment. In a model with four 256-channel 3×3 convolution layers before and after the topk, the segmenter places the entire graph in a single ANE segment, and topk makes the call take 6.0 ms longer than a control of the same shape (along width, k = 4) and 10.1 ms longer (along channels, k = 256). The segmenter's estimates for these operations were only 0.03 and 0.06 ms, underestimating them by a factor of about 170–200. As a result, placing the whole graph on the ANE is slower than using the CPU alone (8.00 vs. 6.23 ms, and 12.59 vs. 11.08 ms) (tools: [topk_ctx.py](../tools/03_compile/topk_ctx.py), [predict.swift](../tools/common/predict.swift); data: [topk_timing.txt](../data/03_compile/opdispatch/topk_timing.txt)) 〔timing, inferred〕. These timings were obtained after a single warm-up and use the median of 20 calls. They do not follow the continuous warm-up and interrupt-count reading procedure specified in Section 3.4. Therefore, neither whether all topk operations executed on the ANE nor the source of the additional time has been directly confirmed. This report also did not measure whether topk is equally slow on the M6.

The dispatch conclusions above can be extended to the M6, but this extension rests on the compiler rather than on measurements on the M6. Espresso includes an ahead-of-time (AOT) driver that can target any E5 platform. E5 platform names are written as H16G, H18G, and so on, which correspond to the ANE compilation targets h16g and h18g; H18G is the t8152 of the M6. In a process of our own, we ran this driver in a mode that performs only segmentation and does not invoke the ANE compiler. We processed all 490 models with H16G and with H18G as targets (two nms models could not be parsed and produced no output). A dynamic library captured the per-operation estimates that the segmenter wrote to the system log (Section 3.2; tools: [aotdrv.cpp](../tools/common/aotdrv.cpp), [oslogtap.c](../tools/common/oslogtap.c), [opd_aotcost.sh](../tools/03_compile/opd_aotcost.sh); data: [aotfull/](../data/03_compile/opdispatch/aotfull/), [aotcost/](../data/03_compile/opdispatch/aotcost/)) 〔compiler〕. The per-operation estimates and segmentation results for the two targets are identical byte for byte.

As a control, we compared other platforms on 16 of these models. H13G, H17G, and H19 each have estimates for some operations that differ from those of H16G, mainly for convolution and matrix multiplication. For example, the ANE estimate for the 3×3 convolution in the table above is 6.52 µs on H13G, 4.67 µs on H16G and H18G, 8.77 µs on H17G, and 5.43 µs on H19. This shows that the cost model does distinguish platforms, but H18G reuses the parameters of H16G 〔compiler〕. Of the 6489 nonzero estimates that Core ML produced in actual compilation on the M4, 6483 appear in the output of the AOT driver targeting H16G. The six that do not appear are all estimates for input-side type conversions. This indicates that the AOT driver's results represent actual behavior 〔compiler〕.

On this basis, we infer that Core ML's dispatch on the M6 is the same as on the M4 〔compiler, inferred〕. This inference also implies the premise that the M6 runtime uses the H18G platform, which we have not directly confirmed on the M6. Because the segmenter does not know that the M6 has two engines and a higher clock, it still uses the M4 estimates to decide which operations are placed on the ANE. For M6, this threshold is therefore conservative, and some small segments that would already be cost-effective on the ANE of the M6 are still placed on the CPU 〔inferred〕.

### 5.3 The ANE Compiler Pipeline

In the compilation pipeline, the compiler service spawned by the daemon aned is responsible for passing the segments that the segmenter assigns to the ANE to the ANE compiler. The remaining sections of this chapter study precisely this step: into what the compiler translates the program it receives, and which additional steps and costs the dual-engine chip introduces. Experiments of this kind bypass segmentation and call the compilation interface directly (Section 5.1), so that the programs handed to the ANE compiler can be controlled.

The ANE compiler takes MIL (Model Intermediate Language), the intermediate representation of Core ML, as its input. A MIL program is a typed static single-assignment form, declared with `program(1.3)` and `func main<ios18>`, and its tensors use the NCHW layout. The compiler first uses a set of validation functions to check whether each operation can be mapped to a hardware layer of the ANE. On the M6, the compiler exports 50 such functions (`_ANECValidate*Layer`), covering convolution, matrix multiplication, elementwise operations, reductions, softmax, layer normalization, scaled dot-product attention (SDPA), gather, sorting, Top-K, and various shape transformations. There are 33 activation function types (`ZinIrNeuronType`), including several variants of relu, as well as sigmoid, tanh, gelu, swish, exp, log2, sin, cos, rsqrt, and others (list: [compiler_layers_neurons_m6.txt](../data/03_compile/compiler_layers_neurons_m6.txt)) 〔compiler〕.

After validation, the compiler fuses adjacent operations into hardware layers (for example, fusing a convolution, a bias addition, and a relu into one layer). It legalizes and tiles the layers subject to the capacity limits in the hardware parameter table, allocates L2 and DRAM space for tensors, generates the TD sequence, and finally packs everything into an HWX file. For the h18g target, after tiling, the compiler performs a set of steps specific to dual-ANE compilation: identification of subgraphs that can be split (`BondedSplitSubgraphIdentification`), search for parallel branches (`ZinMirFindInherentParallelism`), estimation of split latency (`LatencyInfo::ComputeSplitLatencyBonded`), and code generation for dual-engine asynchronous groups (`CodegenAsyncGroupsForBonded`). The roles and costs of these steps are discussed in Sections 5.8 and 5.9 〔compiler〕.

The `LC_IDENT` load command of an HWX file records the compiler version and the compilation options actually used. The compiler version used in this report is `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`. For compilation to h18g, the recorded options include `-t h18g`, `--fno-fold-scale=true`, `--fdram-allocator=ffreuse`, and `--fdram-tensor-priority=sizebyliverange`. When a model is loaded through Core ML, the kernel log also shows options such as `--ne-frequency=-1.000000` and `--pstate-soc=-1`, where −1 indicates that the compiler adopts the architecture's default value 〔HWX, kernel〕.

### 5.4 Compilation Targets and the Hardware Parameter Table

Each compilation target in the compiler corresponds to a target class (for example, `TargetH18g`). When it is constructed, the target class selects one hardware parameter table (the HAL parameter table described in Section 3.5) and one set of SoC parameters. The HAL parameter table is a static constant table for each ANE generation, 0xa10 bytes in size, and it records capacity limits, alignment requirements, feature switches, and performance-model constants. We exported these tables from each generation by calling its `GetParams()` function within a process. By disassembly, we traced the reads of the table in 3233 functions that take the table as a parameter, and we found readers for 293 offsets (tools: [dump2.c](../tools/re/dump2.c), [hal_readers.py](../tools/re/hal_readers.py)). Because the parameter tables and the disassembly are derived from Apple binaries, the raw exports are not distributed with the repository. Readers can regenerate them on their own machines with the tools above 〔HAL〕.

The layout of the parameter table in this version of the compiler differs from the layout described by Bryngelson [5]. The compiler has inserted new fields into the table, so the offsets given in that work must be shifted by amounts ranging from 0x8 to 0xb8, depending on the section. Table 5-3 lists the fields relevant to later chapters, with the offsets given in that work for comparison.

*Table 5-3. Fields of the HAL parameter table relevant to this report (the 2026BaseLine table used by h18g)*

| Offset in this version | Offset in [5] | Value | Main readers | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0x008 | — | 11 | — | HWX CPU subtype; determines the TD layout version |
| 0x00c | — | 16 | `DetermineNumNesToUse` | NEs per ANE |
| 0x014 | 0x0c | 8 | `ComputeMaxOcgSize`, `HasAccDoubleBuffering` | Accumulator depth per NE |
| 0x1d8 | 0x1b8 | 2 MiB | `HandleL2Config`, `LowerNEMatMulToNEConv` | On-chip residency limit for a single operand |
| 0x1e0 | 0x1c0 | 16 | `GetL2Alignment` | DRAM and L2 access alignment (bytes) |
| 0x1e8 | 0x1c8 | 64 | `ZinMirBankConflictOptimizer` | Number of L2 banks |
| 0x210 | 0x1f0 | 256 KiB | — | L2 residency threshold |
| 0x220 | 0x200 | 64 KiB | `GetKernelTensorBatchSplitFactor`, and others | Resident-weight limit per NE |
| 0x230 | 0x210 | 16 MiB | `OptimizeCoutBatchtoFitInKBuf` | Limit for streamed weight reads |
| 0x2a0 | — | 64 | `PERasterization::GetWorkunitSize` | PE work unit |
| 0x330 | — | 16 | `SetChainBufferNEWorkUnit` | NE work unit |

We compared the parameter tables of each generation and found that the tables of the variants within the same generation (for example, H16, H16g, H16s, and H16c) are numerically identical byte for byte, differing only in the name pointer in the table header and in the addresses of several vectors. Therefore, in this version of the compiler, the information that distinguishes variants of the same generation (such as the number of NEs and the number of engines) is no longer in this table. Whether dual-ANE programs are generated is controlled by compilation options (such as `EnableBondedNetworks`), not by variants recorded in the parameter table, which provides only capability switches. For example, dual-ANE splitting requires the bit at offset 0x73c to be set (Section 5.8.3) 〔HAL, compiler〕.

From H17 (the M5 generation) to H18, the values in the parameter table related to the size of the multiply-accumulate array, L2 capacity, and bandwidth remain unchanged. The changes are concentrated in execution modes and data formats (for other switches, such as dual Int8 mode, see Section 15.3). First, the partitioning of work units is more flexible: a half-work-unit mode is added (offset 0x55c, read by `CanUseHalfWorkUnitMode`), and the non-power-of-two work-unit candidate values 16 and 20 are added (offsets 0x5a0 and 0x5a8). Second, more data formats are supported: the number of natively supported weight formats increases from 13 to 21 (vector at offset 0x8a0); 3-bit palettized weights no longer need to be promoted to 4 bits; and new data-format switches for inputs, outputs, and weights are added (offsets 0x5ff and 0x600) 〔HAL〕. Vector palettization with vectors of length at most 4 (offsets 0x72f and 0x730) had already appeared in the H17 generation 〔HAL〕.

We compared the three tables 2026BaseLine, H18, and H19 byte by byte. 2026BaseLine and H19 are identical except for the name pointer in the table header and the addresses of the vectors, and the contents of each vector are also identical. In contrast, 2026BaseLine differs from H18, the single-engine target of the previous generation (subtype 10, TD v20), in several fields. The two do not belong to the same generation, so this difference does not contradict the rule above that variants of the same generation are byte-identical. Examples include the multi-palette mode (offsets 0x5c7 and 0x5c8), the double-rate mode (offset 0x5f7), the FIFO buffer size (offset 0x888), and the dimension limits for gather and texture operations (offsets 0x1a8–0x1b8). These fields are enabled or relaxed in 2026BaseLine and H19 but not in H18 〔HAL〕. In other words, from the compiler's perspective, the hardware parameters of the M6 are those of H19. The only difference between the two in the compiler lies in the SoC parameters (frequency table, number of DRAM channels, and so on; Section 4.4). This agrees with the finding in Section 5.7.7 that h18g and h19 use the same TD layout (v24) and nearly identical instruction streams.

### 5.5 The HWX Format and the Two Programs

HWX is a file in Mach-O format. Table 5-4 compares the compiled artifacts of the baseline model on the five targets (data: [data/03_compile/hwx/](../data/03_compile/hwx/)) 〔HWX〕.

*Table 5-4. Artifact formats for the five compilation targets*

| Target | CPU subtype | File size | Load commands | Format | Dual-ANE program |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| h16g (M4) | 7 | 96 KB | 14 | Legacy format | No |
| h17s (H17 generation) | 9 | 96 KB | 14 | Legacy format | No |
| h18 | 10 | 96 KB | 14 | Legacy format | No |
| h18g (M6) | 11 | 128 KB | 8 | New format, with a `__RUNTIME` segment | Yes |
| h19 | 11 | 128 KB | 8 | New format, with a `__RUNTIME` segment | Yes |

The HWX of the M4 adopts the legacy format described by maderix [4]: the `__TEXT` segment stores a single TD sequence, the `__KERN_0` segment stores the weights, and two `__FVMLIB` segments describe the inputs and outputs. The HWX of the M6 adopts the new format, which adds a `__RUNTIME` segment, and its `__TEXT` segment contains three TD sequences, as shown in Table 5-5.

*Table 5-5. Segment layout of the h18g compiled artifact (baseline model)*

| Segment | Virtual address | Contents |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `__RUNTIME` | 0x30000000 | Runtime operation graph (RTGraph), 0x102c bytes |
| `__TEXT` | 0x30004000 | Single-ANE program: uses only ANE0 |
|  | 0x30008000 | Portion of the dual-ANE program executed by ANE0 |
|  | 0x3000c000 | Portion of the dual-ANE program executed by ANE1 |
| `__TEXT,__const` | — | Constants |
| `__KERN_0` | 0x30014000 | Weights, 0x6c00 bytes; shared by both programs and both ANEs |
| `LC_IDENT` | — | Compiler version and compilation options |
| `LC_NOTE` | — | Source model information (`src model info`) and runtime version (`rt.version`) |
| `LC_SYMTAB` | — | 111 symbols |

The `__RUNTIME` segment describes the operations that the runtime must perform when executing each program, and its contents can be read directly from the symbol names. The `main__nonbonded` procedure maps the weights, maps the inputs and outputs, and maps the TD sequence of ANE0, and then starts ANE0 once through `rt_op_ane_kick_…_ane_0`. The `main__bonded` procedure maps the same weights to ANE0 and ANE1 separately, then maps the inputs, outputs, and TD sequences of each, and finally starts the two ANEs once each, through `rt_op_ane_kick_…_ane_1` and `rt_op_ane_kick_…_ane_0`. The `main` procedure is the entry point of the entire compiled program 〔HWX〕.

Among the five compilation targets, only the artifacts of h18g and h19 adopt this new format, and the two belong to the same generation, whose ArchCompareValue is 10 (subtype 11, TD v24). h18 belongs to the previous generation, and its artifacts remain in the legacy format and contain no dual-ANE program 〔HWX, compiler〕. Therefore, in the compiler, the "two programs" appear together with this ANE generation, and h19 also generates dual-ANE programs. The compiled artifacts alone cannot determine whether two engines are unique to the M6; how many engines the A20 actually has must be confirmed on the device 〔HWX, inferred〕.

It should be noted that the choice of which program to execute occurs at runtime, whereas the way each segment of a dual-ANE program is split (across both ANEs or kept entirely on ANE0) is already determined at compile time. If the compiler decides not to split, the dual-ANE program contains no TD sequence for ANE1, and even if the runtime selects the dual-ANE program, the request uses only ANE0. The interrupt counts in Section 6.3.2 indicate that, as long as the dual-ANE program contains a TD sequence for ANE1, Core ML selects the dual-ANE program for execution.

### 5.6 Weight Splitting

In the artifact of the baseline model, the weights of each layer are split into 16 blocks in the `__KERN_0` segment. The symbol names are `K<sha256>_ne_0` through `K<sha256>_ne_15`, and each block is 0x240 (576) bytes. This size can be derived from the shape of the layer. The 64 output channels are divided evenly among the 16 NEs, so each NE is responsible for 4 output channels. Four output channels × 64 input channels × 2 bytes equals 512 bytes; adding the bias and aligning to 64 bytes gives 576 bytes. Each TD also contains 16 consecutive configuration words (for example, `00000021` and `00010021`), whose number equals the number of NEs. We speculate that these are the weight-DMA configurations of the individual NEs 〔inferred〕. These facts indicate that the hardware distributes the computation of a layer over 16 NEs by output channel, which is consistent with the `NumANECores = 16` entry in ioreg 〔HWX〕.

When the weights required by each NE exceed 64 KiB, the compiler has two ways of handling them. When the input is large, the compiler splits the layer into multiple TDs by output channel, so that the weights per NE in each TD do not exceed 64 KiB; the number of TDs per layer is approximately ⌈weights per NE ÷ 64 KiB⌉. We examined artifacts for several convolution kernel shapes on a 16×32 input (256 channels), and most agree with this relation. For example, a 3×3 convolution requires about 72 KiB of weights per NE and has 2 TDs per layer; a 5×5 convolution requires about 200 KiB and has 4 TDs per layer; and a 1×9 convolution after rewriting (Section 7.4.1) requires about 154 KiB and has 3 TDs per layer, whose output channels are 64, 96, and 96. A 1×9 convolution on a 32×32 input likewise has 3 TDs per layer. There are also exceptions. A 7×7 convolution requires about 392 KiB, which by the formula above would give 7 TDs, but there are actually 8; a rewritten 1×15 convolution requires about 256 KiB, which should give 4 TDs, but there are actually 6 (Table 7-6) 〔HWX〕. We speculate that this is because the compiler splits by whole groups of output channels, so that each TD often does not fully use 64 KiB 〔inferred〕.

When the input is small, the compiler can also place all output channels in a single TD and read the weights in a streaming manner during execution. We observed both cases among the artifacts of 288 shapes (data: [bsdecide.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsdecide.txt)). On a 4×32 input, all convolution kernels, including 3×3, 5×5, and 15×1, have only one TD per layer, although most of them require more than 64 KiB of weights per NE. On an 8×32 input, natively executed kernels still conform to the rule above (3×3 and 9×1 have 2 TDs, and 5×5 has 4 TDs), whereas rewritten 1×9 to 1×15 convolutions have only one TD per layer 〔HWX〕. The choice the compiler makes between these two methods has not been determined in this report. Section 7.4.2 shows that, under both methods, these layers re-read the weights in every layer, and in both cases the latency is determined by the weight-reading stages.

The 64 KiB boundary agrees with the value of the resident-weight limit at offset 0x220 in the HAL parameter table (64 KiB), and it also agrees with the result obtained from output-channel grouping in Chapter 7. On this basis, we can determine that the compiler uses a weight buffer of 64 KiB per NE. Whether the physical capacity is larger cannot be read directly in this report 〔HWX, HAL, inferred〕. This capacity determines that the output-channel grouping for large convolution kernels shrinks as the number of input channels decreases (Section 7.3.1). By contrast, the step in output channel count from 256 to 272 in Section 7.3.2 results from the limit of at most 256 output channels per round across the 16 NEs, and it is unrelated to weight capacity.

Figure 5-1 summarizes the two splitting methods described in this section. The weights of a layer are divided evenly by output channel among 16 NEs. When each NE's weights exceed 64 KiB, a layer with a large input is further split into multiple TDs by output channel.

![Weight splitting](../figs/en/light/fig5-1_weight_split.png)

*Figure 5-1. Weight splitting. Left: the weights of the baseline model (1×1 convolution, 64 → 64 channels) are divided evenly by output channel among 16 NEs, with one 576-byte block per NE. Right: a 1×9 convolution with 256 channels on a 16×32 input requires about 154 KiB of weights per NE after rewriting, and it is split by output channel into three TDs with 64, 96, and 96 channels. When the input is small (all convolution kernels on a 4×32 input, and the rewritten kernels on an 8×32 input), all output channels are placed in a single TD.*

### 5.7 Format of the Task Descriptor

maderix [1] observed that the compiled artifacts store configuration parameters rather than instructions. The analysis in this report further shows that a TD is in effect a sequence of register-write packets. Each TD sequence begins with a sequence header of four words (values 1, 0, 0, 0). Each TD begins with a 12-word task header, followed by several write packets, each consisting of a packet header and data, and ends with several address packets. Figure 5-2 illustrates this structure. The following subsections discuss, in order, the task header, the encoding of write packets, the grouping of registers, the convolution configuration word, the opcodes of elementwise operations, address packets, and the layout version.

![TD format](../figs/en/light/fig5-2_td_format.png)

*Figure 5-2. Structure of a TD (v24 layout). At the top is the TD sequence of one ANE. Below it, one TD is expanded: ① the task header, ② the write packets arranged by register group, and ③ the address packets at the end. The register groups of the write packets are arranged in the direction of data flow. ④ gives the bit format of the packet header words, illustrated by three header words from ② and ③. The example is taken from the artifact of a 1×9 convolution with 256 channels, an 8×32 input, and four layers on h18g.*

#### 5.7.1 Task Header

The compiler's assertion strings refer to the 12 words of the task header as `td_header0` through `td_header11`. We disassembled the field-reading function for the v24 layout (`ZinGetRegisterProgramming<24>`) and the functions that write the task header (`HandleTdHeader`, `SetEventFlags`, and others), and we compared them with the actual values in the artifacts. This yielded the field meanings listed in Table 5-6 〔compiler, HWX〕.

*Table 5-6. TD task header (v24)*

| Offset | Field | Writer | Observed values and notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| +0x0 | `tid` (low 16 bits); `TaskSize` (bits 16–26, in words) | `SetField` | TD number and length. For example, `0x006d0000` denotes TD 0 with a length of 109 words |
| +0x4 | `exe_cycles` (low 16 bits) | `HandleTdHeader` → `CalculateExeCycles` | The compiler's estimate of the layer's latency, in units of about 1 µs, with an upper limit of 0xffff; 0 for layers whose input is read in a chain from L2 |
| +0x8 | `log_events` | `SetEventFlags` | 0x68 for the first TD, 0x04000000 for the last TD, and 0 for the others |
| +0xc | `exception` | — | 0 |
| +0x10 | `debug_log_events` | `SetEventFlags` | 0x00fff800; the low bits of the first and last TDs are additionally set |
| +0x14 | `debug_exception` | — | 0 |
| +0x18 | `dram_log_events` | `SetEventFlags` | 0 |
| +0x1c | `dram_log_events` (second word) | `SetField` | Mask for segment changes, such as 0x1ff and 0x1ff0 |
| +0x20 | Flag word | `SetLdtid`, `SetPublishBit`, and others | Bit 0 is `Tsr`, bit 1 is `Tde`, bit 3 is set only in the last TD, bit 4 enables performance tracing, bit 5 is `Xtde`, and bit 6 is `PublishBit`; bits 16–18 are 5 in convolution TDs and 0 in elementwise TDs |

Two fields are analyzed further in later sections. The first is the `exe_cycles` field, which is written by the function `CalculateExeCycles` in units of about 1 µs. For example, the values for the three TDs of a 1×9 convolution with 256 channels on a 16×32 input are 7, 11, and 11, which are proportional to the output channel counts 64, 96, and 96. The value for a 1×1 FP16 convolution TD agrees with the time obtained by converting at 20.6 TFLOPS. For dense layers, `exe_cycles` agrees with the per-TD estimates of the compiler's performance model. For pruned sparse layers, it does not agree, and the algorithm responsible has not been determined in this report (Section 7.8). The second is the three groups of event masks, which determine at which moments during TD execution the firmware records which events. Because the masks of the first and last TDs differ from those of the other TDs, we can obtain the task-start and task-end timestamps of each invocation from kdebug traces. Chapter 6 uses these timestamps to decompose the time of each invocation, and Chapter 11 uses them to infer clock frequency 〔compiler, HWX〕.

#### 5.7.2 Encoding of Write Packets

The content after the task header consists of three kinds of packets, whose encodings are shown in Table 5-7 〔HWX, compiler〕.

*Table 5-7. The three kinds of packets in a TD*

| Type | Encoding | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Contiguous write | Bit 31 is 0; the low 15 bits are the word address of the starting register; bits 15–30 hold the number of writes minus 1; the packet header is followed by the corresponding number of values | `00010001`: writes three values (Win, Hin, Cin) starting at register 1. `000f1559`: writes 31 values starting at register 0x1559, including the weight offsets of 16 NEs |
| Masked write | Bit 31 is 1; the low 15 bits are the base; bits 15–30 hold a 16-bit mask; the packet writes the base register itself and, for each bit i set in the mask, the register "base + 1 + i"; the header is followed by 1 + popcount(mask) values | `80c11340`: base 0x1340, mask 0x182, writes the four registers 0x1340, 0x1342, 0x1348, and 0x1349 |
| Address packet | Same format as a contiguous write, with bit 29 as a marker; bits 21–28 are the BAR number | See Section 5.7.6 |

The word immediately after the input-channel count in a convolution TD is the masked-write packet header. Its value varies with the model. Most convolutions, such as 1×1 and 1×3, have `0x91498005`; 1×8 convolutions and dilated convolutions have `0x93498005`; and 3×3 convolutions in W8A8 mode have `0xb3498005` (samples: [xword_samples.txt](../data/03_compile/xword_samples.txt)). Decoded according to the masked-write format, `0x91498005` denotes base register 5 (Wout) and mask 0x2293, and it writes seven registers in order: Wout, Hout, Cout, the convolution configuration word, TileHeight, the task information word, and register 19. It follows that this word is not a register value; it is a packet header generated during serialization according to which fields must be written. The differences between models can be explained on this basis. In W8A8 mode, one additional register must be written (base + 15, that is, register 20), so the mask gains one more bit, corresponding to bit 29. When the same layer is split into several TDs, register 16 is written in some TDs and omitted in others, so the bit corresponding to bit 25 in the mask varies from TD to TD 〔HWX, compiler〕. The TD parsing tool is [tdpkt.py](../tools/lib/tdpkt.py).

#### 5.7.3 Register Groups and the Pipeline

For each TD layout version, the compiler defines a class `ZinAneTdHw_vN`. Field-reading functions are named `ZinGetRegisterProgramming<N>::GetXxx`, and each function reads one word at a certain offset of the TD object and extracts several bits. We disassembled all of the reading functions and obtained the offset and bit width of each field (summary: [td_field_offsets.txt](../data/03_compile/td_field_offsets.txt)). Arranged by offset, the groups of fields appear in the order shown in Table 5-8 〔compiler〕.

*Table 5-8. Order of the field groups in a TD (by object offset in v31 and v36)*

| Order | Field group | Representative fields |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Task header | `TaskSize`, `Tsr`, `Tde`, `PublishBit` |
| 2 | Common: shape and task type | Win, Hin, Cin, Din, Wout, Hout, Cout, Dout (17 bits each); `NumGroups`; broadcast and transpose; `CommonTaskType` |
| 3 | Input Tile DMA | Enables for Src1 and Src2, `DoubleRate`, format, interleaving, compression, vertical cropping offset |
| 4 | Texture and gather unit | `TextureMode`; index dimensions for gather; texture source size |
| 5 | L2 source and ring buffer | `Ephemeral` for Src1 and Src2 and the L2 DMA `DoubleRate`; `Ephemeral` for the result; ring-buffer configuration |
| 6 | PE | `PEIndexingEnabled` |
| 7 | NE | `NEAccBiasShift` (starting at bit 11); `NEPostRightShift` (bits 19–23) |
| 8 | Output Tile DMA | Enable, format, interleaving, compression, vertical cropping offset |

This order is consistent with the direction of data flow in the hardware. Data is read from DRAM by the input Tile DMA, enters the NE and PE through L2, and is written back to DRAM by the output Tile DMA. It also agrees with the data flow that Bryngelson [5] derived from the M1 register groups (DRAM → L2 → NE → L2 → PE → DRAM). The NE field group contains an accumulator bias-shift field and a 5-bit output right-shift field, which are the configuration required by fixed-point accumulators: accumulation takes place in a fixed-point format, and the output is right-shifted and converted to FP16. Chapter 8 directly measures the accumulator as a Q15.16 fixed-point format with constructed inputs, which is consistent with these fields. The `DoubleRate` fields of the input Tile DMA and the L2 DMA have existed since v24. The `CanEnableDoubleRateMode` switch in the parameter table (offset 0x5f7) should be the condition for enabling these fields; it is 0 in H18 and 1 in 2026BaseLine, which M6 uses, and in H19. Nevertheless, neither the FP16 nor the INT8 models used in this report triggered double-rate mode 〔compiler, HAL, inferred〕.

#### 5.7.4 Convolution Configuration Word

The geometry of a convolution is encoded together in a 32-bit configuration word (offset 0x2a0 in the compiler's object; register 10). We disassembled the 13 functions that write this word (`ZinAneTd<31>::SetCommonConvCfg*`, and others) and obtained the bit definitions listed in Table 5-9. The parameters of a three-dimensional convolution have a second configuration word (offset 0x2a4), in which Kd occupies bits 0–4, Sz bits 6–7, Pz bits 8–11, and Oz bits 13–14 〔compiler〕.

*Table 5-9. Bit definitions of the convolution configuration word*

| Bits | 0–5 | 6–11 | 12 | 13–14 | 15–16 | 17–21 | 22–26 | 27 | 28–29 | 30–31 |
| --- | --- | ---- | --- | ----- | ----- | ------- | ------ | --- | ----- | ----- |
| Field | Kw | Kh | Reserved | Sx | Sy | PadLeft | PadTop | Reserved | Ox | Oy |

For example, the configuration word of a 1×3 convolution is `0x5002a043`, which decodes to Kw = 3, Kh = 1, Sx = 1, Sy = 1, PadLeft = 1, Ox = 1, and Oy = 1. The configuration word of a 3×3 convolution is `0x5042a0c3`. In all compiled artifacts, bits 12 and 27 are 0, and the functions that write this word do not modify these bits, so they are reserved. Ox and Oy denote the number of output pixels produced per input stride. Field tracing (Section 5.7.8) shows that Ox and Oy are 2 for transposed convolutions and upsampling, which are implemented as an ordinary convolution with interleaved output, writing every other position 〔compiler, HWX〕. Although the kernel-width field has 6 bits, the largest kernel width that the compiler encodes directly with stride 1 is 8. For kernel widths of 9–15, the compiler rewrites the convolution in the form Sx = 2 and Ox = 2, sets the output-width field to half the actual width, and stores the weights as two sets of kernels offset by one position. Section 7.4.1 compares the configuration words and weight volumes for different kernel widths and gives the full justification for this rewriting rule (data: [convcfg_scan.txt](../data/03_compile/convcfg_scan.txt); tool: [convcfg.py](../tools/03_compile/convcfg.py)) 〔compiler, HWX〕.

#### 5.7.5 Opcodes of Elementwise Operations

maderix [4] reports that ALU operations are controlled by an opcode byte, with 256 possible values. To confirm the encoding of opcodes on the M6, we compiled a fused layer consisting of a 1×1 convolution followed by a two-input elementwise operation, using five operations: addition, subtraction, multiplication, maximum, and minimum. The TD sequences of the five artifacts have the same length (0x294 bytes), and they differ in substance only in a single word at offset 0x240 within the sequence, as shown in Table 5-10 〔HWX〕.

*Table 5-10. Encoding of elementwise operations*

| Operation | Value of this word | bits 2–3 | bit 18 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| add | 0x00080000 | 0 | 0 |
| sub | 0x000c0000 | 0 | 1 |
| mul | 0x00080004 | 1 | 0 |
| maximum | 0x00080008 | 2 | 0 |
| minimum | 0x0008000c | 3 | 0 |

Table 5-10 shows that, in this fused layer, the operation type is encoded in a 2-bit field (0 for addition, 1 for multiplication, 2 for maximum, and 3 for minimum), and subtraction is implemented as addition with the second input negated (bit 18). The TD sequence of division (real_div) is longer (0x338 bytes). We speculate that it is split into two steps, a reciprocal (computed with a lookup table and interval reduction) and a multiplication 〔HWX, inferred〕. This experiment covers only this one type of fused layer. Other layer types may use more encodings, so the results of this report do not contradict the 256 opcodes described by maderix.

#### 5.7.6 Address Packets and BARs

Address packets are located at the end of each TD. Each address packet writes an offset within a buffer into a base register, and its BAR number indicates which buffer the offset is relative to. We counted more than 1600 address packets and found that they write only five groups of registers (tool: [hwxreloc.py](../tools/03_compile/hwxreloc.py)). To determine the buffer corresponding to each register group, we constructed a model with two inputs and two outputs: o1 = relu(conv(x) + y), o2 = relu(conv(y)) (generation script: [io2.py](../tools/03_compile/io2.py); artifacts: [l2hwx/io2](../data/03_compile/l2hwx/io2/)). Table 5-11 gives the results of the analysis 〔HWX, compiler〕.

*Table 5-11. Registers written by address packets and their BAR numbers*

| Register | Field name in the compiler | BAR number | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0x1544 / 0x1545 | `KernelSrcBaseAddrLo / Hi` | 4 | Weights; the `__TEXT` segment has an 8-byte static relocation entry pointing to `__KERN_0` |
| 0x1346 / 0x1347 | Tile DMA `Src1BaseAddrLo / Hi` | Assigned per tensor | First input; x is 32 in the io2 model |
| 0x134c / 0x134d | Tile DMA `Src2BaseAddrLo / Hi` | Assigned per tensor | Second input; y is 36 in the io2 model |
| 0x1444 / 0x1445 | Tile DMA `DstBaseAddrLo / Hi` | Assigned per tensor | Output; o1 is 24 and o2 is 28 in the io2 model |
| 0x1344, 0x1345, 0x1442 | DSID of Src1, Src2, and Dst | 20 | Data-flow tags of the system-level cache |

From Table 5-11, the BAR numbering rule can be summarized as follows. Slot 4 is for weights, and slot 20 is for DSID parameters. Slots starting from 24 are assigned to the tensors in sequence, outputs first and then inputs, with four slots for each tensor. All 64-bit address packets use even BAR numbers, which is consistent with the assertion string in the compiler, "64-bit relocation, BarIdx must be even". The base address of the weights is determined at load time through static relocation. The base addresses of inputs and outputs have no relocation entries; instead, the runtime fills them in on each invocation according to the BAR number, and mapping entries such as `x` and `o1@output` in the `__RUNTIME` segment describe this correspondence. The values in address packets are offsets within buffers. For example, in a dual-ANE program, the offset carried by the input address packet of ANE1 points to row 32 (Section 5.8.1). In the artifacts of h18 (v20 layout), the same register uses a different BAR number (for example, 28 for weights), which shows that the BAR layout varies across versions 〔HWX〕.

#### 5.7.7 Layout Versions

The register layout of the TD changes with the architecture. We determined the layout version for each target along the following path. The constructor of `ZinAneTd<N>` is inlined into the function `CodegenCreateInstructions<N>`. The sole caller of the latter, `arch_dispatch<CodegenCreateInstructionsDispatcher>`, uses `AneArchEnum − 1` as an index into a 36-entry jump table, in which entry N jumps to `CodegenCreateInstructions<N>`. It follows that the template parameter N is the value of `AneArchEnum`. `AneArchEnum` is in turn obtained from the CPU subtype of the HWX by the function `ZinCpuSubtypeToArchValue` (tools: [adrref.c](../tools/re/adrref.c), [jtab.c](../tools/re/jtab.c), [tblread.c](../tools/re/tblread.c)). Table 5-12 lists the relevant part of the lookup results 〔compiler〕.

*Table 5-12. Correspondence between CPU subtype and TD layout version*

| CPU subtype | Layout version | Compilation target |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4 | v7 | H13 (M1), T1 |
| 6 | v8 | H15 |
| 7 | v17 | All of H16 (M4 is h16g) |
| 9 | v19 | All of H17 (M5 is h17) |
| 10 | v20 | h18 |
| 11 | v24 | h18g (M6), h19 |
| 12, 14, 16 | v31, v36, v28 | No externally visible target name in this compiler version |
| 17 | v26 | M12 (internal target name in the compiler) |

To verify Table 5-12, we compiled the same model (256-channel 1×9 convolution, 8×32 input, four layers) to all available targets and compared the register addresses written by each target (data: [data/03_compile/tdv/](../data/03_compile/tdv/)). The results show that changes in register layout correspond one-to-one with changes in the version number. v17 and v19, relative to v8, changed the DMA register groups. v20, relative to v19, inserted two registers in the register groups related to L2. v24, relative to v20, inserted one register after the first NE register; the write addresses of the NE group change from 0x1240, 0x1241, and 0x1244 to 0x1240, 0x1242, and 0x1245. The TD sequences of h18g and h19 are almost identical word for word. They total 17948 bytes, and the differences are confined to one field in the task header and one command word at the end of a TD. In addition, the positions and bit definitions of the shape fields, the convolution configuration word, TileHeight, TileOverlap, and the task information word are exactly the same in v19, v20, v24, and v31. Therefore, the decoding results for the v31 functions in Section 5.7.4 also apply to the M6 〔compiler, HWX〕.

Of the four layout versions in Table 5-12 with numbers larger than v24, v26 corresponds to the target named M12 internally by the compiler, which should not be confused with the M-series chips. Its ArchCompareValue is the same as that of v24, so v26 is more likely a variant of the same architecture generation on another product line. v28, v31, and v36 are the architectures newer than the M6, and Section 15.3 discusses the fields they add 〔compiler, inferred〕. The lookup results of this report differ from Bryngelson [5] in two places. First, Bryngelson records the layout of the M1 as v10, whereas the lookup in this report gives v7 for H13 (M1). In this version of the compiler, v10 belongs to target M11, whose ArchCompareValue equals that of v8 for H15. Second, Bryngelson states that the M5 uses the v20 layout, whereas the lookup in this report gives v19 for H17 (the M5 generation) and v20 for h18. Both differences may arise from different compiler versions, because the layout version number is an internal template parameter of the compiler, and different compiler versions may assign different numbers to the same architecture. Without the corresponding compiler version and device, this report does not verify this further. Chapter 15's comparison table uniformly adopts the lookup results of this report and notes the notation used in the literature.

#### 5.7.8 Dynamic Tracing of Fields

By tracing the calls to field functions dynamically, we can further understand how each field is actually read and assigned. On the M4, we compiled a batch of small models for h18g with the same version of the compiler. We used lldb to set breakpoints in the 403 field-setting functions of the TD class `ZinAneTd<24>` for the v24 layout. We recorded the arguments and callers of each call, as well as the changes in register values in the TD object before and after each call (Section 3.2; tools: [tdtrace_gen.py](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace_gen.py), [tdtrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace_lldb.py), [tdtrace.sh](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace.sh), [tdtrace_fit.py](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace_fit.py)) 〔compiler〕. The correspondence between register addresses and object offsets was taken from the disassembly of `ZinAneTdHw_v24::GetRegisterValueFromAddress`. The first batch of 35 models covers various convolution kernels, strides, dilation, grouping, transposed convolutions, activation functions, normalization, sparse and quantized weights, matrix multiplication, elementwise operations, pooling, reductions, softmax, concatenation, transposition, padding, upsampling, and dual-ANE programs. Across these models, 226 setter functions were called 47508 times in total. The register values reconstructed from the trace agree with the actual values in the HWX in 89% of cases. The disagreements are concentrated in address-type registers, which are rewritten by address allocation and relocation after TD generation. We then extracted from the disassembly the bits that each setter clears and sets ([tdstatic.py](../tools/re/tdstatic.py)), and merged them with the trace results and the actual HWX values on a "register → bit field" basis ([tdmanual.py](../tools/03_compile/tdmanual.py)). This yielded 312 bit fields: 104 vary with the model, 86 are constant, and 122 do not appear in this batch of models. For these 122 bit fields, we designed a second batch of 32 models by field name, and we added several variants with compilation options. This reduced the number of bit fields that do not appear to 79 〔compiler, HWX〕. Because this field table is derived from the compiler, this report provides only field names and value patterns, and it does not distribute the field table itself. Table 5-13 lists the fields relevant to other sections of this report.

*Table 5-13. Selected fields determined by dynamic tracing (v24 layout)*

| Register and bits | Setter function | Pattern of values |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0x000f, bits 4–7 | `SetCommonTaskType` | Task type: convolution 0, pooling 1, data movement such as concatenation 2, elementwise add and multiply 3, reductions (layernorm, reduce_mean) 4–6 |
| 0x000f, bit 27 | `Set1DWinogradMode` | 1 for ordinary and grouped 3×3 convolutions in the traced models (also 1 for artifacts of 1×3 convolutions); 0 for depthwise convolutions, 5×5, dilated convolutions, stride-2 convolutions, and pruned 3×3 (Section 7.4.3) |
| 0x0010, bits 0–2 | `SetNEOcgSize` | log₂ of the output-channel group size (Section 7.3.1): depthwise 0, grouped convolution 1, W8A8 3 |
| 0x0010, bit 6 | `SetNEHalfWUMode` | 1 only in W8A8 |
| 0x000a, bits 28–31 | `SetCommonConvCfgOx`, `Oy` | 2 for transposed convolutions and upsampling; Ox = 2 for rewritten kernels with kernel width 9–15 (Section 7.4.1) |
| 0x0013, bits 21–28 | `SetNID` | 0 for pure PE operations (elementwise, pooling), 1 for others; presumably indicates whether the NE is used |
| 0x1240, bit 8 | `SetKernelSparseFmt` | 1 for pruned weights, rewritten kernels with kernel width 9–15, dilated convolutions, stride-2 convolutions, and bilinear upsampling (Section 7.4.2) |
| 0x1240, bit 16 | `SetKernelAlignmentFormat` | 1 for matrix multiplication with two dynamic inputs and for softmax, that is, one activation tensor is fed to the NE as weights |
| 0x1240, bit 28 | `SetKernelDetectZeros` | Zero-weight detection (Section 7.5) |
| 0x1242 | `SetNENonLinearMode` | 0 for no activation, 1 for ReLU, 2 for sigmoid, tanh, GELU, and similar functions (table lookup, Section 8.5.1) |
| 0x1242 | `SetNEBinaryPoint`, `SetNEPostScale` | Binary point −4 for FP16 and 0 for Int8; post-scaling is used only in Int8 |
| 0x1340 onward | Tile DMA source | Default cache hint 2; 4 for dual-ANE programs; in dual-ANE programs, the vertical cropping offset of ANE1 points to the starting row it is responsible for |
| 0x1540, bit 6 | `SetKernelDmaSrcEnable` | 1 when weights are present; 0 for elementwise, pooling, and layernorm |

The second batch of models also revealed several hardware functions not treated in other sections of this report 〔compiler, inferred〕. Gather-type operations (gather, gather along an axis, and embedding) write the entire group of texture fields (0x1372–0x137a, `TextureMode` = 1), indicating that the ANE's gather borrows the texture sampling unit. This corresponds to the "accelerated texture sampling" switch newly added to the 2026BaseLine parameter table (Section 15.3). A group of fields named RCAS (0x1246) appears only in topk. Combined with strings such as `rcas_kernel` and `ne_supports_rcas` in the compiler, we speculate that it is a comparison-and-selection unit inside the NE. Large mirror (reflect) padding is implemented with circular-addressing fields (0x1059–0x105a). 3D convolutions use the depth-dimension shape fields and the three-dimensional configuration word described in Section 5.7.4, and they are natively supported operations. The compiler option `ForcePerfTracerRegisters` writes seven groups of performance-tracing configuration for L2, PE, NE, Tile DMA source and destination, and weight DMA, indicating that the hardware has per-unit performance counters. Under the constraints of this report, however, these counters cannot be read (Section 16.1).

The 79 bit fields that still do not appear are mostly features that this version of the compiler does not use by default on h18g: activation compression (about 30 bit fields, which the compilation option `EnableIntermediateCompression` also fails to make appear), weight coefficient DMA (about 12), multiple palettes (5), static circular addressing and index addressing for L2 and PE (about 15), and `TileOverlap` and stochastic rounding (`StochasticRound`).
