In other words, the TD layout reserves fields for these features, but neither the existing compiler nor the coremltools models use them. This agrees with the observation in Section 15.3 that a capability opened in the parameter table is not necessarily used 〔compiler, inferred〕.

### 5.8 Dual-ANE Programs

This section examines three properties of dual-ANE programs: how the TD sequences of the two ANEs differ from those of a single-ANE program; how the two ANEs share data and synchronize when data must be exchanged; and what the compiler relies on to decide whether to split. This section discusses only the compiler's decisions. How the runtime selects between the two programs is described in Section 6.3.2, and the speedup actually achieved by splitting is described in Chapter 10.

Figure 5-3 summarizes the main content of this section.

![Dual-ANE programs](../figs/en/light/fig5-3_bonded.png)

*Figure 5-3. Splitting, synchronization, and the split decision for dual-ANE programs. ① Split by rows: the two ANEs each process half of the rows and each read a complete copy of the weights (Section 5.8.1). ② Split by output channel: the passes already present in the single-ANE program are divided evenly between the two ANEs (Section 5.8.3). ③ When data must be exchanged, the two ANEs map the same shared scratch buffer, and sync records are inserted before the corresponding layers (Section 5.8.2). ④ The compiler compares latency estimates before and after splitting to decide whether to split; the factor × 0.5 assumes that the two ANEs do not interfere with each other (Section 5.8.3).*

#### 5.8.1 Differences at the TD Level

For the baseline model, the single-ANE program and each of the two TD sequences of the dual-ANE program contain three TDs, with about 0x4ac bytes per sequence. A word-by-word comparison of the three sequences yields the differences listed in Table 5-14 〔HWX〕.

*Table 5-14. Differences between the TDs of the single-ANE and dual-ANE programs for the baseline model*

| Location | Single ANE | Dual ANE, ANE0 | Dual ANE, ANE1 | Interpretation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Height field in each TD | 64 | 32 | 32 | Output height halved |
| Data-volume field in each TD | 0x82000 | 0x41000 | 0x41000 | Data volume halved |
| Count fields in each TD | 4 / 2 / 6 | 2 / 1 / 3 | 2 / 1 / 3 | Counts halved |
| Offset of the input address packet | — | — | 32 rows | ANE1 starts reading at row 32 |
| Offset of the output address packet | — | — | 32 rows | ANE1 starts writing at row 32 |
| Bit 6 of the flag word (`PublishBit`) | 0 | 1 | 1 | Set only in dual-ANE programs |
| 16 NE configuration words | 0x00000021 | 0x00010021 | 0x00010021 | Bit 16 is set in dual-ANE programs |
| `tid` | Starts at 0 | Starts at 0x70 | Starts at 0x75 | TD numbers of the two ANEs do not overlap |

As Table 5-14 shows, for this pointwise convolution chain, the dual-ANE program splits the tensor into upper and lower halves along height. ANE0 computes rows 0–31, and ANE1 computes rows 32–63. Both use the same weights, and each uses all 16 NEs. Each row of a pointwise convolution depends only on the same row of the input, so no data needs to be exchanged between the two ANEs, and accordingly the TD sequences contain no cross-ANE sync records 〔HWX〕.

#### 5.8.2 Shared Scratch Buffer and Sync Records

To study cases that require exchanging data, we compiled four use cases: a three-layer 64-channel 1×1 convolution, a three-layer 64-channel 3×3 convolution, a reduce_sum along height, and a softmax along width (generation script: [bonded_cases.py](../tools/lib/bonded_cases.py); summary tool: [hwx_bonded.py](../tools/lib/hwx_bonded.py); artifacts: [hwx_cases/](../data/03_compile/hwx_cases/)). Except for the 1×1 convolution, the dual-ANE procedures of the other three use cases contain three additional classes of runtime operations. `rt_op_alloc_bss_for_ane16383_P_main__bonded` allocates a scratch buffer, where 16383 (0x3fff) should be a mask denoting all ANEs. `rt_op_map_shared_bss__ane0` and `__ane1` map this buffer to the two ANEs, respectively. `rt_param_main__bonded_ane_{0,1}_hi_replaceable_dsid_usage_dsid_relocation` supplies each ANE with a DSID relocation parameter. Because the DSID is a tag that the SoC's system-level cache assigns to data flows, and because the compiler contains a symbol named `McacheHiBondedKernelReadDSID`, we infer that data exchange between the two ANEs is related to the system-level cache 〔HWX, inferred〕. Whether a dedicated channel also exists, and the specific role of the DSID in the exchange, cannot be determined in this report (Section 16.2). In the reduce-along-height use case, the input and output are mapped only to ANE0. ANE1 maps only the shared scratch buffer and its own TD sequence; that is, ANE1 computes the partial sums for half of the tensor and writes them to the shared scratch buffer, and ANE0 performs the final merge.

In the TD sequences of these use cases, we counted the words whose top four bits are 0xf. `ffc01540` appears at a fixed position in every convolution TD and is a fixed field of the TD. `f0003211` appears at the end of every sequence and exists in both single-ANE and dual-ANE programs, and it should be the stage-end record. `f0003281` appears only in dual-ANE sequences that exchange data. Table 5-15 lists the number of occurrences of `f0003281` 〔HWX〕.

*Table 5-15. Number of occurrences of cross-ANE sync records*

| Use case | Occurrences of `f0003281` per ANE | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1 convolution, 3 layers | 0 | The two halves do not depend on each other |
| 3×3 convolution, 3 layers | 2 | Before layers 2 and 3 begin, the boundary rows computed by the other ANE must be obtained; layer 1 reads the input, with overlapping rows, directly from DRAM |
| softmax, along width | 1 | — |
| reduce_sum, along height | 0 | ANE0 has three `f0003211` words and ANE1 has one; waiting is done through staged end records |

The number of occurrences of `f0003281` agrees with the number of times data must be exchanged between the two ANEs. The words surrounding this record have a fixed format: `c0301440 010N00e1 00000400 00010000 f0003281`, where N is the sequence number of the sync point, and the low byte 0xe1 marks a cross-ANE sync. The corresponding word in an ordinary end record has low byte 0x21. In ANE1, both kinds of records are followed by two additional words, `00001457 00000020`, which we speculate are the semaphore number and count used when ANE1 notifies ANE0. On this basis, we infer that `f0003281` is a cross-ANE sync record, corresponding to what maderix [4] and Bryngelson [5] call WAIT_EXT. Because we did not execute a modified program, this conclusion rests only on the position and number of occurrences of this record 〔HWX, inferred〕.

#### 5.8.3 Cost Model for Splitting

When generating dual-ANE programs, the compiler must decide, for each subgraph, whether to split it across the two ANEs or to keep it entirely on ANE0. The spatial-splitting steps (`ZinMirSpatialSplitter` and `BondedSplitSubgraphIdentification`) are responsible for this decision. They first identify splittable subgraphs, then use `ClusterSplitCostModel::FindOptimalSplit` to evaluate candidate splitting schemes, and finally use `IsCurrentGraphMinLatencyInBonded` to compare latencies before and after splitting. We disassembled the latter and the `LatencyInfo::ComputeSplitLatencyBonded` it calls, and we obtained the following criterion:

```
Latency after splitting = (Σ compute latency of each tile after splitting + Σ copy-and-merge latency) × 0.5
Splitting is performed if and only if Σ latency before splitting > latency after splitting, and the split is not worse than further subdivision
```

In these formulas, all three terms are per-layer estimates from the performance model. They are stored at offsets 0x150, 0x168, and 0x1b0 of the subgraph-split information object, and they are summed by accumulating over layers. The coefficient 0.5 implies that the model assumes the two ANEs run completely in parallel without interference. None of the three terms accounts for contention between the two ANEs for DRAM bandwidth, and none accounts for tiles of unequal size. The criterion considers only the sum of the latencies of the tiles, not the larger of them 〔compiler〕.

To validate this criterion on actual artifacts, we used the `exe_cycles` values in the TD headers, because these shapes are all dense layers and `exe_cycles` agrees with the performance-model estimate. The sum of `exe_cycles` over the TDs of a single-ANE program equals the latency before splitting. When splitting is forced with the option `EnableForcedMaximalBondedSplit`, the sum of `exe_cycles` over the TDs of a dual-ANE program equals the compute latency of the tiles after splitting. Combined with whether splitting actually occurred, this allows the range of values of the copy-and-merge latency term to be inferred back. We performed this analysis for 288 shapes with 256 channels; height × width of 4×32, 8×32, and 16×32; and depths from 2 to 24 layers (tool: [bsdecide.py](../tools/08_bonded/bsdecide.py); data: [bsdecide.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsdecide.txt)). The main results are as follows 〔compiler, HWX〕.

On the chain of 8×32, 256 channels, and convolution kernels from 1×2 to 1×8, there is a clear threshold for splitting: a single-ANE program is not split when the sum of `exe_cycles` is 44 or less, and it is split when the sum reaches 48. For example, for a 1×4 convolution with four layers, the sum is 24 and no splitting occurs; with eight layers, the sum is 48 and splitting occurs. For a 1×7 convolution with four layers, the sum is 44 and no splitting occurs; with eight layers, the sum is 88 and splitting occurs. By back-calculation, the copy-and-merge latency for this class of shapes lies between 44 and 48, about 45. A 1×1 convolution chain is split as early as 16 layers, although the sum of its `exe_cycles` is only 17. The reason is that most layers of the 1×1 chain are read in a chain from L2, and their `exe_cycles` are recorded as 0, so the total significantly underestimates the actual latency.

Further experiments show that whether the copy-and-merge latency is counted depends on the number of pixels in each tile after splitting (it is counted only when a tile has no more than 128 pixels). When it is counted, its size is roughly proportional to the number of activation bytes, as shown in Table 5-16 (tools: [bs_split3.py](../tools/08_bonded/bs_split3.py), [ioch.py](../tools/common/ioch.py)) 〔compiler, HWX〕.

*Table 5-16. Inferred copy-and-merge latency (1×4 convolution chain)*

| Model | Activation size | Per-tile size after splitting | Pixels per tile | Copy-and-merge latency |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 8×32, 160–384 channels | 80–192 KiB | 4×32 | 128 | About 30–70, roughly proportional to the number of channels |
| 4×64, 256 channels | 128 KiB | 2×64 | 128 | 34–46 |
| 8×33, 256 channels | 132 KiB | 4×33 | 132 | Less than 6 |
| 9×32, 256 channels | 144 KiB | 5×32 and 4×32 | 160 and 128 | Less than 8 |
| 8×64, 128 channels | 128 KiB | 4×64 | 256 | At most 8 |
| 8×64 or 16×32, 256 channels | 256 KiB | 4×64 or 8×32 | 256 | Less than 14 |
| 8×32, 512 channels (split by output channel) | 256 KiB | — | — | About 0 |

As Table 5-16 shows, the model counts a substantial copy-and-merge latency only when every tile after splitting has no more than 128 pixels, and its magnitude is about 0.35 units per KiB of activation. As soon as any tile exceeds 128 pixels, this term approaches zero, and chains of one or two layers are split. We excluded the following alternative explanations: whether the merged tensor hits the system cache (the threshold is 16 MiB, far larger than these tensors); whether the sum of the subgraph's inputs and outputs exceeds the 256 KiB L2 residency threshold in the HAL; whether the activations are no larger than 128 KiB; and whether the weights per NE exceed 64 KiB.

The compiler adopts two splitting methods. The first is split by rows. The entire convolution chain is fused into several tiles, each ANE executes all layers on the rows it is responsible for, and the results are finally merged. When the tensor height is 1, the compiler splits along width instead; for example, a 1×256 tensor is split into two 1×128 tensors. The cost of splitting by rows is that convolution kernels with a vertical size greater than 1 must read adjacent overlapping rows (halo), and the more layers there are, the more overlap there is. In addition, each of the two ANEs reads a complete copy of the weights. For example, after a 3×1 convolution chain on a 16×32 input is split, each ANE reads 9–10 input rows and outputs 8–9 rows. The second is split by output channel. If a layer in the single-ANE program has already been split into several passes because of weight-buffer capacity, and the passes have the same size, the compiler divides these passes evenly between the two ANEs. For example, a 13×1 convolution on an 8×32 input has 2 passes of 128 channels per layer in the single-ANE program; after splitting, each ANE executes one pass. A 5×5 convolution has 4 passes of 64 channels per layer; after splitting, each ANE executes two passes. This method requires no copying and merging 〔HWX〕.

We further disassembled the flow of spatial splitting and verified it in a compilation process of our own with lldb (Section 3.2), obtaining the specific rules for splitting 〔compiler〕. Only the "global refinement" path generates dual-ANE splits. `ZinMirSpatialSplitter::Tile` enters `TileWithGlobalRefinement` when the top-level option `GlobalRefinementInSpatialSplit` is enabled (the default), and it requires that the program be dual-ANE and that the bit at offset 0x73c of the HAL parameter table be set (it is 1 for 2026BaseLine). When this option is turned off, the compiler uses only the single-engine tiling criterion and performs no dual-ANE splitting at all, so the dual-ANE program degrades to executing entirely on ANE0 (Section 5.9).

Spatial splitting occurs at two levels 〔compiler, inferred〕. For subgraphs whose intermediate tensors must be tiled to fit the L2 capacity (Section 9.2.2), a tiling grid of T tiles is first obtained, and the tiles are then assigned to the two ANEs. When the layers in the subgraph have not designated an engine, tile i is assigned to ANE1 when i ≥ T/2 and to ANE0 otherwise, with T/2 rounded down. Therefore, when the number of tiles is odd, the extra tile necessarily goes to ANE1. This is the direct cause of the uneven work partition discussed in Section 10.5 (defect B8). The remaining subgraphs are split by the dual-ANE splitting step into exactly two tiles along one dimension. A tensor of height 1 is split along width; otherwise it is split along height. Cut points are aligned to 256 bytes, that is, to 128 columns of FP16 (or 64 columns when the width is too small). The first tile takes half of the width, rounded up to a multiple of 128. If the remaining part is then less than 128 columns, the rounding is changed to round down. For example, a width of 300 is split into 128 and 172, 375 into 128 and 247, and 640 into 384 and 256.

Refinement uses an iterative scheme based on a worklist of subgraphs. If the current overall splitting scheme has already reached a better result, further refinement stops. Otherwise, the subgraphs are divided further at candidate cut points, and the newly created subgraphs are added back to the worklist until it is empty. In addition, to bound compilation time, the compiler terminates refinement early when the splitting gain is less than 2% and the product of the number of layers and the number of tiles exceeds a preset threshold 〔compiler, inferred〕. Notably, this criterion does not explicitly consider load imbalance, so the compiler may still accept a scheme even when the benefit of splitting is very small. This behavior can be observed in a 16-layer 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels and height 1. When the input width is 128, the estimated latencies of the unsplit and split schemes are 0.0550 ms and 0.1022 ms, respectively. Splitting significantly increases latency, so the compiler keeps execution on a single ANE. When the width increases to 256, the estimated latencies of the two schemes are 0.10454 ms and 0.10374 ms. Although splitting yields an estimated performance improvement of only about 0.8%, the compiler still accepts the scheme and divides the computation across the two ANEs. This explains the phenomenon in Chapter 10: at width 128, the model uses only a single ANE, while at width 256, both ANEs are enabled.

#### 5.8.4 Cases Without Splitting

Among the 288 shapes, the cases without splitting can all be explained by the criteria above. The first class is where each tile after splitting is too small. All 4×32 shapes are not split, because each ANE would receive only two rows, and the gain is offset by the copy-and-merge latency. The second class is where no usable splitting method exists. The 1×9 to 1×15 convolutions on 8×32 stream their weights within a single TD, so there are no passes to distribute, and splitting by rows would not be faster. The two passes of 3×3 and 9×1 convolutions contain 32 and 224 channels, respectively; these are very unequal and cannot be split by channel, while splitting by rows would require overlapping rows. The two passes of 11×1 convolutions contain 96 and 160 channels, with the same problem. The 3×1 to 7×1 convolutions have no more than 64 KiB of weights per NE, so they have only one pass, and splitting by rows would again require overlapping rows. The third class is where the spatial size is too small. Tensors with very few spatial pixels (such as 1×64 and 1×1), and matrix multiplications with constant weights (M = 1, K = N = 2048, and M = 64, K = N = 1024), have TD sequences only for ANE0 in the dual-ANE program. M = 1 matrix multiplication is the typical shape of the decoding phase of large language models, which implies that matrix multiplication for a single small batch does not become faster because the M6 has two ANEs 〔HWX〕. It is the size of the tensor, not the number of rows, that determines whether splitting occurs. A 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels and height 1 is not split at widths 64 and 128, but it is split along width at width 256. Likewise, shapes that contain the same 4096 pixels (1×4096, 2×2048, up to 64×64) are all split 〔HWX〕.

The cost model above also does not consider the weight-reading stage that the two ANEs share. Under splitting by rows, each ANE reads a complete copy of the weights, yet the model expects the post-split latency to be halved. Section 10.4 shows that on layers limited by weight reads, such splitting does not yield a speedup (defect B9).

### 5.9 Compilation Cost

Generating dual-ANE programs requires the additional steps listed in Section 5.3. h18 is one generation earlier than h18g, but the differences between their parameter tables lie mainly in capability switches and a few limit fields (Section 5.4), and the main difference between the two in the compilation workflow is whether dual-ANE programs are generated. Therefore, the difference in compilation time between them can be approximately regarded as the cost of generating dual-ANE programs. Table 5-17 gives the compilation times and artifact sizes of three models on five targets (data: [h7_compile_time.txt](../data/03_compile/h7_compile_time.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 5-17. Compilation time on five targets*

| Model | h16g | h17s | h18 | h18g | h19 | Artifact size (h18 → h18g) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1 convolution chain, 64 channels, width 1024, 256 layers | 0.67 s | 0.68 s | 0.68 s | 1.19 s | 1.19 s | 160 KB → 352 KB |
| Elementwise operations, 64 steps (128 elementwise layers) | 0.09 s | 0.09 s | 0.09 s | 1.10 s | 1.10 s | 80 KB → 144 KB |
| Two 1×1 convolution layers, intermediate tensor 16 MB | 0.03 s | 0.03 s | 0.03 s | 0.05 s | 0.05 s | 176 KB → 224 KB |

The three single-engine targets span three generations, yet their compilation times are identical, which shows that the generation itself does not change the compilation time. The compilation times of h18g and h19 are also identical. We therefore infer that the additional time comes from generating dual-ANE programs 〔timing, inferred〕. To determine how compilation time scales with model size, we constructed an elementwise chain y ← y·u + u, in which each step contains one multiplication and one addition. We varied the number of steps n and compiled the chain separately to h18 and h18g. The results are shown in Table 5-18. Table 5-18 and Table 5-17 come from different measurement rounds, and the timings for the same model differ by less than 0.03 s 〔timing〕.

*Table 5-18. Compilation time of the elementwise chain*

| Steps | Elementwise layers | h18 | h18g | Ratio |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 32 | 64 | 0.06 s | 0.18 s | 3 |
| 64 | 128 | 0.10 s | 1.13 s | 11 |
| 96 | 192 | 0.14 s | 4.57 s | 33 |
| 128 | 256 | 0.18 s | 13.1 s | 73 |
| 192 | 384 | 0.28 s | 61.3 s | 219 |
| 256 | 512 | 0.39 s | 178.6 s | 458 |

The compilation time of the single-engine version grows linearly with the number of steps, at about 1.5 ms per step. The compilation time of h18g is approximately proportional to the 3.5–3.8 power of the number of steps: when the number of steps increases from 64 to 128, the compilation time increases 11.6 times, and from 128 to 256, it increases 13.6 times. In contrast, the compilation time of the convolution chain grows slowly; the 256-layer chain takes only 0.5 s longer than on the single-engine target. This indicates that the elementwise layers trigger a search in one of the dual-ANE steps whose complexity grows superlinearly 〔timing, inferred〕. Figure 5-4 plots this difference on log-log axes.

![Compilation time as a function of the number of layers](../figs/en/light/fig5-4_compile_time.png)

*Figure 5-4. Compilation time as a function of the number of layers (log-log axes). Circles show the compilation time of the elementwise chain y ← y·u + u (two elementwise layers per step) compiled to h18 (single engine) and to h18g (dual engine); squares show the control, a 256-layer 1×1 convolution chain. The time for h18 is approximately proportional to the number of layers, whereas h18g scales approximately with the 3.5–3.8 power of the number of layers, a 458-fold difference at 512 layers. The convolution chain takes only about 0.5 s longer on h18g (data: [h7_compile_time.txt](../data/03_compile/h7_compile_time.txt)).*

When a model is first loaded through Core ML, compilation is a further 2.7 times slower. We used a tool that times only the `MLModel` loading process ([cmload.m](../tools/common/cmload.m)), together with the system log of the compiler service and the busy time of each CPU core in IOReport, to compare the compilation time of a 128-step elementwise chain under different methods. The results are shown in Table 5-19 (data: [cm_load_vs_compile.txt](../data/03_compile/cm_load_vs_compile.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

*Table 5-19. Compilation time under different methods (128-step elementwise chain, h18g)*

| Method | Time | Cores that ran the compilation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| In-process direct compilation, default priority | 12.65 s | Not recorded (single-threaded; inferred to be S cores) |
| In-process direct compilation, `taskpolicy -c utility` | 12.66 s | Not recorded (inferred to be S cores) |
| In-process direct compilation, `taskpolicy -b` (background) | 83.9 s | E cores (low frequency) |
| First Core ML load, client QoS default, utility, user-initiated, or user-interactive | 33.7–34.3 s | E cores: the six E cores were busy for about 30 s in total, and the S cores for about 0.2 s |
| First Core ML load, client QoS background | 98.8 s | E cores (low frequency) |

The log of the compiler service shows that only one compilation was performed during Core ML loading, taking 34.05 s, and that the remaining steps of loading took less than 0.3 s in total. The ratio of 34.05 s to 12.65 s is 2.7, which is comparable to the ratio of single-thread performance between S and E cores. Therefore, the main reason compilation becomes slower is that the compiler service runs on E cores, not a difference in compilation options 〔timing, inferred〕. The mechanism is as follows. The launchd configuration of the daemon aned sets `ProcessType = Adaptive`, so that it runs at background priority when idle. The compiler service (`ANECompilerService.xpc`) is spawned by aned and inherits this role. Although it is elevated in priority when the client requests compilation (the log shows `clientQos=21 threadQos=21`), its threads are still scheduled onto E cores. The executable of the compiler service contains no code that binds it to a CPU cluster or actively lowers its priority, so this is the scheduling policy the system applies to XPC services spawned by daemons. The client adjusting QoS can only make compilation slower, not place it on S cores 〔timing, inferred〕. Compilation results are cached, so the above overhead occurs only on first loading.

The compilation cost for real models is even more pronounced. Taking the Whisper large-v3-turbo Core ML package released by WhisperKit as an example, we used [cmload.m](../tools/common/cmload.m) under a new process name to force first-time loading of its four submodels, because the Core ML and aned compilation caches are keyed by process name. At the same time, we recorded the available disk space and the CPU utilization of the compiler service once per second (tools: [whisper_load.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_load.sh), [whisper_tmp.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_tmp.sh); data: [whisper_compile/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/)). On the M6, the mel spectrogram (0.39 s), the context prefill (0.04 s), and the 328 MB text decoder (2.29 s) were all compiled quickly, and when loaded again under the same process name, the compilation results could be reused (0.01–0.08 s). However, the 1.2 GB audio encoder had not finished compiling after 438 s. The disk footprint had increased by about 44 GB, and we aborted the compilation because the available space fell below 15 GB. During compilation, the compiler service, running as root, wrote swap files named `anecompiler.swap.*` into the cache directory of aned, and its CPU utilization was about 200%. The same encoder, loaded for the first time through Core ML on the M4 (h16g), took only 80 s, with a peak disk footprint of about 1.3 GB and no swap files. Both machines have 16 GB of memory 〔timing〕.

To rule out the influence of Core ML, after compilation had started, we retrieved from the cache directory the model that Core ML handed to the ANE compiler (15849 operations, including 5120 einsum operations, which WhisperKit splits by head and by block to fit the ANE). We then compiled it directly in our own process with `anecc` (tools: [whisper_capture.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_capture.sh), [whisper_anecc.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_anecc.sh)). Compiling to h16g and to single-engine h18 both took 41 s, with a peak disk footprint of 3.8 GB. When compiling to h18g, `anecc` wrote the same swap file into the output directory; after about 220 s, the disk footprint had reached 44 GB, while the peak memory was only about 6 GB 〔timing〕. We then truncated the model to its first N encoder layers ([whisper_trunc.py](../tools/03_compile/whisper_trunc.py)), with the results shown in Table 5-20.

*Table 5-20. Compilation cost of the Whisper encoder truncated to its first N layers (`anecc`)*

| Number of encoder layers | 1 | 2 | 4 | 8 | 16 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| h18g time | 12 s | 21 s | 47 s | 88 s | 182 s |
| h18g peak disk usage | 2.7 GB | 4.7 GB | 10.0 GB | 19.4 GB | 39.0 GB |
| h18 time (disk usage 0) | 5 s | 6 s | 8 s | 13 s | 23 s |

The disk footprint of h18g is proportional to the number of layers, at about 2.4 GB per layer, which is about 60 times the weights of that layer (about 39 MB). By extrapolation, the complete 32-layer encoder would require about 78 GB. Compilation through Core ML is even slower. In the evaluation, loading through Core ML took about 24 minutes, which is 3–4 times the time extrapolated for `anecc` (about 6 minutes) and larger than the 2.7-fold difference measured above 〔timing, inferred〕. The 16-layer model was finally compiled, which indicates that this is a resource problem proportional to the number of layers rather than an infinite loop. During compilation, memory was capped at 5–6 GB while the disk kept growing. This indicates that the compiler deliberately stores this data in files, rather than that the system swaps it out when memory is insufficient.

Stack sampling of the h18g compilation of a 4-layer model shows that 97% of the samples lie in `ZinMirSplitSpatially → ZinMirSpatialSplitter::TileWithGlobalRefinement → MirOpt::MergeConvolutions → MirOpt::CreateMergedNEConvLayer` 〔compiler〕. To find out what the swap file contains, we compiled one encoder layer on the M4 with the same version of the compiler, targeting h18g. The model graph was taken from the real model captured on the M6, while the weights were generated as fake data in the MIL storage format, because the real weights reside only on the M6. The resulting HWX size (73,089,024 bytes) and the peak drop in remaining disk space (2725 MB) both agree with the results on the M6. We then used lldb to record the file creation, deletion, extension, and memory-mapping calls of the compilation process, together with their call stacks (tools: [swaptrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/swaptrace_lldb.py), [whisper_fakeweights.py](../tools/03_compile/whisper_fakeweights.py); data: [swaptrace/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/swaptrace/)) 〔compiler〕.

The results show that the swap file comes from the compiler's file-backed allocator `ZinIrFileBacking`. When compilation starts, the compiler first creates a file named `anecompiler.swap.*` in the output directory, then immediately removes its directory entry while keeping the file handle open, and it continuously allocates space through file extension and memory mapping. This space is used mainly to store constant data converted to float32, so FP16 weights occupy about twice the storage after conversion. The allocator supports only space allocation and provides no mechanism for independently freeing allocated regions, so the backing file keeps growing throughout compilation. There were 104 extensions in total, and the file size grew from 8 MB to 2656 MB; the space was released only after compilation finished. Because the directory entry is removed right after creation, this space usage does not appear in the output directory and can be observed only through changes in the system's free disk space 〔compiler〕. Of the 2644 MB of mappings, 2372 MB come from `MirOpt::CreateMergedNEConvLayer`. During global refinement, one encoder layer generated 101 merged convolution layers, each creating three float32 weight blocks of about 7.7 MB. A large number of convolution merges arises from earlier weight sharding: a large convolution is first split into multiple weight shards by output channel, and during global refinement these shards are further split as independent subgraphs. After graph rewriting, several small convolutions sharing the same input appear within the same spatial block, and MergeConvolutions then merges them back into one larger convolution and generates the corresponding concatenated weights. This process causes significant inflation of the weight data. For a one-layer encoder, the weights generated by merging in each program are about 307 MB, while the original weights are only about 39 MB 〔compiler, inferred〕. By contrast, single-engine h18 does not undergo global refinement, so it does not trigger the above convolution merging and does not produce the corresponding swap file.

Global refinement is also a necessary step for this model to be split across the two ANEs. When the top-level compilation option `GlobalRefinementInSpatialSplit` is set to false, the h18g compilation of the 4-layer model returns to 8 s with zero disk usage. However, the resulting dual-ANE program contains only the ANE0 TD sequence, which means that the compiler abandoned splitting. Therefore, disabling this option does not solve the problem 〔HWX〕. Even under the default settings, splitting itself is unbalanced. By the sum of `exe_cycles` estimated by the compiler itself, ANE1 is 1.36–1.48 times ANE0 (for 1 layer, 7074 versus 4783; for 4 layers, 20244 versus 14875), which is 15–19% longer than the critical path under an even split 〔HWX〕. This is the result of several rules in Section 5.8.3 operating together, not a deliberate division of labor. The attention portion has a tile width of 375, and aligning to 128 columns can only split it into 128 and 247. When a tensor of width 1500 is split into three tiles, the extra tile goes to ANE1, becoming 512 and 988. The compiler's cost model does not explicitly consider such imbalance. An attention head group has an estimated gain of −14% when executed on a single engine, but in the dual-ANE split evaluation this cost is simply multiplied by 0.5, so it is still judged to benefit from splitting 〔compiler〕.

When loading through Core ML, two peripheral problems further magnify the compilation cost into a condition in which the whole machine becomes unusable. Ending the loading process does not stop the compiler service, so the swap file keeps growing, and only ending `ANECompilerService` as root releases it. In addition, the compiler does not check the remaining disk space. During the evaluation, the compiler service filled the system disk until only 121 MB remained. The system then purged the ANE compilation cache, after which the next load of every model required recompilation (Section 14.4). We record the disk cost of global refinement as defect A3, and the continued operation of the compiler service after the client exits, which fills the disk, as defect A4 (Appendix D).

### 5.10 Compilation Depth Limit of Convolution Chains

On one class of convolution chains tested in this report, the dual-ANE compilation path also causes the compiler to crash when the number of layers is large. Following the setup of maderix [4], we used a 1×1 convolution chain with 64 channels and width 1024 (each tensor 128 KiB, with weights shared across layers). After changing the number of layers, we compiled the chain with `ANECCompile` for three targets, with the results shown in Table 5-21 (data: [h9_depth_compile.txt](../data/03_compile/h9_depth_compile.txt); generation script: [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 5-21. Compilation results at different depths*

| Layers | h16g | h18 | h18g |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 384, 386 | Success, 208 KB | Success, 208 KB | Success, 512 KB |
| 387, 388, 400, 512 | Success | Success | Crash, return code 138 (SIGBUS) |

The crash reports show that the exception occurred on the guard page of the thread stack, which indicates a stack overflow. The call stack shows that `ZinIrOpLayerGraphScheduler::Schedule` recurses once for each layer of the network, and finally exhausts the thread stack in the call chain `ZinIrScheduleGraph::CompressAttentionBlockBranches` → `ZinIrParallelExecutionOpportunityFinder::FindIntraANEParallelism` → `FindAllAttentionBranchesInRegion` → `ZinIrNgraph::TopologicalSortImpl`. The step that searches for parallel attention branches is executed only on h18g, so h16g and h18 do not crash even when compiled to 512 layers. From this it can be determined that the 386-layer limit of this convolution chain on the M6 originates from the recursive implementation of the compiler rather than from a hardware limit 〔timing, compiler〕. This limit is not a layer-count bound that holds for all graphs: the chain with 512 elementwise layers in Table 5-18 of Section 5.9 can be compiled on h18g. The crash occurs in the step that searches for parallel attention branches, and how deep the recursion can go may depend on the structure of the graph and the compilation path taken, not only on the number of layers 〔inferred〕.

*Table 5-22. Behavior when loaded through Core ML*

| Layers | M6 | M4 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 64–386 | Executes on the ANE (interrupts observed on both ANEs), 191–330 µs per invocation | Layers 383, 384, and 386 execute on the ANE |
| 387, 392 | Loading hangs permanently: the calling process waits for more than 11 minutes, CPU usage is 0, and the compiler service is idle; there is neither an error nor a fallback to the CPU | Layer 387 executes on the ANE |
| 388, 400–1024 | Silently falls back to CPU execution (zero ANE interrupts), 27–70 ms per invocation, more than 100 times slower than the ANE | Layers 388, 400, and 448 execute on the ANE |

Under Core ML loading of the same group of models, the behavior of the system is not consistent. Table 5-22 compares the M6 and the M4 when loading the same `.mlmodelc` under the same macOS version (data: [d5_depth_run.txt](../data/04_schedule/d5_depth_run.txt), [d5_depth_m4.txt](../data/04_schedule/d5_depth_m4.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕. When the compiler service crashes or is terminated, aned keeps waiting for its reply, and all ANE model loading in every process is blocked from then on until aned is restarted. The above two problems are recorded as defects A1 and A2, respectively (Appendix D).

### 5.11 Comparison with the M4

Table 5-23 compares the compiled artifacts and compilation behavior of the M4 and the M6.

*Table 5-23. Compiled artifacts and compilation behavior of the M4 and the M6*

| Item | M4 (h16g) | M6 (h18g) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HWX structure | One TD sequence and weights | Runtime operation graph, three TD sequences, and shared weights |
| TD layout version | v17 | v24 |
| Resident-weight limit per NE | 64 KiB (HAL) | 64 KiB (HAL); when the input is large, the number of TDs per layer is approximately ⌈weights per NE ÷ 64 KiB⌉ (one or two more for some shapes); when the input is small, the weights may be read in a streaming manner within a single TD |
| Compilation time for 512 elementwise layers | Not measured; in Table 5-17, h16g and h18 take the same time, presumably of the same order as the 0.39 s of h18 | 178.6 s |
| Compilation depth limit for 1×1 convolution chains (64 channels, width 1024) | maderix [4]: 383 blocks can be executed, and the compilation service disconnected at 384 blocks; in this report on macOS 27: at least 448 layers | 386 layers (compiler stack overflow); chains of 512 elementwise layers can be compiled |

maderix [4] reported on an earlier system version that on the M4 the compilation service disconnected at 384 blocks, which may be a compiler crash of the same type. On macOS 27, used in this report, the same model on the M4 can reach at least 448 layers, whereas on the M6 it fails to compile from 387 layers onward, because the recursion of the dual-ANE path becomes too deep.

### 5.12 Summary

This chapter has addressed how compilation arranges work: which operations are handed to the ANE, what the ANE programs look like, and what each of the two engines does. The Core ML segmenter selects a backend for each operation under a fixed cost. An ANE segment must save at least 0.25–0.375 ms before it is selected, and on the basis of the AOT driver, we infer that the M6 retains the cost model of the M4. The ANE compiler generates, for each model, a single-ANE and a dual-ANE program that share one copy of the weights. The programs consist of TD sequences that contain no instructions or branches. The weights of each layer are divided evenly among the 16 NEs by output channel. When the weights exceed 64 KiB per NE, they are split into multiple TDs, or they are read continuously within a single TD. Whether a subgraph is split across the two ANEs is determined by a latency model that assumes the two ANEs do not interfere with each other and does not consider tiles of unequal size. Cut points are aligned to 128 columns, and the extra tile of an odd count is assigned to ANE1. When data must be exchanged, the two ANEs cooperate through a shared scratch buffer and through dedicated words that we infer to be cross-ANE sync records. In terms of compilation cost, the dual-ANE compilation path causes superlinear compilation time, compiler crashes from 387 layers onward, and disk usage proportional to the number of layers (defects B1, A1, and A3).

This chapter establishes only how the compiler arranges work. The questions it leaves open are answered in later chapters: how these programs are selected and submitted at runtime (Chapter 6), and how much the splitting cost model, which ignores shared resources and workload imbalance, actually loses (Chapter 10). The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are collected in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 6. Scheduling and Execution

Chapter 5 described the programs generated by the compiler and how the compiler decides what each of the two engines does. This chapter examines how the runtime Espresso submits, schedules, and completes requests, and what overhead each call incurs. How much speedup these arrangements actually deliver is examined in Chapter 10. We combine three kinds of data. Read-only analysis of the kernelcache and the ANE firmware gives the structure of scheduling; firmware and driver events in kdebug give timestamps for each stage; and Core ML timing together with IOReport interrupt counts give the concurrency behavior. This chapter has four main conclusions. First, scheduling has three layers: the ANE driver in the host kernel handles priority, the selection of engine and program variant, and throttling; the ANE firmware queues requests by priority and dispatches tasks; and the NE DVFS state is determined by the CLPC in the host kernel. Second, for synchronous calls, each call incurs about 30 µs of submission-side overhead and 130–325 µs of completion-side overhead in addition to the ANE task. The completion-side overhead depends on how deeply the CPU that waits for the result sleeps, and it does not depend on the number of layers in the program. Third, in the programs measured, mutually independent branches do not overlap; whole-graph timing cannot distinguish whether this arrangement results from the compiler or from a hardware limitation. The fixed overhead per layer is at most about 13 ns. Fourth, within a single request, whenever a dual-ANE program contains work for ANE1, the runtime executes that work. Across multiple requests, requests from multiple threads that synchronously call the same program are serialized, whereas requests to different programs, requests from different processes, and multiple asynchronous requests to the same program are all assigned to both ANEs.

### 6.1 Methods

This chapter uses four methods to analyze the scheduling and execution of programs.

The first method uses kdebug events. Within the kdebug tracing facility of the macOS kernel, events of class 0x06 and subclass 0x1b are produced by the ANE driver and firmware. In tracing sessions started as root, we recorded only events of this subclass (scripts: [d1b_ktrace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_ktrace.sh), [sw_ktrace.sh](../tools/07_memory/sw_ktrace.sh)). By comparing these events with call timing, we determined the meanings of the events listed in Table 6-1. The event names are assigned by this report based on the times at which the events occur and on their parameters; Apple has not published definitions for these events 〔kdebug, inferred〕.

*Table 6-1. kdebug events used in this chapter (class 0x06, subclass 0x1b)*

| Event ID  | Meaning                  | Notes                                                                                                                              |
| --------- | ------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `61b01a9` | Driver receives submission | One per call                                                                                                                     |
| `61b0125` | ANE task start           | Missing in some experiments for single-ANE programs (see below); the CPU column reads 36 for ANE0 and 33 for ANE1                  |
| `61b0126` | ANE task end             | Appears in both single-ANE and dual-ANE programs                                                                                   |
| `61b00b8` | Firmware posts message   | Count equals the interrupt count                                                                                                   |
| `61b0020` | Interrupt handling       | —                                                                                                                                  |
| `61b00a0` | Completion marker        | The event whose first argument is 1 is used                                                                                        |
| `61b0170` | Driver completion handling ends | —                                                                                                                           |
| `61b0024` | User thread returns      | Synchronous return from the Core ML call                                                                                           |

In some experiments, the task-start events for single-ANE programs were not recorded or were largely missing, and the cause has not been determined. In the experiments of Chapters 11 and 12, this event is complete. For consistency, we split each call of a single-ANE program into two segments: "submission to task end" and "task end to next submission." The former segment includes the submission latency and the task itself.

The second method uses IOReport interrupt counts. The `Interrupt Statistics` group of IOReport provides one subgroup for each ANE (`ane` and `ane1`), and the first-level interrupt-handling counts within these subgroups can be read without root privileges. We use these counts to determine which ANE a request is assigned to and whether a model actually executes on the ANE (tools: [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m), [mtrun.m](../tools/common/mtrun.m)) 〔IOReport〕. We calibrated the ANE interrupt counters with a set of control experiments. First, in an idle window of 0.5 s, the interrupt counts of both ANEs are zero. For a dual-ANE program that, after compilation, contains a TD sequence only on ANE0 (for example, a 1×64 1×1 convolution), each model call corresponds to about 3.8 interrupts on ANE0, while ANE1 remains at zero. For a program that contains TD sequences on both ANEs, the counts on the two sides are essentially equal, at about 2–3.5 per call. As a further control, when Core ML executes a model entirely on the CPU (for example, with constant weights, or a matrix multiplication with M = 1), the interrupt counts of both ANEs remain zero. These results indicate that the counter reflects the actual activity of the two ANEs and can be used to determine whether each ANE participates in computation during model execution 〔IOReport〕. Raw data are in bond_calib_run.txt. Accordingly, this counter can reliably distinguish the two ANEs. Because the IOReport counts are system-wide, in multi-process experiments each process reads the total interrupt count summed over all processes.

The third method uses Core ML timing. All models were executed through the public Core ML interface, with the compute units set to `CPUAndNeuralEngine`. Each model was first called continuously for at least 1 s as a warm-up, then called 500–1000 times in succession, and the median or the 10th percentile is reported. Each data point was measured in at least two rounds 〔timing〕.

The fourth method is static analysis. We extracted the kernelcache and the ANE firmware image from the system files of the M6; the firmware is a Mach-O file in RTKit format. The analysis relies mainly on text strings in these files, including the log format strings and assertion messages of the driver and firmware. Where data structures or the conditions of a check must be confirmed, we disassembled the relevant functions (tools: [kcdis.py](../tools/re/kcdis.py), [fwref.py](../tools/re/fwref.py), [fwfunc.py](../tools/re/fwfunc.py)). The firmware and kernelcache are Apple binaries and are not distributed with the repository 〔firmware, kernel〕.

### 6.2 Software Stack and Three-Layer Scheduling Structure

On the host side, a Core ML inference passes successively through Core ML, the Core ML runtime framework Espresso, the AppleNeuralEngine framework, and the ANE driver in the kernel. Within Espresso, the E5 module selects a backend for each operation and partitions the graph into segments when the model is loaded (Section 5.2). During inference, the segments are invoked in order according to the resulting execution plan, and the ANE segments are submitted through the AppleNeuralEngine framework. Model compilation and loading are performed by the daemon aned and the compiler service it spawns (Chapter 5). The compilation results of E5 are cached in directories distinguished by process name, so loading the same model under a new process name triggers recompilation (Section 5.9). maderix [4] observed on the M4 that execution requests do not pass through aned; instead, the client process submits them directly to the kernel driver. The driver hands requests to the firmware inside the ANE through a mailbox, and the firmware dispatches tasks to the hardware. The events in Table 6-1 cover each node along this path.

Based on the analysis of the kernelcache and the firmware, the scheduling of the M6 can be divided into three layers, as shown in Table 6-2 〔kernel, firmware〕.

*Table 6-2. Three-layer scheduling structure of the M6 ANE*

| Layer       | Component                                                                                                                                  | Responsibilities                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Host kernel | ANE driver (`H11ANEInUserClient`; driver bundle `AppleH16ANEInterface`)                                                                    | Priority mapping; selection of engine (ANE0 or ANE1) and program variant (dual-ANE or single-ANE); handling of inter-request dependencies; throttling; power control; reporting work to the CLPC |
| Host kernel | The CLPC (`AppleCLPC`, including modules `ane_job_tracking`, `ane_perf_sampler`, `ane_forced_idle_limiter`, and others)                    | Determines the DVFS state and the throttling ratio of the ANE                                                                                                                                     |
| ANE         | Firmware (source paths `sne/aneEngine/exeLoop`, `sne/drivers/tm`, `itq`, `PowerControlService`, `CLoadMonitor`, and others)                | Queues requests by priority; dispatches requests to hardware task queues; dynamic power gating; reporting the load of the firmware processor itself                                              |

This structure is largely consistent with Bryngelson's [5] description of an earlier product, but there is one important difference. The IORegistry of the M6 does not contain the multi-engine arbitration component `AppleANELoadBalancer` that Bryngelson describes; the selection of engines is performed by the ANE driver itself (Section 6.3). The driver and firmware layers are discussed in turn below, and the behavior of the CLPC is discussed in Chapter 12.

### 6.3 Driver: Priority, Engine Selection, and Throttling

The ANE driver sits between the client and the two compute engines. It is the intermediate layer that connects upper-level requests to the underlying execution resources, and it therefore has visibility into all pending requests and into the running state of both engines. Once a request enters the driver, the driver is responsible for determining the following: the priority at which the request should execute; the engine to which it should be assigned and the program variant it should execute; and, during execution, whether it must be limited because of power, temperature, or firmware load. In addition, the driver reports the execution of each request to the CLPC, which uses this information as the basis for frequency scaling. This section analyzes the mechanisms of the driver in this order. The evidence in this section comes mainly from function names, log format strings, and assertions in the driver. These show which mechanisms the driver has and which factors each mechanism considers, but they generally cannot give specific weights or thresholds.

#### 6.3.1 Determination of Priority

Each request carries a logical priority and a QoS value; the driver's log format `programHandle … priority: %u, qos: %u` records both values. The logical priority is an abstraction exposed to clients, whereas the firmware maintains only its own six-level priority scheme (Section 6.4). For this reason, the driver first maps the logical priority to a firmware priority through `applyFirmwarePriorityMapping`. The mapping table differs by SoC. If a logical priority has no corresponding firmware priority on the current SoC, the driver logs `Logical priority %u has no firmware mapping for this SoC` and truncates the request to the highest available level. On the M6, the highest firmware priority, `kANEFirmwareProgramPriority0`, is marked as not yet supported by the firmware, so requests that would use this level fall back to a lower level. Higher priorities are not open to all clients. Real-time and system-reserved priorities require the entitlements `com.apple.ane.realtime-priority-client` and `com.apple.ane.allow-system-reserved-priorities`, respectively, and ordinary applications that submit requests through Core ML do not hold these entitlements 〔kernel〕.

Strict priority scheduling could leave low-priority requests unexecuted for long periods, so the driver also implements an anti-starvation mechanism. The driver sets a starvation threshold for low-priority requests (log `Low-priority starvation threshold set to %u ms`). When the waiting time of a low-priority request exceeds this threshold, the function `shouldYieldToLowerPriorityRequest` causes higher-priority requests to yield temporarily. To support this decision, the driver counts submitted requests and their execution time separately for each priority 〔kernel〕.

#### 6.3.2 Request Placement: Dependencies, Engines, and Program Variants

After the priority is determined, the driver must decide when a request can be dispatched and which engine will execute it. Dependencies between requests determine when a later request may be dispatched. The driver supports two forms of dependency (fences). In the `LocalFast` form, a later request may be submitted to the firmware while the earlier request is still executing; in the `LocalStrict` form, a later request is held in the driver and is submitted only after the earlier request completes 〔kernel〕.

Next is the selection of the engine. The driver function `SelectBestANECore` computes a composite score for each engine every time a request is placed, and selects the engine with the best score. Its log lists six components that enter the computation, shown in Table 6-3 〔kernel〕.

*Table 6-3. Components used by the driver to select an engine*

| Component    | Meaning                                                                                                   | Factor             |
| ------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------ |
| `WRK`        | Work already queued on the engine                                                                         | Load               |
| `HOL`        | Head-of-line blocking ratio for this priority on the engine (normalized and weighted from `holRatio`)     | Load               |
| `CLPC`       | Available clusters recommended by the CLPC (`allowedClusters`)                                            | Power management   |
| `THROT`      | Percentage of the engine currently throttled                                                              | Power management   |
| `PWR`        | Whether the engine is powered on                                                                          | Power management   |
| `clientPref` | Client's preference hint for ANE0 or ANE1                                                                 | Client preference  |

These six components fall into three groups of factors. `WRK` and `HOL` reflect the load of the engine and favor placing a request on a less busy engine. `CLPC`, `THROT`, and `PWR` reflect the state of power management and favor engines that the CLPC allows, that are not throttled, and that are already powered on. Judging from their names, these components appear intended to avoid unnecessarily waking the other engine, although Section 12.5 observes that the other engine is also powered on briefly when only one ANE is used 〔inferred〕. `clientPref` allows clients to express a preference. Clients can supply only a preference; the final choice is made by the driver, which combines the load and power states.

Once an engine is selected, the driver uses `ANEProcedureVariantSelector` to choose, from the compiled artifact, whether to execute the dual-ANE program or the single-ANE program (Section 5.5). If a client requires that only one designated ANE be used, and the program has no single-ANE variant, the driver reports `requires program to have single-ANE variant` 〔kernel〕. From this we speculate that the compiled artifacts of the M6 retain single-ANE programs as well, and that one purpose is to keep a program available when a request is restricted to a single engine 〔inferred〕.

The actual selection between the two sets of programs by the runtime Espresso can be verified through the interrupt counts described in Section 6.1. Across all models tested, whenever a dual-ANE program contains an ANE1 sequence, both ANEs produced the same number of interrupts during execution; whenever a dual-ANE program contains only an ANE0 sequence, the ANE1 interrupt count was zero during execution 〔HWX, IOReport〕. We conclude that, within a single request, whenever work for ANE1 actually exists in the dual-ANE program, the Core ML runtime executes the dual-ANE program, so that one request uses both ANEs at once. Otherwise, each request executes on only one ANE, and synchronous calls always use ANE0. Accordingly, whether a model can use both ANEs within a single request depends on the compiler's split decisions (Sections 5.8.3 and 5.8.4). How multiple requests are distributed is a separate mechanism, which is discussed below and in Section 6.7.

The mechanisms above can explain the concurrency experiments in Section 6.7. When two processes simultaneously submit requests for a single-ANE model, ANE0 already has queued work, so the `WRK` and `HOL` terms should cause the driver to place the request from the other process on ANE1; this is consistent with the observation that the two engines work in parallel 〔inferred〕. When multiple threads in one process synchronously call the same program, requests always execute on ANE0 in sequence; the location of this serialization is described in Section 6.7.2.

#### 6.3.3 Limits During Execution

After a request is placed, the driver may limit its execution for two reasons.

The first reason is throttling caused by power or temperature. The throttling percentage is determined by the CLPC and enforced by the driver. The driver first queries the CLPC for the current throttling percentage; the query result carries a cache and an expiration time. The driver then converts this percentage into duty-cycle windows, which it logs with the format `Duty cycle windows calculated: throttle=%d%% active=%ums inactive=%ums period=%llums`. In each period, the driver permits ANE work for the active milliseconds and then stops dispatching for the inactive milliseconds. Stopping and resuming are controlled by the pulse timer `ANEHWThrottlePulseTimer`, together with an adaptive budget; when the budget is exhausted, the driver logs `budget exhausted … setting inactive` and enters the stop phase 〔kernel〕. Therefore, ANE throttling on the M6 has two mechanisms: the CLPC lowers the DVFS state, and the driver lets the ANE pause intermittently according to a duty cycle. In our experiments, five minutes of continuous full load did not trigger any throttling (in the sustained-load test of Section 12.6, the variation in per-call time was less than 0.3%, and the counts of all frequency-reduction event types in IOReport were zero). We therefore did not observe the practical effect of duty-cycle throttling.

The second reason is the load of the firmware processor. The ANE firmware runs on a general-purpose processor, and if the host dispatches commands too quickly, this processor itself can become a bottleneck. The firmware reports the CPU load of this processor to the driver through the `CLoadMonitor` module, and the driver runs `ANECPULoadThrottler` on the basis of this report. When the load is above the high-water mark, the driver suspends dispatch on high-priority channels; when the load is at a medium level, the driver limits the number of concurrently dispatched requests to 1; when the load is low, the driver applies no limit 〔kernel, firmware〕. The purpose of this mechanism is to prevent the firmware processor from receiving an excessive number of tasks in a short time, and it is unrelated to the NE DVFS state.

#### 6.3.4 Interaction with the CLPC

The CLPC perceives the ANE workload through two kinds of information: the start and end of each job reported by the driver, and the sampling of the ANE performance counters and frequency-reduction counts by the CLPC's own `ane_perf_sampler` module. At startup, the driver registers as a performance-control client of the CLPC (log `Registering with PerfControl with %u tokens`; on registration failure, it logs `running without CLPC`). Thereafter, for each request, the driver calls `submitWorkToPerfController`, `workBeginForThrottling`, and `notifyPerfController` in turn, to report the submission, start, and completion of the work, respectively. For work known to be forthcoming, the driver can also announce it in advance through `notifyPerfControllerOfFutureWork` 〔kernel〕. The `ane_job_tracking` module of the CLPC tracks these jobs, estimates ANE utilization from them, and selects the DVFS state (Chapter 12).

Clients can also pass information to the CLPC through the driver. The driver accepts session-start, session-stop, and session-abort hints (`kANEClientHintTypeSessionStart`, `Stop`, `Abort`), as well as a hint that marks a workload as energy-efficient (`ANEClientEnergyEfficientWorkload`). A client can also set a timer that powers down the engine after it becomes idle; passing 0 disables this timer (log `Client passed 0 … disabling sleep timer`) 〔kernel〕. Judging from their names, the session hints appear to let the CLPC adjust the DVFS state in advance for a continuous inference session, and the energy-efficiency hint appears to let the CLPC select a lower state. However, this report has not verified the effects of these hints experimentally 〔inferred〕. We did not find settings in the public Core ML interface that correspond to these hints, and it is not known whether ordinary applications can use them.

In summary, the driver holds a decision-making position throughout the lifetime of a request. It determines the priority, the engine, and the program variant of the request, enforces throttling, and provides the CLPC with the load information needed for frequency scaling. The controls that developers can directly influence are mainly the manner of submission, for example synchronous or asynchronous, and the use of one process or several. For the various hints supported by the driver, we did not find corresponding interfaces at the Core ML level.

### 6.4 Firmware: Queues, Slots, and Preemption

The ANE firmware runs on the RTKit coprocessor attached to each ANE. Assertions in the firmware show that firmware priority has six levels (`fwPriority < 6`). There are eight job queues, plus one additional queue for the ANE network used by the image signal processor (AFPP), with the assertion written as `foundQueue == 8 + 1` 〔firmware〕.

The firmware maintains a scheduling information table indexed by queue number (`scheduleInfo[queueId]`). After it finds an available queue and slot according to priority, it places the request into a hardware task queue (TQ). Each TQ has two slots (`reqRunning[index][0/1]`, log `check HW slot … [0]=%d, [1]=%d`): a request in one slot is executing, while the next request may be placed in advance in the other slot. This amounts to double buffering at the firmware level, allowing two requests to run back to back on the hardware 〔firmware〕. However, Section 6.5 shows that under the synchronous call mode of Core ML, the next request is submitted only after the previous request returns; in practice, this double buffering is not utilized.

The firmware supports switching between requests of different priorities. Related strings include `Clear switched out req`, `should not have switched out request when ANE is idle`, and `Pending deprioritize commands present for dsid`; the hardware task manager driver class is named `CSneTMDrvH18g`. The driver sends the firmware a "context-switch latency threshold" (`CSNE_CMD_ANE_DEFAULT_SETTING_SET for setting Ctx Switch Latency Threshold`), and the kernel also maintains a histogram of context-switch latency. From this we infer that, when a high-priority request arrives, the firmware can swap out the executing low-priority request at a TD boundary 〔firmware, kernel, inferred〕. In addition, the firmware has a low-latency direct path, whose assertion is `Direct path should be executed when everything is idle`, meaning that it is used only when the engine is completely idle 〔firmware〕.

The firmware does not select the NE DVFS state. In the firmware, we found only the generic RTKit power-state voting interface (`_getPowerStateVote`), which is used to switch the ANE's own power domains; a `PerfMode` property written by the driver; and a lookup function that translates latency requirements into state numbers (on failure, it outputs `Latency to P State Translation Failed`). The table of this function is populated at runtime, and we did not find any connection between it and the NE clock. The firmware also contains no frequency table for the NE 〔firmware〕. Combining the driver analysis in Section 6.3 with the measurements in Chapter 12, we found no evidence that the firmware takes part in selecting states; the NE DVFS state should therefore be determined by the CLPC on the host side 〔inferred〕.

### 6.5 Time Breakdown of a Single Call

#### 6.5.1 Overall Composition

We first used kdebug to measure the ANE task duration and the time outside ANE tasks for three models under back-to-back calls. The results are shown in Table 6-4 (tool: [d1_trace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1_trace.sh); data: [d1_out/](../data/04_schedule/d1_out/)) 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 6-4. Composition of time per call*

| Model                                              | Per call | ANE task duration          | Outside ANE tasks | Share outside ANE tasks |
| -------------------------------------------------- | -------- | -------------------------- | ----------------- | ----------------------- |
| 1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×128, 16 layers, single ANE | 218 µs   | 58 µs                      | 160 µs            | 73%                     |
| Same as above, 384 layers, single ANE              | 1605 µs  | 1269 µs                    | 336 µs            | 21%                     |
| 1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×256, 384 layers, dual ANE | 1676 µs  | 1310 µs (both engines concurrently) | 367 µs   | 22%                     |

The slope of ANE task duration with respect to layer count is 3.29 µs per layer, which agrees with the marginal cost of 3.26 µs obtained from timing by the slope method in Chapter 10. This agreement indicates that the slope method indeed measures ANE execution time. For small models, the time outside ANE tasks accounts for most of each call. The time outside ANE tasks for dual-ANE programs is about 30 µs longer than for single-ANE programs; we speculate that this reflects the startup and synchronization overhead between the two engines. Each call produces 2–3.8 interrupts, and the exact count varies with the idle depth of the host CPU and with the model (Section 6.5.3). In the table, each engine produces 2 interrupts for each of the two 384-layer models 〔kdebug, IOReport〕.

The time outside ANE tasks increases from 160 µs to 336 µs in Table 6-4. This increase may be related to the layer count or to the task duration. To distinguish the two, we designed a set of control experiments.

#### 6.5.2 Separating Layer Count from Task Duration

We constructed two groups of single-ANE 1×1 convolution chains (width 128, with layers sharing weights). Group A has 512 channels and 16–384 layers; group B has 1024 channels and 4–96 layers, with each layer requiring four times the computation of a group A layer. As a result, the two groups span the same range of task durations, but at equal task duration, the layer counts differ by a factor of four. If a fixed per-layer overhead exists, group A should be slower than group B at equal computation; if the overhead depends only on task duration, the two groups should agree. Table 6-5 gives the measurement results (tools: [d1b_ktrace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_ktrace.sh), [d1b_stages.py](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_stages.py); data: [d1b_kt/](../data/04_schedule/d1b_kt/), [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)) 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 6-5. Call-time breakdown of the two model groups (units: µs; group A / group B; each column gives the median, and the sum of the two parts may differ from the per-call time by a few microseconds)*

| Computation | Model                 | Per call        | Submission to task end | Task end to next submission | of which driver completion handling | of which user-thread return |
| ----------- | --------------------- | --------------- | ---------------------- | --------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| 1.07 GFLOP  | A 16 layers / B 4 layers   | 227 / 212       | 80.2 / 80.9            | 147 / 131                   | 21 / 21                             | 81 / 64                     |
| 2.15 GFLOP  | A 32 layers / B 8 layers   | 263 / 268       | 134.6 / 136.4          | 128 / 129                   | 32 / 32                             | 51 / 50                     |
| 4.29 GFLOP  | A 64 layers / B 16 layers  | 371 / 422       | 243.7 / 252.3          | 127 / 167                   | 32 / 33                             | 47 / 88                     |
| 6.44 GFLOP  | A 96 layers / B 24 layers  | 503 / 550       | 351.2 / 361.3          | 153 / 183                   | 41 / 55                             | 60 / 81                     |
| 8.59 GFLOP  | A 128 layers / B 32 layers | 710 / 752       | 448.4 / 472.2          | 255 / 278                   | 88 / 92                             | 111 / 134                   |
| 12.88 GFLOP | A 192 layers / B 48 layers | 992 / 1012      | 664.4 / 692.8          | 326 / 317                   | 109 / 108                           | 156 / 155                   |
| 17.18 GFLOP | A 256 layers / B 64 layers | 1200 / 1233     | 869.1 / 912.6          | 325 / 320                   | 109 / 109                           | 156 / 155                   |
| 25.77 GFLOP | A 384 layers / B 96 layers | 1618 / 1676     | 1292.8 / 1358.2        | 324 / 318                   | 106 / 105                           | 157 / 154                   |

Table 6-5 supports three conclusions.

First, the ANE side has no measurable fixed overhead per layer (an upper bound is given in Section 6.6.2). The segment from submission to task end has a strictly linear relationship with computation. Group A gives 30.8 µs + 49.0 µs/GFLOP (equivalent to 20.4 TFLOPS), and group B gives 27.5 µs + 51.6 µs/GFLOP (equivalent to 19.4 TFLOPS); the residuals are all within ±5 µs.

Second, the overhead after task end varies with task duration, not with layer count, and it saturates. When the task duration does not exceed about 350 µs, this overhead is about 127–183 µs (the 16- and 24-layer points of group B are higher). As the task duration continues to increase, the overhead rises; when the task duration reaches about 660 µs or more, the overhead stabilizes at about 320 µs. At the same task duration, groups A and B have nearly identical overhead: the 192-, 256-, and 384-layer points of group A are all about 325 µs, and the 48-, 64-, and 96-layer points of group B are all about 318 µs (Figure 6-2).

Third, the growth occurs entirely on the host side. We further split "task end to next submission" into the six stages listed in Table 6-6.

*Table 6-6. Stages after task end (group A, units: µs)*

| Stage                 | Start and end events | 16 layers | 64 layers | 128 layers | 384 layers |
| --------------------- | -------------------- | --------- | --------- | ---------- | ---------- |
| Firmware posts message | `0126` → `00b8`    | 7.4       | 18.5      | 20.5       | 19.9       |
| Interrupt handling    | `00b8` → `0020`      | 22.3      | 15.0      | 14.2       | 17.6       |
| Completion marker     | `0020` → `00a0`      | 7.5       | 4.9       | 7.5        | 11.4       |
| Driver completion handling | `00a0` → `0170` | 20.9      | 32.4      | 87.8       | 105.5      |
| User-thread return    | `0170` → `0024`      | 81.2      | 46.5      | 111.3      | 157.0      |
| Next submission       | `0024` → `01a9`      | 7.0       | 6.8       | 7.7        | 7.8        |

The three stages of firmware message posting, interrupt handling, and completion marking together take about 35–50 µs and are essentially independent of task duration. The driver completion handling increases from about 21–32 µs to about 106 µs, and the user-thread return increases from about 50 µs to about 157 µs. Both of these segments execute on the host CPU. We speculate that they slow down because the CPU waiting for the result enters a deeper idle state during task execution, so that the time needed to wake it is longer 〔inferred〕; the next subsection examines this case.

#### 6.5.3 Effect of Host CPU Idle Depth

To verify this explanation, we ran 12 spinner processes at nice 20 in the background, which prevents the CPU from entering deep idle, and then repeatedly measured the group A models with 16, 96, and 384 layers. Under this condition, the overhead after task end for the 384-layer model decreased from 324 µs to 215 µs, and the driver completion handling decreased from 106 µs to 41 µs. The user-thread return decreased only from 157 µs to 126 µs, because the spinner processes compete with the test thread for the CPU. For the 16-layer and 96-layer models, whose task durations are below the threshold, the overhead was already at the level of shallow idle, and the spinner processes could not reduce it further. The user-thread return of the 96-layer model instead increased from 60 µs to 108 µs, again as a result of CPU contention (Figure 6-2; data: [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)) 〔kdebug〕. A second set of experiments gave consistent results. For a model with a task duration of about 370 µs, slightly above the threshold, increasing the number of spinner processes from 0 to 12 reduced the 10th-percentile per-call time from 580 µs to 519 µs, and the number of interrupts per call returned from 2.0 to 3.2, that is, to the characteristics of the shallow-idle state (data: [sw_spinner.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_spinner.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

When the task duration is close to the threshold, the idle state of the CPU is not deterministic. The CPU idle governor predicts the length of the next idle period from the lengths of the most recent idle periods and selects a sleep depth accordingly. When the task duration approaches about 350 µs, the two choices alternate, so the same model may fall into the fast or the slow state in different measurement rounds. For example, a model with a task duration of about 355 µs produces an average of 2.4–2.6 interrupts per call, between the shallow-idle state (3 interrupts) and the deep-idle state (2 interrupts), which indicates that its calls mix the two states. Models with task durations of about 343 µs and about 370 µs are stable at 3 and 2 interrupts, respectively (data: [sw_ktrace_sum.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_ktrace_sum.txt)) 〔kdebug, IOReport, inferred〕. Consequently, in the range where the task duration is near about 350 µs, the timing slope incorporates state transitions on the host side (Section 9.3.2).

Synthesizing the above results, the time per call for synchronous calls can be written as:

```
Per call ≈ 30 µs (submission side) + ANE task duration + 130–325 µs (completion side; increases as the task duration grows between about 350 and 660 µs)
```

This overhead has a direct effect on ANE utilization. For the model used in Chapter 12 (about 1.27 ms per task), with back-to-back synchronous calls, the median idle gap between adjacent ANE tasks is 384 µs, and this value varies with task duration (data: [gov_kt/](../data/10_clpc/gov_kt/)). Moreover, no next request is submitted before a task has ended 〔kdebug〕. This implies that the two-slot task queue of the firmware is never filled under synchronous calls, that the ANE is necessarily idle between two calls, and that the busy fraction is bounded above by about 77%. How this idle time affects the DVFS state is described in Section 12.2. Figure 6-1 shows the timeline of one synchronous call.

![Timeline of a single synchronous call](../figs/en/light/fig6-1_call_timeline.png)

*Figure 6-1. Timeline of one synchronous call (single ANE, 1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×128; data: [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)). The upper panel shows 16 layers and the lower panel shows 384 layers. Circled numbers mark kdebug events, and horizontal lengths are drawn to scale in time (the task segment of the 384-layer case is truncated). The submission segment is drawn using the intercept of the linear fit of "submission to task end" against task duration (dashed box, about 31 µs). The overhead after task end increases from 146 µs to 319 µs. The increase is concentrated in the driver completion handling and user-thread return segments, that is, on the host side.*

![Host overhead versus task duration](../figs/en/light/fig6-2_host_overhead.png)

*Figure 6-2. Host overhead after task end as a function of task duration (each point is a median). The horizontal axis is the time from driver submission to task end, which is approximately the task duration plus about 30 µs of submission-side overhead; the vertical axis is the time from task end to the next submission. Group A has 512 channels and 16–384 layers; group B has 1024 channels and 4–96 layers.
