Each pair of models with equal computation differs in layer count by a factor of four, and the labels give the layer counts for several pairs. The overhead in both groups varies with task duration: it begins to rise above about 350 µs and stabilizes at about 320 µs above about 660 µs, independent of layer count. Hollow diamonds denote group A with 12 spinner processes running in the background, which prevent the CPU from entering deep idle. The overhead for 384 layers falls from 324 µs to 215 µs. The 16-layer and 96-layer models were already in shallow idle, so the spinner processes cannot lower their overhead; the 96-layer model increases slightly because of CPU contention (data: [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)).*

### 6.6 Execution Order Within a Program

#### 6.6.1 Execution Order of Independent Branches

To test whether mutually independent work within one program can execute in parallel, we placed two mutually independent branches in the same model and compared the execution time with that of each branch executed alone. Except for D, the tensors of all branches have shape [1, 512, 1, 128]. There are four kinds of branch. A is a 1×1 convolution chain that mainly occupies the MAC array. B is an elementwise chain, y ← y·u + u, that mainly occupies the PE. T performs bilinear 2× upsampling followed by average pooling and is presumed to occupy the resampling unit. D is a transpose of a 32 MB input that contains almost no computation and mainly occupies the DMA (models generated by [overlap.py](../tools/04_schedule/overlap.py) and [texprobe.py](../tools/04_schedule/texprobe.py)). Each call has a fixed overhead of about 150 µs, so the predicted time when the two branches are fully serialized is X + Y − 150 µs, and the predicted time when they fully overlap is max(X, Y). Table 6-7 gives the results of two rounds of measurement, with each model called 500 times per round (data: [d3_overlap_run.txt](../data/04_schedule/d3_overlap_run.txt), [d3_overlap_run2.txt](../data/04_schedule/d3_overlap_run2.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 6-7. Latency of two independent branches placed side by side (units: µs; 10th percentile)*

| Combination                      | Branch X alone | Branch Y alone | Predicted, full overlap | Predicted, full serialization | Measured    |
| -------------------------------- | -------------- | -------------- | ----------------------- | ----------------------------- | ----------- |
| MAC array + PE (A64 + B64)       | 374            | 224            | 374                     | 448                           | 486–490     |
| MAC array + resampling (A64 + T64) | 374          | 305            | 374                     | 529                           | 542–583     |
| DMA + PE (transpose + B64)       | 1167           | 224            | 1167                    | 1241                          | 1277–1341   |
| DMA + MAC array (transpose + A64) | 1167          | 374            | 1167                    | 1391                          | 1387–1391   |

In all combinations, the measured time equals or is slightly higher than the fully serialized prediction, and no combination approaches the fully overlapped prediction. For the DMA + MAC array combination, the difference from the serialized prediction is within 4 µs. Some combinations are slower than the serialized prediction. This may be because the side-by-side structure adds an output that must be written back, or because the compiler changes the dual-ANE split after the branches are placed side by side: B64 and T64 use both ANEs when executed alone, but only one ANE when combined. As a control, when the output of A64 is fed into B64 to form a truly serial structure, the measured time is 455–456 µs, consistent with the fully serialized prediction.

We also examined the case that Bryngelson [5] inferred from the compiler, in which the MAC array and the PE run simultaneously, namely softmax in parallel with convolution. Because a standalone softmax chain is placed on the CPU by Core ML, we used as the baseline a serial structure in which the output of a convolution chain feeds a softmax. In two rounds of measurement, the 10th-percentile times were 446.0 µs for the parallel structure and 445.6 µs for the serial structure, which are identical (data: [group1_run.txt](../data/04_schedule/group1_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. In the structures tested in this report, this kind of simultaneous parallelism was not observed.

The interpretation of these results needs to distinguish between conclusions that the experiments directly support and mechanisms that cannot be further determined. What the experiments directly show is the execution behavior of the programs tested: in these programs, the time for two independent branches equals the sum of their individual times, and the branches do not overlap. Two levels of cause are possible. The first is compiler scheduling. Each ANE program is a linear TD sequence (Section 5.7), and when the compiler places two branches into the same sequence, it has already fixed their order; this report found no option that causes the compiler to generate parallel TDs. The second is hardware capability. Even if two adjacent TDs in the TD sequence are mutually independent, the hardware may be able to execute them only one at a time. Whole-graph timing cannot distinguish these two cases. Therefore, the experiments in this section cannot establish that the hardware executes only one TD at a time, and they cannot exclude cross-TD parallelism under other program structures or other compilation methods. The dual-slot task queue of the firmware (Section 6.4) shows only that one request can have the next request queued in advance while it executes, which is a mechanism for chaining requests end to end. It likewise cannot establish whether TDs within a program can execute in parallel 〔inferred〕.

What can be established is that, in all programs tested in this report, no cross-TD overlap was observed within a program. The overlaps that were observed all occur within the pipeline inside a single TD. Convolution, bias addition, and ReLU are fused into one hardware layer. The DMA that reads weights overlaps with computation (evidence in Section 7.4.2: a 3×3 convolution on 4×32 must re-read its weights for every layer, the weight-read rate reaches 148–150 GB/s, and the MAC efficiency remains at about 90%; if the two were serialized, the efficiency would be only about half of this). Long chains are fused block by block, so intermediate results are not written back to DRAM. This result agrees with the measurement of maderix [4] on the M4, which found that independent branches save only about 1.1% of the time. Both are whole-graph timings and therefore share the same limitation.

#### 6.6.2 Fixed Overhead per Layer

To estimate the fixed overhead incurred per executed TD, we constructed convolution chains whose computation is negligible: 1×1 convolutions with 32 channels and spatial size 1×32, with layers sharing weights. The computation per layer is 65.5 KFLOP, which at about 21 TFLOPS for a single ANE takes only about 3 ns. Table 6-8 gives the time per call at different layer counts (data: [d6_tiny.txt](../data/04_schedule/d6_tiny.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕. The 64-layer model is executed by Core ML on the CPU (ANE0 interrupt count zero), and it is therefore not listed.

*Table 6-8. Latency of convolution chains with negligible computation*

| Layers | Per-call median (two rounds) | ANE0 interrupts (per call) |
| ------ | ---------------------------- | -------------------------- |
| 128    | 163.6 / 164.1 µs             | 1.93                       |
| 256    | 166.0 / 166.4 µs             | —                          |
| 384    | 167.5 / 167.5 µs             | 2.01                       |

From 128 to 384 layers, each call increases by only 3.4 µs, so the marginal time per layer is about 13 ns. Of this, the computation itself accounts for about 3 ns or more, since 32 output channels cannot fill all 16 NEs. Converted at the full-clock frequency of 2.58 GHz, the fixed overhead per layer is at most about 34 cycles, which is of the same order as the 44 cycles that Bryngelson [5] read from the compiler cost model of the M1. Because the ANE is idle for most of the time in such models, the clock may not be at its top state; the time-based upper bound (about 13 ns) is therefore more reliable than the cycle-based bound.

### 6.7 Concurrency

#### 6.7.1 Multiple Processes

The experiment used a model that executes only on ANE0. The model consists of 384 layers of 1×1 convolution with input size 1×128 and 512 channels, with layers sharing weights, and the dual-ANE program generated by its compilation also contains only the ANE0 TD sequence. Two independent processes then simultaneously called this model, with the results shown in Table 6-9 (data: [d2_concurrency.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_concurrency.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

*Table 6-9. Two processes calling a single-ANE model simultaneously*

| Case                                                | Per-call median   | ANE0 interrupts (per call) | ANE1 interrupts (per call) |
| --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------- | -------------------------- | -------------------------- |
| Single process                                      | 1598 µs           | 2.00                       | 0                          |
| Two processes, same model file                      | 1795 / 1799 µs    | about 2.84                 | about 2.83                 |
| Two processes, two model files                      | 1796 / 1799 µs    | about 2.84                 | about 2.84                 |
| Two processes, two different models (384 and 256 layers) | 1825 / 1388 µs | present                    | present                    |

When run alone, all requests of this model are handled by ANE0, and the ANE1 interrupt count is zero. When the two processes execute simultaneously, ANE1 shows as many interrupts as ANE0, while each call is only about 12% slower. The combined throughput of the two processes is about 1.78 times that of a single process (2 × 1598 / 1797). This indicates that the driver assigns requests from different processes to the two engines, which execute in parallel. The weights of this model are resident on chip. For how much gain can be obtained on weight-read-bound models, see Section 10.7. This report does not decompose how the 12% per-call slowdown is apportioned among host-side overhead, DRAM contention, and firmware overhead.

#### 6.7.2 Multiple Threads in One Process

We further used multiple threads in the same process to call the model simultaneously. Each thread may share one `MLModel` object, or each may load its own copy. All threads first warmed up for 1 s each, then started together, and called the model continuously for 5 s. Three models were used. A is the single-ANE model described above (384 layers; 1619 µs when run alone). C is a model of the same structure with 256 layers (single ANE; 1195 µs). B is a model with width 256 and 384 layers, whose dual-ANE program uses both ANEs (1691 µs). The results are shown in Table 6-10. Each case was measured in two rounds, and the difference between the two rounds is less than 0.5% (tool: [mtrun.m](../tools/common/mtrun.m); data: [h15_mt.txt](../data/04_schedule/h15_mt.txt), [h15_mt2.txt](../data/04_schedule/h15_mt2.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

*Table 6-10. Concurrency within one process*

| Case                                  | Combined throughput (calls/s) | Per-call median        | ANE1 interrupts        |
| ------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- | ---------------------- | ---------------------- |
| A, 1 thread                           | 616                           | 1619 µs                | 0                      |
| A, 2 threads, sharing one object      | 616                           | 3237 µs                | 0                      |
| A, 2 threads, one object each         | 617                           | 3237 µs                | 0                      |
| A, 4 threads, one object each         | 617                           | 6476 µs                | 0                      |
| B, 1 thread                           | 587                           | 1691 µs                | Same as ANE0           |
| B, 2 threads                          | 587                           | 3390 µs                | Same as ANE0           |
| A and C, 1 thread each                | 1249 (541 + 708)              | 1823 / 1371 µs         | Comparable to ANE0     |
| B and A, 1 thread each                | 762 (381 + 381)               | 2626 / 2626 µs         | More than ANE0         |
| Control: two processes, each running A | 1117 (2 × 558)               | 1781 µs                | Same as ANE0           |

Table 6-10 shows that, within one process, when multiple threads synchronously call the same compiled program, only one request executes at any instant. Whether the number of threads is increased, or each thread loads its own `MLModel` object, the combined throughput remains unchanged; the per-call time increases in proportion to the number of threads; all requests execute on ANE0; and the number of calls completed by each thread differs by less than one call. By contrast, two different programs (A and C) in the same process can execute in parallel. Their combined throughput is 86% of the sum of their standalone throughputs (616 + 843 calls/s), and each call is about 13% slower, consistent with the 12% observed in the multi-process experiment. The same model in different processes can also execute in parallel; its combined throughput is 1.81 times that of a single process.

Accordingly, when multiple threads synchronously call the same program, the requests are serialized. However, the experiments in Section 10.7 show that asynchronous calls to the same `MLModel` object within one process (`predictionFromFeatures:completionHandler:`) are assigned to both engines and execute in parallel when two or more requests are kept in flight. Therefore, serialization is not a restriction on "the same program" imposed by the driver; it occurs on the path of synchronous calls, and this report has not determined its specific location 〔inferred〕. This behavior is recorded as defect B3 (Appendix D).

When a dual-ANE model and a single-ANE model execute together (B and A), the combined throughput is 762 calls/s, about 25–30% higher than the throughput of the two models run alone (587 and 616 calls/s), and ANE1 shows more interrupts than ANE0. This indicates that some requests of the single-ANE model are assigned to ANE1. We speculate that these requests fill the idle time left by the asymmetric workload of the two ANEs in the dual-ANE program, as well as the firmware overhead between tasks 〔timing, IOReport, inferred〕.

### 6.8 Comparison with the M4 and Other Products

Table 6-11 compares the results of this chapter with data for other products reported in the literature.

*Table 6-11. Comparison of scheduling overhead and concurrency behavior*

| Item                          | M1 (Bryngelson [5])                                  | M4 (maderix [2–4])                                                     | M5 Pro (ANEForge [6])                  | M6 (this report)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| ----------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Fixed overhead per call       | About 190 µs, of which the firmware round trip accounts for about 130 µs | About 90 µs (Part 4); 95 µs (Part 2); 160 µs (Part 3)                  | Lower bound of about 70 µs             | Submission side about 30 µs; completion side 130–325 µs                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Fixed overhead per layer      | 44 cycles (compiler cost model)                      | —                                                                      | —                                      | At most about 13 ns (about 34 cycles)                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Overlap of independent branches | —                                                  | Saves about 1.1%                                                       | —                                      | No overlap in the programs measured                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Two requests submitted together | 1.04×                                              | Saves about 16%                                                        | Exposes only one hardware channel      | Multithreaded synchronous calls to the same program: 1.00×; two processes: 1.78–1.81× (relative to a single process); two different programs in one process: 86% of the sum of their standalone throughputs; asynchronous calls to the same program: up to 2.8× (relative to synchronous calls, Section 10.7) |

The per-call overheads of the products are consistent in order of magnitude, all lying between about 0.07 and 0.36 ms. The studies define and measure overhead differently, so these values should not be compared directly. maderix calls private interfaces directly and times with host time; Bryngelson reports a stage-by-stage decomposition; and this report uses firmware timestamps to divide the overhead into a submission part and a completion part, finding that the completion-part overhead depends on the idle depth of the host CPU. In terms of concurrency, the fundamental difference between the M6 and single-engine products is that, whenever multiple requests are in flight, the driver can assign them to the second ANE. The resulting throughput varies with load (see Section 10.7). This applies to requests from different programs or different processes, and also to asynchronous requests to the same program within one process (Section 10.7). Only when multiple threads synchronously call the same program are requests serialized (Section 6.7.2), which resembles the observation on the M1 that only one command executes at any instant.

### 6.9 Summary

This chapter analyzed the runtime execution and scheduling mechanisms of the ANE and obtained three basic conclusions that support the subsequent analysis. First, scheduling decisions are made on the host side: the ANE driver selects the priority, engine, and program variant and enforces throttling; the CLPC determines the DVFS state; and the firmware handles queuing and dispatch, although we found no evidence that it participates in selecting states. Second, synchronous calls incur host overhead of about 0.16–0.36 ms outside the ANE task. The completion-side part of this overhead varies with the sleep depth of the host CPU and is independent of layer count, and it necessarily leaves the ANE idle between two calls. Third, whether a single request can use both engines depends on the compiler, whereas the distribution of multiple requests is decided by the driver; requests are serialized only when multiple threads synchronously call the same program. Within the programs measured, mutually independent branches within one program do not overlap.

This chapter has established how requests are scheduled, rather than how much benefit these arrangements bring. How much throughput rises when requests are assigned to two engines, and why the gain on weight-read-bound workloads is far below twofold, are examined in Chapter 10. How the idle time between calls affects the DVFS state is examined in Chapter 12. Still unresolved are the specific location of the serialization on the synchronous-call path (defect B3), the firmware context-switch threshold and the actual latency of preemption, and whether the nonoverlap of independent branches results from compiler scheduling or from a hardware limitation. Deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are concentrated in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 7. Compute Array

This chapter studies the compute portion of the ANE, namely the multiply-accumulate array formed by 16 NE cores and the planar engine (PE) that performs elementwise operations. It asks how many MACs each NE completes per cycle, which factors limit the utilization of the array, and how convolution kernel shape, data precision, and network depth affect the realized throughput. The evidence comes from three sources: throughput measured with the slope method on Core ML timings and with firmware timestamps from kdebug, the shape fields and output-channel groupings of the TDs in compiled artifacts, and the parameters of the compiler's performance model. The chapter reaches six main conclusions. First, each NE completes 256 FP16 or 512 INT8 MACs per cycle, and the FP16 direct-convolution peak of a single ANE is 21.1 TFLOPS, against a measured marginal throughput of 20.6 TFLOPS. Second, the compiler sets a resident-weight limit of 64 KiB per NE; with FP16, each pass computes at most 16 output channels, and the time steps up when the number of output channels increases from 256 to 272. Third, convolutions with kernel widths of 9 to 15 are rewritten into a stride-2 form whose time is proportional to the number of weight elements; 3×3 convolutions are executed by the hardware Winograd path, which gives an effective throughput of up to 1.47× the direct-convolution peak. Fourth, W8A8 is about 1.8 to 2 times faster than FP16 on compute-dense convolutions (exactly 2 times for 3×3 convolutions), but on 1×1 convolution chains it is limited by the activation path; quantizing only the weights does not raise the MAC peak; compressed weights are read in compressed form, and the compute array skips the MACs of zero weights but not those of zero activations. Fifth, the PE operates in an independent clock domain, and its throughput for single-input operations is about 61 G elements/s per ANE. Sixth, the compiler's performance model is accurate to about 5% for dense, compute-bound layers, but it does not account for Winograd or sparse zero skipping, and it is optimistic about the bandwidth of weight reads and activation DMA.

### 7.1 Test Methodology

The quantity of interest in this chapter is the performance of the MAC array and the PE, but through Core ML only the total time of each call can be observed. That total time includes, besides the computation itself, a fixed overhead of one to two hundred microseconds, which consists of host-side time for submission and completion notification together with the CPU wake-up time that varies with task duration, as described in Chapter 6. When the number of layers is small, this overhead can also overlap with the computation. If the total time of a single model is divided by its amount of compute, the resulting throughput is severely depressed by the fixed overhead and varies with model size. We therefore need a method that separates the per-layer computation time from the total time that the Core ML interface provides.

We use the slope method. Specifically, within each test the shape of every layer is held fixed while only the number of layers is varied. The slope of per-call time against the number of layers serves as the marginal time per layer, and the marginal throughput is then computed from the number of MACs per layer. Because the fixed overhead is independent of the number of layers, it cancels in the slope. To ensure that the measured marginal time reflects only computation, unless stated otherwise, all layers of a model share one set of weights, which keeps the weights resident on chip and thereby eliminates the influence of weight-read bandwidth (model generation: [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py); timing: [runall.sh](../tools/common/runall.sh), [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m)) 〔timing〕.

The slope method is valid only when all points in a set of measurements are in the same execution state. On the ANE, however, four conditions break this premise, and each must be excluded experimentally. First, each call has a floor of about 160 µs. A model whose total workload falls below this floor has a time that barely varies with the number of layers, so the slope obtained from it is meaningless; we therefore use only layer counts whose total workload clearly exceeds the floor. Second, the ANE requires a ramp-up period to go from idle to full speed. Section 11.2.3 measures that the NE clock rises from 852 MHz to above 2448 MHz over about 124–146 ms of ANE execution time, and Chapter 12 explains that the CLPC controls this process. If sampling occurs during ramp-up, models with different numbers of layers sit at different clock states, so before each measurement we run at least 1 s of continuous calls as a warm-up. Third, when the number of layers is varied, the compiler may switch between single-ANE and dual-ANE programs (Section 5.8). The two points on either side of such a switch cannot be used together to fit a slope, so we use the per-ANE interrupt counts in IOReport to confirm the number of engines used at each point. Fourth, Core ML sometimes runs an entire model, or part of its operations, on the CPU without giving any indication. We therefore treat an ANE interrupt count of zero as the identifying flag and discard those points 〔IOReport〕.

Even when these conditions are met, host-side time can still contaminate the timed slope. Section 6.5 shows that the host wake-up overhead after task completion increases with task duration, and task duration increases with the number of layers, so this overhead also appears as a per-layer time. For such measurements, we instead use the firmware-recorded timestamps in kdebug, taking either the interval between ANE task-end events or the "submit → task end" duration (Table 6-1). This gives the execution time of the ANE itself directly 〔kdebug〕. Wherever this chapter reports a result obtained from firmware timestamps, the text says so.

Measurements show how fast the array runs, but they do not directly explain why it runs at that speed. To interpret the measurements, we collected two kinds of evidence from the compiler side. The first is the compiled artifact itself. From each convolution TD in the HWX, one can read the input and output shapes, the size of the output-channel group, and `exe_cycles` (Chapter 5). These fields reflect how the compiler allocates a layer across the 16 NE cores. The second is the compiler's built-in per-TD performance model. Its estimates are normally emitted only on Apple-internal builds of the system. The compile option `DebugMask` passes through a check, `os_variant_has_internal_content("com.apple.ane")`, before it is parsed, and on retail systems this check returns false, so the option is ignored. We wrote a dynamic library ([anevariant.c](../tools/common/anevariant.c)) that, injected through `DYLD_INSERT_LIBRARIES`, makes this check return true only inside our own compilation process. This yields the performance-model estimate for each TD (tool: [perfcsv.py](../tools/05_compute/perfcsv.py); data: [perfcsv/](../data/05_compute/perfcsv/)) 〔compiler〕.

The two kinds of evidence are used together in this chapter. The compiler side supplies the structural parameters of the hardware and the compiler's performance expectations, and the measurement side tests whether these parameters and expectations hold. Only conclusions on which both sides agree are stated as properties of the hardware. Content that appears only in the compiler, and that cannot be verified by measurement through Core ML, is explicitly marked as supported by compiler evidence alone. An example is the higher MAC tier described in Section 7.2.3.

### 7.2 Multiply-Accumulate Operations per NE per Cycle

#### 7.2.1 FP16

We measure the FP16 throughput of a single ANE with a 1×1 convolution chain (512 channels, spatial extent 1×128, with weights shared across layers). This shape is not split across two ANEs, its weights are resident in the NE weight buffer, and its activations remain in L2, so the throughput is limited only by the MAC array. When the number of layers increases from 256 to 384, the marginal time per layer is 3.26 µs, corresponding to a marginal throughput of 20.6 TFLOPS. When the width is doubled to 256, the compiler splits each layer across the two ANEs. The workload per ANE is the same as in the single-ANE case, the marginal time per layer is 3.40 µs, and the marginal throughput is 39.5 TFLOPS, which is 1.92× the single-ANE value (data: [bond_ksweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_ksweep_run.txt); Chapter 10) 〔timing〕. The relation between ANE task duration and amount of compute measured with kdebug is 49.0 µs per GFLOP, equivalent to 20.4 TFLOPS, which agrees with the timing slope (Section 6.5.2) 〔kdebug〕.

To convert the measured throughput into a number of MACs per NE per cycle, the full-load clock frequency of the ANE is also required. The system does not provide a frequency reading for the ANE. We obtain this value in Section 11.2.2 from a separate, independent measurement. The ANE starts from idle and runs the same model continuously, and we record the steps in kdebug task duration during ramp-up. Using only the five lowest DVFS states, which are identical in both frequency tables, we fit "duration = constant ÷ frequency + fixed term" and constrain the fit with an independently estimated fixed term limited to 5–12 µs. We then extrapolate to the full-load state, obtaining 2578–2584 MHz. This fit uses only the inverse relation between task duration and frequency and does not depend on any assumption about MAC width made in this section, so using it to convert MAC width does not constitute circular reasoning. Taking 2.58 GHz, the number of MACs per NE per cycle is 20.6 × 10¹² ÷ (2 × 16 × 2.58 × 10⁹) ≈ 249.5. This value differs from 256 by 2.5%, whereas it differs greatly from other candidate values such as 128 or 512. We therefore infer that each NE completes 256 FP16 MACs per cycle, so the direct-convolution peak of a single ANE is 16 × 256 × 2 × 2.58 GHz = 21.1 TFLOPS, and the measured throughput reaches 97.5% of this peak 〔timing, kdebug, inferred〕. The compiler-side estimate is consistent with this value. At the base tier described in Section 7.2.3, the performance model assumes 256 FP16 MACs per NE per cycle. The `exe_cycles` of a 1×1, 512-channel, 64×64 FP16 TD is 104, which, converted at the measured 20.6 TFLOPS, corresponds to almost exactly 104 µs 〔compiler〕.

#### 7.2.2 INT8

The MAC width for INT8 can be inferred from the following three lines of evidence.

First, the compiler's performance model contains the function `perfmodel::GetNumOutputChannelsPerAccumulator`, which returns the number of output channels computed simultaneously by each accumulator. We disassembled this function and `ToPerfHwParams`, which supplies its arguments, and obtained the logic listed in Table 7-1 〔compiler〕.

*Table 7-1. Number of output channels computed simultaneously by each accumulator (compiler performance model)*

| Condition | Source of value | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Non-convolution operations | HAL 0x440 | 1 |
| Small-input mode 3 / 2 | HAL 0x420 | 16 / 8 |
| Small-input mode 1 | HAL 0x430 | 4 |
| Int8 format, or half-work-unit mode | HAL 0x430 | 2 |
| Other (FP16 convolution) | HAL 0x440 | 1 |

Table 7-1 shows that each accumulator computes one output channel for FP16 convolution and two for INT8 convolution. These parameters take the same values on H16, H17, H18, and H19.

Second, the `exe_cycles` values of the TDs in the compiled artifact and the TD shapes give the same ratio. We compiled the same single-layer convolution (3×3, 512 channels, 64×64) into three versions, FP16, W8 (weights-only quantization), and W8A8, and computed the number of MACs per unit of `exe_cycles` for each convolution TD. The values are 10.3 M for both FP16 and W8, and 20.8 M for W8A8, approximately twice the former (tools: [macw.py](../tools/05_compute/macw.py), [macw_td.py](../tools/05_compute/macw_td.py); data: [macw_td.txt](../data/05_compute/macw_td.txt)) 〔compiler, HWX〕. The fact that W8 matches FP16 indicates that, when only the weights are quantized, the INT8 weights are converted to FP16 before they enter the MAC array.

Third, the measured throughput exceeds the FP16 limit. To avoid interference from the hardware Winograd path and from activation bandwidth, we select only 1×1 convolutions (with at least 1024 channels, so that each byte of activation corresponds to enough MACs) and 3×3 convolutions with dilation rate 2, which do not use Winograd. We convert the throughput to MACs per NE per cycle using the number of engines, the 16 NE cores, and 2.58 GHz. The results are shown in Table 7-2 (tools: [mac3.py](../tools/05_compute/mac3.py), [mac3_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/mac3_fit.py); data: [mac3_run.txt](../data/05_compute/mac3_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 7-2. MACs per NE per cycle for FP16 and W8A8 (measured)*

| Shape | FP16: engines, time per layer, MACs per NE per cycle | W8A8: engines, time per layer, MACs per NE per cycle | W8A8 ÷ FP16 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1, 2048 channels, 16×16 | Dual, 77.6 µs, 168 | Single, 60.8 µs, 428 | — |
| 1×1, 1024 channels, 32×32 | Dual, 71.2 µs, 183 | Dual, 40.4 µs, 322 | 1.76 |
| 3×3 dilated 2, 512 channels, 32×32 | Dual, 171.4 µs, 171 | Dual, 91.6 µs, 319 | 1.87 |
| 1×1, 2048 channels, 32×32 | Dual, 257.5 µs, 202 | Dual, 212.4 µs, 245 | 1.21 |

The first row is the most important. The W8A8 version uses only one ANE and reaches 428 MACs per NE per cycle, which is 35.4 TOPS for a single ANE. A 1×1 convolution does not use Winograd, so the FP16 limit is 256, and the INT8 MAC width must therefore be greater than 256. The value 428 corresponds to 84% of 512. The last row is only 1.21× faster because the compiler assigns the W8A8 version to the two ANEs in a ratio of roughly 1:2. In the dual-ANE program, the sums of `exe_cycles` for the two ANEs are 434 and 880. Calculated on ANE1, which carries the larger workload, the value is 326 MACs per NE per cycle. The DRAM read bandwidths of the two ANEs are only 7.8 and 15.6 GB/s, respectively, so neither is bandwidth-limited (defect B8) 〔HWX, timing〕. On the two shapes where both ANEs participate and the split is even, W8A8 is 1.76 to 1.87 times FP16. The utilization of each format against its own limit (512 and 256, respectively) is also similar, at 62–63% and 67–72%.

Combining the three lines of evidence, we infer that with INT8 each NE completes 512 MACs per cycle. In other words, the number of cells in the MAC array is unchanged, and each accumulator computes two output channels at INT8. Accordingly, the direct-convolution peak of a single ANE is 42.3 TOPS for INT8, and that of two ANEs is 84.5 TOPS 〔compiler, HWX, timing, inferred〕. This conclusion is consistent in ratio with the description given by Bryngelson [5], in which INT8 packing outputs "8 channels per core per cycle" and FP16 outputs 4.

#### 7.2.3 Higher Tiers in the Performance Model

The CSV of the performance model shows that the compiler assumes a higher MAC width for some shapes: 512 MACs per NE per cycle for FP16 and 1024 MACs per NE per cycle for INT8. After analyzing the CSVs of 56 single-layer convolutions and several chained models, we found that the tier depends on the work unit (WU) size that the compiler selects for the TD. The number of MACs per NE per cycle is approximately twice the number of pixels per WU, and this relation holds identically for FP16 and INT8. For FP16, a WU of 128 pixels gives 256 MACs and a WU of 256 pixels gives 512 MACs. For INT8, a WU of 256 pixels gives 512 MACs and a WU of 512 pixels gives 1024 MACs. The larger the spatial size, the more likely the compiler is to select a large WU (data: [perfcsv/](../data/05_compute/perfcsv/)) 〔compiler〕.

We attempted to verify the higher tiers by measurement, and the result was negative. Measuring ANE task duration with kdebug, an FP16 2×2 convolution (256 channels, 32×64) gives 257 MACs per NE per cycle, and the same shape in INT8 gives 413 (data: [hi_kt/](../data/05_compute/hi_kt/); tool: [hi_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/hi_fit.py)) 〔kdebug〕. The reason is that these models all execute as dual-ANE programs at run time, with row-wise splitting, so each ANE receives only half of the rows and its WU is halved accordingly. The rows of the CSV that correspond to dual-ANE programs give precisely the base tier (256 for FP16, 512 for INT8), which agrees with the measurements. We did not find any shape for which Core ML executes a single-ANE program with a large WU. After splitting, every block of these shapes exceeds 128 pixels, and under the cost model of Section 5.8.3 the compiler always chooses to split. When two processes execute the same model simultaneously, the driver lets the two processes take turns using the two ANEs, and the programs still execute as dual-ANE programs; the driver did not reassign them to single-ANE programs (data: [hi2_kt/](../data/05_compute/hi2_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕. Therefore, the higher tier exists only for large-WU shapes in single-ANE programs. On the M6, all programs actually executed through Core ML fall at the base tier.

### 7.3 Weight Buffer and Output-Channel Grouping

#### 7.3.1 Output-Channel Grouping

The number of output channels that each NE computes per pass is called the output channel group (OCG) size. A TD may execute multiple passes, so the OCG differs from the number of output channels of the TD. We disassembled the function in the v24 layout that configures the NE and determined that register 0x10 is the NE configuration register. Its bits 0–2 record the OCG size as a base-2 logarithm, and bit 6 is the half-work-unit mode. Field tracing shows that the half-work-unit mode is enabled only in W8A8, and that the OCG of depthwise and grouped convolutions is 1 and 2, respectively (Table 5-13). We then compiled single-layer convolutions with kernel shapes 1×1, 1×3, 3×1, 2×2, 3×3, 5×5, and 7×7, input channels from 16 to 512, and precisions FP16 and W8A8 (spatial size 32×32), and read the OCG of each TD. Table 7-3 gives the FP16 results (tool: [ocg.py](../tools/05_compute/ocg.py); data: [ocg_table.txt](../data/05_compute/ocg_table.txt)) 〔compiler, HWX〕.

*Table 7-3. OCG size for FP16 convolution*

| Kernel | Cin = 16 | 64 | 256 | 512 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1, 3×1, 2×2 | 16 | 16 | 16 | 16 |
| 5×5 | 16 | 16 | 4 | 2 |
| 7×7 | 16 | — | 2 | 1 |
| 3×3, 1×3 (Winograd) | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 |

Except for Winograd convolutions, the OCG values in Table 7-3 all satisfy the following relation:

```
OCG = min(16, the largest power of 2 such that OCG × Cin × kH × kW × 2 B ≤ 64 KiB)
```

For example, a 2×2 convolution with 512 channels has exactly 16 × 512 × 4 × 2 B = 64 KiB. The OCG for W8A8 is likewise limited by 64 KiB, except that each weight is counted as 1 byte. This is a further independent piece of evidence that each NE has a 64 KiB weight buffer. It agrees with the conclusion drawn in Section 5.6 from the number of TDs for larger inputs, and with the 64 KiB "resident-weight limit" in the HAL parameter table. Winograd convolutions have very small OCGs (1–2) and are not limited by the weight buffer. For a 3×3 convolution with 256 channels, the transformed weights amount to only 2 × 256 × 16 × 2 B = 16 KiB per NE per pass. We conjecture that they are limited by accumulator capacity, because each output must accumulate a 4×4 block in the transform domain 〔inferred〕.

The upper limit of 16 for the OCG agrees with the NE work unit of 16 in the HAL. From this one may conjecture that the 256 MACs per NE per cycle are organized as 16 output channels × 16 input channels (or 16 pixels), but this report cannot distinguish between the two possibilities 〔inferred〕. The accumulator file depth of "8" reported by Bryngelson [5] has no direct counterpart in the M6 OCG, and the units of the two may differ.

#### 7.3.2 Steps in the Output-Channel Count

Because the OCG upper limit is 16 and each ANE contains 16 NE cores, a single pass can cover at most 256 output channels. When the number of output channels exceeds 256, the computation must be completed in multiple passes, and the input data are reread in each pass. To verify this, we measured the per-call time of a 1×1 convolution chain (spatial extent 1×128, single ANE, weights shared across layers) as the channel count C increases from 192 to 1024 in steps of 16. For each channel count we measured two models, with 32 and 160 layers (data: [b2b_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b2b_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. The models with these two layer counts often fall in two different host wake-up states described in Section 6.5, in which the interrupt count per call is about 2 for one and about 3 for the other. Computing the per-layer time from the difference of two points incorporates the difference in overhead caused by the state switch, so the absolute value obtained is too large. For example, at 512 channels it is 4.69 µs, whereas Section 7.2.1 measures 3.26 µs for the same shape over the 256–384-layer range, where both points are in the same state. We therefore do not use the difference of two points. Instead, we compare only measurement points in the same state. For C from 192 to 352, the interrupt count per call of the 160-layer model is about 3 throughout, so these points are in the same state, and their per-call times are given in Table 7-4.

*Table 7-4. Per-call time of the 160-layer 1×1 convolution chain versus channel count (same host wake-up state)*

| C | Per-call time | Increment relative to previous step | Interrupts per call |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 192 | 217.1 µs | — | 3.16 |
| 208 | 253.0 µs | +35.9 µs | 3.02 |
| 224 | 262.6 µs | +9.6 µs | 3.03 |
| 240 | 293.8 µs | +31.2 µs | 3.04 |
| 256 | 302.7 µs | +8.9 µs | 3.02 |
| 272 | 359.7 µs | +57.0 µs | 3.06 |
| 288 | 371.6 µs | +11.9 µs | 3.01 |
| 304 | 383.5 µs | +11.9 µs | 3.05 |
| 320 | 394.9 µs | +11.4 µs | 3.04 |
| 336 | 411.5 µs | +16.6 µs | 3.04 |
| 352 | 423.1 µs | +11.6 µs | 3.04 |

In Table 7-4, when C increases from 256 to 272, the per-call time increases by 57.0 µs, which is the largest increment up to 352. The steps from 272 to 352 each add only 11–17 µs per 16 channels, whereas the increments below 256 alternate between about 9 µs and 31–36 µs. The position of this step corresponds exactly to the limit of 256 output channels per pass, and it is consistent with the output-channel grouping of the compiled artifact (Section 7.3.1). However, the increments below 256 themselves alternate between about 9 µs and 31–36 µs. This report has not explained the source of this alternation, and the existing data cannot fully exclude that part of the increment at 272 comes from the same alternation. Therefore, the correspondence between the step and 256 channels per pass remains an inference 〔timing, HWX, inferred〕. Because the existing data cannot yield an accurate per-layer time, this report does not give the magnitude of the step as a percentage. Bryngelson [5] reports that on the M1 the time becomes 3 times larger when the output-channel count increases from 192 to 256, and the M6 shows no such steep step. We conjecture that the reason is that the input tensor of this shape is very small (256 KB when C = 1024), fully resides in L2, and the cost of rereading the input is small 〔inferred〕. Figure 7-1 shows the per-call time of the 160-layer model as a function of channel count.

The case above 352 cannot be judged by the same method. The three steps from 368 to 400 remain in the same wake-up state (interrupt counts 3.00–3.26), but their increments are generally larger: 352 → 368 is +51.4 µs and 384 → 400 is +68.1 µs, both larger than 256 → 272. This report does not provide an analysis of the cause at present. Therefore, the only supportable statement is that the 256 → 272 step is prominent within 352, not that it is the only step over the entire range 〔timing〕. At 400 → 416, the interrupt count of the 160-layer model drops from 3.00 to 2.03, and thereafter it is largely in another wake-up state (with the exception of C = 832, where the interrupt count is 3.01). Increments across this switch cannot be compared directly. The 512 → 528 and 768 → 784 points also show large increments, but the 160-layer models happen to lie between two states at these points (interrupt counts 2.34 and 2.08, respectively, and C = 960 shows a similar situation), so it cannot be confirmed whether they are real steps.

![OCG step](../figs/en/light/fig7-1_ocg_step.png)

*Figure 7-1. Per-call time of the 160-layer 1×1 convolution chain (1×128, single ANE) as a function of channel count C. (a) C = 192–1024. Markers distinguish host wake-up states by the interrupt count per call; at the diamonds, the two states are mixed, and the increments on either side cannot be confirmed as real steps. (b) Enlarged view of the range of Table 7-4. When C increases from 256 to 272, the per-call time increases by 57.0 µs (data: [b2b_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b2b_run.txt)).*

#### 7.3.3 Utilization with Few Output Channels

The compiler divides a layer across the 16 NE cores by output channel. We measured the marginal throughput of 1×1 convolution chains with 32–256 channels at a very large spatial extent (1×8192, split across two ANEs): 14.3 TFLOPS at 32 channels, 23.6 TFLOPS at 64 channels, 31.6 TFLOPS at 96 channels, and 35.7 TFLOPS at 192 channels (data: [group1_run.txt](../data/04_schedule/group1_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. The compiled artifacts show that at 16, 32, and 64 channels the weights are split into 16 parts, and at 16 channels each NE receives one output channel. In other words, as long as the number of output channels is at least 16, all 16 NE cores are enabled. When the number of channels is small, the throughput is low. We conjecture that the reason is the small workload per NE and the short accumulation length, not that any NE is idle 〔HWX, timing, inferred〕.

For layers with few channels and a large spatial extent, array utilization is generally low, so one may consider rearranging the data to convert the spatial dimension into the channel dimension, thereby improving utilization. The space_to_depth (S2D) transformation is such a rearrangement. Table 7-5 compares the throughput before and after the transformation for a 32-layer 1×1 convolution chain with 64 channels and a 128×128 spatial extent, and it compares these results with the M4 results of maderix [4] (tool: [s2d.py](../tools/05_compute/s2d.py); data: [b8_s2d_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b8_s2d_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 7-5. Effect of space_to_depth on throughput (32-layer 1×1 convolution chain)*

| Model | Time | M6 throughput | M4 throughput [4] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 64 channels, 128×128, direct execution | 450 µs | 9.5 TFLOPS | 3.92 TFLOPS |
| S2D(4) → 1024 channels, 32×32 → D2S(4) | 2168 µs | 31.7 TFLOPS | 16.37 TFLOPS |
| Direct input of 1024 channels, 32×32 | 2149 µs | 32.0 TFLOPS | — |
| S2D(2) → 256 channels, 64×64 → D2S(2) | 876 µs | 19.6 TFLOPS | — |
| Direct input of 256 channels, 64×64 | 874 µs | 19.7 TFLOPS | — |

S2D(4) raises throughput to 3.3× the original. Compared with a model whose input is directly the transformed shape, the overhead of S2D and D2S is about 0.9% for block = 4 and about 0.2% for block = 2. maderix [4] reports that on the M4 the compiler implements S2D(2) as a convolution with one-hot weights, with an overhead of 2.8–3.1%; this problem does not occur on the M6.

The models before and after transformation in Table 7-5 are not equivalent. The 1×1 convolution after S2D(4) acts on 1024 channels, so each layer has 16 times the compute of the original model (1024² × 32² versus 64² × 128²) and 256 times the weights. The semantics of the model also change: it mixes all channels within each 4×4 block of pixels, whereas the original model mixes channels only within a single pixel. Therefore, although throughput increases by 3.3×, the total time increases from 450 µs to 2168 µs. To implement the original model exactly with S2D, the transformed convolution weights must form a block-diagonal matrix composed of 16 identical 64×64 blocks. Executed as an ordinary convolution, this still requires 16 times the compute, and the total time does not shorten; using a grouped convolution instead keeps the amount of compute unchanged. The conclusion supported by Table 7-5 is therefore the following. On the ANE, shapes with many channels and small spatial extent utilize the MAC array better than shapes with few channels and large spatial extent, and the overhead of S2D and D2S themselves is negligible. Table 7-5 does not show that inserting S2D into an existing model speeds it up. The conclusion is more meaningful for model design. For example, when designing a network, one may replace an early high-resolution, few-channel stage with a structure that first reduces resolution and then increases the number of channels, such as patch embedding 〔timing, inferred〕.

### 7.4 Convolution Kernel Shape

#### 7.4.1 Directly Encoded Kernel Widths and Compiler Rewriting

From the convolution configuration word (Table 5-9), we read the Kw, Sx, and Ox fields for different kernel shapes. The results are shown in Table 7-6 (tool: [convcfg.py](../tools/03_compile/convcfg.py); data: [convcfg_scan.txt](../data/03_compile/convcfg_scan.txt), [ks4_hwx/](../data/05_compute/ks4_hwx/)) 〔HWX〕. The per-layer TD counts in the table are for a 16×32 input. For smaller inputs such as 4×32 and 8×32, a rewritten kernel has only one TD per layer (Section 5.6).

*Table 7-6. Encoding of different kernel shapes (256 channels, 16×32)*

| Kernel | Kw in configuration word | Sx | Ox | TDs per layer | Treatment |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×3 to 1×8 | 3–8 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Native execution |
| 1×9 | 10 | 2 | 2 | 3 | Rewritten |
| 1×11 | 12 | 2 | 2 | 4 | Rewritten |
| 1×15 | 16 | 2 | 2 | 6 | Rewritten |
| 1×16, 1×17 | — | — | — | — | Compilation failure |
| 7×1, 9×1, 15×1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1, 2, 2 | Native execution |
| 3×3, 5×5, 7×7 | 3, 5, 7 | 1 | 1 | 2, 4, 8 | Native execution |

Table 7-6 shows that the largest kernel width that the compiler encodes directly with stride 1 is 8. Below, such kernels are called "native kernels." For kernel widths of 9–15, the compiler rewrites the convolution into a form with horizontal stride 2. The Kw in the configuration word is the kernel width plus one, padded to an even number (9 → 10, 10 → 12, ..., 15 → 16), Sx = 2, and Ox = 2. Each step therefore produces two adjacent output pixels. The output-width field correspondingly becomes half of the actual width, and the weights are stored as two kernel sets offset by one position. After rewriting, the weight amount per input–output channel pair is about 2 × (kernel width + 1). For example, a 1×9 convolution after rewriting corresponds to about 19.3 taps on average. This value is obtained by dividing the weight-segment byte count by (input channels × output channels × 2 B), so it is not an integer number of taps. The values for 1×9, 1×10, and 1×12 (19.3, 21.5, and 25.8) are all below 2 × Kw (20, 24, and 28), which indicates that the two offset kernel sets are not stored in full at these widths. For 1×15 the value is 32.05, comparable to 2 × 16 〔HWX〕. This is because the rewritten kernels use a sparse weight format, in which the zeros produced by padding do not need to be physically stored in the weight segment (Section 7.4.2). When the kernel width reaches 16, the rewritten Kw would exceed 16, and `ANECCompile` returns an error for 1×16 and 1×17 convolutions. Under Core ML execution, the segmenter determines at the segmentation step that such convolutions cannot be placed on the ANE (Section 5.2), and it places them on the CPU without reporting an error at load time. The test models in this section that consist of such convolutions therefore execute entirely on the CPU, and their per-layer time is 4–7 times that of a 1×15 convolution 〔HWX, timing〕. The upper limit of 15 on FP16 convolution kernel width given by Bryngelson [5] corresponds, in the compiled artifact, to direct stride-1 encoding up to width 8, and to a stride-2 rewrite up to width 15. No such limit applies vertically: kernels of height up to 15 are all encoded directly with stride 1, and 17×1 convolutions also compile. The M4 compilation target h16g and the H17-generation target h17s likewise rewrite 1×9 convolutions into the form Sx = 2, Ox = 2, except that the Kw in the configuration word is written as 9 (`0x6008c049`). Starting with h18, it is written as 10 (`0x6008c04a`) (data: [tdv/](../data/03_compile/tdv/)) 〔HWX〕.

The limits above are all derived from the compiler's encoding scheme. The two boundaries of 8 and 16 indicate that the compiler considers the hardware not to support kernels wider than 8 at stride 1, and not to support Kw above 16 at stride 2. We have not executed programs with Kw greater than 8 at stride 1, so we cannot rule out that the hardware itself supports wider kernels and that the compiler chooses rewriting for efficiency or other reasons 〔compiler, inferred〕.

#### 7.4.2 Kernel Shape and Efficiency

To determine the effect of kernel shape on efficiency, we measured chains of 2–24 layers (256 channels, weights shared across layers) for 24 kernel types (1×1 through 1×15, odd-sized kernels from 3×1 through 15×1, 3×3, and 5×5) at four spatial sizes: 4×32, 8×32, 16×32, and 32×32. Because the firmware's task-start events were not recorded in this group of single-ANE experiments, we timed every run uniformly from "submission → last task end," and we fit slopes using only layer counts with the same engine mode (tools: [ks4_ktrace.sh](../tools/05_compute/ks4_ktrace.sh), [ks4_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_fit.py); data: [ks4_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_fit.txt), [ks4_kt/](../data/05_compute/ks4_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕. Table 7-7 lists the efficiency of selected kernel shapes relative to the direct-convolution peak (21.1 TFLOPS for a single ANE and 42.3 TFLOPS for two ANEs).

*Table 7-7. Efficiency of different kernel shapes relative to the direct-convolution peak*

| Kernel | Single ANE, 4×32 | Single ANE, 8×32 | Dual ANE, 16×32 | Dual ANE, 32×32 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1 | 91% | — | 75% | 78% |
| 3×1 | 94% | 94% | 61% | 79% |
| 1×3 | 136% | 130% | 132% | 121% |
| 1×5 | 94% | 88% | 91% | 96% |
| 1×8 | 94% | 84% | 97% | 99% |
| 1×9 | 42% | 85% | 43% | 86% |
| 1×12 | 42% | 85% | 76% | 88% |
| 1×15 | 42% | 85% | 42% | 86% |
| 9×1 | 91% | 92% | 73% | 83% |
| 15×1 | 91% | — | 96% | 98% |
| 3×3 | 90% | 131% | 79% | 114% |
| 5×5 | 90% | — | 97% | 95% |

Table 7-7 shows that the kernel shape by itself does not reduce efficiency. Native kernels (horizontal kernels with width no greater than 8, and vertical kernels with height up to 15) reach 90–95% efficiency at the single-ANE 4×32 shape and 84–98% at 8×32. The 1×3 and 3×3 convolutions, whose efficiency exceeds 100%, use the hardware Winograd path (Section 7.4.3). The 1×1 and 3×1 convolutions have lower efficiency in dual-ANE mode (61–79%). Compared with the ideal value obtained by scaling the single-ANE result by the number of rows, the dual-ANE 16×32 shape incurs an additional overhead of about 0.4 µs per layer for 1×1 and 2.8 µs for 3×1. These are much larger than the approximately 0.14 µs cited in Section 10.3, so they cannot be explained by a general cooperation overhead. Possible sources include the overlapping rows read by vertical kernels after row splitting (Section 5.8.3), and other overheads when the computation per layer is small 〔inferred〕. The 9×1 and 3×3 convolutions also reach only 73% and 79% on the dual-ANE 16×32 shape. A "—" in the table indicates that no measurement point exists for this shape in this engine mode, because the compiler selected another mode.

Rewritten kernels (1×9 through 1×15) show a completely different pattern: the time per layer is proportional to the amount of weights and independent of the number of rows. For example, the 1×9 convolution has the same per-layer time at 4×32 and at 8×32 (17.00 µs and 16.89 µs, respectively), so its efficiency at 4×32 is only half of that at 8×32. Using the per-layer time and a weight-read bandwidth of 150 GB/s to back-calculate the number of taps per channel pair, we obtain 19.3, 23.5, and 32.0 for 1×9, 1×11, and 1×15, respectively. These differ from the weight amounts read from the compiled artifact by less than 0.5%. Rewriting doubles the weight amount per layer, to 2.5–4.2 MB, which far exceeds the 64 KiB per-NE resident limit, so the weights must be reread for every layer.

However, these layers are not limited by DRAM weight-read bandwidth. After quantizing the weights of the 1×9 convolution to Int8, the per-layer time does not shorten (Section 7.5). Therefore, the time of the rewritten kernels is proportional to the number of weight elements rather than to the number of weight bytes. The bottleneck is a stage metered per weight element, at a rate of about 75 billion weights per second. The relation "FP16 weight bytes ÷ 150 GB/s" agrees with the measurement only because, when converted at FP16, this rate happens to be close to the weight-read bandwidth 〔kdebug, HWX, inferred〕. Sections 10.4 and 10.7 show that when both engines execute this type of layer simultaneously, they slow each other down, which indicates that the stage is shared by the two engines.

Native kernels whose weights exceed 64 KiB likewise must be reread for every layer. For example, 3×3, 5×5, and 11×1 through 15×1 convolutions at 4×32 imply back-calculated weight-read rates of 148–150 GB/s. For native kernels, the 2048-channel 1×1 convolution of Section 7.5 confirms that the time does vary with the number of weight bytes. The efficiency of these shapes is almost exactly 90%, because 4×32 (128 pixels) lies at the balance point between computation and weight reading: each FP16 weight read supports 128 MACs, and full-speed computation requires about 165 GB/s of weight-read bandwidth.

Field tracing and disassembly of the compiler offer a possible explanation for this bottleneck. When deciding the weight format, the compiler computes the fraction of zeros introduced by rewriting, and it switches to a sparse weight format when this fraction exceeds 1/7. There are also cases, such as dilated convolution, in which the upstream stage directly marks the layer as "must compress." According to the estimate of the compiler function `CalculateSparsityFromPadding`, the padding fraction after rewriting a stride-2 convolution is 0.4375, and that of the rewritten kernel of width 9 is 0.55. Both exceed the threshold. This is the ratio that the compiler derives from the rewriting method. When back-calculated from the size of the weight segment in the compiled artifact, the padding zeros amount to only about 6–10% of the elements actually stored by the rewritten kernel (for 1×9, 2 of 20 elements). This shows that the decision is based on the estimated value, not on the zeros actually stored. Ordinary 3×3 and 5×5 convolutions have no padding and do not use the sparse format 〔compiler〕. In other words, rewritten kernels of width 9–15, dilated convolutions, stride-2 convolutions, and bilinear upsampling all execute sparse weights on the hardware, relying on the zero-skipping hardware described in Section 7.5 to avoid the padding zeros. This explains two phenomena of rewritten kernels: their weight segments are smaller than two full kernel sets, and the throughput of dilated convolution is markedly lower than that of an ordinary 3×3 convolution (Section 7.4.3). We further conjecture that the shared stage described above, metered per weight element, is the decoding stage of the sparse weights. It processes compressed weights on an element basis, so quantizing to Int8 cannot speed it up, and it contends between the two ANEs when both are in use 〔inferred〕. This conjecture is compatible with the observation in the pruning experiments of Section 7.5 that the speedup does not reach the non-zero fraction. In this decision, the compiler considers only the zeros introduced by rewriting and does not scan the weights for zeros. Zero weights written as ordinary constants therefore do not trigger the sparse format (Section 7.5).

Under dual-ANE execution, the efficiency of rewritten kernels depends on the splitting method. The 1×9, 1×10, 1×11, and 1×15 convolutions at 16×32 are split by rows, so both ANEs read a full copy of the weights and share the above bottleneck, and their efficiency is only 42–43%. The 1×12 through 1×14 convolutions are split by output channel, with efficiency of 73–77% (Section 10.4, defect B9).

#### 7.4.3 Hardware Winograd

On a 3×3 convolution chain with 512 channels and a 16×16 spatial extent, the FP16 marginal throughput of a single ANE reaches 31.0 TFLOPS, which is 1.47× the direct-convolution peak. By contrast, a 3×3 convolution with dilation rate 2, which cannot use Winograd, achieves only 14.6 TFLOPS per ANE, about 70% of the direct-convolution peak (data: [h10_w8a8_k3.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_w8a8_k3.txt), [h10_dilation.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_dilation.txt)) 〔timing〕. The weight-segment size in the compiled artifact is exactly 512 × 512 × 9 × 2 bytes, which means that the raw 3×3 weights are stored and the Winograd transform is performed in hardware. This is consistent with the conclusion, reached by Bryngelson [5] through reverse engineering, that the Winograd transform matrices are fixed in hardware 〔HWX〕. F(2×2, 3×3) would theoretically reduce the number of multiplications to 1/2.25, and the measurement achieves about 65% of this theoretical speedup. 1×3 convolutions also exceed the direct-convolution peak, corresponding to the one-dimensional Winograd bit (`1DWinograd`, bit 27) in the TD task information word 〔HWX〕.

The compiler's performance estimation model does not take the Winograd transform into account. For the TD of a 3×3 convolution with 512 channels and 16×16, `exe_cycles` is 58 per layer, and the performance model estimates 58.8 µs per layer. The measured value, obtained in this section and in Section 7.5 over two rounds, is 39–44 µs per layer. This is about 1.3–1.5 times faster, which is comparable to the speedup from Winograd (Section 7.8) 〔compiler, timing〕. Furthermore, Winograd is not enabled for all 3×3 convolutions. Field tracing shows that, among the 3×3-type convolutions traced, the Winograd bit in the task information word is 1 only for ordinary 3×3 and grouped 3×3 convolutions. Depthwise convolution, 5×5, dilated convolution, and stride-2 convolution all have 0 (Table 5-13), which is consistent with the measured results for dilated convolution above 〔compiler, timing〕. However, on a 3×3 convolution chain with 256 channels and an 8×16 spatial extent, the marginal throughput is only 15.3 TFLOPS. This indicates that even for ordinary 3×3 convolutions, some shapes do not use Winograd. The conditions that determine this for the shape are not established in this report.

### 7.5 Low-Precision and Compressed Weights

We used coremltools to generate two low-precision models. The W8A8 model quantizes the activations with `linear_quantize_activations` using calibration, and simultaneously quantizes the weights to Int8. The W8 model quantizes only the weights to Int8 and leaves the activations in FP16 (tool: [w8a8.py](../tools/05_compute/w8a8.py)). Table 7-8 gives the marginal throughput of W8A8 and FP16 for three shapes (data: [h10_w8a8_slope.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_w8a8_slope.txt), [h10_w8a8_k3.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_w8a8_k3.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

*Table 7-8. Marginal throughput of W8A8 and FP16*

| Shape | Engines | FP16 | W8A8 | W8A8 ÷ FP16 | DRAM read bandwidth per ANE under W8A8 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×1, 512 channels, 1×128 | Single | 20.6 TFLOPS (17.6–18.4) | 27.5 TOPS | 1.34 (1.5) | 22–28 GB/s |
| 1×1, 512 channels, 1×256 | Dual | 39.5 TFLOPS (Section 10.3) | 38.6 TOPS | 0.98 | 23.5 GB/s |
| 3×3, 512 channels, 16×16 | Single | 31.0 TFLOPS | 62.1 TOPS | 2.0 | 20–27 GB/s |

In the compute-dense 3×3 convolution, the throughput of W8A8 is exactly 2 times that of FP16, which agrees with the INT8 MAC width inferred in Section 7.2.2. Including Winograd, the effective throughput of W8A8 on a single ANE reaches 62.1 TOPS. However, this 2× advantage does not extend to the 1×1 convolution chain. On a single ANE, W8A8 is only about 1.3–1.5 times faster than FP16, and on dual ANEs it is essentially equal to FP16. The range of the single-ANE speedup arises from differences in fitting intervals. The W8A8 throughput slope is fitted from data over 128–384 layers, whereas the FP16 throughput of 20.6 TFLOPS given above uses data over 256–384 layers, which corresponds to a speedup of about 1.3. If the fit uniformly uses 128–384 layers, the FP16 throughput in the same data file is 17.6–18.4 TFLOPS (the values in parentheses in the table), which corresponds to a speedup of about 1.5. Because the measurement points at 128 and 384 layers lie in different host wake-up states (Section 6.5.3), both conventions carry a certain degree of error. Both yield speedups clearly below the ideal 2× (data: h10_w8a8_slope.txt). The IOReport bandwidth data provide a clue to this difference. The DRAM read bandwidth in the FP16 test is nearly zero, which indicates that the activation tensors can remain resident in the on-chip cache. By contrast, the W8A8 convolution chain produces a sustained DRAM read traffic of about 22–28 GB/s per ANE. Because the layers share weights and the total weight amount is only about 260 KB, this traffic is unlikely to come mainly from weight reads. Further, back-calculating from the read bandwidth and the single-layer execution time, each ANE reads about 64–81 KB per layer, which is of the same order as the 64 KB of Int8 activation data that one layer processes. The compiled artifact further shows that each W8A8 layer is split into three types of TD: input quantization (PE), convolution, and output dequantization. Combined with the above bandwidth characteristics, we infer that quantization and dequantization break the on-chip activation residency of the FP16 convolution chain, so activations pass through DRAM between layers. The performance of 1×1 convolutions is therefore progressively limited by activation data movement, and it cannot fully realize the 2× throughput advantage of the INT8 MAC array 〔timing, IOReport, HWX, inferred〕.

However, this explanation does not fully close quantitatively. The measured read bandwidth of 22–28 GB/s is still below the activation-path limit measured in Section 9.4.2 (about 35–40 GB/s per ANE), and the write traffic in IOReport is close to the noise floor. This does not fully match a simple model in which activation data must be written back to DRAM in full. Therefore, the existing evidence supports that the W8A8 1×1 convolution is affected by additional data-movement overhead, but it is not sufficient to determine the complete path of these traffic streams or their specific bottleneck.

Quantizing only the weights (W8) does not raise the MAC peak. The compiler's performance model gives W8 the same MAC efficiency as FP16 (Section 7.2.2), so on compute-bound layers W8 gives no speedup. On a 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels and 32×32 (about 32 KiB of weights per NE, resident on chip), W8 and FP16 take nearly identical time (1011–1012 µs for 48 layers each). This agrees with the above performance model and with the conclusion of maderix [4] on the M4 (260.4 µs for Int8 weights and 260.5 µs for FP16 weights) (data: [b6_q8_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_q8_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. The effect of W8 is to reduce the number of bytes read for weights and the occupancy of the weight buffer. A 3×3 convolution with 512 channels can accommodate 224 output channels per TD under W8, but only 96 under FP16. Under W8, each NE takes 14 × 512 × 9 × 1 B = 63 KiB per pass, which remains within 64 KiB. Under FP16, each NE has only 6 output channels per pass (54 KiB), whereas 64 KiB would allow 7 〔HWX〕. However, "not raising the MAC peak" does not mean "not improving model speed." For weight-read-bound layers, a narrower weight format may shorten the weight-reading time in proportion to the number of bytes.

To measure this gain directly, we constructed a weight-read-bound native shape: a 1×1 convolution with 2048 input and 2048 output channels, a spatial extent of 32 pixels, and distinct weights in each layer. The FP16 weights of each layer amount to about 8.4 MB, of which each NE receives 512 KiB, far exceeding the 64 KiB resident limit. At about 150 GB/s, reading the weights takes about 56 µs, whereas the computation takes only about 13 µs. Using coremltools, we generated several versions from the same FP16 weights: W8, 6-, 4-, 3-, and 2-bit palettization, grouped-channel palettization (4-bit), int4 block-wise quantization, and vector palettization (`cluster_dim` of 2 and 4). For each version we took 16 and 32 layers and obtained the per-layer time from the difference in kdebug task duration (tools: [wfmt_gen.py](../tools/05_compute/wfmt_gen.py), [wfmt.sh](../tools/05_compute/wfmt.sh), [wfmt_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/wfmt_fit.py), [hwx_kern.py](../tools/05_compute/hwx_kern.py); data: [wfmt.txt](../data/05_compute/wfmt/wfmt.txt)). The results are shown in Table 7-9 〔kdebug, HWX〕.

*Table 7-9. Effect of weight format on weight-read-bound layers (1×1 convolution, 2048 channels, 32 pixels, distinct weights per layer)*

| Weight format | Weights per layer (HWX) | Time per layer | Relative to FP16 | Weight-read bandwidth from weight bytes | Engines used |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FP16 | 8.39 MB | 52.8 µs | 1.00 | 159 GB/s | ANE0 |
| W8 | 4.20 MB | 30.8 µs | 0.58 | 136 GB/s | ANE0 |
| 6-bit palettization | 3.15 MB | 23.6 µs | 0.45 | 134 GB/s | ANE0 |
| 4-bit palettization | 2.10 MB | 16.8 µs | 0.32 | 125 GB/s | ANE0 |
| 4-bit grouped-channel palettization | 2.11 MB | 16.7 µs | 0.32 | 126 GB/s | Two ANEs |
| 3-bit palettization | 3.15 MB (1.57 MB per ANE) | 12.0 µs | 0.23 | See text | Two ANEs |
| 2-bit palettization | 2.10 MB (1.05 MB per ANE) | 8.4 µs | 0.16 | See text | Two ANEs |
| int4 block-wise quantization | Compilation failed | — | — | — | Entire model on CPU |
| Vector palettization (2-D, 4-D) | Compilation failed | — | — | — | Entire model on CPU |

Table 7-9 shows that, on weight-read-bound native layers, the hardware reads the compressed weights directly. The weights in the compiled artifact remain in compressed form, and the single copy stored by one ANE shrinks exactly in proportion to the bit width. The per-layer time decreases with the number of weight bytes. For the formats that use only one ANE, the bandwidth computed from weight bytes lies between 125 and 159 GB/s, which is comparable to the weight-read bandwidth measured in Section 9.3. This shows that the weights are not expanded to FP16 before being read. Compared with FP16, W8 shortens the per-layer time by 42%, 4-bit palettization by 68%, and 2-bit palettization by 84% 〔kdebug, HWX〕. The equivalent bandwidth of the compressed formats (125–136 GB/s) is slightly lower than that of FP16 (159 GB/s). This indicates that the compressed formats carry a small amount of additional per-layer time that does not scale with byte count, possibly from table lookup or dequantization. This report does not decompose it further 〔inferred〕. The 2-bit, 3-bit, and grouped palettization models are split by the compiler across two ANEs, whereas the FP16, W8, 4-bit, and 6-bit models of the same shape use only ANE0. For the 2-bit and 3-bit palettization, the compiled artifacts store a complete copy of the weights for each engine, so the weight segment per layer is twice the size of a single copy. The weight segment of the grouped palettization is not doubled, which we conjecture reflects output-channel splitting (data: [kern_all_h18g.txt](../data/05_compute/wfmt/kern_all_h18g.txt)) 〔HWX, inferred〕.

This raises an unresolved contradiction. If both engines read their own complete copy from DRAM, the combined read rate implied by the per-layer times of 8.4 µs (2-bit) and 12.0 µs (3-bit) is 250–262 GB/s. This exceeds the theoretical DRAM bandwidth of 170.5 GB/s (Section 9.5), which is impossible. Therefore, either each engine reads only part of its own copy, or one of the copies is served from on-chip storage or the SLC. This report has not determined which. If computed per single copy, the effective bandwidth is 125–131 GB/s, comparable to that of the compressed format on a single engine 〔kdebug, inferred〕.

The models with int4 block-wise quantization and vector palettization can be generated normally at the Core ML level, but the ANE compiler cannot compile them. Core ML therefore places the entire model on the CPU, at about 66 µs per layer, which is even slower than FP16 on the ANE. The HAL parameter table enables vector palettization for the M6 (Section 15.3), but with the generation parameters we used it cannot be used. This report has not exhaustively determined whether other block sizes or parameters can be compiled 〔HWX, timing〕.

As a control, we performed the same comparison on the rewritten kernel of Section 7.4.2 (1×9 convolution, 256 channels, 4×32). W8 reduces the per-layer weight amount from 2.54 MB to 1.33 MB, and the lower bound of DRAM read bandwidth from IOReport is also nearly halved. However, the per-layer time changes from 17.0 µs to 17.8 µs, that is, it does not shorten (data as above) 〔kdebug, IOReport〕. This indicates that the bottleneck of the rewritten kernel is not DRAM byte bandwidth. Section 7.4.2 discusses the implications of this result.

We also examined the effect of sparsity, addressing three questions: whether pruned weights are read in compressed form, whether the compute array skips the MACs of zero weights, and whether it skips zero activations. The models start from the same FP16 baseline weights. coremltools' `prune_weights` generates unstructured pruning at 50%, 75%, and 90%, as well as structured 2:4 and 1:4 versions. For the weight-read-bound m2048, we additionally apply joint compression of "50% pruning followed by 4-bit palettization." The compute-bound shapes are a 1×1 convolution chain with weights resident on chip (512 channels, 1×128) and a 3×3 convolution chain (512 channels, 16×16). On the M6, the slope is computed from the "driver submission → task end" duration in kdebug. On the M4, the slope is computed from per-call time. Each machine was tested in two rounds (tools: [sparse_gen.py](../tools/05_compute/sparse_gen.py), [sparse.sh](../tools/05_compute/sparse.sh), [sparse_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/sparse_fit.py), [sparse_err.py](../tools/05_compute/sparse_err.py); data: [sparse.txt](../data/05_compute/sparse/sparse.txt)) 〔kdebug, HWX, timing〕.

First, pruned weights are stored and read in compressed form. The weight segment in the compiled artifact consists of non-zero values plus a mask of 1 bit per element. At 50%, 75%, and 90% pruning, the segment is 0.56, 0.31, and 0.16 times the size of the dense segment, respectively. On the weight-read-bound m2048, the per-layer time is proportional to the compressed byte count. The weight-read bandwidth computed from bytes is about 150 GB/s on the M6 and about 67 GB/s on the M4, which coincide with the weight-read limits of the two machines. On the M6, the per-layer time at 90% pruning is 0.16 times that of the dense version, and 50% pruning combined with 4-bit palettization gives 0.24 times, so the two kinds of compression can be combined 〔HWX, kdebug〕. Figure 7-2 plots the per-layer time against the compressed weight bytes for the various weight formats. The quantization, palettization, and pruning points all lie close to a single weight-read limit line, whereas the rewritten kernel does not lie on it.

![Per-layer time versus weight bytes](../figs/en/light/fig7-2_weight_bytes.png)

*Figure 7-2. Per-layer time of weight-read-bound layers as a function of per-layer weight bytes. (a) 1×1 convolution (2048 channels, 32 pixels, distinct weights per layer) on log–log axes. The horizontal axis is the compressed weight-segment size per layer in the compiled artifact, and the dashed line is the time predicted from the weight-read limit (about 150 GB/s on the M6 and about 67 GB/s on the M4). The 2- and 3-bit palettizations are split across two ANEs, and each engine stores a complete copy of the weights, so the horizontal axis uses the single-copy size (open markers). (b) Rewritten 1×9 kernel (256 channels, 4×32): W8 halves the weight bytes but leaves the time unchanged, differing by a factor of two from the 8.8 µs predicted from bytes (Section 7.4.2). The M6 times come from kdebug (firmware task duration for quantization and palettization; driver submission to task end for pruning), and the M4 times are Core ML per-call times. All are slopes over 16 and 32 layers (data: [wfmt.txt](../data/05_compute/wfmt/wfmt.txt), [sparse.txt](../data/05_compute/sparse/sparse.txt)).*

Second, the compute arrays of both the M6 and the M4 skip the MACs of zero weights. Table 7-10 lists the results for the 1×1 convolution chain with weights resident on chip.

*Table 7-10. Effect of pruning on a compute-bound 1×1 convolution chain (512 channels, 1×128, weights resident on chip). The dense version takes 3.45 µs per layer, about 6% slower than the 3.26 µs of Section 7.2.1. The two values come from different rounds and use different timing conventions. Here the values are the slopes, over 32 and 128 layers, of the submission-to-task-end duration in kdebug.*

| Weights | M6 time per layer | Relative to dense | Equivalent throughput based on dense MAC count | M4 relative to dense | M4 equivalent throughput based on dense MAC count |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FP16 dense | 3.45 µs | 1.00 | 19.5 TFLOPS | 1.00 | 15.5 TFLOPS |
| Pruned 50% or 2:4 | 2.27–2.28 µs | 0.66 | 29.5 TFLOPS | 0.68 | 22.7 TFLOPS |
| Pruned 75% or 1:4 | 1.43–1.44 µs | 0.42 | 46.8 TFLOPS | 0.44 | about 35 TFLOPS |
| Pruned 90% | 1.25 µs | 0.36 | 53.6 TFLOPS | 0.33 | about 47 TFLOPS |

Based on the original dense MAC count, the equivalent throughput of the M6 at 75% and 90% pruning reaches 46.8 and 53.6 TFLOPS, which is 2.2 and 2.5 times the single-engine direct-convolution peak of 21.1 TFLOPS. Because the weights in this test are resident on chip, the performance gain cannot be explained by a reduction in weight reads. It therefore indicates that the compute array itself skips the MACs that correspond to zero weights. The zero-skipping efficiency has no clear relation to whether pruning is structured. At 50% pruning, 2:4 and unstructured pruning give essentially the same performance; at 75% pruning, 1:4 and unstructured pruning likewise match. On the other hand, the speedup is not inversely proportional to the non-zero fraction. When the non-zero fraction decreases from 0.5 to 0.25 and 0.1, the relative time decreases only from 0.66 to 0.42 and 0.36.
