This deviation may come from imbalance in the compressed weight length allocated to each NE, from fixed per-layer overheads, and from the decoding throughput limit of sparse weights 〔inferred〕.

The compiled artifact further confirms the zero-skipping mechanism of the hardware. Register 0x1240 in the convolution TD is the weight configuration word. Checking it bit by bit against the functions in the compiler that write each field, bits 0–1 specify the weight data type, bit 2 and bits 4–7 control the palettization and its bit width, bit 8 (KernelSparseFmt) indicates the use of the sparse weight format, and bit 28 (KernelDetectZeros) controls runtime zero-weight detection. After pruning, this register changes from 0x10000082 to 0x182. That is, KernelSparseFmt is set and KernelDetectZeros is cleared. The corresponding sparse-format flag also appears in the weight DMA configuration register 0x1540. This shows that the pruned model does not dynamically detect zeros in dense weights. Instead, the weights are encoded in sparse format at compile time, and the hardware directly executes the compressed weight stream.

This change is also reflected in the weight layout and the execution cycles. The dense model assigns each NE a fixed 32 KiB of weights, whereas after pruning each NE receives a compressed weight segment of different length. At the same time, `exe_cycles` in the TD header drops from 3 to 1 per layer, although the per-TD estimate of the performance model does not change accordingly (Section 7.8) 〔HWX, compiler〕.

The premise for skipping zero weights is that the weights are supplied to the compiler in sparse format, and the pruning interface of coremltools generates this format. If the zeros in the weights are written into the model as ordinary dense constants, the compiler does not switch to the sparse format even when 50%–100% of the weights are zero. The weight segment, register 0x1240, and estimated cycles are then identical to those of random weights (tool: [kfmt_gen.py](../tools/05_compute/kfmt_gen.py)) 〔HWX, compiler〕. Dense-format layers have an additional zero-weight detection bit, which the compiler decides per layer. On the M6's target h18g, dense convolutions with weights resident on chip (including W8 and palettization) set this bit, whereas m2048, whose weights differ per layer and are read from DRAM, does not. On the M4's target h16g, the compiler does not set this bit for any convolution. The switch that determines this is the bit at offset 0x72c in the HAL parameter table, which has been 1 since H17 (the M5 generation) (Section 15.3) 〔HWX, compiler〕. We measured on the M4 that dense models with 50%–100% zero weights are only 4–8% faster per layer than models with random weights, whereas the sparse format at 75% pruning takes about half the time. Because the M4 does not enable zero-weight detection, this result only shows that dense zero weights barely speed up without zero-weight detection 〔timing〕.

The 3×3 convolution behaves differently. After pruning, the compiler no longer uses Winograd. The Winograd bit in the task information word is cleared, the output-channel grouping changes from 2 to 16, and the number of TDs per layer changes from 6 to 2. The compiler instead uses direct convolution plus zero skipping. Therefore, 50% pruning is slower than the dense version by 5–15%, and benefits appear only at 75% pruning or above. At 90% pruning, the time is 0.32–0.34 times that of the dense version, equivalent to about 87 TFLOPS by dense MAC count 〔HWX, kdebug〕. Part of the weights of the 3×3 convolution chain must be read from DRAM, and a few tasks take significantly longer, which raises the mean task duration by 5–25%. The comparisons above use medians. When means are used, the order of the versions by speed does not change 〔kdebug〕.

Figure 7-3 plots the results of both convolutions on the M6 and the M4 together.

![Pruning ratio and per-layer relative time](../figs/en/light/fig7-3_pruning.png)

*Figure 7-3. Pruning ratio and per-layer relative time for compute-bound layers with weights resident on chip (average of the two passes of the second round). (a) 1×1 convolution chain: both the M6 and the M4 shorten time as the pruning ratio increases. Unstructured (circles) and structured (squares) pruning nearly coincide, but both are slower than the ideal case (dashed) in which time scales with the non-zero fraction. (b) 3×3 convolution chain: once pruned, the M6 no longer uses hardware Winograd, so 50% pruning is 5–15% slower than dense, and benefits appear only at 75% pruning or above. On the M4, pruning yields benefits from 50% onward. Whether the dense 3×3 convolution on the M4 uses Winograd is not determined in this report (data: [sparse.txt](../data/05_compute/sparse/sparse.txt)).*

Third, the compute array does not skip zero activations, although zero activations substantially reduce power consumption (see Section 13.3). Changing only the input cannot measure this, because zeros in the input are mixed away after the first convolution. For this reason, we introduce after each convolution a fixed mask shared across layers. The mask directly controls the fraction of zeros in the next layer's input, while the graph structure and the weights remain unchanged. Multiplying by the mask itself adds about 1 µs of fixed overhead per layer (the 1×1 chain on the M6 takes about 4.4–4.5 µs per layer, whereas the dense version in Table 7-10 takes 3.45 µs). Masked models are therefore compared only with one another. Whether the fraction of zeros in each layer's input increases from 0% to 90%, or half of the channels are entirely zero, the time is the same. On the M6, the 1×1 chain differs by only about 2.4% between "with zeros" and "without zeros," and this difference is independent of the fraction. The differences for the M4 and for the 3×3 chain are both within measurement noise 〔kdebug〕. The 3×3 chain is sensitive to the order of measurement. To reduce the systematic drift caused by measurement order, we executed the complete test sequence in forward and in reverse order, thereby eliminating drift due to measurement order 〔kdebug〕. The ANE outputs of all pruned models agree with the results of running the same pruned models on the CPU, with an error no greater than that of the dense versions.

In summary, weight-only quantization, palettization, and pruning all leave the dense peak of the MAC array unchanged. However, on native layers limited by DRAM weight-read bandwidth, each can shorten the time approximately in proportion to the compressed byte count. Pruning can additionally skip the MACs of zero weights on compute-bound layers, without requiring a structured sparsity pattern. For workloads such as large language model decoding, reducing the bit width per weight or removing some weights can shorten the time of weight-read-bound layers in proportion to byte count. However, end-to-end decoding is also limited by call count and host overhead (Section 14.3), so this is only one route to speedup.

### 7.6 Planar Engine

#### 7.6.1 Throughput

To measure the throughput of the PE, we constructed chains that contain only elementwise operations, and we confirmed that all data remain in L2. In the compiled artifact, the TDs other than the first layer carry no DMA address packets, and at run time the DRAM reads and writes of the two ANEs amount only to a noise floor of about 0.5 GB/s. Because these models are all split across the two ANEs, we measured the task duration of each ANE with kdebug. Table 7-11 gives the results for 256 channels and five spatial sizes (tools: [pe_ktrace.sh](../tools/05_compute/pe_ktrace.sh), [pe_kt.py](../tools/05_compute/pe_kt.py); data: [pe_kt/](../data/05_compute/pe_kt/), [pe_kt_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/pe_kt_fit.txt)) 〔kdebug〕. The compiler merges single-input operations (abs, multiplication by −1, and relu) into one task per two operations.

*Table 7-11. Task duration of PE operations (per ANE)*

| Elements per ANE | Single input: per task (two operations) | Dual input x + y: per layer |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 131 072 (32×32) | 2.38 µs | 4.55 µs |
| 204 800 (40×40) | 3.88 µs | 4.39 µs |
| 294 912 (48×48) | 5.22 µs | 5.19 µs |
| 401 408 (56×56) | 6.86 µs | 11.94 µs |
| 524 288 (64×64) | 8.98 µs | 18.49 µs |

The task durations of single-input operations are closely linear in the number of elements (maximum residual 0.17 µs). Each task has a fixed part of about 0.35 µs. By the number of elements processed per task (each task contains two operations), the throughput is about 61 G elements/s per ANE. Each element reads 2 bytes and writes 2 bytes, which is equivalent to about 243 GB/s of L2 read and write traffic. Addition and multiplication take the same time, and alternating between them also takes the same time, which indicates that the throughput is not limited by the operation type.

Dual-input operations do not exhibit linear behavior. From 32×32 to 48×48, each layer stays at 4.4–5.2 µs. At 56×56 and 64×64 it jumps to 11.9 µs and 18.5 µs, respectively, and in this range the marginal throughput is much lower than that of single-input operations. A field of the HAL parameter table (offset 0x5f6, read by `ZinMirInterleaveInitializer::IsDualInputsPEWithSingleDMAEngine`) is 1 on H16 through H19. Judging by its name, it indicates that dual-input PE operations have only one DMA engine. We conjecture that, when the data reside in L2, the two inputs are likewise read in sequence by one engine. This is consistent with the observation that the unit cost per element of dual-input operations is higher. From 56×56 onward, each of the three tensors on an ANE amounts to about 0.75–1 MiB, and the total exceeds the 2 MiB of L2 used by the compiler, so additional constraints may also apply 〔HAL, kdebug, inferred〕.

#### 7.6.2 Clock and Width

The clock of the PE is independent of the NE clock, and it is very likely located in the shared cluster common to the two ANEs. Section 11.3 gives the complete argument. Only the frequency estimate is used here. The ratio of the PE clock at full load to its lowest state is 2.63. Starting from the low end of the compiler's L2 frequency table, 684–720 MHz, the full-load PE clock is estimated at about 1.8–1.9 GHz (Section 11.3.2) 〔inferred〕.

At this frequency, the PE processes about 61 G ÷ 1.8–1.9 GHz ≈ 32 FP16 elements per cycle. That is, it reads 64 bytes and writes 64 bytes per cycle, which is consistent with the PE work unit of 64 in the HAL (in bytes) 〔inferred〕. It should be emphasized that this width depends on a clock derived from the frequency range of the compiler's L2 table, and it is not a directly measured result.

In the programs tested in Section 6.6.1, PE operations and multiply-accumulate operations in different TDs show no overlap. This report cannot determine whether the hardware can run the two in parallel across different TDs. The activation function fused after a convolution is implemented by the lookup-table mode in the NE configuration, as a 33-point lookup table with linear interpolation (Section 8.5.1). Its numerical behavior is discussed in Chapter 8.

### 7.7 Throughput and Network Depth

maderix [4] gives data on M4 throughput as a function of chain depth. For comparison, we used a similar shape on the M6 (512 channels, 32×32, distinct weights per layer) and adopted the same convention as maderix, namely overall throughput including the fixed overhead of each call. This shape is split across two ANEs on the M6. The results are shown in Table 7-12 (data: [chains_run2.txt](../data/05_compute/chains_run2.txt), [b6_q8_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_q8_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 7-12. Throughput versus chain depth (including the fixed overhead per call)*

| Layers | M6 FP16 | M6 W8A8 | M4 FP16 [4] | M4 W8A8 [4] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 2.8 TFLOPS | 2.6 TOPS | 5.4 | 5.9 |
| 4 | 8.9 | 9.8 | 9.8 | 12.4 |
| 8 | 12.6 | 13.7 | 12.3 | 18.6 |
| 16 | 21.0 | 22.7 | 11.7 | 22.4 |
| 32 | 19.1 | 37.5 | 16.1 | 27.4 |
| 48 | 25.6 | 35.5 | 18.5 (1024 channels, 64×64) | 36.0 (1024 channels, 64×64) |

Within 8 layers, the M6 is not faster than the M4, because the total time is dominated by the fixed overhead per call. For FP16, the M6 is clearly faster starting at 16 layers. W8A8 is clearly faster only at 32 layers (37.5 versus 27.4 TOPS), and at 16 layers the two are essentially the same. At 48 layers, both M4 data points are for 1024 channels and 64×64, a shape different from that on the M6. The FP16 comparison of 25.6 versus 18.5 TFLOPS and the W8A8 comparison of 35.5 versus 36.0 TOPS can therefore only serve as a reference. After subtracting the fixed overhead, the FP16 time for 36 layers and above can be described by a straight line of about 13.1 µs per layer, equivalent to about 40.9 TFLOPS (see below). The measurement at 16 layers deviates from this line because of differing host-side overhead, so the marginal throughput should not be obtained by subtracting the 16-layer and 48-layer points. Figure 7-4 shows the variation of throughput with chain depth on the M6 and the M4.

![Throughput versus chain depth](../figs/en/light/fig7-4_depth_throughput.png)

*Figure 7-4. Overall throughput (including the fixed overhead per call) of the M6 and M4 as a function of chain depth: (a) FP16, (b) W8A8. The M6 uses 1×1 convolution chains of 512 channels and 32×32, with distinct weights per layer. The M4 data are taken from maderix [4], whose 48-layer point has 1024 channels and 64×64. The scattered points in (a) are a fine sweep over 16–64 layers. The dashed line is the throughput corresponding to a straight-line fit of per-call time over 36 or more layers (data: [chains_run2.txt](../data/05_compute/chains_run2.txt), [b6_q8_run.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_q8_run.txt), [b6x_sweep.txt](../data/05_compute/b6x_sweep.txt)).*

Table 7-12 contains an anomaly: the FP16 throughput at 32 layers is lower than at 16 layers. After re-measuring with finer steps over 16–64 layers, we found that the per-call time for 36 layers and above can be described by a single straight line. The fit gives about 390 µs + 13.12 µs per layer, a least-squares fit using, for each layer count, the smaller of the two rounds' values. When the task-duration column is used instead, the fit gives about 376 µs + 13.21 µs per layer. Measurement points below 36 layers deviate from this line (data: [b6x_sweep.txt](../data/05_compute/b6x_sweep.txt), [b6_kt/](../data/05_compute/b6_kt/), [effA_kt/](../data/05_compute/effA_kt/)) 〔timing, kdebug〕. The deviation has two independent causes. Models with fewer layers are faster than the line predicts, while the 32- and 34-layer models are slower than the line predicts.

For the former, the 16-layer model is 184 µs faster than the line predicts. Firmware timestamps show that the ANE task duration stays on the line. The entire difference comes from host-side overhead after task completion, which rises gradually from about 166 µs at 16 layers to about 355 µs at 28 or more layers. This is the host CPU idle-depth effect described in Section 6.5.

For the latter, the 32- and 34-layer models are about 100 µs slower than the line predicts. The execution plans in the E5 cache of Core ML (Section 5.2) show that, of the 28 models in this experiment, only the FP16 32- and 34-layer models have a plan that includes a CPU segment. Its content is "type conversion → last-layer relu → type conversion," meaning that Core ML placed the final relu on the CPU, with the execution plan `AneInference → Cast → CpuInference → Cast` (data: [b6_sample_top.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_sample_top.txt), [b6_pcpu.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_pcpu.txt)) 〔timing, inferred〕. Compiling the complete 32-layer model directly with `ANECCompile` causes no problem, so this partition was made by the Core ML segmenter and not by the ANE compiler rejecting the operation. According to the cost rule of Section 5.2, the estimated time of the final relu is the same on the ANE and on the CPU. Placing it on the ANE costs 0.125 ms for the graph output, and placing it on the CPU costs 0.125 ms for one switch, so the total cost of the two choices is equal, which is a tie. In a tie, on which side the operation falls is not determined. This phenomenon may be related to that tie. However, the equality of the two sides is derived from data for a 64-channel relu on the M4, and the tie alone does not explain why this partition appears only at 32 and 34 layers and only for FP16 〔inferred〕. The 32-layer W8 and W8A8 models are not affected by this effect.

### 7.8 Comparison of the Compiler Performance Model with Measurements

The performance-model CSV described in Section 7.1 lists, for each TD, the bytes of weight reads, activation reads, and activation writebacks, the NE cycle count, and the estimated time. We used the models from the sparse experiments and the field-tracing experiments to re-export these CSVs on the M4 with h18g as the target (data: [perfcsv2/](../data/05_compute/perfcsv2/)), and we reverse-engineered the compiler's timing formula TD by TD 〔compiler〕. The compute part is estimated as NE cycles divided by 2.508 GHz, the highest state of the compiler's frequency table; the measured full-load clock is 2.58 GHz (Chapter 11). Weight reads overlap with computation, and the larger of the two is taken. Weights are assumed to be read at 167.4 GB/s, and every weight-read-bound TD back-calculates to exactly this value, which is close to the theoretical DRAM bandwidth of 170.5 GB/s. Activation reads and writebacks are assumed at 116.0 GB/s and 83.7 GB/s, respectively, and TDs that merely move data, such as elementwise addition, pooling, and softmax, back-calculate to exactly these values. For a TD with only one work unit, activation reads and writes are added directly to the computation. For a large TD with many work units, activation reads and writes are almost entirely hidden behind the computation. The exact rule for cases in between has not been decomposed by us 〔compiler〕. Table 7-13 compares this estimate with the M6 measurements.

*Table 7-13. Per-layer estimate of the compiler performance model versus M6 measurements*

| Scenario | Compiler estimate | M6 measurement | Deviation and cause |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Weight-read-bound (m2048, FP16, 8.39 MB per layer) | 50.1 µs | 52.8–55.5 µs | Optimistic by 5–10%: with weights at 167 GB/s, two rounds of measurement give 151–159 GB/s (Table 7-9 and the sparse experiments) |
| Same, pruned 75% (2.62 MB per layer) | 15.7 µs | 17.5 µs | Optimistic by about 11%: weights are counted in compressed bytes, which agrees with measurement, but the bandwidth is again too high |
| Compute-bound (512-channel 1×1, FP16) | 3.27 µs | 3.45 µs | Optimistic by about 5%: computed with 256 MACs per NE per cycle and 2.508 GHz |
| Same, pruned 75% | 3.27 µs | 1.44 µs | Pessimistic by 2.3×: NE cycle count does not account for zero skipping at all (Section 7.5) |
| 3×3, FP16 | 58.8 µs | 39–44 µs | Pessimistic by about 1.4×: computed as direct convolution, without hardware Winograd (Section 7.4.3) |
| 3×3, pruned 50% / 90% | 66.2 / 62.5 µs | 46 / 13.9 µs | Pessimistic by 1.4× / 4.5×: zero skipping is not counted, but the cost of losing Winograd after pruning is counted |
| Activation reads and writes | Read 116, write 83.7 GB/s | About 35–40 GB/s per ANE (Section 9.4.2) | Optimistic by about 3× |

Table 7-13 shows that the compiler's performance model is quite accurate for compute-bound dense convolutions that do not use Winograd, with an error of about 5%. It is optimistic by about 10% for weight reads and about 3× for activation DMA. It does not consider at all the two acceleration features of the M6, namely hardware Winograd and sparse zero skipping, and it therefore systematically overestimates the time of 3×3 convolutions and pruned layers 〔compiler, kdebug〕. These deviations directly affect the compiler's decisions. Dual-ANE splitting and global refinement (Section 5.8.3) both rely on this estimate. The splitting decisions for 3×3 layers and pruned layers are based on times overestimated by 1.4–4.5×, and the optimistic estimate of activation DMA causes the compiler to underestimate the cost of copy-and-merge after splitting 〔inferred〕. It should further be noted that `exe_cycles` in the TD header of the HWX and the CSV estimate are not the same quantity. The per-layer `exe_cycles` of a pruned 1×1 convolution is about 1 (3 when dense), whereas the CSV estimate remains 3.27 µs. The algorithm that produces `exe_cycles` has not been determined in this report.

### 7.9 Comparison with the M4

Table 7-14 summarizes the comparison of the compute arrays of the M4 and the M6.

*Table 7-14. Compute arrays of the M4 and M6*

| Item | M4 | M6 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| NEs per engine | 16 (maderix regards these as 8 compute groups [4]) | 16 |
| MACs per NE per cycle | Not measured | FP16 256, INT8 512 |
| NE full-load clock | Not measured (highest entry in the compiler table, 2364 MHz) | 2580 MHz (highest entry in the compiler table, 2508 MHz) |
| FP16 peak (single engine) | About 19 TFLOPS [4] | Direct convolution 21.1 TFLOPS (measured 20.6); 3×3 with Winograd, effective 31.0 TFLOPS |
| INT8 / W8A8 peak (single engine) | About 38 TOPS (W8A8) [4] | Direct convolution 42.3 TOPS; 3×3 effective 62.1 TOPS |
| Zero-weight skipping | Yes (measured in this report on the M4; 90% pruning gives 0.33 times dense); does not skip zero activations | Yes (90% pruning gives 0.36 times dense); does not skip zero activations |
| Weight-only quantization | No speedup [4] | MAC peak unchanged, compute-bound layers not accelerated; on weight-read-bound native layers, W8 shortens time by 42% and 4-bit palettization by 68% |
| Weight buffer per NE | 64 KiB resident (HAL) [4] | 64 KiB (HAL, TD counts for larger inputs, and OCG, all in agreement) |
| S2D(2) | One-hot convolution, overhead 2.8–3.1% [4] | Overhead about 0.2% |

The HAL parameter tables of the M4 and the M6 take the same values in the fields related to the MAC array and the weight buffer (Section 5.4). The compute arrays of the two generations should therefore be structurally identical. The increase in the single-engine peak of the M6 should derive mainly from its higher NE clock, while the doubling of the overall peak comes from the second engine 〔HAL, inferred〕.

### 7.10 Summary

This chapter analyzed the peak computational capability of the ANE compute array and the conditions for achieving it. Each ANE has 16 NE cores, and each NE completes 256 FP16 or 512 INT8 MACs per cycle. At the full-load clock of 2.58 GHz, the direct-convolution peak of a single ANE is 21.1 TFLOPS for FP16 and 42.3 TOPS for INT8. Whether the peak can be approached depends on shape and precision. The resident-weight limit of 64 KiB per NE determines the output-channel grouping, and the time steps up when the output-channel count just exceeds 256. Kernel widths of 9–15 are rewritten into a form that doubles the weight amount, and widths of 16 and above are placed on the CPU. The 3×3 and 1×3 convolutions are executed by hardware Winograd, with effective throughput reaching about 1.47× the direct-convolution peak. W8A8 is about 1.8–2× FP16 on compute-dense convolutions, and exactly 2× for 3×3 convolutions. Compressed weights are read in compressed form, and layers limited by weight reads have their time shortened in proportion to byte count. The compute array skips zero weights stored in sparse format but does not skip zero activations. The PE is in an independent clock domain, and single-input operations run at about 61 G elements/s. The compiler's performance model is accurate to about 5% for dense, compute-bound layers, but it does not account for Winograd or zero skipping.

The single-engine peaks in this chapter form the baseline against which Chapter 10 evaluates dual-engine scaling and against which Chapter 14 states its expectations. Still undetermined are how the 256 MACs are organized (16 output channels multiplied by 16 input channels, or 16 pixels), whether the higher MAC tier in the performance model can be reached, which stage is the bottleneck of rewritten kernels, and why the speedup from pruning falls below the non-zero fraction (Section 16.2). The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are concentrated in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 8. Numerical Behavior

Chapter 7 concerns the compute performance of the ANE, and this chapter turns to its numerical precision and internal arithmetic behavior. The inputs and outputs of the ANE are both FP16, but this is only the storage format of data in DRAM and L2. It does not reveal the format in which the multiply-accumulate array accumulates internally, how overflow is handled, or how results are rounded, and these details determine whether a network can obtain results on the ANE that agree with those on the CPU. We constructed specific inputs and weights, compared the outputs of the ANE and the CPU, and determined each of these behaviors item by item. The main conclusions of this chapter are four. First, the accumulator for FP16 convolutions and matrix multiplications is a 32-bit fixed-point number (Q15.16); at any point during accumulation, if its absolute value reaches 32768, the result becomes infinity and does not recover. Second, the bias is the initial value of this fixed-point accumulator, rather than being added in floating point to the output. Third, the INT8 path uses a separate 32-bit integer accumulator, elementwise operations take a floating-point path, and neither path has the 32768 limit. Fourth, activation functions are implemented by a 33-point lookup table with linear interpolation, and the table values for some functions are fitted values rather than exact samples of the function. The basic behavior of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path was retested on the M4, and the results are identical to those on the M6 (Section 8.1).

### 8.1 Test Methodology

To determine the behavior of the accumulator, we need to control precisely each product that participates in the accumulation. For this purpose we designed a 1×1 test convolution whose input x has the shape [1, 64, 1, 256], that is, 64 input channels and a width of 256 columns. For a 1×1 convolution, the value of output channel 0 at column j equals the sum of the products of the 64 input values in that column with the 64 weights of output channel 0, that is, Σ_c x[c, j] · w[c], and it is independent of the other columns. We therefore need only set the weights of output channel 0 to the numerical values under test and fill each column with different inputs; each column then constitutes an independent test case, so that a single model can check 256 cases at once (tools: [numerics2.py](../tools/06_numerics/numerics2.py) through [numerics6.py](../tools/06_numerics/numerics6.py)).

However, a convolution this small is placed on the CPU by Core ML, and what would be measured is then no longer the behavior of the ANE. To make the model actually execute on the ANE, we zero-padded the output channels to 2048 and added to the model an unrelated "companion" branch (four convolution layers followed by ReLU), so that Core ML assigns the entire model to the ANE. The raw output of the test convolution is read out as a separate model output. We confirmed that the model actually executed on the ANE by two means. First, we observed ANE interrupts during execution (tool: [anewho.c](../tools/common/anewho.c)). Second, we executed the same model once more in `CPU_ONLY` mode, and its results differ from those of the ANE 〔IOReport, numerical〕.

The expected value of each case is determined from both the FP16 result of the CPU and the exact float64 value. Elementwise operations were tested by the same method. The test methods for the INT8 path and for activation functions are described in Sections 8.3 and 8.5, respectively. All numerical experiments were completed on the M6. Among them, a set of basic tests examining the range, overflow, subnormal inputs, rounding and scale of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path was repeated once on the M4 with the same program (data: [numerics2_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics2_m6.txt), [numerics2_m4.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics2_m4.txt)).

### 8.2 FP16 Multiply-Accumulate Path: Fixed-Point Accumulator

#### 8.2.1 Range and Overflow

We first test the range of the accumulated result. Table 8-1 lists representative cases (data: [numerics2_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics2_m6.txt), [numerics3_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics3_m6.txt)) 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-1. Range and overflow of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path*

| Case                                             | ANE   | CPU   |
| ------------------------------------------------ | ----- | ----- |
| 32752 (single input, weight 1)                   | 32752 | 32752 |
| 32768                                            | inf   | 32768 |
| 16384 + 16376 (sum 32760, rounded to 32768 in FP16) | 32768 | 32768 |
| 16384 + 16384                                    | inf   | 32768 |
| 16384 + 16384 − 16384 − 16384 + 1                | inf   | 1     |
| 16384 + 2⁻¹⁶ − 16384                             | 2⁻¹⁶  | 0     |
| 16384 + 2⁻¹⁸ − 16384                             | 0     | 0     |

Table 8-1 illustrates three properties of the accumulator. First, the absolute value of an accumulated result is bounded by 2¹⁵: the case with a sum of 32760 is output correctly (rounded to 32768 in FP16 at the output), whereas the case whose sum is exactly 32768 becomes infinity. Second, overflow is irreversible: once the value at some point during accumulation reaches 32768, the final result remains infinity even if subsequent products reduce it to a smaller value. Third, the accumulator retains precision that FP16 cannot represent. Adding 2⁻¹⁶ to 16384 and then subtracting 16384 yields 2⁻¹⁶ on the ANE, whereas the FP16 computation on the CPU yields 0. This shows that the resolution during accumulation reaches 2⁻¹⁶, much finer than the resolution of FP16 near 16384 (16).

Together, these three properties indicate a 32-bit fixed-point accumulator with 1 sign bit, 15 integer bits and 16 fractional bits, in which the least significant bit (LSB) is 2⁻¹⁶ and the representable absolute value is below 2¹⁵. To confirm that this scale is fixed, we set the weight to 1, 16, 256 and 4096 and repeated the above tests. The overflow point remained 32768, and the LSB remained 2⁻¹⁶. This indicates that the compiler does not adjust the scale of the accumulator on a per-layer basis 〔numerical〕. Section 5.7.3 states that the NE field group of the TD contains an "accumulator bias shift" field and a 5-bit "output right shift" field. These fields may serve other data formats, and in the FP16 convolutions tested in this report the scale did not change 〔compiler, inferred〕.

We also examined whether partial sums in FP16 occur during accumulation. Bryngelson [5] reports that the first level of the reduction tree on the M1 sums in FP16 with radix 4. If the M6 does the same, a +1 inserted in the range ±2048 would be discarded in an FP16 partial sum, because the resolution of FP16 near 2048 is 2. In measurements, the +1 was not discarded regardless of where it was placed, which indicates that the M6 accumulation contains no intermediate partial sums stored at FP16 precision 〔numerical〕. Figure 8-1 gives the data formats of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path.

![Q15.16 accumulator](../figs/en/light/fig8-1_accumulator.png)

*Figure 8-1. Data formats of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path. The upper two rows show the computation of FP16 convolutions and matrix multiplications: input decoding, multiplication, alignment of the products to 2⁻¹⁶ according to the sum of exponents, Q15.16 fixed-point accumulation with the bias as the initial value, and rounding of the output. The "mantissa product and exponent sum" is an internal representation speculated from measurements. The next row compares the numerical formats of three paths: FP16 multiply-accumulate, INT8 multiply-accumulate, and elementwise operations.*

#### 8.2.2 Alignment of Tiny Products

The fixed-point accumulator can represent only integer multiples of 2⁻¹⁶, so each product must be aligned to 2⁻¹⁶ before it enters the accumulator. We found that whether a product is retained during alignment depends not on its actual magnitude but on the sum of the exponents of its two factors. To distinguish between these two possibilities, we specifically constructed cases whose exponent sum is −17 but whose mantissa product is close to 2. The results are shown in Table 8-2 (tools: [numerics5.py](../tools/06_numerics/numerics5.py) and [numerics6.py](../tools/06_numerics/numerics6.py); data: [numerics5_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics5_m6.txt), [numerics6_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics6_m6.txt)) 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-2. Treatment of products with small exponent sums (actual product value within 2 LSB)*

| Weight w      | Input x                  | Exponent sum | Actual product value | ANE output |
| ------------- | ------------------------ | ------------ | -------------------- | ---------- |
| 1.875 × 2⁻³   | 1.875 × 2⁻¹⁴             | −17          | 1.76 LSB             | 0          |
| 1.99 × 2⁻³    | 1.99 × 2⁻¹⁴              | −17          | 1.98 LSB             | 0          |
| 1.5 × 2⁻⁴     | 1.9 × 2⁻¹³               | −17          | 1.43 LSB             | 0          |
| 0.2           | 0.99 × 2⁻¹⁴ (subnormal)  | −17          | 0.79 LSB             | 0          |
| 0.125         | 1.5 × 2⁻¹³               | −16          | 1.5 LSB              | 2 LSB      |
| 0.25          | 0.5 × 2⁻¹⁴ (subnormal)   | −16          | 0.5 LSB              | 1 LSB      |

Table 8-2 shows that the rule depends on exponents. When the sum of the exponents of x and w (with the exponent of a subnormal number counted as −14) is not less than −16, the product is aligned to 2⁻¹⁶ with rounding, with halfway cases rounded away from zero. When the exponent sum is not greater than −17, the entire product is discarded, even though its actual value is close to 2 LSB. We speculate that the multiplier outputs the mantissa product (lying in the interval [1, 4)) and the exponent sum, and that the alignment shifter retains a rounding bit only for products whose exponent sum is not less than −16, while smaller products are shifted out directly 〔inferred〕. This rule implies that when a very small activation is multiplied by a small weight, it is truncated, and the amount truncated is at most close to 2 LSB (about 3 × 10⁻⁵). This is coarser than conventional rounding, in which a product is discarded only when it is smaller than half an LSB.

#### 8.2.3 Bias

A convolution bias can be added at two positions: as the initial value of the accumulator, or as a floating-point addition after accumulation is complete and the result has been converted to FP16. The two approaches behave differently with respect to overflow and rounding, so they can be distinguished experimentally. We set all weights to 1, so that output channel 0 equals Σx + bias. The results are shown in Table 8-3 (data: [numerics5_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics5_m6.txt)) 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-3. Positions at which the bias is added*

| bias   | Input         | ANE   | CPU        | Note                                                                 |
| ------ | ------------- | ----- | ---------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| −16384 | 16384 + 16384 | 16384 | 16384      | No overflow: the bias is already in the accumulator when accumulation begins |
| −16384 | 16384 × 3     | inf   | 32768      | Still overflows when the final value reaches 32768                   |
| +32    | 32736         | inf   | 32768      | Bias and products are both subject to the ±32768 limit               |
| 2⁻²⁰   | 0             | 0     | 9.5 × 10⁻⁷ | The bias is first quantized to 2⁻¹⁶                                  |
| 2⁻¹⁷   | 0             | 2⁻¹⁶  | 2⁻¹⁷       | 0.5 LSB rounded away from zero                                       |
| 2⁻¹⁷   | 2⁻¹⁷ (weight 1) | 2⁻¹⁵ | 2⁻¹⁶      | Each term is rounded to 1 LSB before addition                        |

The first row is the most instructive. The sum of two 16384 values would be expected to overflow in the accumulator, but with a bias of −16384 the result is correct and does not overflow. This is possible only if the bias enters the accumulator before the products. The subsequent rows further show that the bias is rounded away from zero to 2⁻¹⁶ before it enters the accumulator. It follows that the bias is the initial value of the fixed-point accumulator 〔numerical〕. This design has two consequences. A large bias combined with large activations is more likely to overflow. Conversely, a large negative bias can offset part of the positive products, so that the intermediate value does not overflow.

#### 8.2.4 Input Decoding and Output Rounding

After accumulation completes, the fixed-point result is converted to the FP16 output. This conversion rounds to nearest, with halfway cases rounded away from zero, rather than using the IEEE 754 default of rounding halfway cases to even. For example, 2049 is output as 2050, −2049 as −2050, 4098 as 4100, and 1 + 2⁻¹¹ as 1 + 2⁻¹⁰. Under round-half-to-even, these values would be 2048, −2048, 4096 and 1, respectively. The output for −0 is +0 〔numerical〕.

Input decoding also has a nonstandard feature: FP16 values whose exponent field is all ones (that is, inf and NaN) are decoded as ordinary numbers. +inf is decoded as 2¹⁶, the NaN 0x7E00 as 1.5 × 2¹⁶, and 0x7FFF as 131008. These decoded values exceed the range of the accumulator and therefore cause the result to overflow to inf. Bryngelson [5] reports that NaN becomes +inf on the ANE, and the decoding described above explains the cause of this phenomenon. On the other hand, large inputs within the normal range do not overflow ahead of the accumulator: 65504 × 2⁻⁴ yields the correct 4094 〔numerical〕.

### 8.3 INT8 Path: 32-Bit Integer Accumulator

The accumulator of the INT8 path cannot be tested by the method of Section 8.1, because activation quantization in coremltools requires calibration, so the scaling factors are not under control. Instead, we manually constructed in MIL the structure "quantize, dequantize, then convolve with Int8 weights", so that the quantized inputs and weights are all the same Int8 value (127 or −128). In this way, the integer accumulated sum S at each output point can be computed exactly. The scaling factors of both the inputs and the weights are taken as 2⁻ᵏ, so the theoretical output is S × 2⁻²ᵏ. Each model was executed in both `CPU_AND_NE` and `CPU_ONLY` modes. Throughout each group of runs, the IOReport interrupt count was used to confirm that the ANE took part in the computation (tool: [numerics_i8.py](../tools/06_numerics/numerics_i8.py); data: [numerics_i8/](../data/06_numerics/numerics_i8/)) 〔numerical, IOReport〕. The interrupt count is aggregated over a group, so it cannot indicate on which device each model executed. Agreement between ANE and CPU outputs also cannot serve as a criterion that a model fell back to the CPU, because a correct ANE result may happen to equal the CPU result. Table 8-4 lists only those models whose ANE output differs from their `CPU_ONLY` output. Their results cannot have come from the CPU, and therefore they were certainly computed by the ANE. For the models not listed, the basis of judgment is the dispatch rule of Section 5.2 rather than the outputs. A model with an input channel count N of 120000 or more has a tensor dimension exceeding the 65536 limit of Table 5-2 and cannot be placed on the ANE, while a model with N not exceeding 1024 is so small in computation that the segmenter's cost rule would place it on the CPU 〔inferred〕. The model with N = 2080 is an example in which the outputs agree but which need not have executed on the CPU. Both the ANE and the CPU output 2048, which is the correct rounding of the theoretical value 2047.6. It is most likely computed by the ANE, and its result merely coincides with the CPU result. The conclusions of Table 8-4 do not depend on these points. In addition, to reach S = 2³¹ on the ANE without being limited by the channel-count ceiling, we switched to kh×1 convolutions with N = 65536. Table 8-4 gives the results 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-4. Accumulation range of the INT8 path*

| Convolution                         | Integer accumulated sum S | Scaling      | Theoretical output | ANE output  |
| ----------------------------------- | ------------------------- | ------------ | ------------------ | ----------- |
| 1×1, N = 4096–16384, 127 × 127      | 2²⁶–2²⁸                   | 2⁻¹²         | 16129–64516        | 16128–64512 |
| 1×1, N = 65536, (−128) × (−128)     | 2³⁰                       | 2⁻¹⁶ / 2⁻¹⁸  | 16384 / 4096       | Accurate    |
| 2×1, N = 65536                      | 2³¹                       | 2⁻¹⁶ / 2⁻¹⁸  | 32768 / 8192       | inf         |
| 3×1, alternating signs (partial sums not exceeding about 2³⁰) | 1.008 × 2³⁰ | Same as above | 16512 / 4128 | Accurate |

Table 8-4 shows that the accumulator of the INT8 path is a 32-bit signed integer: the result is accurate when S = 2³⁰, and it overflows when S = 2³¹ (that is, one more than the maximum value of int32). Overflow is independent of the magnitude of the output value. With a scaling of 2⁻¹⁸, the output corresponding to S = 2³¹ is only 8192, and it still overflows. As on the FP16 path, the overflowed result is infinity rather than saturation. The output of the first row reaches 64512, exceeding the 32768 ceiling of the FP16 path. This indicates that accumulation on the INT8 path is performed in the integer domain, with scaling applied at the end, and does not pass through the fixed-point accumulator of the FP16 path 〔numerical〕. The maximum of an Int8 × Int8 product is 2¹⁴, so an output point must accumulate about 130,000 maximum products before it overflows. The product of the input channel count and the kernel area in common networks is far smaller than this quantity, so overflow on the INT8 path almost never occurs in practice.

### 8.4 Element-Wise Operations: Floating-Point Path

Elementwise operations are executed by the PE. We tested addition and multiplication by the method of Section 8.1, and the results are shown in Table 8-5 (data: [numerics3_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics3_m6.txt), [numerics4_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics4_m6.txt)) 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-5. Numerical behavior of elementwise operations*

| Case          | ANE   | CPU   | Note                                                                 |
| ------------- | ----- | ----- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 2048 + 1      | 2050  | 2048  | Rounding is the same as in the multiply-accumulate path: halfway cases rounded away from zero |
| 65504 + 0     | 65504 | 65504 | No 32768 limit                                                       |
| 16384 + 16384 | 32768 | 32768 | —                                                                    |
| 2⁻²⁴ + 0      | 2⁻²⁴  | 2⁻²⁴  | Subnormal number preserved                                           |
| 2⁻¹² × 2⁻¹²   | 2⁻²⁴  | 2⁻²⁴  | —                                                                    |
| 256 × 256     | inf   | inf   | Overflows only when the FP16 range is exceeded                       |
| NaN + 1       | inf   | NaN   | Input decoding is the same as in the multiply-accumulate path        |

Table 8-5 shows that the results of elementwise operations differ from those of the multiply-accumulate path in two respects. In range, the result of elementwise addition can exceed 32768. For example, 65504 + 0 yields the correct 65504, and overflow to infinity occurs only when the result exceeds the maximum value of FP16. In precision, subnormal numbers such as 2⁻²⁴ are preserved in full, whereas the resolution of the multiply-accumulate path is only 2⁻¹⁶. Neither property is consistent with the Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator; both are consistent with the behavior of FP16 floating-point arithmetic. We therefore infer that the PE operates in FP16 floating-point format and uses a different arithmetic unit from the one used by the MAC array 〔numerical, inferred〕. This is consistent with the division of labor described by Bryngelson [5], in which the NE performs multiply-accumulate and the PE performs elementwise operations. On the other hand, the two paths share the same rounding method (halfway cases rounded away from zero) and the same input decoding method (inf and NaN are decoded as ordinary numbers), and we speculate that the two paths share the same format-conversion logic 〔inferred〕.

### 8.5 Activation Functions

#### 8.5.1 Structure of the Lookup Tables

maderix [4] found on the M4 that activation functions such as sigmoid are implemented by 33 FP16 sampled points stored in the `__KERN_0` segment, combined with linear interpolation. To obtain the complete implementation of each function on the M6, we compiled for each activation function a small model consisting of "a 1×1 convolution plus the activation function", and read the non-weight data from the `__KERN_0` segment of the compiled artifacts (tool: [opvariants.py](../tools/06_numerics/opvariants.py); artifacts: [opv_hwx/](../data/06_numerics/opv_hwx/)). The table for sigmoid and similar functions is located at offset 0x8 from the beginning of the segment. The tables for sqrt, rsqrt, inverse, log and floor have separate symbols of the form `K<hash>_actlut`. Field tracing shows that, in the NE configuration, the nonlinearity mode distinguishes only three classes, "none", "ReLU" and "table lookup". Sigmoid, tanh, GELU and others are all written as the same "table lookup" value (Table 5-13), and which function is implemented is determined entirely by the table contents 〔compiler〕. Table 8-6 summarizes the table contents of each function 〔HWX〕.

*Table 8-6. Contents of the activation-function lookup tables (33 FP16 values each)*

| Function             | Table contents                                                                                     | Implementation                                                                 |
| -------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| relu, abs, sign, square | No table                                                                                        | Computed directly by the ALU                                                   |
| sigmoid              | σ(−8), σ(−7.5), …, σ(8)                                                                            | 33 equally spaced points on [−8, 8], storing exact sampled values              |
| tanh                 | tanh(0), tanh(0.125), …, tanh(4)                                                                   | Only the positive half-axis is stored; odd symmetry is exploited               |
| erf                  | erf(0), erf(1/16), …, erf(2)                                                                       | Only the positive half-axis is stored; odd symmetry is exploited               |
| sin                  | sin(0), sin(π/64), …, sin(π/2)                                                                     | Only a quarter period is stored; the rest is obtained from symmetry            |
| exp                  | 2^(0/32), 2^(1/32), …, 2^(32/32)                                                                   | Converted to powers of 2: the integer part is written into the floating-point exponent, and the table covers only the fractional part within [0, 1] |
| log                  | 4 special values, followed by log₂(0.5) through log₂(0.9375) at spacing 1/64                       | Range reduction: log₂(mantissa) plus exponent, then multiplied by ln 2         |
| sqrt, rsqrt          | 4 special values, followed by 25 function values for arguments 0.5 to 2.0 at spacing 1/16; other positions are 0 | Range reduction, split by the parity of the exponent                  |
| inverse              | 4 special values, followed by 1/0.5 through 1/0.9375 at spacing 1/64                               | Range reduction                                                                |
| gelu                 | [−4, 4] at spacing 0.25; values are fitted (for example, gelu(0.25) should be 0.1497, but the table contains 0.1436) | Fitted to reduce the overall error                               |
| silu                 | [−8, 8] at spacing 0.5; values are fitted (silu(0.5) should be 0.311, but the table contains 0.296) | Fitted to reduce the overall error                              |
| floor                | The header entries are −inf and inf; the others are 0                                              | Mechanism differs from ordinary table lookup                                   |

All table-lookup functions use linear interpolation between 33 sampling points, but the sampling grids differ among functions. The compiler uses two techniques to let the limited 33 points cover as high a precision as possible. For functions with symmetry (tanh, erf, sin), only part of the interval is stored, and the rest is obtained from symmetry. For exp, log, sqrt, rsqrt and inverse, the input is first reduced to one octave, the exponent part is handled separately, and the table covers only the reduced interval. In addition, the table values for gelu and silu are not exact sampled values of the functions. They are results fitted to minimize the error over the entire interval 〔HWX〕.

#### 8.5.2 Errors

We took about 16,000–20,000 distinct FP16 inputs over the domain of each function (using a logarithmic grid for functions defined on positive inputs), used the exact float64 values as reference, and compared the errors of the ANE and of the CPU (FP16). The results are shown in Table 8-7 and Figure 8-2 (tools: [lut_err.py](../tools/06_numerics/lut_err.py), [lut_plot.py](../tools/06_numerics/lut_plot.py); data: [f3_lut_m6.txt](../data/06_numerics/f3_lut_m6.txt), [lut_m6/](../data/06_numerics/lut_m6/)) 〔numerical〕.

*Table 8-7. Errors of the activation functions (reference: exact float64 values)*

| Function              | Test interval | Distinct input points | ANE max. absolute error | ANE max. relative error (ulp) | CPU max. absolute error | Points where ANE and CPU results differ |
| --------------------- | ------------- | --------------------- | ----------------------- | ----------------------------- | ----------------------- | --------------------------------------- |
| sigmoid               | [−12, 12]     | 18092                 | 3.4 × 10⁻³              | 811                           | 7.0 × 10⁻⁴              | 81.9%                                   |
| tanh                  | [−6, 6]       | 18092                 | 1.6 × 10⁻³              | 11.0                          | 9.6 × 10⁻⁴              | 85.0%                                   |
| gelu (EXACT)          | [−8, 8]       | 18432                 | 6.2 × 10⁻³              | 12632                         | 1.7 × 10⁻³              | 81.0%                                   |
| gelu (TANH approximation) | [−8, 8]   | 18432                 | 6.2 × 10⁻³              | 12632                         | 3.9 × 10⁻³              | 92.6%                                   |
| silu                  | [−12, 12]     | 18092                 | 1.5 × 10⁻²              | 28560                         | 4.8 × 10⁻³              | 88.7%                                   |
| erf                   | [−4, 4]       | 18432                 | 8.6 × 10⁻⁴              | 3.0                           | 9.8 × 10⁻⁴              | 58.0%                                   |
| sin                   | [−8, 8]       | 18432                 | 6.3 × 10⁻⁴              | 45.3                          | 2.4 × 10⁻⁴              | 34.8%                                   |
| exp                   | [−12, 10]     | 18084                 | 1.0 × 10²               | 9.1                           | 1.0 × 10¹               | 47.9%                                   |
| log                   | [2⁻¹⁰, 1000]  | 20433                 | 6.6 × 10⁻³              | 91.0                          | 2.0 × 10⁻³              | 51.2%                                   |
| sqrt                  | [2⁻¹⁰, 1000]  | 20433                 | 1.8 × 10⁻²              | 1.2                           | 7.8 × 10⁻³              | 22.8%                                   |
| rsqrt                 | [2⁻⁶, 1000]   | 16337                 | 8.1 × 10⁻³              | 2.1                           | 2.2 × 10⁻²              | 79.2%                                   |
| inverse               | [2⁻⁶, 1000]   | 16337                 | 4.4 × 10⁻¹              | 13.5                          | 5.9 × 10⁻¹              | 78.4%                                   |

The table reports both absolute and ulp errors. The absolute error is the absolute value of the difference between the ANE output and the exact value. The ulp error divides this difference by the distance between two adjacent FP16-representable numbers within the exponent interval containing the exact value, and so measures the error in units of FP16 representation intervals. Specifically, when |exact value| ∈ [2ᵉ, 2ᵉ⁺¹), 1 ulp = 2ᵉ⁻¹⁰; when |exact value| < 2⁻¹⁴, 1 ulp = 2⁻²⁴. The size of the ulp therefore varies piecewise with the exponent interval, consistent with how NumPy's spacing function defines it for FP16 (tool: lut_ulp.py; data: lut_ulp_m6.txt). The two metrics reflect error scales of different magnitudes and must be interpreted together with the output characteristics of each function. For functions whose outputs are bounded and approach zero over some intervals (sigmoid, gelu, silu), the FP16 ulp is extremely small when the exact value is close to zero. Even when the absolute error is small, it may correspond to hundreds or even thousands of ulps, so the absolute error better reflects the practical deviation. For example, the maximum error of sigmoid reaches 811 ulps, occurring at x ≈ −9.94. There the exact value is only about 4.8 × 10⁻⁵, while the ANE saturates directly to 0 for inputs outside [−8, 8]. Conversely, for functions with a large dynamic range of outputs (exp, sqrt, rsqrt, inverse), the spacing of FP16 representable values grows as the output magnitude increases. A larger absolute error therefore does not necessarily indicate lower numerical precision, and the ulp error is more comparable. For example, the maximum absolute error of exp can reach 100, but after conversion it is only 9.1 ulps.

![Activation-function errors](../figs/en/light/fig8-2_lut_error.png)

*Figure 8-2. Errors of the activation functions on the ANE (tool: [lut_plot.py](../tools/06_numerics/lut_plot.py); data: [lut_m6/](../data/06_numerics/lut_m6/)).*

The distribution of errors corroborates the structure of Table 8-6, and Figure 8-3 enlarges the error curves of four of these functions. The error of sigmoid drops to its minimum at each sampling point, and at the sampling points the error is only 4–11% of the error at the midpoint of the adjacent interval, a typical feature of equally spaced sampling with linear interpolation. Beyond ±8, the output of sigmoid saturates directly. The discontinuity of tanh at ±4 arises precisely because the table covers only [0, 4], and values beyond that range saturate directly. The ANE output of silu agrees with x multiplied by the ANE sigmoid(x) at only 38% of points, indicating that silu uses its own lookup table rather than being composed from sigmoid. The zeros of the error of gelu and silu do not lie on the equally spaced grid, which is a consequence of the table values being fitted. The errors of sqrt and rsqrt do not exceed 1.2 and 2.1 ulps, respectively. maderix [4] concluded from errors of this kind that these functions are computed by dedicated hardware. The results of this report show that range reduction alone is sufficient for a 33-point lookup table to reach this accuracy, so dedicated hardware is not necessarily required 〔HWX, numerical, inferred〕. One further point deserves attention. On the ANE, the EXACT mode and the TANH approximation mode of gelu use the same table, and their outputs are bit-identical, whereas on the CPU the two modes differ at 78% of points. Consequently, the choice of gelu mode on the ANE does not affect the result.

![Close-up of the absolute errors of four activation functions](../figs/en/light/fig8-3_lut_error_zoom.png)

*Figure 8-3. Close-up of the absolute errors of sigmoid, gelu, silu and tanh on the ANE (ANE output minus the exact float64 value). The light blue vertical lines mark intervals of 0.5, corresponding to the grids of the sigmoid and silu lookup tables; the grids of tanh and gelu are 0.125 and 0.25, respectively (tool: [lut_plot.py](../tools/06_numerics/lut_plot.py); data: [lut_m6/](../data/06_numerics/lut_m6/)).*

### 8.6 Comparison with the M4 and the Literature

We repeated the basic tests of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path on the M4 (h16g). These tests covered the overflow points for a single input and for the sum of two numbers, the retention and discarding of subnormal inputs, output rounding, and the scaling under different weights. The results are identical item by item to those on the M6 (data: [numerics2_m4.txt](../data/06_numerics/numerics2_m4.txt)) 〔numerical〕. This shows that the Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator and the rounding method have remained unchanged since at least the H16 generation. The decoding of inf and NaN was not tested separately on the M4. The bias, the exponent rule for tiny products, the INT8 path, elementwise operations and activation functions were tested only on the M6. Table 8-8 compares the results of this report with the description by Bryngelson [5].

*Table 8-8. Comparison of numerical behavior with the literature*

| Item              | Bryngelson [5]                                  | M6 and M4 (this report)                                                  |
| ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Accumulator       | FP32-class wide accumulator                     | 32-bit fixed point, Q15.16                                               |
| Behavior near 32768 | M1 and A14 saturate at 32768; M5 does not saturate | Reaching 32768 gives inf, which persists as inf                        |
| Reduction order   | First level on M1 is FP16 partial sums with radix 4 | No FP16 partial sums                                                 |
| Subnormal numbers | M1 flushes to zero; M5 preserves them           | The multiply-accumulate path decides retention by the exponent sum; the elementwise path preserves them |
| Output rounding   | Halfway cases rounded to even                   | Halfway cases rounded away from zero                                     |
| NaN input         | Becomes +inf                                    | Decoded as a large ordinary number, causing the accumulator to overflow to inf |

The differences between the two may have two sources. First, hardware of different product generations may actually differ. Second, the test methods may differ. For example, the saturation at 32768 described by Bryngelson and the overflow to inf at 32768 reported in this report point to the same bound, 2¹⁵, and this bound is exactly the limit of the integer part of the Q15.16 fixed-point format.

### 8.7 Summary

This chapter addressed how the ANE computes numerical results on its different arithmetic paths. The FP16 convolution and matrix multiplication use a 32-bit fixed-point accumulator (Q15.16). Its absolute value overflows to infinity upon reaching 32768, its resolution is 2⁻¹⁶, the bias is the initial value of the accumulator, and the final result is rounded to FP16 with halfway cases rounded away from zero. The INT8 convolution uses a 32-bit integer accumulator, and scaling is performed after accumulation completes. Elementwise operations use FP16 floating-point arithmetic that preserves subnormal numbers. The overflow results of all three paths are infinity rather than saturation. Activation functions are implemented by lookup tables with 33-point linear interpolation, and the maximum errors of commonly used activation functions are on the order of 10⁻³ to 10⁻². The basic behavior of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path was retested on the M4, and the results are identical.

The conclusions of this chapter are all measured on individual operators. The accumulation test uses a 1×1 convolution with 64 input channels, which completes accumulation within a single pass. For layers that require multiple passes or multiple TDs to complete accumulation, we did not test whether partial sums are relayed through FP16, and this report does not evaluate how these numerical behaviors accumulate in a complete network. In Chapter 14, when images are processed one at a time, the top-1 results disagree with the GPU, and the conclusions of this chapter can only rule out the summation order within a single pass as a cause. Whether the M1 and M5 likewise use a fixed-point accumulator, and whether the difference from the "FP32-class accumulator" in the literature arises from hardware or from test methods, remain to be verified (Section 16.2). The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are collected in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 9. Memory Hierarchy and Data Movement

Chapter 7 measured the capability of the compute array under the conditions that weights are resident and activations stay on chip. In actual networks, data often must be moved between DRAM and on-chip storage, and the capacity and bandwidth of the data paths likewise determine performance. The data moved by the ANE falls into two categories. Weights are the parameters of the model; they are determined at compile time, and the Kernel DMA reads them from DRAM into the weight buffer of each NE during each inference. Activations are tensors that flow through the network and vary with the input of each inference. The input and output of the network reside in DRAM and are moved between DRAM and L2 by the Tile DMA. Intermediate results between layers remain in L2 when L2 capacity permits and when the compiler can execute adjacent layers together; otherwise they are written back to DRAM and read back by the next layer. This chapter examines, in turn, L2, the weight path, the activation path and DRAM. The main conclusions are four. First, the compiler uses the L2 of each ANE as exactly 2 MiB. When an intermediate tensor exceeds 2 MiB, it is split into several tiles, and each tile executes the complete chain in succession, so intermediate results do not leave the chip. Second, the bandwidth at which native kernels read weights is about 150 GB/s, independent of the size of each layer's weights and of the kernel shape (rewritten kernels are limited by another stage measured per weight element; Section 7.4.2). A single ANE already approaches the upper limit of DRAM bandwidth, and two ANEs together cannot substantially exceed it. Third, the bandwidth of the activation path is only about 35–40 GB/s per ANE, and each TD reads at most two and writes at most one DRAM tensor. Fourth, the theoretical DRAM bandwidth is 170.5 GB/s; the ANE alone can reach about 150–159 GB/s, and when the ANE is used concurrently with the GPU, the two roughly share the bandwidth equally.

### 9.1 Test Methodology

The questions in this chapter fall into two categories, which require different methods. The first category concerns the capacity and organization of on-chip storage: how large L2 is, how intermediate tensors are tiled, and how banks are mapped. This information is directly reflected in the compiler's memory allocation, and timing experiments can provide little useful information for it. As will be seen below, when the intermediate tensor grows from 0.5 MB to 16 MB, the time per pixel decreases rather than increasing, with no inflection point at all. For this category of question, we therefore mainly compare compiled artifacts. By changing the tensor size or the compilation options, we observe how the length of the TD sequence, the number of tiles and the address registers change (tools: [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m), [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py)) 〔HWX, HAL〕.

The second category concerns the bandwidth of each data path. Such questions must be answered by direct measurement, but the data-movement time must be separated from the compute time and the host overhead. For the weight path, we construct a model with very little computation (for example, a chain of 1×1 convolutions of width only 16), so that the time to read weights is much greater than the compute time. We then compare two versions: one in which the weights differ for each layer, and one in which all layers share a single set of weights. In the latter, the weights remain resident on chip, so the difference in time between the two versions is the cost of reading weights. For the activation path, we construct a model that contains only elementwise operations and has tensors large enough that reading and writing activations becomes the bottleneck. Chapter 6 shows that the total call time of Core ML contains a host wake-up overhead that varies with task duration. This overhead would mix into the timing slope and cause the bandwidth to be underestimated. Therefore, the bandwidth figures in this chapter are determined from the ANE task duration obtained from kdebug firmware timestamps, and results obtained from timing are used only as a reference 〔kdebug, timing〕. In addition, we use the DRAM bandwidth and frequency-state statistics in IOReport to observe the state of the memory controller. We also have the GPU read memory at the same time, measuring the sharing of DRAM between the ANE and the GPU 〔IOReport〕.

### 9.2 L2

#### 9.2.1 Capacity

The physical capacity of L2 cannot be read directly, but how the compiler uses L2 can be probed through compilation options. `ANECCompile` accepts an option named `L2Size` (in units of kB, where 1 kB is taken as 1024 bytes), which is passed to `setL2Size` of the compiler parameter object. We compiled the same model with different values and compared the resulting TD sequences. The reason for comparing only the TD sequences, rather than entire files, is that the version information in a file records the compilation options themselves. Table 9-1 gives the results (data: [l2sz/](../data/07_memory/l2sz/); model generation: [l2res.py](../tools/07_memory/l2res.py)) 〔HWX〕.

*Table 9-1. Changes in the TD sequence under different values of `L2Size`*

| Model                                                    | ≤ 1024 kB       | 1280 kB         | 1536–2047 kB                 | 2048 kB                     | ≥ 2049 kB                   |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | --------------- | --------------- | ---------------------------- | --------------------------- | --------------------------- |
| 1×1 convolution chain, 4 MiB tensors                     | Longer sequence | Longer sequence | Same as default              | Same as default             | Same as default             |
| Residual block, 1 MiB tensors, multiple tensors live simultaneously | Longer sequence | Longer sequence | Same length, different content | Word-for-word identical to default | Word-for-word identical to default |
| Residual block, 2 MiB tensors                            | —               | Longer sequence | Same length, different content | Word-for-word identical to default | Word-for-word identical to default |

Two points in Table 9-1 deserve attention. First, for the residual blocks in which multiple tensors are simultaneously live, the TD sequence is word-for-word identical to the default compilation only when the value is not less than 2048 kB. At 2047 kB it already differs, which indicates that the default value is exactly 2048 kB. A single convolution chain is identical to the default already at 1536 kB and above, which is not sufficient to discriminate. Second, when the value is increased from 2049 kB to 4 GB, the TD sequence remains identical to the default. This indicates that the effective value is capped at 2 MiB, or that the allocator cannot exploit a larger space.

Comparing the default compilation with the residual-block product compiled at `L2Size = 2047` shows that the compiler indeed uses the top of the 2 MiB range. The two differ in 48 words, located mainly in a group of address registers in the 0x10xx range (for example, 0x1044, 0x1052 and 0x1053). We infer that this group of registers describes addresses and strides within L2 〔inferred〕. In the default compilation, a 0.5 MiB buffer is placed at offset 0x180000, occupying the interval [1.5 MiB, 2 MiB) and exactly reaching the boundary 0x200000. When the L2 capacity is reduced by 1 kB, that address interval can no longer accommodate the buffer, and the compiler therefore reallocates it to offset 0x080000 〔HWX〕. This result is consistent with two further lines of evidence. In the v24 layout, the L2 address and stride fields occupy 17 bits from bit 4 upward; at a granularity of 16 bytes, the addressable limit is exactly 2²¹ bytes, that is, 2 MiB. In the HAL parameter table, the on-chip limit for a single operand is 2 MiB (offset 0x1d8), and the maximum L2 stride is 2 MiB − 16 (offset 0x218) 〔compiler, HAL〕.

Taken together, we conclude that the compiler uses the L2 of each ANE as exactly 2 MiB and fills this space, so that the two ANEs together total 4 MiB. maderix [2] earlier inferred from a performance drop near 24–32 MB on the M4 that the on-chip SRAM is about 32 MB. The author later described it in Part 4 [4] using a 2 MiB per-operand threshold, and the results of this report agree with the latter.

#### 9.2.2 Tiling of Intermediate Tensors

For intermediate tensors that exceed 2 MiB, the experiments described below reveal the tiling behavior of the compiler. We first used timing measurements to determine the time per layer of a 1×1 convolution chain (256 channels, height 1, width 1024–32768, intermediate tensors 0.5–16 MB). The results are given in Table 9-2 (data: [c12_run.txt](../data/07_memory/c12_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 9-2. Intermediate tensor size and time per layer (1×1 convolution chain with 256 channels, dual-ANE)*

| Intermediate tensor | Time per layer | Time per thousand pixels | Marginal throughput |
| ------------------- | -------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------- |
| 0.5 MB              | 5.4 µs         | 5.27 µs                  | 24.9 TFLOPS         |
| 2 MB                | 19.0 µs        | 4.63 µs                  | 28.3 TFLOPS         |
| 4 MB                | 32.7 µs        | 3.99 µs                  | 32.9 TFLOPS         |
| 8 MB                | 61.5 µs        | 3.75 µs                  | 34.9 TFLOPS         |
| 16 MB               | 114.0 µs       | 3.48 µs                  | 37.7 TFLOPS         |

As the tensor becomes larger, the time per pixel instead becomes shorter, and no inflection point appears at 2 MiB. If the 16 MB intermediate tensor were written back to DRAM and read again at every layer, the required bandwidth would be about 280 GB/s, which exceeds the bandwidth that DRAM can provide. This indicates that the intermediate results do not enter DRAM.

The compiled artifacts reveal the mechanism behind this behavior. We built the same models in single-layer and two-layer versions, compiled them without executing them, and compared the lengths of the TD sequences (data: [c1_hwx/](../data/03_compile/c1_hwx/)). The TD sequence of the single-layer model is always 0x1d8 bytes long, independent of tensor size, which indicates that the input and output of a single-layer model reside directly in DRAM and that no tiling is required. The two-layer model behaves differently. When the intermediate tensor is 0.5 MB or 1 MB, the TD sequence of the single-ANE program is 0x340 bytes long; starting at 2 MB, each additional 2 MiB increases the sequence by 0x320 bytes, that is, one additional TD for each of the two layers. We therefore derive the number of tiles as follows:

```
number of tiles = ⌊intermediate tensor size ÷ 2 MiB⌋ + 1
```

That is, every tile must be strictly smaller than 2 MiB: a 2 MB tensor is split into 2 tiles, a 4 MB tensor into 3 tiles, and a 16 MB tensor into 9 tiles (about 1.78 MB each) 〔HWX〕. This rule was derived from a two-layer model with a single intermediate tensor. In longer chains, the input and output intermediate tensors of a layer must reside in L2 simultaneously, and the tiles are therefore finer. The 256-channel chains with widths 6144, 8192, 12288 and 14336 in Table 10-4 of Section 10.5 (intermediate tensors of 3–7 MiB) are split into 4, 5, 7 and 8 tiles per layer, respectively, which is consistent with a number of tiles equal to ⌊intermediate tensor size ÷ 1 MiB⌋ + 1 〔HWX, inferred〕. Tile widths are integer multiples of 128 columns and are kept as equal as possible. After tiling, each tile executes all layers of the complete chain in succession before the next tile is processed, and intermediate results remain in L2 throughout. This is why no inflection point is visible in the timing experiments: tiling changes only the order of execution and requires no additional DRAM reads or writes. This rule is consistent with the 2 MiB per-operand limit in the HAL parameter table, and also with maderix [4], who reports that tensors exceeding 2 MiB are processed in streamed tiles. Figure 9-1 illustrates the tiling of intermediate tensors.

![Tiling of intermediate tensors](../figs/en/light/fig9-1_tiling.png)

*Figure 9-1. Tiling of intermediate tensors. (1) A 16 MB intermediate tensor (256 channels, width 32768 columns) is split into 9 tiles of about 1.78 MB each. (2) Each tile executes the complete chain in succession, intermediate results remain in L2, and data is read from DRAM only at the first layer and written to DRAM only at the last layer.*

In a dual-ANE program, the compiler retains the tiling grid used for the single ANE and then assigns the list of whole tiles to the two ANEs. When the number of tiles is odd, the workloads of the two ANEs cannot be divided equally, and the extra tile is always assigned to ANE1. This causes the execution time of the dual-ANE program to be determined by ANE1. Across the widths measured, the execution time is at most about 19% longer than an even split, which corresponds to a throughput reduction of about 16%. This problem is discussed in detail in Chapter 10.

#### 9.2.3 Banks and Row Stride

maderix [4] found on the M4 that the SRAM consists of 64 banks interleaved at 16 bytes, and that the compiler selects an appropriate row stride to avoid bank conflicts. To verify this on the M6, we compiled 3×3 convolution chains (64 or 128 channels, height 16, width 8–136). Each model was compiled once with the default options and once with bank-conflict optimization disabled (`DisableL2BankConflictOpt=true`), and we compared the stride registers of L2 tensors (tool: [l2bank.py](../tools/07_memory/l2bank.py); data: [l2bank_table.txt](../data/07_memory/l2bank_table.txt)) 〔HWX〕.

With the optimization disabled, the values of registers such as 0x1044 and 0x1053, which we infer are the channel strides of L2 tensors, are all integer multiples of 1024 bytes. In the default compilation, the compiler adds 512 or 768 bytes to these values, and the two alternate with width. 1024 bytes is exactly 64 banks × 16 bytes, that is, the length of one cycle of the bank mapping. If the stride of each channel plane is an integer multiple of 1024 bytes, all planes start at the same bank, and accesses to adjacent channels conflict. Adding 512 or 768 bytes offsets the starting points of the planes by 32 or 48 banks. This result agrees with the bank count (64, offset 0x1e8) and access granularity (16 bytes, offset 0x1e0) in the HAL parameter table, and it provides evidence from M6 compiled artifacts for the structure of 64 banks with 16-byte interleaving 〔HWX, HAL〕. The `exe_cycles` values of the two compilations are exactly identical, which indicates that the compiler's performance model does not assign a penalty for bank conflicts on these shapes.

### 9.3 Weight Path

#### 9.3.1 Storage of Weights

Sections 5.6 and 7.3.1 have already described that the compiler sets a resident-weight limit of 64 KiB for each NE, so that 16 NEs total 1 MiB. Weights exceeding this capacity must be read from DRAM at every call. To confirm this, we compared two versions on a 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels and width 16: one with weights that differ for each layer, and one in which all layers share a single set of weights (0.5 MB per layer). The results are shown in Table 9-3 (data: [c2b_run.txt](../data/07_memory/c2b_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 9-3. Time for independent and shared weights (512 channels, width 16, single ANE)*

| Layers | Total weights | Independent weights | Shared weights | Difference |
| ------ | ------------- | ------------------- | -------------- | ---------- |
| 16     | 8 MB          | 195 µs              | 163 µs         | 33 µs      |
| 32     | 16 MB         | 264 µs              | 164 µs         | 100 µs     |
| 64     | 32 MB         | 382 µs              | 195 µs         | 187 µs     |
| 128    | 64 MB         | 733 µs              | 271 µs         | 462 µs     |

When the total weight is 8 MB, the version with independent weights is already 33 µs slower than the version with shared weights. The difference grows with the total weight, roughly in proportion to it (about 4–7 µs per MB). This timing includes host-side effects, and the exact weight-read bandwidth is given by the firmware timestamps in Section 9.3.2. The difference indicates that the weights cannot reside on chip as a whole and must be streamed in from DRAM at every call. The 16 MiB "streaming weight limit" in the HAL parameter table (offset 0x230) should describe the upper bound on the weights that the compiler streams in at one time, rather than the on-chip capacity 〔timing, HAL, inferred〕.

#### 9.3.2 Bandwidth

The differences in Table 9-3 are derived from Core ML call times, which contain host wake-up overhead that varies with task duration (Section 6.5). Computing bandwidth directly from these differences would underestimate the bandwidth of the weight path. We therefore determined this bandwidth from the ANE task duration recorded by kdebug. For the shapes used in Table 9-3, the ANE task duration grows smoothly with the amount of weights, at about 6.9 µs per MiB, that is, about 152 GB/s, with no step between 48 MiB and 52 MiB (data: [sw_ktrace_sum.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_ktrace_sum.txt)) 〔kdebug〕.

The measurements above cover only the narrow range of 48–52 MiB. To confirm that this bandwidth still holds when the amount of weights is much larger, we selected a structure with a very large amount of weights that, in practical applications, is limited by weight-read bandwidth: the multilayer perceptron of the decoding phase of a large language model. In the decoding phase, the full set of weights must be read once for every generated token, while the amount of computation is small, so it is a typical weight-read-bound workload on the ANE. We constructed a multilayer perceptron with the shape 2560 → 9216 → 2560, in which each pair of linear layers has about 94 MB of weights, and chained 1 to 4 such pairs, giving total weights of 94–377 MB. Each call of these models takes about 1 ms or longer.
