According to Section 6.5.2, at this duration the host-side completion overhead has saturated and no longer varies with task duration, so the timing slope can be used directly to obtain the bandwidth. The slope of time against the amount of weights gives 152 GB/s, consistent with the firmware-timestamp result (data: [bw_mlp_run.txt](../data/07_memory/bw_mlp_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. The total weights of this group of models far exceed the on-chip storage and the 16 MiB system-level cache, so the value of about 150 GB/s reflects the rate at which weights are read from DRAM.

The read-weight bandwidth measured from the Core ML timing slope is only about 130 GB/s when the total weight is small, and a step of about 55 µs appears near 48–50 MiB. Firmware timestamps show that the ANE task itself does not change before or after the step. The step and the lower slope both arise entirely from the host wake-up overhead described in Section 6.5. When the task duration exceeds about 350 µs, the CPU waiting for the result enters a deeper idle state, and the completion-side overhead of each call increases accordingly. This overhead grows with the number of layers and is mixed into the timing slope.

The bandwidth for reading weights is independent of the size of each layer's weights and of the kernel shape. With the total weight held fixed, changing the weights per layer from 128 KB to 8 MB (a factor of 64) gives timing-slope bandwidths between 127 and 133 GB/s, and the total times of the four partitionings are also nearly identical (data: [bw_blocksize_run.txt](../data/07_memory/bw_blocksize_run.txt)). On the small 8×8 spatial size, 3×3, 9×1 and 1×1 (1536 channels) convolutions, each with 4.5 MB of weights per layer, are all weight-read-bound, and the time per layer for each is about 33.4 µs (data: [bw_kshape_run.txt](../data/07_memory/bw_kshape_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. It follows that a weight block of 128 KB is already sufficient for the path to reach full speed.

#### 9.3.3 Weights and the System-Level Cache

The M6 SoC contains a system-level cache (SLC) shared by the CPU, GPU and ANE (called MCache in the compiler, with a capacity of 16 MiB given in the parameter table). Whether weights pass through the SLC affects the bandwidth for reading weights and the interference with other processors. Two lines of evidence indicate that, on the default Core ML path, weights do not use the SLC. First, the Core ML runtime compilation parameters recorded in the kernel log include `--disable-cache-prefetch-mask=1` and `--enable-memcache-kernel-allocation=false`, that is, weight cache prefetching and cache allocation are disabled. The driver implements complete SLC prefetch logic, but it is not enabled on this path 〔kernel〕. Second, if the total weights were smaller than the SLC capacity (16 MiB) and could hit in the SLC, models with small amounts of weights should be read faster. No such tendency was observed in the measurements, as the timing slope in the 8–32 MB range is not higher than that for larger amounts of weights 〔timing〕. In a dual-ANE program, when data is exchanged between the two ANEs, the compiler sets a DSID parameter for each ANE. We speculate that in this case the data exchange passes through the SLC (Section 5.8.2), but this does not mean that weights can be read from the SLC.

#### 9.3.4 Sharing of Weight Bandwidth Between Two ANEs

In a dual-ANE program, each of the two ANEs reads its own copy of the weights (Section 5.8.3). If the two ANEs each had independent weight-read bandwidth, a dual-ANE program should also obtain nearly a twofold speedup when it is limited by weight reads, and measurements do not support this. First, when a single ANE reads weights it already reaches about 150–159 GB/s (Sections 9.3.2 and 7.5), which is about 90% of the theoretical DRAM bandwidth of 170.5 GB/s (Section 9.5). Even if the second engine had an independent path, it could obtain only a small part of the remainder. Second, on native kernels, when the convolution chain is changed to use different weights at each layer, the combined weight-read rate of the two engines in dual-ANE mode is about 140 GB/s. The speedup relative to a single ANE falls from about 1.9× with shared weights to about 1.4× (Section 10.4) 〔timing〕. Finally, each ANE has two DRAM links (L0 and L1) in IOReport, and when weights are read, the two links each carry half, with the ratio not varying with the load (data: [pmp_L96.txt](../data/07_memory/pmp_L96.txt), [pmp_L100.txt](../data/07_memory/pmp_L100.txt)) 〔IOReport〕. Therefore, the bottleneck of weight reads does not lie on the link of a single ANE but on the DRAM side or on the path shared by the two ANEs 〔inferred〕.

Two other groups of experiments cannot be used to judge the sharing of DRAM bandwidth. For rewritten kernels with kernel widths 9–15 that are split by rows, each ANE processes only half of the rows, and the time per layer is twice that of a single ANE processing the same number of rows, so the overall throughput equals that of a single ANE (Sections 7.4.2, 10.4 and 10.7). The bottleneck of these kernels, however, is a stage whose cost is measured per weight element, and what it shows is that the two engines share this stage. For the 2-bit and 3-bit palettized models split across two ANEs in Section 7.5, each of the two engines stores a complete copy of the weights, and how much each engine actually reads from DRAM remains unclear.

### 9.4 Activation Path

#### 9.4.1 Inputs and Outputs per TD

The Tile DMA moves activation tensors between DRAM and L2. Section 5.7.6 states that the registers of the Tile DMA are divided into three groups: the first input (Src1), the second input (Src2) and the output (Dst). This register structure implies that a single TD can read and write at most three DRAM tensors directly. To determine how the compiler handles more inputs and outputs, we compiled a group of elementwise operation models and listed, TD by TD, the Tile DMA address packets written by each TD. The results are shown in Table 9-4 (tool: [tdma.py](../tools/07_memory/tdma.py)) 〔HWX〕.

*Table 9-4. TD structure of elementwise operation models*

| Model                    | TD structure                                                                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| a + b, a × b, a + a      | One TD: Src1, Src2, Dst                                                                                                                                            |
| x × 1.5                  | One TD: Src1, Dst                                                                                                                                                  |
| a + b + c                | Two TDs per channel block: the first reads a and b, and its result remains in L2; the second reads c as Src2, chains in the other operand from L2, and writes the result as Dst |
| a + b + c + d            | Three TDs per channel block, which read a and b, then c, then d in turn; the last TD writes the output                                                             |
| o1 = a + b, o2 = a − b   | Two TDs, each with one Dst                                                                                                                                         |
| o1 = a + b, o2 = relu(o1) | Two TDs; the second reads o1 back from DRAM as Src1                                                                                                              |

Table 9-4 shows that each TD reads at most two tensors from DRAM and writes at most one. This is a restriction that the compiler always observes, and we speculate that it derives from the structure of the hardware 〔HWX, inferred〕. When there are more than two inputs, the compiler splits the operation into several TDs, with intermediate results passed along a chain through L2. When there is more than one output, a separate TD must be started.

#### 9.4.2 Bandwidth

To measure the bandwidth of the Tile DMA, we constructed elementwise operation models with FP16 tensors of shape [1, 256, H, W] (H = W = 128, 192 and 256, that is, 8, 18 and 32 MB), and measured the task duration of each ANE with kdebug. All of these models were split by rows across the two ANEs, each processing half, with the two tasks fully overlapping. Table 9-5 gives the combined read and write bandwidth of each ANE (tool: [tdma_ktrace.sh](../tools/07_memory/tdma_ktrace.sh); data: [tdma_kt/](../data/07_memory/tdma_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 9-5. Activation read and write bandwidth per ANE*

| Model                      | 8 MB    | 18 MB   | 32 MB   | Slope from 18 to 32 MB |
| -------------------------- | ------- | ------- | ------- | ---------------------- |
| x × 1.5 (1 read, 1 write)  | 79 GB/s | 52 GB/s | 42 GB/s | 33.7 GB/s              |
| a + b (2 reads, 1 write)   | 56 GB/s | 41 GB/s | 38 GB/s | 35.7 GB/s              |
| a × b                      | 56 GB/s | 41 GB/s | 39 GB/s | 35.8 GB/s              |
| a + a (counted as two reads) | 62 GB/s | 50 GB/s | 43 GB/s | 35.9 GB/s            |

Computed from the slope between 18 MB and 32 MB, the combined read and write bandwidth per ANE for single-input and two-input operations lies between 34 and 36 GB/s and is nearly identical. If the two inputs each had their own path, the time for a + b should be close to that for x × 1.5. In the measurements, however, the time for a + b is 1.6–2.1 times that for x, which exceeds the ratio of the bytes read and written (1.5 times). This indicates that the two inputs do not add their bandwidths, and that a single TD having two inputs is only a compiler-level structure that does not correspond to two independent bandwidths. A field in the HAL parameter table (offset 0x5f6, read by `IsDualInputsPEWithSingleDMAEngine`) is 1 on H16 through H19. This indicates that a two-input operation has only one DMA engine, which serves the two inputs in a time-multiplexed manner 〔kdebug, HAL〕. The result for a + a also shows that when the same tensor is used as both inputs, it is read twice, and the compiler does not merge the two reads.

Small tensors achieve noticeably higher bandwidth; single-input operations reach 79 GB/s at 8 MB. We speculate that this is because the inputs have just been written by the CPU, and a portion of them is still located in the SLC. The larger the tensor, the closer the bandwidth approaches 35–40 GB/s 〔inferred〕. It follows that the bandwidth of the Tile DMA is about 35–40 GB/s per ANE, and about 70–80 GB/s for the two ANEs together. This is far below the approximately 150 GB/s of the weight path, and also below the bandwidth that DRAM can supply to the ANE. The limit therefore lies in the Tile DMA path itself, not in DRAM. Operations involving multiple TDs (two outputs or multiple inputs) show a further decline in bandwidth at 32 MB, to 14–27 GB/s.

This result is consistent with the absence of speedup for the W8A8 1×1 convolution chains in Section 7.5, although the quantitative agreement is not yet fully reconciled (see Section 7.5). In a W8A8 chain, the activations of every layer pass through DRAM, and the bandwidth of the activation path is only about one quarter of that of the weight path. Layers of low compute density, such as 1×1 convolutions, are therefore limited by activation bandwidth. The result also shows the importance of layer fusion and tiling (Section 9.2.2): as long as intermediate results remain in L2, they are not limited by Tile DMA bandwidth.

### 9.5 DRAM

#### 9.5.1 Structure and Frequency

We read the memory structure of the M6 (16 GB) from the device tree. The memory is a Micron product, and the type reported by the system is LPDDR5 (this applies only to the test machine we used). Because 10656 MT/s exceeds the highest rate of the LPDDR5 standard, the memory more likely belongs to the LPDDR5X class, although the type name reported by the system does not distinguish this 〔inferred〕. The memory is managed by one memory controller (AMCC). Under the controller there are eight DCS channels, all enabled. Each channel has the identifier of one DRAM die, and 16 GB divided by 8 equals one 2 GB die per channel, that is, a single-rank configuration. ECC is enabled 〔ioreg〕. The DRAM has eight available voltage and frequency states (device-tree `voltage-states2`, corresponding to DCS F2 through F9 in IOReport), but the frequency fields are omitted from the device tree. The SoC parameters in the compiler, the constant region of the CLPC driver, and the firmware of the PMP (Power Measurement Processor, as written in the Asahi Linux documentation [28]) each contain a DRAM frequency table. The three are consistent, with the highest state being 5328 MHz, that is, 10656 MT/s 〔compiler, kernel, firmware〕.

Under three workloads (the ANE alone reading weights, the GPU alone reading memory, and both running at the same time), IOReport shows that the residency fraction of the DCS F9 state exceeds 99.8% in each case (tool: [dramco.sh](../tools/07_memory/dramco.sh); data: [dramco/](../data/07_memory/dramco/)) 〔IOReport〕. Therefore, at full load the DRAM operates at the highest state, 10656 MT/s, and the theoretical bandwidth of the eight 16-bit channels is 170.5 GB/s.

#### 9.5.2 Bandwidth and Sharing

Table 9-6 gives the bandwidth of the ANE and the GPU, both alone and when reading memory simultaneously. The bandwidth of the ANE is derived from firmware timestamps, and that of the GPU is measured by a read-only Metal kernel (tools: [membw.swift](../tools/common/membw.swift), [bwco_ktrace.sh](../tools/07_memory/bwco_ktrace.sh); data: [bwco_kt/](../data/07_memory/bwco_kt/), [membw64_alone.txt](../data/07_memory/membw64_alone.txt), [membw64_co.txt](../data/07_memory/membw64_co.txt)) 〔kdebug, timing〕.

*Table 9-6. DRAM bandwidth of the ANE and the GPU*

| Condition            | ANE read of weights | GPU read          | Combined            |
| -------------------- | ------------------- | ----------------- | ------------------- |
| ANE alone            | About 153 GB/s      | —                 | About 153 GB/s      |
| GPU alone            | —                   | About 143–146 GB/s | About 145 GB/s     |
| Concurrent execution | About 78 GB/s       | About 85–90 GB/s  | About 163–168 GB/s  |

When the two run at the same time, the combined bandwidth reaches about 163–168 GB/s, which is about 96–99% of the theoretical value, showing that the memory channels themselves do not lose speed. However, each processor alone reaches only about 84–90% of the theoretical bandwidth. In this group of experiments, the ANE reached at most about 153 GB/s and the GPU about 143–146 GB/s. When both run concurrently, they roughly divide the total bandwidth equally. The row for concurrent execution combines ANE and GPU data from two different runs, so the combined value is given only as a range. The 32 GB version of the M6 has two ranks per channel, and interleaved access may improve the access efficiency of each processor, but this report does not have a 32 GB machine and cannot verify this 〔inferred〕.

This result has a direct consequence for the use of the ANE. When the ANE and the GPU concurrently execute workloads that are limited by read bandwidth, the ANE's weight-read bandwidth falls to about half.

### 9.6 Comparison with the M4 and the Literature

Table 9-7 compares the results of this chapter with the results of maderix [4] on the M4 and with the data of Bryngelson [5].

*Table 9-7. Comparison of memory and data-movement parameters*

| Item                    | M4 (maderix [4], except where otherwise noted)                | M5 (Bryngelson [5])            | M6 (this report)                                                                                                    |
| ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| On-chip limit per operand | 2 MiB (HAL)                                                 | —                              | 2 MiB (HAL and compiled artifacts; L2 used as 2 MiB)                                                                |
| L2 banks                | 64, interleaved at 16 bytes                                   | —                              | 64, interleaved at 16 bytes; strides padded by 512 or 768 bytes                                                     |
| Beyond the limit        | Streamed in tiles, with double buffering                      | Timing inflection at about 4.72 MB | In two-layer models, number of tiles = ⌊size ÷ 2 MiB⌋ + 1; for longer chains, about ⌊size ÷ 1 MiB⌋ + 1; each tile executes the entire chain in succession, with no inflection in timing |
| Resident weights per NE | 64 KiB; up to 16 MiB streamed in 16 blocks                    | —                              | 64 KiB; the excess is read from DRAM at each call                                                                   |
| Weight-read bandwidth   | About 67 GB/s (measured on the M4 in this report, Section 7.5) | About 145 GB/s                 | About 150 GB/s (shared between two ANEs)                                                                            |
| Activation bandwidth    | Each additional input plane slows execution by about 995 µs   | About 24 GB/s                  | About 35–40 GB/s per ANE                                                                                            |
| DRAM                    | 120 GB/s (Apple's published specification)                    | —                              | 8 channels, 10656 MT/s, theoretical 170.5 GB/s                                                                      |

The on-chip storage organization of the M4 and the M6 (the 2 MiB per-operand limit, 64 banks, and the 64 KiB resident-weight limit) is completely identical in compiler parameters. This is consistent with the conclusion in Section 5.4 that the relevant fields of the HAL parameter table did not change. The changes in the M6 lie mainly outside L2. The DRAM bandwidth rises from 120 GB/s to 170.5 GB/s, and the weight-read bandwidth of a single ANE is about 150 GB/s. maderix reports that each additional independent input plane costs about 995 µs, and notes at the end that this figure reflects compiler scheduling rather than the width of the DMA. Table 9-4 of this report provides an explanation for this: each TD has at most two inputs, and additional inputs must be split into extra TDs.

### 9.7 Summary

This chapter addressed where data is stored, through which paths it is moved, and what the bandwidths are. Viewed from the way the compiler uses the hardware, the storage hierarchy consists of the 64 KiB resident-weight space of each NE, the L2 of each ANE used as 2 MiB, the system-level cache, and 8-channel DRAM. When an intermediate tensor exceeds 2 MiB, the compiler splits it into tiles; each tile executes the complete chain in succession, and intermediate results do not leave the chip. Weights and activations are moved through two different paths. The weight-read bandwidth is about 150 GB/s; a single ANE already approaches the upper limit of DRAM bandwidth, and two ANEs together cannot substantially exceed it. The activation path provides only about 35–40 GB/s per ANE, and each TD reads at most two and writes at most one DRAM tensor. DRAM operates at 10656 MT/s, and when the ANE and the GPU are used together, they roughly divide the bandwidth equally.

The conclusion that the two engines share the weight-read bandwidth is the main basis for the explanation in Chapter 10 of why dual-engine acceleration falls short. What remains undetermined is the physical capacity of the on-chip storage, the effect of bank conflicts on actual execution time, the cause of the decline in activation bandwidth for operations requiring multiple TDs on large tensors, and the bandwidth of machines with other memory configurations (Section 16.2). The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are collected in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 10. Dual-Engine Cooperation

Compared with the M4, a principal architectural change in the M6 is the integration of two complete ANEs on the same die (Section 4.3). Chapters 5 and 6 have already described two mechanisms by which the second ANE is used. Within a single request, the compiler splits part of a subgraph across the two ANEs and generates the corresponding dual-ANE program (Section 5.8). Across multiple concurrent requests, the driver assigns requests to the two engines, and for dual-ANE programs that have already been generated, the runtime executes the work assigned to ANE1 (Sections 6.3.2 and 6.7). This chapter does not revisit these mechanisms themselves. Instead, it examines the practical performance gains they produce, focusing on two questions: how much speedup the second ANE can provide, and why the practical speedup falls short of the ideal factor of two. To this end, we first derive the expected performance from the mechanisms described above (Section 10.2). We then verify each expectation experimentally, and we use the compilation, scheduling and hardware structure described earlier to explain the differences between prediction and measurement.

This chapter reaches four main conclusions. First, for compute-bound networks, the performance of dual-ANE programs broadly matches expectations: their marginal throughput reaches 1.92× that of a single ANE, and the gap from the ideal factor of two mainly corresponds to a dual-engine cooperation overhead of about 0.14 µs per layer. Second, for weight-read-bound networks, the practical performance deviates most clearly from the ideal case. The two ANEs share a weight-read bandwidth of about 150 GB/s, and the compiler's cost model does not account for this limit. When row split is used, the two engines must each read a complete copy of the weights. As a result, convolution chains whose layers use different weights obtain only about 1.4× speedup, while rewritten kernels using row split show almost no speedup. Third, the compiler assigns work to the two ANEs in units of complete tiles and cannot split an individual tile further. When the number of tiles is odd, the resulting load imbalance increases execution time by 12.5–19% relative to an ideal even split, and it reduces throughput by 11–16%. Fourth, the benefit of request-level concurrency likewise depends on where the bottleneck lies. For weight-resident models, the two engines deliver throughput close to twice that of one engine. For weight-read-bound models, total throughput increases by only about 1.4×, and this gain comes mainly from filling host-side idle time between synchronous calls; the second engine itself contributes only about 4% additional processing capacity. The term "weight-read-bound" in this chapter covers two cases. In the first, native operators are limited by the DRAM bandwidth for weight reads. In the second, rewritten kernels are limited by the weight data path shared by the two engines (Section 7.4.2).

### 10.1 Method

This chapter uses the interrupt counts reported by IOReport to determine which ANEs actually participate in executing each call. The calibration method is described in Section 6.1 (tool: [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m)) 〔IOReport〕. For experiments that require the execution duration of each engine, we use the task start and end events of kdebug. When the CPU column of these events is 36, they denote ANE0; when it is 33, they denote ANE1 (Table 6-1) 〔kdebug〕.

A methodological limitation of this chapter is that we cannot execute the same model separately in single-ANE and dual-ANE form. The program set that runs is determined by the runtime, and Core ML provides no option for selecting it. We therefore compare two models of similar shape that lie on either side of the compiler's splitting threshold. Specifically, we fix the number of channels and the layer structure and vary only the width. For a 1×1 convolution chain (512 channels, height 1) with width 128, the dual-ANE program has only a single sequence on ANE0. When the width is 256, the compiler splits each layer into two halves along the width, so that each ANE is responsible for exactly 128 columns. The total work is then identical to that of the single-ANE model with width 128 (tool: [bond_sweep.py](../tools/08_bonded/bond_sweep.py); data: [bond_wsweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_wsweep_run.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕. In this way, the work of each engine is the same in both models, so the difference in execution time reflects the overhead of dual-engine cooperation. We measure the throughput of both models with the slope method (Section 7.1), using 600–1000 calls per point and two measurement rounds, which differ by less than 1%.

### 10.2 Expected Speedup

The expectations examined in this chapter follow from the mechanisms of Chapters 5 and 6. At the program level, the compiler decides whether to split according to "latency after splitting = (Σ block latencies + Σ copy-and-merge latencies) × 0.5" (Section 5.8.3). In other words, the latency of the split subgraph is expected to be halved. At runtime, the work of ANE1 is executed whenever a dual-ANE program contains it (Section 6.3.2). Consequently, once the compiler decides to split, this expectation is either fulfilled or unfulfilled on every request. At the request level, the driver assigns concurrently in-flight requests to the two engines (Sections 6.3.2 and 6.7). If the two engines do not interfere with each other, their combined throughput should be twice that of a single engine.

Both expectations rest on several implicit assumptions. This chapter tests four of these five assumptions. The fourth is left open because no control model is available. First, the coordination overhead of the two engines is negligible (Section 10.3). Second, the two engines do not contend for shared resources. Third, the two engines receive equal workloads, whereas the cost model considers only the sum of block latencies and not the largest block (Section 10.5). Fourth, the cost of synchronization is small when data must be exchanged (Section 10.6). Fifth, the gain from request-level parallelism is attributed entirely to the second engine; however, under synchronous calls, a single engine already spends about 23% of its time waiting for the host (Section 6.5), so these two components must be separated (Section 10.7).

### 10.3 Scaling Efficiency When Compute-Bound

This section examines the first assumption. If coordination incurs no overhead, the marginal throughput of a dual-ANE program should be twice that of a single ANE. Table 10-1 compares the latency of 1×1 convolution chains with width 128 (single ANE) and width 256 (dual ANE) at several layer counts. The layers in both groups of models share the same weights, which are resident on chip, so throughput is limited only by computation (data: [bond_ksweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_ksweep_run.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 10-1. Latency of single-ANE and dual-ANE programs (512-channel 1×1 convolution chain, shared weights, median)*

| Layers | Single ANE (width 128) | Dual ANE (width 256) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 16 | 229 µs | 227 µs |
| 48 | 330 µs | 388 µs |
| 128 | 718 µs | 806 µs |
| 256 | 1178 µs | 1234 µs |
| 384 | 1595 µs | 1669 µs |

In the range of 256 to 384 layers, where the chain is longest and most linear, the marginal latency of a single ANE is 3.26 µs per layer, corresponding to 20.6 TFLOPS. The dual ANE takes 3.40 µs per layer. Because each layer of the dual-ANE model performs twice the computation of the single-ANE model, this corresponds to 39.5 TFLOPS, which is 1.92× that of a single ANE. The speedup is therefore 1.92, and the scaling efficiency of the two engines (speedup ÷ number of engines) is about 96%. The dual ANE takes 0.14 µs (about 4%) more per layer than the single ANE. The work of each engine is the same in the two groups of models, so this portion represents extra time incurred by the cooperation of the two engines. It is simply the difference between the marginal latencies of the two groups, and this report does not decompose it further. After the convolution chain is split by width, the two engines need not exchange data, and the TD sequence contains no cross-ANE sync records (Sections 5.8.2 and 10.6). This time therefore should not be attributed to inter-layer synchronization. Possible sources include contention between the two engines for shared resources, and a difference in NE DVFS state between the two programs (3.40 ÷ 3.26 ≈ 1.04; a frequency difference of about 4% would suffice to explain it) 〔timing, inferred〕. The firmware timestamps in Section 6.5.1 also indicate that a dual-ANE program spends about 30 µs more per call outside tasks than a single-ANE program, which we speculate is used for startup and completion synchronization of the two engines. Both of these overheads are small. Consequently, on compute-bound networks, dual-ANE programs achieve a speedup close to the ideal factor of two. Figure 10-1 shows how the latency of single-ANE and dual-ANE programs varies with the number of layers.

![Latency of single-ANE and dual-ANE programs](../figs/en/light/fig10-1_bonded_scaling.png)

*Figure 10-1. Time per call as a function of the number of layers for a single-ANE program (width 128) and a dual-ANE program (width 256), each a 512-channel 1×1 convolution chain with shared weights. The dashed line is the extrapolation from the slope between the 256- and 384-layer points. The dual-ANE layer performs twice the computation of the single-ANE layer, yet the latency increases by only 0.14 µs per layer; the speedup computed from the ratio of marginal throughputs is 1.92 (data: [bond_ksweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_ksweep_run.txt)).*

At small layer counts, the latency differences between the two groups of models in Table 10-1 cannot be directly attributed to cooperation overhead. For example, at 16 layers the latencies of the dual ANE and the single ANE are nearly identical, whereas at 48 layers the dual ANE is 58 µs slower. In this range, each call takes only a few hundred microseconds, and the host wake-up overhead described in Section 6.5 is in a regime that varies with task duration. Because the task durations of the two groups differ, this overhead is mixed into the latency differences. Therefore, this section relies only on the slopes above 256 layers as the basis for the scaling efficiency.

### 10.4 Scaling Efficiency When Weight-Read-Bound

The compiler's cost model expects latency to be halved after splitting, but it does not account for the weight-read stage that the two ANEs share (Section 5.8.3). Section 9.3.4 notes that a single ANE already reaches about 90% of the DRAM bandwidth when reading weights. Even if the second engine has an independent path, it can obtain only a very small share of the remaining bandwidth. Moreover, with row split, each of the two ANEs reads a complete copy of the weights, so the weight traffic doubles. The two groups of experiments in this section test this inference separately on rewritten kernels and on native kernels. Accordingly, when a network is limited by weight reads, we expect the computing capability of the second engine to translate into speedup only to a very limited extent, and this is especially true under row split.

The long-strip convolution experiment in Section 7.4.2 provides the most direct evidence. On a 1×9 convolution chain of 256 channels with a 16×32 shape, the compiler chooses row split, with each ANE responsible for 8 rows and all 256 output channels. Firmware timestamps show that the tasks of the two ANEs start and end at the same time and have equal busy durations. However, the latency per layer is 33.0 µs, which is exactly twice the latency of a single ANE processing the same number of rows. The prediction calculated as "two copies of the weights ÷ 150 GB/s" is 33.7 µs, which differs from the measurement by about 2%. In other words, the two engines run fully in parallel, but the overall throughput is identical to that of a single ANE, so the second engine provides no speedup at all. According to the analysis in Section 7.4.2, the two engines here share a bottleneck of the rewritten kernel that is measured in weight elements. For native kernels, the sharing instead arises from the DRAM bandwidth ceiling (Section 9.3.4) (data: [ks4_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_fit.txt); tool: [ks4_engines.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_engines.py)) 〔kdebug, HWX〕. Convolutions of 1×12 to 1×14 at the same spatial size are split by output channel, so each ANE reads only half of the weights; the latency per layer is 24.9–30.4 µs, corresponding to an efficiency of 73–77% relative to the direct-convolution peak of the dual ANE. For the 32×32 shape, increasing the number of rows roughly balances computation against weight reads, and the efficiency of row split recovers to 86–89% (for all shapes from 1×9 to 1×15, Table 7-7 lists only some of them; data: [ks4_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_fit.txt)). We record this phenomenon as defect B9 (Appendix D).

This trend also appears in conventional convolution chains. We changed the models of Section 10.3 so that each layer uses different weights (0.5 MB per layer). Between 48 and 128 layers, the marginal latency per layer of a single ANE changes only slightly, from 4.85 µs with shared weights to 5.1 µs. For the dual ANE, it increases from 5.2 µs to 7.3 µs (data: [bond_dsweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_dsweep_run.txt)) 〔timing〕. In dual-ANE mode, the two engines each read 0.5 MB, for a total of 1 MB per layer. Reading this amount in 7.3 µs corresponds to about 140 GB/s, which is already close to the upper limit of the weight-read bandwidth. Normalized by the computation per layer, the speedup of the dual ANE relative to the single ANE falls from about 1.9× with shared weights to about 1.4×. The timing slope in this range may include host wake-up overhead, so the specific multiples are for reference only. The direction of the conclusion, however, agrees with the firmware-timestamp results in the preceding paragraph.

These results indicate that, for workloads limited by weight reads, such as the decode stage of large language models, the benefit of using two ANEs is limited. If the compiler chooses row split, each of the two ANEs reads a copy of the weights, so the second engine not only fails to provide speedup but also consumes additional DRAM bandwidth. For such workloads, a better choice for the compiler is to split by output channel, so that each ANE reads only half of the weights, or simply not to split at all, which saves the power of the second engine (Chapter 13 measures that the second engine adds about 4.6 W at full load). Because users cannot control the compiler's splitting decisions, this improvement can only be implemented by the compiler.

### 10.5 Work Partitioning with Tiling

The compiler's cost model evaluates a split by summing the latencies of the computation blocks after splitting, and it does not consider the critical path determined by the largest block (Section 5.8.3). That is, it assumes by default that the two engines receive equal amounts of work. Section 9.2.2 notes that when an intermediate tensor exceeds 2 MiB, the compiler divides the tensor into several tiles, and each tile executes the complete chain in succession. In dual-ANE programs, this tiling scheme interacts with the division of work between the two engines. This interaction invalidates the default assumption and produces a measurable loss.

We measured the task durations of the two ANEs separately with kdebug. The model is a 1×1 convolution chain with 256 channels and height 1, in which all layers share weights, with widths from 6144 to 16384. Table 10-2 gives the results for 40 layers (tools: [asym_ktrace.sh](../tools/08_bonded/asym_ktrace.sh), [asym_split.py](../tools/08_bonded/asym_split.py); data: [asym_kt/](../data/08_bonded/asym_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 10-2. Task durations of the two ANEs under tiling (256-channel 1×1 convolution chain, 40 layers; the median is reported separately for each column)*

| Width | ANE0 task | ANE1 task | ANE1 ÷ ANE0 | ANE1 finishes later than ANE0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 6144 | 845 µs | 846 µs | 1.00 | −0.5 µs |
| 8192 | 913 µs | 1341 µs | 1.47 | 427 µs |
| 12288 | 1478 µs | 1903 µs | 1.29 | 420 µs |
| 16384 | 2041 µs | 2466 µs | 1.21 | 421 µs |

The task start times of the two ANEs are essentially the same. However, once the width reaches 8192, the ANE1 task becomes clearly longer than the ANE0 task, and the total call time is determined by ANE1. To identify the cause, we used [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py) to count, for an 8-layer model, the number of TDs and the number of columns processed per layer by each engine. The results are shown in Table 10-3 (data: [align_hwx.txt](../data/07_memory/align_hwx.txt)) 〔HWX〕.

*Table 10-3. Workload assigned to the two ANEs (8-layer model)*

| Model | ANE0 TDs | ANE1 TDs | Total column count ÷ 8 (ANE0 / ANE1) | Column-count ratio | Measured time ratio |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 256 channels, width 6144 | 28 | 28 | 7680 / 7680 | 1.00 | 1.00 |
| 256 channels, width 8192 | 28 | 42 | 8320 / 12160 | 1.46 | 1.41–1.47 |
| 256 channels, width 12288 | 42 | 56 | 13440 / 17280 | 1.29 | 1.28–1.29 |
| 128 channels, width 24576 | 42 | 56 | 26880 / 34560 | 1.29 | 1.23–1.29 |

The "total column count ÷ 8" in the table is the sum of the numbers of columns processed by all TDs of each engine, divided by the number of layers. Because some TDs process the columns of two tiles at once (for example, 3328 = 2 × 1664), this value does not equal the width of each layer. It is used only to compare the relative workloads of the two engines. The column counts assigned to the two ANEs sum to the total of the single-ANE program, but the distribution is unbalanced. At width 12288, ANE0 and ANE1 carry about 44% and 56%, respectively, and at width 8192 they carry about 41% and 59%. The ratio of column counts agrees with the measured time ratio, so the asymmetry in time is fully explained by the asymmetry in workload.

The distribution of tile widths within each engine further reveals the source of the load imbalance. The dual-ANE program reuses the tiling grid of the single-ANE program and divides the tile list into two groups only at tile boundaries. Taking width 12288 as an example, the tile-width distribution of the single-ANE program equals the sum of the tile-width distributions of ANE0 and ANE1. When the number of tiles in a layer is odd, the tile list cannot be divided evenly, and the extra tile is always assigned to ANE1. The corresponding rule in the compiler assigns tile i to the ANE numbered (i ≥ T/2) (Section 5.8.3). Table 10-4 lists the split and the assignment for each layer at each width 〔HWX〕. Figure 10-2 shows the tile assignment of the dual-ANE program.

![Tile assignment of the dual-ANE program](../figs/en/light/fig10-2_split.png)

*Figure 10-2. Tile assignment of the dual-ANE program (256-channel 1×1 convolution chain). The dual-ANE program reuses the tiling grid of the single-ANE program and assigns whole tiles to the two ANEs; when the number of tiles is odd, the extra tile always goes to ANE1. The numbers in the boxes are tile widths (columns); the positions of the tiles in the tensor are schematic.*

*Table 10-4. Tiling of each layer and assignment to the two ANEs*

| Width | Split per layer | Tiles | ANE0 / ANE1 | Predicted time ratio | Measured |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 6144 | 4 × 1536 | 4 | 2 / 2 tiles | 1.00 | 1.00 |
| 8192 | 1536 + 4 × 1664 | 5 | 2 / 3 tiles | 1.46 | 1.41–1.47 |
| 10752 | 6 × 1792 | 6 | 3 / 3 tiles | 1.00 | TD counts equal on both sides |
| 12288 | 2 × 1664 + 5 × 1792 | 7 | 3 / 4 tiles | 1.29 | 1.29 |
| 14336 | 8 × 1792 | 8 | 4 / 4 tiles | 1.00 | TD counts equal on both sides |

The assignment rule above implies that the parity of the number of tiles directly affects the load balance of the two ANEs. An even number of tiles can be divided equally, whereas an odd number necessarily gives one engine an additional computation tile. Measurements confirm this prediction. At width 14336 (8 tiles), the amount of computation is 17% larger than at width 12288 (7 tiles), yet the latency per layer is 2% smaller (49.3 µs versus 50.3 µs), so the throughput is about 19% higher (data: [bsplit_even.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsplit_even.txt)) 〔timing〕. Ideally, the latency of a dual ANE program equals half of the total workload. The actual latency, however, is determined by the share of ANE1. At width 12288, ANE1 carries 56.25% of the columns, so the latency is 12.5% longer than an even split, and the corresponding throughput is 1 ÷ 1.125 of the ideal value, that is, about 11% lower. At width 8192, ANE1 carries 59.4% of the columns, so the latency is about 19% longer than an even split, and the throughput is about 16% lower. Neither of the compilation options `DisableNetworkImbalanceAnalysis` and `EnableForcedMaximalBondedSplit` changes this partition (data: [bsplit_flags.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsplit_flags.txt)). We record this problem as defect B8 (Appendix D). A more extreme example appears in the 2048-channel, 32×32 W8A8 1×1 convolution chain, where the compiler divides the work between the two ANEs at about 1 : 2. As a result, the speedup of W8A8 relative to FP16 falls from 1.76–1.87× on balanced shapes to 1.21× (Table 7-2).

### 10.6 Data Exchange Between the Two Engines

Different operators have different cross-engine communication requirements after dual-ANE splitting. For pointwise convolution chains split by row, each engine can execute independently and need not exchange intermediate data. In contrast, operations such as 3×3 convolution, reduction along the height dimension, and softmax require data exchange across engines. For this purpose, dual-ANE programs use a scratch buffer shared by the two ANEs and cross-ANE sync records. For example, each ANE of a three-layer 3×3 convolution synchronizes once before layers 2 and 3 begin (Section 5.8.2).

This report does not directly measure the cost of this data exchange. The reason is that, for operations that require data exchange, we could not find a control model with the same workload that does not require exchange. Moreover, these operations are split differently in dual-ANE programs than in single-ANE programs (for example, after a 3×3 convolution is split, each ANE must read one additional row of overlapping input), so the latency difference cannot be attributed to synchronization alone. In the convolution chain of Section 10.3, which does not require data exchange, the cost is only about 0.14 µs per layer. However, that figure is the difference between the marginal latencies of two groups of models. It does not isolate synchronization itself, and it cannot be generalized to operations that require data exchange. Therefore, it cannot serve as a lower bound on the cost of data exchange.

### 10.7 Comparison of Two Parallel Modes

Section 6.7 shows that the driver assigns requests from different processes and different programs to the two engines. According to the expectation in Section 10.2, if the two engines do not interfere with each other, their combined throughput should be twice that of a single engine. The 1.78–1.81× measured in Section 6.7 is close to this expectation, but it was obtained with 1×1 convolution chains whose weights are resident on chip, a workload that reads almost no weights from DRAM. To determine whether request-level concurrency can circumvent the shared weight-read bandwidth described in Section 9.3.4, we followed the method of Section 6.7. We invoked a weight-read-bound model simultaneously in two processes and recorded the task times of the two engines with kdebug (tools: [d2_wbound.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d2_wbound.sh), [d2_wbound_fit.py](../tools/04_schedule/d2_wbound_fit.py); data: [d2_wbound.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_wbound.txt)). The test model is an 80-layer chain of 1×9 convolutions with 256 channels, with input shapes of 4×32 or 8×32. Neither shape is split across the two ANEs (Section 5.8.4), and each layer re-reads 2.54 MB of weights within one TD. Strictly speaking, this rewritten kernel is limited by a stage shared by the two engines that is measured in weight elements rather than by DRAM byte bandwidth (Section 7.4.2). However, like native layers limited by DRAM weight reads, its combined rate does not increase when the two engines run simultaneously, so this chapter refers to both collectively as weight-read-bound. The control group is the original model from Section 6.7. The results are shown in Table 10-5 〔timing, kdebug, IOReport〕. The requests of the two processes were indeed assigned to the two engines: each engine had about two interrupts per call, and the results were identical whether the two processes used the same model file or separate model files. The combined throughput of the control group is 1.89×, which reproduces the result of Section 6.7 (slightly higher than 1.78×, mainly because the single-process latency was longer in this round). The two weight-read-bound models achieve only 1.36×. The results for 8×32 are almost identical to those for 4×32, which indicates that at this number of layers both are completely limited by weight reads, rather than being in an intermediate regime in which computation and weight reads each account for about half.

*Table 10-5. Weight-read-bound models invoked simultaneously from two processes*

| Model | Per-call time, single process | Per-call time, two processes | Combined throughput | Single task duration (single process → two processes) | Average rate converted from FP16 weight bytes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×9, 4×32, 80 layers | 1821 µs | 2686 / 2678 µs | 1.36× | 1391 → 2103 µs | 111 → 151 GB/s |
| 1×9, 8×32, 80 layers | 1826 µs | 2682 / 2683 µs | 1.36× | 1397 → 2104 µs | 111 → 151 GB/s |
| Control: 1×1, 512 channels, 1×128, 384 layers (weight-resident) | 1749 µs | 1853 / 1851 µs | 1.89× | 1326 → 1334 µs | — |

The kdebug task times explain the origin of the 1.36×. The rates below are all converted from FP16 weight bytes. Section 7.4.2 shows that the bottleneck of the 1×9 rewritten kernel is actually a shared stage measured in weight elements rather than DRAM byte bandwidth, so these figures reflect the rate of that stage. When a single engine runs alone, the rate of each task, converted from 2.54 MB × 80 layers, is about 145 GB/s. When the tasks of the two engines overlap, each engine achieves only about 76–78 GB/s, for a combined rate of about 150–156 GB/s. This is only about 3–8% higher than a single engine running alone, so the duration of each individual task increases from about 1390 µs to about 2100 µs. The combined throughput remains above 1.0× because, in single-process operation, the ANE spends about 23% of its time waiting for the host to submit the next request (Section 6.5). The two processes fill these gaps for each other, and the time during which both engines are idle drops to 0–2%. The average read bandwidth converted from weights therefore rises from 111 GB/s to 151 GB/s, and this ratio of 1.36 matches the combined throughput exactly 〔kdebug, inferred〕. In other words, the gain comes mainly from eliminating host-side idle time, not from additional processing capacity provided by the second engine. For native layers limited by DRAM weight-read bandwidth, we would expect the same outcome, because a single engine is already close to the upper limit of DRAM bandwidth (Section 9.3.4) 〔inferred〕. For the control group, whose weights are resident on chip, the duration of each task barely changes while the two engines run simultaneously, so the combined throughput approaches 2×.

Because the gain stems from eliminating host idle time, keeping multiple requests in flight within a single process should produce the same effect. To verify this, we used [asyncrun.m](../tools/common/asyncrun.m) to invoke the same `MLModel` object in one process in three ways: synchronous calls one at a time; asynchronous calls through `predictionFromFeatures:completionHandler:` with K requests kept in flight; and `predictionsFromBatch:`, which submits B inputs at a time. Each mode ran continuously for 8 s, two rounds were measured, and the tasks of the two engines were recorded with kdebug (tools: [d2_async.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d2_async.sh), [d2_async_fit.py](../tools/04_schedule/d2_async_fit.py); data: [d2_async.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_async.txt)). Table 10-6 lists the main results 〔timing, kdebug, IOReport〕.

*Table 10-6. Throughput of different submission modes within a single process (range over two rounds)*

| Model | Submission mode | Throughput (relative to synchronous) | Busy fraction of the two engines | Single task duration |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1×9, 4×32, 80 layers (weight-read-bound) | Synchronous | 1.00 (521–526 calls/s) | 77–78% / 0% | 1396–1398 µs |
| | Asynchronous, 2 in flight | 1.40–1.41 | 79% / 79% | about 2115 µs |
| | Asynchronous, 4 in flight | 1.41–1.42 | 100% / 100% | 2660–2671 µs |
| | Batched, 8 per batch | 1.35–1.37 | 83–85% / 84–85% | about 2660 µs |
| | Two processes, 2 asynchronous calls in flight each | 1.41–1.42 | 100% / 100% | 2667–2674 µs |
| 1×1, 512 channels, 384 layers (weight-resident) | Synchronous | 1.00 (523 calls/s) | 77% / 0% | 1326 µs |
| | Asynchronous, 2 in flight | 2.05–2.06 | 72% / 72% | about 1333 µs |
| | Asynchronous, 4 in flight | 2.82–2.83 | 100% / 100% | 1328–1338 µs |
| | Batched, 8 per batch | 2.46 | 76–78% / 78% | about 1330 µs |
| | Two processes, 2 asynchronous calls in flight each | 2.82 | 100% / 100% | 1330–1342 µs |

Figure 10-3 plots the throughput of the two models under seven submission modes together with the busy fractions of the two engines.

![Throughput of different submission modes within a single process](../figs/en/light/fig10-3_submit_modes.png)

*Figure 10-3. Throughput of different submission modes within a single process (relative to synchronous calls; the average of two rounds, which differ by no more than 0.05×). (a) Weight-resident 1×1 convolution chain; (b) weight-read-bound rewritten 1×9 kernel. The second line beside each bar gives the busy fractions of ANE0 / ANE1. With synchronous calls, only ANE0 works, and it waits for the host for about 23% of the time. Once two or more requests are kept in flight, both engines begin to work. For weight-resident models, throughput reaches about 2.8× that of synchronous calls; for weight-read-bound models, it reaches only about 1.4×, and both engines are already 100% busy, so the gain comes almost entirely from eliminating host-side idle time (data: [d2_async.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_async.txt)).*

Table 10-6 raises three points. First, under asynchronous calls, the requests for the same `MLModel` object in the same process are assigned to both engines. As long as two or more requests are in flight, both engines have tasks. This differs from the result of Section 6.7.2, in which requests from multiple threads that synchronously call the same model are serialized. This indicates that the serialization there occurs on the synchronous-call path (defect B3). Second, for weight-read-bound models, the saturated throughput of single-process asynchronous calls is identical to that of two processes (about 742 calls per second, or about 150 GB/s when converted from FP16 weight bytes), so adding a second process does not make it faster. At saturation, both engines are 100% busy, and each receives only about half of the rate (about 76 GB/s). The duration of each single task therefore increases from 1.40 ms to 2.66 ms. Compared with about 145 GB/s for a single engine running alone, the two engines together add only about 4% (3–8% in the two-process experiment of Table 10-5). If one engine ran alone with no host idle time, the throughput would be about 1.37× that of synchronous calls, and the measured value is 1.41×. Therefore, the gain of about 1.4× comes almost entirely from eliminating host idle time 〔kdebug, inferred〕. The synchronous baseline of this group (521–526 calls/s) is about 5% slower than in the experiment of Table 10-5, and synchronous throughput fluctuates by 5–8% across time periods, whereas the absolute saturated throughput is identical each time. The 1.41× here is therefore not inconsistent with the 1.36× of Table 10-5.

Third, for weight-resident models, single-process asynchronous calls with 4 requests in flight reach 2.82–2.83× the throughput of synchronous calls. If task execution speed were unchanged, two fully loaded engines relative to one engine that is 77% busy would yield only 2 ÷ 0.77 ≈ 2.60×, and the surplus comes from the fact that tasks are themselves slower under synchronous calls. The task durations in Table 10-6 are medians, whereas the busy fraction is the ratio of the sum of task durations to the time window, which is equivalent to using means. Following the pairing method of [d2_async_fit.py](../tools/04_schedule/d2_async_fit.py), we recomputed the statistics from the same kdebug records and the same time window (about 7.5 s per segment after discarding the first 1.5 s). Under synchronous calls, the number of tasks corresponds to 524 per second, consistent with the 523 measured by asyncrun. The median task duration is 1326 µs, but the mean is 1472 µs, and the 90th percentile reaches 1887 µs, meaning that about one tenth of the tasks are noticeably slower. With 4 asynchronous calls in flight, the mean is 1353 µs, close to the median of 1329–1336 µs. Thus 2.83 ≈ (2 ÷ 0.77) × (1472 ÷ 1353) ≈ 2.60 × 1.09, where the two factors correspond, respectively, to the elimination of host idle time and to the speed-up of individual tasks 〔kdebug〕.

Weight-read-bound models also satisfy the same relation. Under synchronous calls, the mean task duration is 1473 µs and the median is 1396 µs. Under saturated asynchronous calls, the mean is about 2695 µs. (2 ÷ 0.77) × (1473 ÷ 2695) ≈ 1.42, which agrees with the measured 1.41–1.42×.

The shortening of individual task durations under asynchronous concurrency can be explained by the frequency-scaling mechanism of the CLPC analyzed in Chapter 12. The statement that the CLPC stabilizes the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77% presupposes that host-induced idle time exists between tasks. Tasks that are slower under synchronous calls most likely run at a lower DVFS state. Once two or more requests are kept in flight, tasks run back to back. The mean and the median then differ by less than 2%, which indicates that almost all tasks run at the same DVFS state 〔kdebug, inferred〕.

Batched submission is less effective than asynchronous submission. With 8 per batch, it achieves only 2.46×, and measured by the sum of task durations, the two engines still have about 20% idle time. In batched submission, about one tenth of the tasks lack the firmware start event, so the paired task durations are biased toward shorter values, and this proportion may be too high 〔timing, kdebug〕.

Table 10-7 summarizes the ways of using two ANEs and compares the measured results with the expectations of Section 10.2.

*Table 10-7. Ways of using two ANEs: expectation, measurement and gap*

| Approach | Condition | Expectation | Measurement | Main cause of the gap |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dual-ANE program, compute-bound | Compiler decides to split; spatial dimensions are large enough | 2× | 1.92× (marginal; scaling efficiency about 96%) | Cooperation time of about 0.14 µs per layer (Section 10.3) |
| Dual-ANE program, weight-read-bound and row-split (rewritten kernel) | Same as above | 2× | About 1.0× | The two engines share a rewritten-kernel stage measured in weight elements, and each reads a copy of the weights (Sections 7.4.2 and 10.4) |
| Dual-ANE program, weight-read-bound (native kernel, different weights per layer) | Same as above | 2× | About 1.4× (timing slope, including host-side effects) | The two engines share the weight-read path (Sections 9.3.4 and 10.4) |
| Dual-ANE program, data exchange required | Same as above | Synchronization cost is small | Not measured | No control model with equal workload (Section 10.6) |
| Dual-ANE program, odd number of tiles | Intermediate tensor exceeds 2 MiB | 2× | 11–16% below the ideal value | Whole-tile assignment; ANE1 receives an extra tile (Section 10.5) |
| Two processes running the same model, weight-resident | Driver assigns requests to two ANEs | 2× | 1.78–1.89× | About 6–12% longer per call (across rounds); not decomposed in this report (Section 6.7.1; Table 10-5) |
| Two processes running the same model, weight-read-bound | Same as above | 2× | 1.36× | The two engines together still reach about 150 GB/s; the gain comes almost entirely from filling host idle time |
| Two different programs in the same process | Same as above | Sum of the two running alone | 86% of the sum of the two running alone | Each call about 13% slower; not decomposed in this report (Section 6.7.2) |
| Multithreaded synchronous calls to the same program in one process | Requests serialized on the synchronous-call path | — | 1.0× | Serialization (Section 6.7.2; defect B3) |
| Asynchronous calls to the same program in one process, 2 or more in flight | Requests assigned to both ANEs | About 2.6× relative to synchronous (2 ÷ 0.77) | Weight-resident 2.8×; weight-read-bound 1.4× | Weight-resident: some tasks are slower under synchronous calls; weight-read-bound: the weight stage shared by the two engines (rewritten kernel) |
| Mixed dual-ANE and single-ANE programs | Requests for single-ANE programs fill idle time | — | 25–30% higher than running the two separately | — |

In the "measurement" column of the table, values are throughput relative to a single engine unless otherwise noted. The asynchronous-call row is relative to synchronous calls (data: Section 6.7 and this chapter) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

Dual-ANE programs and request-level parallelism serve two different optimization goals: the latency of a single call and the overall throughput. Dual-ANE programs suit situations in which a single request is latency-sensitive and the network computation is compute-bound, such as convolutional network inference on a single image. They can shorten the computation time of a single call by nearly half, but users cannot control whether the compiler splits, and weight-read-bound networks obtain only limited speedup. Request-level parallelism suits situations in which throughput matters, such as processing multiple inputs at the same time. It does not depend on the compiler. It can be achieved by keeping several requests in flight asynchronously within a single process, without requiring multiple processes, but it does not reduce the latency of a single call. For weight-read-bound networks, request-level parallelism faces the same limitation as dual-ANE programs. Tables 10-5 and 10-6 show that, when the two engines read weights simultaneously, the combined rate remains about 150 GB/s, and the gain of about 1.4× comes almost entirely from eliminating host-side idle time.

### 10.8 Comparison with the Literature

Without access to M6 hardware, maderix [4] estimated, from public information such as dual 16-core engines and a memory bandwidth of 170 GB/s, that the M6 has a peak compute capability of 38 TFLOPS (FP16) and 76 TOPS (W8A8). maderix also raised three questions that could not yet be confirmed: whether the two engines share SRAM, whether one inference requires one or two dispatches, and whether a single compute graph can execute across the two engines. The experiments in this chapter provide further measured results for these estimates and questions. In terms of performance, the theoretical direct-convolution peak of the two engines combined is 42.3 TFLOPS (FP16) and 84.5 TOPS (INT8), both higher than maderix's estimates. This difference may arise mainly from the NE operating frequency of about 2.58 GHz measured in this report 〔inferred〕. The measured marginal throughput for FP16 is 39.5 TFLOPS. In terms of storage, each of the two engines has its own 2 MiB L2, allocated independently by the compiler, whereas data exchange across engines uses a shared scratch buffer allocated by the runtime. In terms of scheduling, the compiler can partition a single compute graph across the two engines and generate the corresponding dual-ANE program. When such a program is executed once, the runtime starts ANE0 and ANE1 once each. Therefore, a single compute graph can execute across the two engines, but its cross-engine partition is determined at compilation time.

Bryngelson [5] speculated, based on the kernel driver of an earlier product, that multi-die products dispatch requests to the least busy die in units of whole submissions, and that tensors are not exchanged between dies. The compiler contains an implementation of collective communication, but it is not enabled on any target. The M6 differs from this picture. Its two ANEs are on the same die, and they cooperate within a single submission, exchanging data, through dual-ANE programs. Meanwhile, the driver also dispatches requests for different programs to different engines in units of whole submissions (Section 6.3.2). In other words, the M6 combines the request-level dispatch described by Bryngelson with a new program-level cooperation.

### 10.9 Summary

This chapter tested by measurement the dual-engine mechanisms of Chapters 5 and 6. Ideally, both ways of using the two engines should double performance: the compiler's cost model assumes that latency is halved after splitting, and request-level parallelism expects the two engines to double the combined throughput. The gap between measurement and expectation depends on the workload. On compute-bound networks, the marginal throughput of a dual-ANE program is 1.92× that of a single engine, a gap of only about 4%, corresponding to a cooperation time of about 0.14 µs per layer. Request-level parallelism also approaches a twofold gain on weight-resident models, and asynchronous calls within a single process reach 2.8× the throughput of synchronous calls when four requests are kept in flight. The largest gap occurs for weight-read-bound networks. The two ANEs share a weight-read bandwidth of about 150 GB/s, which the compiler's cost model does not account for, and under row split each reads a copy of the weights. As a result, convolution chains whose layers use different weights achieve only about 1.4×, and rewritten kernels using row split show no speedup. Request-level parallelism on these models is likewise limited to about 1.4×, and its gain comes almost entirely from eliminating host-side idle time. Another gap arises from work partitioning. The compiler reuses the tiling grid of the single-engine program and assigns whole tiles. When the number of tiles is odd, ANE1 receives an extra tile, which lengthens the execution time by 12.5–19% relative to an even split and reduces the throughput by 11–16% relative to the ideal. Overall, these gaps all stem from the same kind of simplification in the mechanisms. The compiler treats the two engines as resources that do not interfere with each other and have equal workloads, whereas in fact they share the weight-read path, and the workloads assigned to them are not necessarily equal.

This chapter, together with Chapters 5 and 6, completes the argument for the dual engines. Chapter 5 explains how compilation arranges the work, Chapter 6 explains how execution proceeds at runtime, and this chapter gives the actual gains and their differences from expectation. Questions that remain are the synchronization cost of operations that require data exchange, and whether the approximately 0.14 µs per layer of cooperation time stems from resource contention or from differences in NE DVFS state. The former requires a control model with equal workload but a different number of exchanges, and the latter requires reading out the NE DVFS states under the two programs (Section 16.2). The power cost of the second engine is covered in Chapter 13. Deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are concentrated in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 11. Clock Domains

The preceding discussion relied on two assumptions. Chapter 7 converted peak throughput using the full-load clock of 2.58 GHz, and Section 7.6 relied on the conclusion that the PE clock is independent of the NE clock. This chapter provides the justification for both. It examines how many independent clock domains the ANE has, which DVFS states each domain has, what frequency each actually runs at under full load, and whether the frequencies assumed by the compiler agree with the hardware. The system provides no readout of the ANE's "current frequency," so we can infer frequency only from changes in task duration. The main conclusions of this chapter are four. First, the NE clock of each ANE has 32 DVFS states, with an operating range of 852–2580 MHz, and it runs at 2580 MHz under full load, rather than at the top state of 2508 MHz in the compiler's frequency table. Second, the PE sits in an independently scheduled clock domain. It ramps up slowly, and it is repeatedly pushed back to low states when the GPU is active simultaneously. If the compiler's mapping table is followed, the PE runs at about 1.8–1.9 GHz under full load, although this estimate is not independent (Section 11.3.2). We speculate that this clock domain is the shared cluster used by both ANEs, and that the L2 is likely located within it. Third, we infer that the five states of the table that the compiler calls the "DMA frequency table" actually describe the frequencies of the SoC interconnect. The ANE has no voltage domain of its own. Instead, it votes on the SoC voltage domain to obtain the voltage it requires. Fourth, the DRAM runs at its top state of 10656 MT/s when the ANE is at full load.

### 11.1 Measurement Method

The ANE clock frequency has no directly readable system interface, so indirect methods are required. We examined all possible sources. The ANE driver node in the IORegistry records only the power state. Among the 8288 channels of IOReport, the memory controller, the CPU and the GPU all report residency times for each state, whereas the ANE reports only the PLL switching, the total time its clock is enabled, and counts of ramp-down events. The ANE has hardware performance counters, but they can be read only through a private interface that attaches a statistics buffer when a request is submitted, which exceeds the constraints of this report 〔ioreg, IOReport, kernel〕. Therefore, we can measure frequency only indirectly.

The indirect measurement exploits the inverse relation between execution time and clock frequency for a fixed workload. For a task with a fixed amount of computation whose data all reside on chip, the execution time T and the clock frequency f satisfy T = C / f + D, where C is the number of clock cycles required by the task and D is a fixed time that does not depend on frequency. If task durations can be measured at different DVFS states and the frequencies of those states are known, C and D can be fitted. The full-load frequency can then be inferred back from the task duration at full load. When the ANE starts work from idle, its clock steps up from low states to high states one at a time. This behavior is controlled by the CLPC, and Chapter 12 studies its pattern. The process thus provides an opportunity to measure the same task at multiple states. Task durations are obtained from the task start and end events that the firmware records in kdebug (Table 6-1), with a time resolution of one period of the 24 MHz timer 〔kdebug〕.

The frequency values of the states come from three mutually independent static sources: the frequency tables of the power-management nodes in the device tree, the SoC parameters built into the compiler (Section 4.4), and the constant regions of the CLPC and power-management drivers in the kernelcache 〔ioreg, compiler, kernel〕. For clock domains that have no frequency table, we determined whether different functional units belong to the same clock domain by comparing how their task durations change over time. We observed the state of the interconnect with the SoC voltage-domain state statistics reported by IOReport 〔kdebug, IOReport〕.

### 11.2 NE Clock

#### 11.2.1 Frequency Table

The two properties `voltage-states8` and `voltage-states29` of the power-management node in the device tree each list the frequencies of 32 states, and their contents are identical 〔ioreg〕. Table 11-1 lists these 32 frequencies.

*Table 11-1. ANE frequency table in the device tree (MHz)*

| States 1–8 | States 9–16 | States 17–24 | States 25–32 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 804 | 1188 | 1716 | 2280 |
| 828 | 1248 | 1788 | 2352 |
| 852 | 1308 | 1872 | 2364 |
| 960 | 1368 | 1908 | 2436 |
| 996 | 1404 | 1968 | 2448 |
| 1032 | 1476 | 2052 | 2484 |
| 1092 | 1548 | 2112 | 2508 |
| 1128 | 1632 | 2196 | 2580 |

The correspondence between voltage tables and performance domains in the device tree is recorded in the `perf-domains` property: `voltage-states8` corresponds to ANE0, and `voltage-states29` corresponds to ANE1. This agrees with the two performance domains (8 and 29) of the ANEs in the CLPC described in Section 4.3 〔ioreg〕. In other words, the NE clock of each ANE is an independent performance domain with its own 32-state frequency table, whereas the other parts of the ANE have no voltage table of their own in the device tree. The ANE frequency table used by the CLPC in the kernelcache (`_anePerfStateFrequenciesMHz`, 32 states) is provided at runtime by the power-management driver and is consistent with the device tree 〔kernel〕.

The compiler's NE frequency table (`Soc2026BaseLine`, Section 4.4) also has 32 states. Its first 24 states are identical to those of the device tree, entry by entry, while the last 8 differ. The device tree lists 2280 to 2580 MHz for these states, whereas the compiler lists 2244 to 2508 MHz. The compiler's top state of 2508 MHz corresponds only to state 31 of the device tree 〔compiler, ioreg〕.

#### 11.2.2 Full-Load Frequency

We measured this with a single ANE, in which all layers share weights and DRAM is not read: a 1×1 convolution with 512 channels, a 1×128 shape, and 384 layers. In each round, the ANE is first left idle for 2.5 s so that its clock drops to a low state. It then executes 800 calls in succession, and we record the complete ramp of the clock from a low state to full load. Six rounds were measured in total, yielding 4920 tasks (tools: [freq_ktrace.sh](../tools/09_clock/freq_ktrace.sh), [freq_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/freq_fit.py); data: [freq_kt/](../data/09_clock/freq_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕.

The task duration in each round begins at about 3809 µs, passes through a series of steps, and finally stabilizes at 1267.5 µs. The steady segment accounts for 79% of all tasks, and the medians of the steady segments across the six rounds differ by no more than ±0.7 µs. To avoid presupposing which frequency table is correct, we fitted only the five lowest major steps. We excluded the step at about 1188 MHz, which has very few tasks. The states corresponding to these five steps are identical in the two tables. The fit results are shown in Table 11-2.

*Table 11-2. Fit of the low-state steps (T = C / f + D)*

| Step duration | Tasks | Corresponding state | Fit deviation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3809.1 µs | 25 | 852 MHz | +0.29% |
| 2382.1 µs | 24 | 1368 MHz | +0.06% |
| 2108.3 µs | 45 | 1548 MHz | −0.02% |
| 1904.7 µs | 43 | 1716 MHz | −0.11% |
| 1748.9 µs | 27 | 1872 MHz | −0.22% |

This fit first requires the range of the fixed time D to be constrained. If D is unconstrained, the deviations of the five-step fit keep decreasing as D increases (reaching only 0.02% at D ≈ 27 µs), so the solution is not unique. Such solutions would convert the full-load duration of 1267.5 µs to about 2598 MHz, which does not appear in any frequency table. The value of D can be estimated from an independent measurement. In the first round of experiments in Chapter 6, the mean task durations of models of the same shape with 16 and 384 layers were 58.1 µs and 1269.5 µs, respectively. The intercept at zero layers obtained by linear extrapolation is about 5.4 µs (data: [d1_out/](../data/04_schedule/d1_out/)) 〔kdebug〕. We take this intercept as the lower bound and relax the upper bound to about twice that value, so that D is constrained to 5–12 µs. The upper bound is chosen to accommodate the measurement error of the intercept and has no other basis. Within this range, the best fit has an RMS deviation of 0.17–0.24%, and the solution is unique (the suboptimal solution has a deviation of 0.57%). Each task corresponds to about 3.24–3.26 × 10⁶ cycles, which varies slightly with D within this range 〔kdebug, inferred〕.

Based on this fit, the duration of a full-load task would be 1269.5 µs at 2580 MHz and 1305.6 µs at 2508 MHz. The measured value is 1267.5 µs, which implies a full-load frequency of 2578–2584 MHz. This result cannot be explained by a limitation from another bottleneck at full load, because such a limitation would only make tasks longer than predicted. The measured value, by contrast, is about 3% shorter than the prediction at 2508 MHz.

Clustering by task duration shows that the six rounds pass through more steps than the 10 in the typical trajectory listed in Section 11.2.3. Some rounds also pass through states near 1034, 1128, 1509, 1786 and 2045 MHz, and the paths of the rounds are not entirely the same. We tested both frequency tables using the 13 steps that contain at least 20 tasks. Three of these steps (1390.8, 1397.7 and 1409.0 µs) all correspond to 2352 MHz, two (1493.9 and 1505.7 µs) correspond to 2196 MHz, and two (1267.5 and 1275.6 µs) correspond to 2580 MHz, so fewer than 13 states are actually involved. With D at a small value, the device-tree table plus 2580 MHz gives an RMS deviation of 0.62%, whereas the compiler table plus 2508 MHz gives 1.13%. As a control, the 1st percentile of the deviations for randomly selected steps is 0.8%. If D is allowed to reach about 60 µs, the deviation of the compiler table can be reduced to 0.41%, which is actually lower than that of the device-tree table. However, a fixed time of 60 µs differs by an order of magnitude from the intercept of about 5.4 µs given above, so we consider it untenable. Therefore, under the premise that D is a few microseconds, the NE clock runs at the top state of the device-tree table, 2580 MHz, under full load 〔kdebug, inferred〕. Figure 11-1 shows the steps in task duration when calls are issued continuously from idle.

![Ramp-up steps](../figs/en/light/fig11-1_ramp_steps.png)

*Figure 11-1. Task duration measured with kdebug as a function of time when the same model (1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×128, 384 layers) is called continuously after each round's 2.5 s idle period (6 rounds, 4920 tasks). The left panel shows the first 330 ms, with the device-tree states converted from T = C / f + D on its right-hand scale. After 20 warm-up calls, a pause of about 28 ms returns the clock to 852 MHz, and the clock then steps up to 2580 MHz (1267.5 µs). The right panel shows the steady segment after 330 ms, with an enlarged vertical axis, where brief dips to about 1326 µs occur about once every 300 tasks (data: [freq_kt/](../data/09_clock/freq_kt/)).*

This result also yields the conversion used in Section 7.2.1. The model requires about 8450–8480 cycles per layer and performs 33.55 million MAC operations, that is, about 3960–3970 MACs per cycle, or about 247–248 MACs per cycle per NE. This is about 97% of 256. The timing slope in Section 7.2.1 gives 249.5 MACs per cycle (97.5%). The two values come from cycle-count fitting and timing-slope methods, respectively, and from measurements in different rounds, and they differ by less than 1%.

#### 11.2.3 Operating Range

The states encountered during ramp-up, in order, are 852, about 1188, 1368, 1548, 1716, 1872, 2196, 2352, 2448 and 2580 MHz 〔kdebug〕. At the start of each round, the measurement tool first executes 20 warm-up calls (about 60 ms, during which the clock rises only to about 1716 MHz). It then pauses for about 28 ms to establish the timing window, and during the pause the clock falls back to 852 MHz. In subsequent full ramps, rising from 852 MHz to 2448 MHz requires 69–85 tasks and 124–146 ms of ANE execution time (wall-clock time 152–181 ms), and rising to 2580 MHz requires 158–189 ms of wall-clock time 〔kdebug〕. This is consistent with the step-wise ramp-up measured under the same host idle conditions in Section 12.3, which also shows that the speed of ramp-up depends on the host idle time between calls. Once the steady segment is reached, a brief dip occurs about every 300 tasks, consisting of about 15 tasks at about 2448 MHz (1326 µs) 〔kdebug〕. Notably, the ramp-up starts from state 3 (852 MHz), not from the lowest state in the table, 804 MHz. In Section 12.2, when the steady state is measured with a sleep of more than 400 µs before each call, the lowest state selected by the CLPC is likewise 852 MHz. Therefore, the actual operating range of the NE clock is 852–2580 MHz, and the two lowest states (804 and 828 MHz) never appeared in the experiments of this report.

IOReport shows that each ANE has two PLLs. When the ANE is idle, both PLLs are off. They turn on within about 0.1 s after work begins and remain on throughout full load 〔IOReport〕. Because the PLLs are already on during the ramp-up, the ramp-up proceeds state by state within the frequency table, rather than through a transition of the PLLs from off to on.

### 11.3 Clock of the Shared Cluster

#### 11.3.1 The PE Is Not in the NE Clock Domain

The compiler's performance model places the PE in a clock domain different from that of the NE. For a pure-PE TD, the estimated latency equals the number of PE cycles divided by 1.812 GHz. For this purpose, the compiler has a built-in table mapping NE frequency to L2 frequency (`GetMapNEToL2Frequencies`). The M6 mapping table has 32 entries, corresponding one-to-one to the NE frequency table. The L2 frequency is about 0.72–0.85 times the NE frequency, and at 2508 MHz for the NE it corresponds to 1812 MHz (data: [socfreq_ne2l2.txt](../data/09_clock/socfreq_ne2l2.txt); tool: [socfreq_map.c](../tools/re/socfreq_map.c)) 〔compiler〕. According to this mapping table, the PE clock should vary in step with the NE clock.

Measurement rejects this assumption. We alternately started the convolution model and the PE model from the idle state and recorded the task durations of both. The convolution task dropped from 3810 µs to 1267.5 µs, a ratio of 3.01, consistent with the ratio of the NE clock at 2580 MHz to that at 852 MHz (3.03). The PE portion (the difference between the 96-layer and 32-layer models, that is, 64 PE operations) dropped from 754.5 µs to 287.2 µs, a ratio of only 2.63.
