When three processes were executed interleaved, there were also cases in which the convolution had reached full load while the PE was still in the lowest state, and cases in which the convolution was still in a low state while the PE had already sped up (data: [pefreq_kt/](../data/09_clock/pefreq_kt/), [pefreq_kt2/](../data/09_clock/pefreq_kt2/), [pefreq_kt3/](../data/09_clock/pefreq_kt3/); tools: [pefreq_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/pefreq_fit.py), [pefreq_fit3.py](../tools/09_clock/pefreq_fit3.py)) 〔kdebug〕. These results establish that the PE clock is scheduled independently, and that the compiler's mapping table is merely an assumption of its performance model.

Symbols and strings in the CLPC driver in the kernelcache provide corroborating evidence for this conclusion. In addition to the performance domains of the two ANEs, the CLPC manages an "ANE shared cluster" (`ANE Shared Cluster`, `aneshared`, `SharedANEClusterMask`), which frequency-scales the parts shared by the two ANEs as a single independent cluster. The frequency table of each cluster is provided at runtime by the power-management driver (`dvfm table provided by PMGR for %s cluster %u`) 〔kernel〕. We speculate that the PE and the L2 are located in this shared cluster 〔inferred〕.

#### 11.3.2 Frequency and Scheduling Behavior

Unlike the NE clock, the system provides no direct readout that determines the absolute frequency of the shared cluster. We found no frequency table for it in the device tree, the compiler, the power-management driver, the CLPC driver, the ANE firmware or the PMP firmware. In the device tree, the frequency fields of three voltage tables (`voltage-states0`, `2` and `11`) are withheld. Of these, 0 corresponds to the SoC voltage domain, 2 to DRAM, and 11 to the display, and none of them is the ANE shared cluster 〔ioreg, kernel, firmware〕. We can therefore only estimate its frequency. The ratio of the PE clock from its lowest state to full load is 2.63. The lower end of the compiler's L2 frequency mapping table is 684–720 MHz. Multiplying this starting point by 2.63 gives about 1.8–1.9 GHz under full load 〔inferred〕. It should be noted that this estimate is not independent of the mapping table. The ratio from the low end to the high end (1812 MHz) of the mapping table is about 2.5–2.65, which is close to the measured 2.63, so the result is in effect close to the high-end value of the mapping table. The only measured fact that can be established is the 2.63-fold ramp ratio; the absolute frequency remains unknown.

Although the absolute frequency cannot be determined, the dynamic frequency-scaling behavior of the shared cluster can still be observed indirectly through PE task durations. At the same time, we need to exclude another possibility: the slowdown of the PE may be due not to a reduction in the clock of the shared cluster, but to insufficient supply voltage on the SoC. The SoC voltage domain has five voltage levels, the lowest being VMIN and the highest VOVD2. Each component on the SoC can vote separately to request a voltage no lower than a given level. IOReport reports both the actual level of the voltage domain and the individual votes, as well as the lower bound after aggregation. The actual level is not lower than this bound but may be higher. For example, when idle, the lower bound is VMIN, while the actual level is mostly VMAX. For this purpose, we wrote a sampling tool that reads, about every 25 ms, the actual level of the SoC voltage domain, the lower bound aggregated from all votes, and the votes that the ANE casts for interconnect bandwidth. At the same time, kdebug records the duration of each PE task. The two data sets are aligned by the system time base, so that the order of changes in PE speed and SoC voltage can be compared (tools: [socsamp.c](../tools/common/socsamp.c), [socpe_ktrace.sh](../tools/09_clock/socpe_ktrace.sh), [socpe_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/socpe_fit.py); data: [socpe_kt/](../data/09_clock/socpe_kt/)) 〔kdebug, IOReport〕. The results show three points.

First, the shared cluster ramps up very slowly. When only the PE model is executed, PE task durations remain at the lowest state (about 1193 µs) for about 0.4–0.5 s after a cold start before speeding up. During the speed-up, task durations oscillate between 496 and 1012 µs, and only after about 0.8 s do they settle at 466 µs at full load. For comparison, under the same conditions the NE clock reaches full speed within about 150–200 ms (Section 11.2.3).

Second, when the GPU is active simultaneously, the shared cluster is repeatedly pushed back to low states. We first let the GPU read memory for 1.5 s, and then cold-started the PE model. The PE likewise stays in a low state for 0.3–0.7 s. After it speeds up, it is pushed back into the low state, and this repeats many times. Throughout the measurement round (about 1 s), it never stabilizes at full load.

Third, the state of the SoC voltage domain does not determine the speed of the PE. In the second case, the SoC voltage domain stays at the top state, VOVD2, throughout, yet the PE is still held in the low state. In the first case, the SoC voltage domain rises to VOVD2 only after the PE has sped up. This indicates that the rise in SoC voltage is not the cause of the PE speed-up and is more likely its result 〔inferred〕. Figure 11-2 puts these three points together.

![Frequency scaling of the shared cluster](../figs/en/light/fig11-2_pe_ramp.png)

*Figure 11-2. Frequency-scaling behavior of the shared cluster (PE). (a) Ramp-up after a cold start, with speed measured relative to the first task of the round (lowest state): the NE, running the convolution model, exceeds twofold speed within about 150 ms and then varies between 2.2× and 3× under control of the CLPC; the PE, running a model containing only elementwise operations, stays in the lowest state for about 0.45 s and stabilizes at the full-load duration of 466 µs only after about 0.8 s. Thick lines are representative rounds; thin lines are the other rounds of the same kind. (b) PE task durations: when run alone, they stabilize at full load after about 0.8 s; when started after the GPU has read memory for 1.5 s, both speed-ups of the PE are pushed back to the lowest state, and the PE never stabilizes at full load during the round. (c) Actual SoC voltage-domain state aligned with (b) (sampled about every 25 ms; blank where there is no residency record in the window): while the GPU is active, the voltage stays at the top state VOVD2 throughout, yet the PE remains in a low state; when run alone, the voltage rises to VOVD2 only after the PE has sped up. Each curve is a sliding median over 5 tasks (data: [socpe_kt/](../data/09_clock/socpe_kt/)).*

These behaviors agree with the independent scheduling of the shared cluster in the CLPC and with the existence of the fabric power limiter (`fabric_power_limiter`, `FabricComputePowerRatio`). We speculate that, when the GPU is active simultaneously, the power budget of the interconnect is occupied by the GPU, which confines the shared cluster to low states 〔kernel, inferred〕. The practical consequence is that inferences lasting less than about 0.5 s run their PE portion (elementwise operations, activation functions, normalization, and so on) largely in low states. In applications where the ANE and the GPU are active at the same time, the PE portion is slowed by up to about 2.6× in our measurements, whereas convolutions and matrix multiplications on the NE are unaffected. This problem is recorded as defect B10 (Appendix D).

### 11.4 SoC Interconnect and Voltage

The compiler's SoC parameters include a five-state frequency table (534, 712, 1068, 1150 and 1308 MHz), which the compiler labels "DMA frequency" in the function `GetValidDMAFrequencies`. We found exactly the same five values in the constant region of the CLPC driver. There they appear in the table of the Fabric DVFM module (source file `fabric_dvfm_table.cpp`), immediately adjacent to the DRAM transfer-rate table 〔kernel〕. The SoC voltage domain also happens to have five voltage levels (VMIN to VOVD2). From this we infer that the so-called "DMA frequency table" in the compiler actually describes the five frequency states of the SoC interconnect, and that each frequency state corresponds to one voltage level of the SoC voltage domain 〔inferred〕.

The ANE has no voltage domain of its own. The voltage domains managed by the PMP firmware are only three: DRAM (DCS), the SoC and the display. The ANE requests a voltage floor from the SoC voltage domain through the vote items visible in IOReport 〔ioreg, IOReport, firmware〕. We compared the state of the SoC voltage domain under three kinds of load, and the results are shown in Table 11-3 (tool: [iorlist.c](../tools/common/iorlist.c)) 〔IOReport〕.

*Table 11-3. State of the SoC voltage domain under different loads*

| Item | Idle | PE chain (256 × 64²) | Convolution chain (1×1, 512 channels, 384 layers) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Actual SoC state | Mostly VMAX | VOVD2, 93–99% of the time | VMAX, 97% of the time |
| ANE interconnect bandwidth votes (`ANE0-AF-BW`, `ANE1-AF-BW`) | VMIN | VOVD2, 89% of the time | VNOM, 97–98% of the time |
| ANE's own SoC floor votes (`SOC Floor / ANE0`, `ANE1`) | 0 | 0 | 0 |

The ANE's own direct votes for the SoC voltage floor are always 0. What actually raises the voltage floor is the bandwidth vote between the ANE and the SoC interconnect. The two kinds of load behave markedly differently. Although the DRAM read/write traffic of the PE chain is only about 0.5 GB/s, it is enough to push the SoC voltage to its top state. By contrast, the convolution chain raises the voltage floor only to the intermediate VNOM state. From this we infer that the PE chain continuously uses the data path between the ANE and the SoC interconnect, whereas convolutional computation depends much less on this path. Combined with the structural analysis above, this phenomenon further supports the speculation that the data paths of the L2 and the PE connect to the SoC interconnect, or that the L2 itself lies on the interconnect side 〔IOReport, inferred〕. It is also consistent with the structural judgment that the PE and the L2 belong to the ANE shared cluster rather than to the interior of either ANE.

### 11.5 DRAM

Section 9.5.1 has already shown that, when the ANE reads weights alone, when the GPU reads memory alone, and when both run simultaneously, the residency fraction of DRAM in state F9 is above 99.8%. F9 corresponds to 5328 MHz, that is, 10656 MT/s. The DRAM frequency tables in the compiler, the CLPC driver and the PMP firmware agree. The lowest state (732 MHz) corresponds to F1, which is never used. Therefore, when the ANE is at full load, DRAM does not impose a frequency limitation 〔IOReport, compiler, kernel, firmware〕.

### 11.6 Compiler Frequency Assumptions

The results above show that the frequencies used in the compiler's performance model disagree with the hardware in two respects. First, the compiler takes 2508 MHz as the highest frequency of the NE, whereas the hardware runs at 2580 MHz under full load, so the compiler's estimate is about 3% slow. The partitioning decision in Section 5.8.3 compares the latencies before and after splitting for the same model, so the two latencies deviate by the same proportion. The effect of this discrepancy on splitting decisions should therefore be very small. It may simply be a conservative value in the performance model, or an input error when the model was built 〔inferred〕. This problem is recorded as defect B6 (Appendix D). Second, the compiler assumes that the frequencies of the L2 and the PE vary with the NE frequency under a fixed mapping. In fact, the shared cluster is scheduled independently and may remain in a low state even when the NE is already at full load. In such cases, the compiler's latency estimates for PE-class operations are markedly optimistic.

When a model is loaded through Core ML, the compilation options recorded in the kernel log include `--ne-frequency=-1.000000`, `--pstate-soc=-1` and `--pstate-dcs=-1`. These indicate that the architectural default values are used for the NE frequency and for the SoC and DRAM states 〔kernel〕. When we call `ANECCompile` directly and set `NeFrequency` to 1308 MHz, the `exe_cycles` of the same TD rise from 176 to 338, which shows that this option does enter the performance model 〔compiler〕. In other words, the compiler's performance model estimates latency at a fixed full-load frequency and does not consider the state that the CLPC actually selects at runtime.

### 11.7 Comparison with the M4 and the Literature

maderix [4] did not measure the clock frequency on the M4, and listed the number of channels of the MAC array and the clock frequency as open questions. The NE frequency table built into the compiler for the M4 (H16g) has 26 states, with a maximum of 2364 MHz (Section 4.4). If the M4 hardware, like the M6, also runs at frequencies higher than those in the compiler table, then the number of MACs per NE per cycle derived from the compiler table would be overestimated. This has yet to be verified on the M4. Bryngelson [5] reports that the ANE firmware contains no temperature or throttling logic, and that these functions are handled by SoC power management. This report likewise found no frequency table or state-selection logic in the M6 firmware (Section 6.4). The NE clock and the shared-cluster clock are both managed by the CLPC in the host kernel, which is consistent with that report.

### 11.8 Summary

The ANE of the M6 involves four kinds of clock domain. The NE clock of each ANE is an independent performance domain with 32 DVFS states, and its operating range is 852–2580 MHz. Under the premise that the fixed time of a task is a few microseconds, it runs at the top state of the device-tree frequency table, 2580 MHz, under full load. This is about 3% higher than the 2508 MHz assumed by the compiler. Measurements show that the PE is located in an independently scheduled clock domain. We speculate that this clock domain is the shared cluster used by both ANEs in the CLPC, and that the L2 is likely also within it 〔inferred〕. Its absolute frequency cannot be read. If the compiler's mapping table is followed, it is about 1.8–1.9 GHz under full load. It ramps up slowly and is repeatedly pushed back to low states when the GPU is active simultaneously. The SoC interconnect has five frequency states, corresponding to the five voltage levels of the SoC voltage domain, and the ANE indirectly influences them through interconnect bandwidth votes. DRAM runs at its top state of 10656 MT/s when the ANE is at full load. All of these clocks are managed by the CLPC and the power-management driver in the host kernel, and we found no evidence that ANE firmware participates.

The question of who selects the NE DVFS state, and according to what rule, is addressed in Chapter 12. Still undetermined are the absolute frequency of the shared cluster and the number of elements the PE processes per cycle (Section 16.2). Deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are concentrated in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 12. Dynamic Frequency Scaling

Chapter 11 established the NE DVFS states, and the static analysis in Chapter 6 indicated that the CLPC in the host kernel selects these states. This chapter examines the rules by which the CLPC selects states: which signals it uses to scale frequency, which state it holds the ANE at in steady state, how quickly it responds to changes in load, and how the ANE is ramped down and powered off after it becomes idle. The code of the CLPC is not public, so we treat it as a black box and determine its behavior by controlling the cadence at which the host submits requests and observing the NE DVFS state of the ANE. The main conclusions of this chapter are four. First, the CLPC scales frequency according to utilization. When the idle time between calls is no more than about 1 ms, it holds the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77%. When the idle time is longer, the busy fraction decreases monotonically with idle length, while the DVFS state remains far from the lowest state. This behavior can be approximated by an exponential moving average (EWMA) of utilization with a time constant of about 6.5 ms and a target of about 80%. Second, under synchronous calls, ramp-up and ramp-down proceed at comparable speeds, and raising the state above 2448 MHz from the lowest state typically requires 100–200 ms. The shorter the host idle time between calls, the faster the ramp-up. Third, the ANE is ramped down to the lowest state about 100 ms after it becomes idle, both ANEs are powered off simultaneously about 5.7 s after the last inference ends, and a subsequent call requires about 50 ms to restart the firmware. Fourth, sustained full load for 5 minutes triggered no ramp-down and no throttling.

### 12.1 Measurement Method

To study the CLPC as a black box, we need two measured quantities: one that we can vary and that affects the decisions of the CLPC, which serves as the input of the experiment, and one that reflects the outcome of those decisions, which serves as the output. The inputs of the CLPC cannot be set directly, but they can be influenced indirectly. Section 6.3.4 indicates that the main way the CLPC learns about the load on the ANE is through the submission, start, and end of each job reported by the driver. The utilization that the CLPC observes should therefore depend on the cadence at which the host submits requests, and this cadence is under our control. Concretely, before each synchronous Core ML call, we put the calling thread to sleep for a fixed time G, and vary G to change the idle time between two tasks on the ANE (tools: [gov_sweep.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_sweep.sh); the timing program is [bondrun.m](../tools/common/bondrun.m)). The model used is the same as in Section 11.2.2 (single ANE, 1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×128, 384 layers, with weights shared across layers), and its task duration depends only on the clock frequency of the NE.

The output of the CLPC is the NE DVFS state. The system does not provide a readout of the state, but Section 11.2.2 has already established the relationship between task duration and frequency for this model, T = C / f + D, so the state in which each task runs can be inferred from its duration. Task durations and the idle gaps between tasks are both obtained from firmware events in kdebug, and the busy fraction of the ANE is defined as the sum of task durations divided by the total time (tools: [gov_ktrace.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_ktrace.sh), [gov_fit.py](../tools/10_clpc/gov_fit.py)) 〔kdebug〕. To observe the dynamic response, we alternate G periodically between two values and record the change of the DVFS state over time (tools: [gov_step.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_step.sh), [gov_step_fit.py](../tools/10_clpc/gov_step_fit.py)). For ramp-down and power-off after idleness, we measured the latency of the first call after idle periods of different durations on the one hand. On the other hand, we used IOReport to record the power state of the ANE firmware processor, and we used the system log to record driver events (tool: [coldwake.m](../tools/common/coldwake.m)) 〔timing, IOReport, kernel〕.

### 12.2 Steady State: Utilization Target

We first observe the effect of G on the per-call time using timing alone, and the results are shown in Table 12-1 (data: [gov/](../data/10_clpc/gov/)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 12-1. Sleep time before each call and per-call time*

| Sleep time G | Median per-call time | 10th–90th percentile | State |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 1651 µs | 1645–1665 µs | At full load |
| 50 µs | 1567 µs | 1558–1581 µs | At full load |
| 100 µs | 1541 µs | 1536–1553 µs | At full load |
| 150 µs | 2126 µs | 1643–2456 µs | Oscillating between intermediate DVFS states |
| 200 µs | 3240 µs | 1915–3672 µs | Oscillating, mostly at low DVFS states |
| 300 µs | 3551 µs | 2552–4299 µs | Oscillating, mostly at low DVFS states |
| 400 µs | 4312 µs | 3664–4363 µs | At low DVFS states |
| 1000 µs | 4563 µs | — | At low DVFS states |
| 3000 µs | 4582 µs | — | At low DVFS states |

When the extra sleep time between calls exceeds about 150 µs, the per-call time rises noticeably. When the sleep time reaches 400 µs or more, the per-call time settles at about 4.3–4.6 ms, which is about 2.8 times slower than at full load. At G of 50 µs and 100 µs, calls are slightly faster than at G of 0. We speculate that a brief sleep allows the calling thread to respond to completion notifications more quickly, which is consistent with the host wake-up effect in Section 6.5 〔inferred〕.

However, the timing results show only that "a slightly longer sleep leads to a ramp-down"; they do not show the basis on which the CLPC makes its decisions. To address this, we used kdebug to measure the task duration, DVFS state, and busy fraction of the ANE at each G, and the results are shown in Table 12-2 (data: [gov_kt/](../data/10_clpc/gov_kt/)) 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 12-2. DVFS state and busy fraction of the ANE under different sleep times (measured in a different round from Table 12-1; G is the nominal sleep time, and the actual sleep may be longer, so the call periods at the same G do not fully agree between the two tables)*

| Sleep time G | Number of tasks | Median task duration | Median DVFS state | DVFS state distribution (top three) | Median idle time between tasks | ANE busy fraction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 1529 | 1267.5 µs | 2580 MHz | 2580: 84%; 2448: 5%; 2112: 2% | 384 µs | 77% |
| 100 µs | 1425 | 1267.5 µs | 2580 MHz | 2580: 81%; 2448: 6% | 433 µs | 75% |
| 150 µs | 1558 | 1789 µs | 1788 MHz | 1872: 42%; 1548: 12%; 1716: 10% | 517 µs | 77% |
| 200 µs | 1561 | 2695 µs | 1188 MHz | 1188: 37%; 1548: 14%; 1368: 12% | 808 µs | 77% |
| 300 µs | 1504 | 3171 µs | 1032 MHz | 1032: 65%; 852: 19% | 975 µs | 77% |
| 400 µs | 1465 | 3807 µs | 852 MHz | 852: 63%; 1032: 31% | 1132 µs | 76% |
| 1000 µs | 1143 | 3807 µs | 852 MHz | 852: 95% | 2270 µs | 62% |

Table 12-2 does not include the case of G = 3000 µs in Table 12-1. The trace session for this set of experiments was fixed to record only 75 s, and the segment with G = 3000 µs began only at the 74th second. When the trace ended, the segment was still in the first 1 s, the warm-up phase of consecutive calls, and no task had been recorded during the phase of calls at 3000 µs intervals. Therefore only timing data are available for this case, and the idle length and busy fraction cannot be computed.

Comparing the DVFS-state and busy-fraction columns of Table 12-2 reveals a clear pattern. As G increases from 150 µs to 400 µs, the DVFS state falls steadily from about 1.8 GHz to 852 MHz, while the busy fraction of the ANE remains almost unchanged, staying at 76–77%. This indicates that the CLPC is a utilization controller: when the idle time between tasks lengthens and utilization drops, it lowers the NE DVFS state so that each task takes longer, thereby pulling utilization back to about 77% 〔kdebug〕. Section 12.4 will show that this conclusion holds only when the idle time per call is no more than about 1 ms. The busy fraction deviates from this target only in two cases. When G is no more than 100 µs, utilization reaches only about 77% even at the top state of 2580 MHz, and the DVFS state has already reached its upper limit. When G is 1000 µs, the DVFS state has fallen to the lowest state of 852 MHz and cannot fall further, and the busy fraction is only 62%. The lowest state, 852 MHz, is the third entry of the device-tree frequency table and is consistent with the starting point of ramp-up in Section 11.2.3. The two lowest entries of the table, 804 MHz and 828 MHz, were never selected in the experiments. Even in steady state, the DVFS state jitters among several adjacent states; for example, at G of 150 µs, all states between 1548 and 1872 MHz occur, which is normal jitter for a closed-loop control.

Table 12-2 also reveals an important fact: when G is 0, there is still an idle time of about 384 µs between two adjacent tasks. This idle time arises from host-side overhead under synchronous calls (Section 6.5). In all measurements, the next request was never submitted before the current task had ended. Therefore, for a model whose single task takes about 1.3 ms, the utilization of the ANE is only about 77% even if the host inserts no additional waiting, which lies just at the target of the CLPC. This coincidence allows "back-to-back synchronous calls" to sustain the top state. For a model with shorter single tasks, however, the fixed idle time of 384 µs accounts for a larger share, so utilization falls below 77% and the CLPC ramps down. In other words, the larger the host-side overhead, the lower the DVFS state assigned to the ANE.

### 12.3 Dynamic Response

Steady-state behavior reveals the target of the CLPC, whereas the dynamic response reveals how fast the CLPC reaches that target. We alternated G between two values about every 1.5 s, and recorded the DVFS state of each task (data: [gov_step/](../data/10_clpc/gov_step/)) 〔kdebug〕.

The measurement program switched G according to the number of calls. Group A consisted of 270 calls at G = 1000 µs and 900 calls at G = 0 in each cycle, for a total of 6 cycles; group B consisted of 440 calls at G = 200 µs and 900 calls at G = 0 in each cycle, for a total of 5 cycles. Because each call corresponds to exactly one task, the switching instants can be determined precisely from the task indices (tool: [gov_step_fit.py](../tools/10_clpc/gov_step_fit.py); results: [fit.txt](../data/10_clpc/gov_step/fit.txt)).

During the ramp-up after G is switched from 1000 µs to 0 (6 instances), the DVFS state starts from 852 MHz. The times to rise above 2448 MHz were 55, 101, 202, 149, 148, and 138 ms, respectively (median about 143 ms), and reaching 2580 MHz took 59–259 ms 〔kdebug〕. The median trajectory over the 6 instances is as follows: about 1032 MHz after 5 ms, 1368 MHz after 20 ms, 1548 MHz after 30 ms, 1716 MHz after 50 ms, about 2052 MHz after 100 ms, about 2484 MHz after 150 ms, and 2580 MHz after 200 ms. In the early phase, the ANE moves up one state every few milliseconds, and the closer it gets to the top state, the slower the ramp-up becomes. The first switch was clearly faster than the other five, and the host idle time in this switch was also clearly shorter. The median host idle time between two consecutive tasks after the switch was 192 µs, compared with 387–391 µs in the other five switches 〔kdebug〕. This difference can be explained by the utilization target. When the host idle time is about 390 µs, the busy fraction of the ANE at 852 MHz is about 91%, while at 2448 MHz and 2580 MHz it is only about 77% and 76%, respectively, which is already comparable to the target of about 77%. The closer the ANE is to the top state, the smaller the deviation that drives the CLPC to ramp up. When the host idle time is 192 µs, the busy fraction at 2580 MHz is still about 87%, so the deviation remains large and the ramp-up is much faster 〔timing, inferred〕.

During the ramp-down after G is switched from 0 to 1000 µs (6 instances), the CLPC first holds the DVFS state above 2448 MHz for 17–48 ms (in one switch, the state was only about 2112 MHz before the switch, so there was no such hold phase), and then steps down one state at a time. The median trajectory is about 2406 MHz after 30 ms, about 2238 MHz after 50 ms, about 1794 MHz after 75 ms, and 1548 MHz after 100 ms. It reaches 852 MHz at about 150 ms, and the final step often jumps directly from about 1548 MHz to the lowest state. Reaching the lowest state required 138–167 ms (94 ms in the one switch that did not start from the top state) 〔kdebug〕. Under these conditions, therefore, ramp-down and ramp-up proceed at comparable speeds.

Convergence is slower when the target lies at an intermediate state. During the switch from G = 0 to G = 200 µs, the DVFS state first stayed above 2448 MHz for 160–230 ms (in one of the five switches, it stayed only about 29 ms), and only then ramped down. After several hundred milliseconds it settled roughly between 1188 and 1548 MHz, oscillating within this range and occasionally moving outside it. Returning from an intermediate state to above 2448 MHz required 125–141 ms 〔kdebug〕.

We also measured the change in the DVFS state after the ANE becomes fully idle. Specifically, we ran the ANE at full load, left it idle for a period, and then resumed consecutive calls, and we observed the DVFS state of the first task after resumption. The results are shown in Table 12-3 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 12-3. DVFS state when calls resume after idle*

| Idle duration | DVFS state of the first task after resumption | Time to return above 2448 MHz |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 14 ms (gap after warm-up ended) | 2508 MHz | Immediate |
| 30 ms | 2196 MHz | About 110 ms |
| 60 ms | 1968 MHz | About 190 ms |
| 111 ms | 852 MHz | About 0.29–0.36 s |
| 212 ms | 852 MHz | About 0.25 s |

Table 12-3 shows that the DVFS state also decreases while the ANE is idle: it drops by 8 states in about 30 ms (from 2580 to 2196 MHz), and it reaches the lowest state at about 100 ms. Notably, ramp-up after idleness is slower than the step ramp-up described above. After idle periods of 111 ms and 212 ms, the ANE likewise starts from 852 MHz, but it requires about 0.25–0.36 s to return above 2448 MHz, whereas the median step ramp-up is about 0.14 s. This report does not identify the cause of this difference. In the approximate model with a time constant of about 6.5 ms in Section 12.4, the effect of an idle period of 100–200 ms decays within about 20 ms, so the model cannot explain why recovery is about twice as slow 〔inferred〕.

Taken together, the CLPC is a controller for the ANE that targets utilization. The speeds of both ramp-up and ramp-down depend on the deviation of the actual utilization from the target, rather than on a fixed step size. When the target lies at an intermediate DVFS state, convergence requires several hundred milliseconds and is accompanied by oscillation. We did not extract the specific gains and smoothing constants from the code. Figure 12-1 and Figure 12-2 show, respectively, the DVFS state and busy fraction of the ANE under different sleep times, and the step responses of ramp-up and ramp-down.

![Utilization target of the CLPC](../figs/en/light/fig12-1_utilization_target.png)

*Figure 12-1. DVFS state and busy fraction of the ANE under different sleep times G before each call. (a) Fraction of tasks at each DVFS state (circle area) and median DVFS state; (b) busy fraction of the ANE. For G of 150–400 µs, the DVFS state falls from about 1.8 GHz to 852 MHz while the busy fraction stays at 76–77%; for G ≤ 100 µs, the DVFS state has reached the top state, and for G of 1000 µs, it has reached the lowest state, where the busy fraction falls to 62% (data: [gov_kt/](../data/10_clpc/gov_kt/)).*

![Step response of the CLPC](../figs/en/light/fig12-2_step_response.png)

*Figure 12-2. Step response of the CLPC. (a), (b) Ramp-up and ramp-down when the sleep time G before each call is switched between 1000 µs and 0 (6 instances each); (c), (d) ramp-up and ramp-down when G is switched between 200 µs and 0 (5 instances each). Thin lines show the DVFS state trajectories after each switch, the thick line shows their median, the orange dashed line shows the first switch in (a), and the gray dashed line shows 2448 MHz (data: [gov_step/](../data/10_clpc/gov_step/)).*

### 12.4 Deviation at Long Idle Times and an Approximate Model

The experiments in Section 12.2 covered only one model (single task of about 1.3 ms) and short idle times (no more than about 2.3 ms). To test whether the target of about 77% holds more generally, we conducted three additional rounds of experiments. The models covered single-ANE and dual-ANE configurations, and both cases in which the weights remained resident on chip and cases in which they were read from DRAM in each call. The single-task duration at full clock was 1.3–5.3 ms, and the sleep time before each call was extended to more than ten milliseconds (tools: [gov_target.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_target.sh), [gov_target2.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_target2.sh), [gov_target3.sh](../tools/10_clpc/gov_target3.sh), [gov_target_fit.py](../tools/10_clpc/gov_target_fit.py), [gov_target2_fit.py](../tools/10_clpc/gov_target2_fit.py); data: [gov_tg/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg/), [gov_tg2/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg2/), [gov_tg3/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg3/)). Each segment ran for a fixed 12–16 s, and the first 4 s were discarded. Table 12-4 summarizes the results by the idle length per call, excluding points where the DVFS state had reached the top state of 2580 MHz or the lowest state of 852 MHz 〔kdebug〕.

*Table 12-4. Idle length per call and busy fraction of the ANE (three rounds of experiments)*

| Idle time per call (including host overhead) | Busy fraction | Models involved |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No more than about 1 ms | 75–78% | All models; task duration after ramp-down 1.9–3.7 ms |
| 2.6–2.9 ms | 65–68% | Single ANE reading DRAM (2.7 ms); dual ANE (2.6 ms, 5.2 ms) |
| 3.5–4.1 ms | 60–64% | Single ANE reading DRAM (2.7 ms, 5.3 ms); dual ANE (2.6 ms, 5.2 ms) |
| 4.7–5.3 ms | 58–59% | Single ANE reading DRAM (5.3 ms); dual ANE (5.2 ms) |
| About 7 ms | 56% | Same as above |
| About 10 ms / about 14 ms | 53% / 50% | Dual ANE (5.2 ms); equivalent DVFS state about 1221 / 1009 MHz |

Table 12-4 shows that a busy fraction of about 77% holds only when the idle time per call is no more than about 1 ms. Within this range, the busy fraction remains stable at 75–78% regardless of whether the model uses one or two ANEs, whether it reads DRAM, or how long its tasks are, so the conclusion of Section 12.2 holds. When the idle time is longer, the busy fraction decreases monotonically with idle length, whereas the NE DVFS state is far from the lowest state. For example, for a dual-ANE model whose single task takes 5.2 ms at full clock, the busy fraction is only 56% at an idle time of about 7 ms per call, and the equivalent DVFS state, converted from task duration, is still about 1505 MHz. A controller that merely drove the busy fraction toward 77% would continue to ramp down in this situation, but the CLPC does not do so.

We ruled out two possible explanations. First, the deviation is not caused by reading DRAM: dual-ANE models with weights resident on chip and single-ANE models that read DRAM show the same degree of deviation. Second, the deviation depends mainly on the absolute length of the idle time, not on the ratio of idle time to task duration; for the same idle time, longer tasks yield a slightly higher busy fraction. When computed from the job intervals reported by the driver, the busy fraction is likewise not constant: it is 79–82% when the idle time is short and falls to about 57% as the idle time lengthens. The job intervals reported by the driver are longer than the task durations recorded by the firmware by about 80–180 µs.

To describe this behavior, we propose an approximate model. We assume that the CLPC computes an exponential moving average of the busy state of the ANE with time constant τ, reads this average at the moment each job ends, and compares it with the target value to decide whether to ramp up or ramp down. For a periodic load that is busy for d and idle for g, the average at the job-end instant is

```
r = (1 − e^(−d/τ)) / (1 − e^(−(d+g)/τ))
```

When g is much smaller than τ, r is close to the true busy fraction d / (d + g). When g is comparable to or longer than τ, most of the "not busy" time accumulated during the idle period has decayed, and the value read at the job end is markedly higher than the true busy fraction. Taking τ = 6.5 ms as an example, with a busy period of 1.27 ms and an idle period of 0.38 ms, the true busy fraction is 77% and r is 79%. With a busy period of 8.9 ms and an idle period of 7.0 ms, the true busy fraction is only 56%, yet r is still 82%. Consequently, the longer the idle time, the higher the utilization that the CLPC reads, and the earlier it stops ramping down. We jointly fitted this model to the 33 points from the last two rounds of experiments (gov_tg2 and gov_tg3) in which the DVFS state was not at either limit and the host calling thread did not stall. The fit yields a target of about 80% and a τ of about 6.5 ms, with a root-mean-square (RMS) error of 2.9%. As a control, when the true busy fraction itself is taken as the controlled variable, the RMS error is 9.0%; when the target is fixed at 77%, the optimal τ is about 8.2 ms, with an RMS error of 3.2% 〔kdebug, inferred〕. Figure 12-3 compares the individual points with the model.

![Idle length per call and busy fraction of the ANE](../figs/en/light/fig12-3_idle_busy.png)

*Figure 12-3. Idle length per call and busy fraction of the ANE (last two rounds of experiments). (a) Colors indicate the task duration at full clock; filled markers denote single-ANE models and hollow markers denote dual-ANE models. Gray hollow points, whose DVFS state has reached the top state of 2580 MHz or the lowest state of 852 MHz, are excluded from the fit. Red crosses denote the dual-ANE model with a task of about 1.4 ms at full clock, which jumps to an operating state near 2540 MHz once the idle time exceeds about 2.5 ms; they are marked separately and are also excluded from the fit. The dashed line shows the 77% of Section 12.2, which holds only for idle times of no more than about 1 ms. The solid line shows the steady-state busy fraction given by the exponential moving average model sampled at job end (τ = 6.5 ms, target 80%). (b) Residuals, that is, the difference between the model reading r and the target, for each fitted point. Residuals are within ±5 percentage points at short idle times, while the two points at idle times of about 10 ms and 14 ms show +5 and +8.5, indicating that the model leaves unexplained components at very long idle times (data: [gov_tg2/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg2/), [gov_tg3/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg3/)).*

This model is only an approximation that gets the direction right, not the exact algorithm of the CLPC. For the two points at idle times of 10 ms and 14 ms, the residuals of the model are +5% and +8.5%, respectively. That is, at very long idle times the readings of the actual CLPC are not as high as the model predicts, which indicates that there are components not included in the model. We also observed an operating state that the model cannot explain. For a dual-ANE model with a single task of about 1.4 ms at full clock, once the idle time per call exceeds about 2.5 ms, the DVFS state inferred from the task duration settles near full clock at about 2540 MHz, while the busy fraction is only 32–36%. This occurred at all four sleep times, and one of them was repeated three times with identical results. By contrast, at an idle time of about 2.4 ms, the same model runs at about 910 MHz, inferred from task duration, with a busy fraction of 58%. Other models showed no such jump at comparable idle times, and the cause of this behavior is unknown.

These results can be understood as design trade-offs of the CLPC 〔inferred〕. When the idle time is short, the margin of about 20% can be understood as a compromise between the need to detect a rising load and the need to ramp down to save energy. If utilization were driven to 100%, a load that is just sufficient could not be distinguished in utilization from a load that exceeds the processing capacity. Moreover, when there are only a few hundred microseconds of idle time between tasks, the ANE remains powered on, and ramping down the frequency and voltage consumes less energy than completing the task quickly and then waiting idle. Section 13.4 measures that the ANE energy per inference at low states is about one-third of that at the top state. The schedutil frequency governor of the Linux kernel adopts a similar approach: it selects frequency as "utilization × 1.25," which corresponds to a utilization target of about 80% [11]. When the idle time is long, the exponential moving average sampled at the moment each job ends keeps the CLPC at a relatively high DVFS state for intermittent loads. This benefits the latency of each inference, but sacrifices some opportunity for energy saving. From the perspective of a user, this means that for intermittent inference with intervals of a few milliseconds, the CLPC does not ramp the DVFS state down as far as the steady-state utilization would suggest.

### 12.5 Idle and Power-Off

After the DVFS state has ramped down to the lowest state, if the ANE remains idle, the driver eventually powers off the ANE. We measured the latency of the first call after idle periods of different durations, and at the same time we used IOReport to record the power state of the ANE firmware processor (IOP). The results are shown in Table 12-5 (data: [e2_coldwake.txt](../data/10_clpc/e2_coldwake.txt), [e2_coldwake_states.txt](../data/10_clpc/e2_coldwake_states.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

*Table 12-5. Latency of the first call after idle (single ANE, 384 layers, steady state about 1.6 ms)*

| Idle duration | First call | Second call | First call ÷ steady state | IOP state during idle |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 ms | 1.73–1.84 ms | 1.64 ms | 1.08–1.15 | Running |
| 30 ms | 1.89–2.01 ms | 1.79–1.91 ms | 1.2–1.3 | Running |
| 100 ms | 5.4–5.7 ms | 3.9–4.4 ms | 3.4–3.5 | Running |
| 1 s | 5.9–6.1 ms | 4.6–4.7 ms | 3.7–3.8 | Running |
| 3 s | 6.1–6.4 ms | 4.8 ms | 3.8–4.0 | Running |
| 10 s | 53–56 ms | 4.0–4.3 ms | 32–35 | Powered off for part of the time |

The idle duration and the cost of the first call can be divided into three regimes. When the idle duration does not exceed about 30 ms, the cost is small, and the first call is only 8–30% slower. For idle durations of 0.1–3 s, the firmware processor remains running, but the DVFS state has already dropped to the lowest state. The first call therefore takes about 4 ms longer, and the ANE must then keep running for about another 0.25–0.36 s before it returns above 2448 MHz (Table 12-3). For idle durations of about 6 s or more, the firmware processor is powered off, and the next call must wait for the firmware to restart, which makes the first call about 50 ms longer.

To determine the exact moment of power-off, we recorded the states of the firmware processors of both ANEs at 100 ms intervals during idle periods, and we captured the kernel driver log at the same time (data: [h16_iop_trace.txt](../data/10_clpc/h16_iop_trace.txt), [h16_stream_raw.txt](../data/10_clpc/h16_stream_raw.txt)). The log shows that the driver has two timers, both of which are counted from the completion of the last inference 〔kernel, IOReport〕. The first is the power-off timer. It fires after 5.68 s of idle time (log entry `HWDevicePowerOffTimerTimeOut`), at which point both ANEs are deinitialized and the firmware processor is powered off. The second is the maintenance timer of the scheduler. It fires after 10.20 s of idle time, at which point both ANEs are powered on again (the firmware startup recorded in the log takes about 25 ms), the driver unloads the idle programs, and the driver then records `Not re-enabling maintenance timer`. At about 15.9 s, which is about 5.7 s after the maintenance timer fires, the power-off timer fires again, and the ANE remains powered off thereafter. Within the 40 s observation window, the maintenance wake-up occurred only once and was not periodic.

These two timers further divide the latency of the first call after idleness into four phases, as shown in Table 12-6 (data: [h16_idle_sweep.txt](../data/10_clpc/h16_idle_sweep.txt)) 〔timing〕.

*Table 12-6. Breakdown of idle duration and latency of the first call (same model, steady state about 1.68 ms)*

| Idle duration | State | First call | ÷ steady state |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 4–5.5 s | Firmware processor running, program still in firmware | 6.6–6.8 ms | 4.0 |
| 6–9 s | Firmware processor powered off | 47–60 ms | 28–36 |
| 11–14 s | Firmware processor woken by maintenance timer, program unloaded | 9.1–9.6 ms | 5.4–5.7 |
| 16–25 s | Firmware processor powered off again | 53–58 ms | 32–35 |

Table 12-6 shows that the costly part is the cold start after the firmware has been powered off: the first call is about 50 ms longer, of which the firmware startup recorded in the log accounts for about 25 ms. Re-preparing a program after it has been unloaded takes only about 3 ms. The timers of the two ANEs are synchronized. Even when the model uses only ANE0, ANE1 is also powered off, powered on, and unloaded at the same moments, and the fraction of time that ANE1 is powered on during idle periods is nearly identical to that of ANE0 〔IOReport, kernel〕.

### 12.6 Sustained Load and Throttling

Section 6.3.3 indicates that the driver supports two throttling mechanisms: lowering the NE DVFS state through the CLPC, and making the ANE pause intermittently according to a duty cycle. To test whether sustained full load triggers throttling, we ran a dual-ANE model (1×1 convolution, 512 channels, 1×256, 384 layers, about 1.68 ms per call) for 180,000 consecutive calls, a total of about 302 s. We sampled the frequency-reduction event counts and the temperature-band counts in IOReport once every 10 s (data: [e4_sustained_series.txt](../data/10_clpc/e4_sustained_series.txt), [e4_sustained_ioreport.txt](../data/10_clpc/e4_sustained_ioreport.txt)) 〔timing, IOReport〕.

Over the full 5 minutes, the median per-call time in each 10 s segment stayed between 1674 and 1678 µs, varying by less than 0.3%, and the slowest single call took 2.2 ms. The counts of the nine classes of frequency-reduction events in IOReport (software, hardware, ADCLK, DITHER, PPT, and four external trigger sources) were zero in all 31 sampling windows. The temperature-band counts show that the SoC spent 2.3 s in band 1 in the first window, and thereafter remained in band 2 and never entered band 3. These results show that on this M6, at room temperature, the ANE sustaining full load for 5 minutes neither ramped down nor triggered any throttling, and its sustained performance equals its peak performance. This conclusion applies only to a duration of 5 minutes and room-temperature conditions. Behavior over longer durations or at higher ambient temperatures was not measured.

### 12.7 Comparison with the Literature

Bryngelson [5] reports that on the M1, the first call on the ANE after 5 s of idle takes about 260 ms, that it is about 0.5 ms longer after about 100 ms of idle, and that the ANE does not ramp down during 210 s of continuous operation. The cold-start cost on the M6 is about 50 ms, which is clearly smaller than on the M1. The cost after about 100 ms of idle is about 4 ms, which is larger than on the M1, because the CLPC of the M6 ramps the DVFS state down to the lowest state within about 100 ms of idle. The conclusion that there is no ramp-down under sustained load is consistent with the M1. maderix [1, 2] reports that the ANE draws zero power when idle and is powered off as a whole, and lists frequency-scaling trigger sources such as ANE_ADCLK_TRIG, ANE_DITHR_TRIG, and ANE_PPT_*. This report observes counters with the same names in the IOReport of the M6, and confirms that they remain at zero under sustained full load. Neither maderix nor Bryngelson measured the frequency-scaling behavior of the CLPC. The results of this report show that the NE DVFS state of the ANE is not determined by the load of the ANE itself. Rather, it is determined indirectly, through utilization, by the cadence at which the host submits requests.

### 12.8 Summary

The NE DVFS state of the M6 is selected by the CLPC in the host kernel, and the CLPC scales frequency according to utilization over a working range of 852–2580 MHz. When the idle time between calls does not exceed about 1 ms, it holds the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77%. When the idle time is longer, the busy fraction decreases monotonically with idle length to about 50%, whereas the DVFS state remains far from the lowest state. This behavior can be approximated as "an exponential moving average of utilization with a time constant of about 6.5 ms, read at the end of each job and compared with a target of about 80%." However, the high DVFS states observed for very long idle times and for the short-task dual-ANE model indicate that the actual algorithm includes other components. Under synchronous calls, the CLPC ramps up and ramps down at comparable speeds: reaching above 2448 MHz typically requires 100–200 ms, and ramping down from the top state to the lowest state takes about 140–170 ms. The speed of ramp-up depends on the host idle time, and when the target lies at an intermediate DVFS state, several hundred milliseconds are required for convergence. When the ANE is idle, the DVFS state falls to the lowest state in about 100 ms. The two ANEs are powered off simultaneously 5.68 s after the last inference completes, and a subsequent call requires about 50 ms to restart the firmware. At 10.2 s, the maintenance timer wakes the ANE once and unloads the programs. Sustained full load for 5 minutes triggers no throttling.

This chapter treats the CLPC as a black box, and what it obtains is an approximate model rather than the actual algorithm. The residuals of the approximate model at idle times above 10 ms, the reason that ramp-up is slower after 0.1–0.2 s of idle time, and the high DVFS state of the short-task dual-ANE model at longer idle times all remain unexplained (Section 16.2). The effect of the DVFS state on the energy per inference is discussed in Section 13.4. The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are collected in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 13. Power and Energy Efficiency

This chapter analyzes the power characteristics of the ANE and its principal sources of energy consumption. Compared with the preceding chapters, this chapter faces a more fundamental difficulty: on the M6 there is no power rail that supplies only the ANE and can be read separately. We therefore first establish a measurement method, and then decompose the power into the parts attributable to multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations, elementwise operations, data movement, and the portion shared by the two ANEs. The main conclusions of this chapter are six. First, the SMC power rail PP0b supplies power to both the ANE and the S and P cores of the CPU. After the power of the CPU cluster is subtracted, a single ANE draws about 6.2 W at full load, two ANEs draw about 10.8 W at full load, and the ANE draws 0 W when idle. Second, there is a shared overhead of about 1.6 W between the two ANEs. After this portion is subtracted, an FP16 MAC consumes about 0.58 pJ, and an INT8 MAC consumes about 70–80% of that. Third, an elementwise operation consumes about 50–60 pJ per element, and reading data from DRAM consumes about 42 pJ/B. Fourth, when the CLPC selects a low state, the ANE energy per inference is about one-third of that at the top state. Fifth, when a small model is called at high frequency, most of the energy is consumed on the host CPU side rather than in the ANE itself. Sixth, when all activations of a convolution are zero, the ANE power decreases by about 58% on both the M6 and the M4 while the latency is unchanged. When the activations are partially zero, the power saving increases monotonically with the fraction of zeros, but it is lower than expected.

### 13.1 Measurement Method

#### 13.1.1 Locating the ANE Power Rail

`powermetrics` cannot report the power of the ANE on the M6 and macOS version used in this report. At full load, it reports a CPU power of 0 mW and has no entry for the ANE, and the energy model group of IOReport likewise reports only the energy of the GPU 〔IOReport〕. We therefore read the power rails in the SMC instead. The SMC provides 59 power keys beginning with P, which can be read through the AppleSMC user client without root privileges (tool: [smcpower.c](../tools/common/smcpower.c)) 〔SMC〕. Comparing the idle state with the state of dual-ANE full load, PP0b reads 0.00 W when idle and 11.3 W under dual-ANE full load, so it varies markedly with the ANE load.

However, PP0b is not a power rail dedicated to the ANE. While the ANE was idle, we ran several CPU threads in spin loops, and we simultaneously read PP0b, the energy histogram of the CPU clusters in IOReport, and the activity of each core. The results are shown in Table 13-1 〔SMC, IOReport〕.

*Table 13-1. PP0b readings with the CPU spinning (ANE idle)*

| Spinning threads | Cores on which the threads run | PACC0 cluster power | PP0b | PP0b − PACC0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | — | 1.1 W | 0.0 W | −1.1 W |
| 1 | S core | 8.6 W | 7.5 W | −1.1 W |
| 2 | 2 S cores | 11.7 W | 10.3 W | −1.4 W |
| 3 | 2 S cores and 1 P core | 12.6 W | 10.8 W | −1.8 W |
| 4 | 2 S cores and 2 P cores | 15.2 W | 13.0 W | −2.2 W |
| 6 | 2 S cores and 4 P cores | 16.4 W | 14.4 W | −2.0 W |
| 6 (background priority) | 6 E cores | 1.2 W | 0.0 W | — |

When the ANE is completely idle, PP0b reaches 7.5 W as soon as a single thread spins on an S core, and it remains at 0 W when the threads are confined to E cores. This shows that PP0b supplies power to both the ANE and the `PACC0` cluster of the CPU. The CPU of the M6 contains 2 S cores, 4 P cores, and 6 E cores. The S cores and P cores belong to the `PACC0` cluster, whereas the E cores belong to a different cluster that is not on this power rail. No other SMC key contains a channel that varies with the ANE alone. We performed the same test on the M4, and the result was the same: PP0b supplies power to both the ANE and the P-core cluster 〔SMC, IOReport〕.

#### 13.1.2 Subtracting the Power of the CPU Cluster

On this basis, we compute the net power of the ANE as follows. Once per second, we read PP0b and the power histogram of the `PACC0` cluster in IOReport, with the histogram averaged using weights given by the residence time in each state. We take the difference between the two, and subtract the baseline of the same difference when the ANE is powered off (about −1.1 to −1.2 W) (tools: [pclus.c](../tools/common/pclus.c), [power_parts.py](../tools/11_power/power_parts.py), [power_parts_fit.py](../tools/11_power/power_parts_fit.py)):

```
ANE net power = (PP0b − PACC0 cluster power) − the same difference with the ANE powered off
```

This method has two sources of error. First, the power histogram in IOReport is quantized in 1 W steps, so the error of a single reading is about ±0.5 W, and the error is substantially reduced by long-term averaging. Second, Table 13-1 shows that the difference between PP0b and the `PACC0` cluster is not constant. It is about −1.1 to −1.4 W when only S cores are busy, and about −2 W when P cores are also busy. When the ANE is at full load and the calling thread is blocked for most of the time, the power of the `PACC0` cluster is only 1.2–1.4 W, and the error of the baseline is within ±0.3 W. If the CPU is also busy while the ANE is running, the error of the baseline may reach about 1 W. When reporting the results for each load, this chapter also gives the power of the `PACC0` cluster, so that readers can judge the magnitude of the error.

In addition, we read two auxiliary power rails. The sum of PP2b and PP4b rises markedly when DRAM is read: it is about 2 W when the workload is weight-read-bound and less than 0.1 W when it is compute-bound, with the PE workload as an exception (see Section 13.3). We speculate that this sum supplies power to the memory controller and DRAM, and we refer to it below as the memory power rail. PSTR is the power of the whole system 〔SMC, inferred〕. The SMC readings are refreshed only about once per second, so each workload was run continuously for about 12 s, and the average was taken from 4 s after the start of the workload to 0.3 s before its end. This chapter also uses another set of measurements that read only PP0b, during which the CPU cluster remained close to idle; we refer to these below as direct-read PP0b measurements. The energy per MAC is defined as the net power of the ANE divided by the effective MAC rate. The effective rate is calculated by dividing the amount of compute of the model by the time per call (including host overhead). The energy per MAC is therefore the total ANE energy, including all components inside the ANE, amortized over each effective MAC.

### 13.2 Power by Workload Class

Table 13-2 gives the net power of the ANE for each workload class after subtracting the power of the CPU cluster (data: [power_parts4/](../data/11_power/power_parts4/)) 〔SMC, IOReport〕.

*Table 13-2. Power of each workload class*

| Workload | Per call | ANE net power | PACC0 cluster | Memory power rail | Whole system | Effective rate |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single ANE, FP16 convolution, shared weights (384 layers) | 1650 µs | 6.21 W | 1.25 W | 0.07 W | 9.9 W | 7.81 TMAC/s |
| Same as above, second round | 1652 µs | 6.12 W | 1.24 W | 0.12 W | 10.0 W | 7.80 TMAC/s |
| Dual ANE, FP16 convolution, shared weights (384 layers) | 1728 µs | 10.77 W | 1.28 W | 0.16 W | 16.8 W | 14.91 TMAC/s |
| Single ANE, W8A8 convolution, reading 100 MB of weights per call | 1045 µs | 7.31 W | 1.33 W | 2.19 W | 18.1 W | 12.34 T Int8 MAC/s |
| Single ANE, weight-read-bound (100 MB per call) | 1042 µs | 2.19 W | 1.33 W | 1.83 W | 11.0 W | 0.77 TMAC/s |
| Dual ANE, PE elementwise addition (data in L2) | 2218 µs | 2.47 W | 1.36 W | 1.14 W | 7.8 W | 45.4 G elements/s |
| Same as above, all inputs zero | 2220 µs | 2.04 W | 1.35 W | 0.68 W | 6.0 W | 45.4 G elements/s |
| Dual ANE, PE elementwise multiplication | 1626 µs | 2.65 W | 1.44 W | 1.11 W | 7.9 W | 41.3 G elements/s |
| Single ANE, FP16 convolution, 16 layers | 268 µs | 2.08 W | 7.29 W | 0.35 W | 14.0 W | 2.00 TMAC/s |
| Idle (ANE powered off) | — | 0 W | — | 0.02 W | 1.3 W | — |

Except for the small 16-layer model, the power of the `PACC0` cluster during each workload is only 1.2–1.4 W, close to the idle level, so the error in the ANE net power for these rows is within ±0.3 W. When compute-bound, the ANE power is about 6.2 W for a single ANE and about 10.8 W for dual ANEs. Based on the effective rate, the energy efficiency is about 2.5 TFLOPS/W for a single ANE and about 2.8 TFLOPS/W for dual ANEs. When idle, the ANE power is 0 W, consistent with the powered-off behavior described in Section 12.5.

Using direct-read PP0b measurements, we compared the energy efficiency of W8A8 and FP16 on the same network (48 layers, 512 channels, 32×32, with different weights in each layer, dual ANE; this is the 48-layer network in Table 7-12). In this round, the throughput of W8A8 was 54% higher than that of FP16. For the same network, Table 7-12 gives 39%; the two values come from different rounds and carry some error. The PP0b power was only about 10% higher, so the energy efficiency was about 1.4 times that of FP16 (3.4 TOPS/W versus 2.45 TFLOPS/W) (data: [power_smc.txt](../data/11_power/power_smc.txt)) 〔SMC〕. The CPU cluster was close to idle during these workloads. According to the analysis in Section 13.1.2, the difference between PP0b and the ANE net power is within ±0.3 W, so this comparison still holds.

### 13.3 Decomposition of Power

Because the ANE has no readable per-component power, we decompose the power by comparing differences between workloads. Each of the following items is an estimate based on differences 〔SMC, inferred〕.

We first consider the shared overhead between the two ANEs. The power under dual-ANE full load (10.77 W) is less than twice the power under single-ANE full load (6.21 W), with a difference of about 1.6 W. This indicates that as long as one ANE is working, about 1.6 W of power is shared by the two ANEs, and the second ANE does not need to incur it again. We speculate that this portion arises from the interconnect and clock shared by the two ANEs, or from the shared cluster in the CLPC (Section 11.3), but no per-component power readings are available to confirm this. After subtracting the shared overhead, the compute portion of each ANE is about 4.6 W, which amortizes to about 0.58 pJ per FP16 MAC. Without this subtraction, the single-ANE total is about 0.79 pJ/MAC and the dual-ANE total is about 0.72 pJ/MAC.

Next, we consider INT8 MACs. Each call of the W8A8 workload reads about 100 MB of weights from DRAM, so the power of reading weights must first be subtracted. The weight-read-bound workload reads the same amount of weights at a similar rate, and its ANE net power is about 2.2 W. Subtracting this from the W8A8 workload leaves about 5.1 W. Both workloads use only one ANE, so under the model above the shared overhead is present in both and cancels in the subtraction. The 5.1 W is therefore the power of the INT8 computation itself, which amortizes to about 0.42 pJ/MAC at 12.34 T Int8 MAC/s. This should be compared with the FP16 value of 0.58 pJ/MAC after the shared overhead has been subtracted; it is about 71% of that value. The weight-read-bound workload itself also contains a small amount of computation (0.77 TMAC/s), and the subtraction also removes the power of this computation. If we add back about 0.45 W, computed from 0.58 pJ per FP16 MAC, the INT8 value becomes about 0.45 pJ/MAC, which is 77% of FP16. The direct-read PP0b measurement gives about 77% under the same convention (adding back the computation in the weight-read workload) as well. As a cross-check, without any subtraction, the ANE net power of the W8A8 workload amortizes to 0.59 pJ per MAC, and that of FP16 to 0.79 pJ, giving a ratio of about 75%. Taken together, we estimate that the energy per INT8 MAC is about 70–80% of that of FP16. The INT8 throughput is about 1.58 times that of FP16, while the power is only about 18% higher, so the energy efficiency is about 1.3 times that of FP16. This is similar to the result above that the energy efficiency of W8A8 is about 1.4 times that of FP16, although the two sets of workloads differ.

The power of reading DRAM can be separated from the weight-read-bound workload. The weight-read-bound workload reads weights at about 96.6 GB/s, and the sum of the ANE net power and the memory power rail is about 4.0 W, which corresponds to about 42 pJ per byte. Of this, the memory power rail accounts for about 19 pJ/B, and the ANE net power (including DMA and a small amount of computation) accounts for no more than about 23 pJ/B.

Power measurement of elementwise operations requires separate treatment. In the experiment, the input and output data of the PE elementwise addition both reside in L2 and generate no significant DRAM traffic. The ANE net power for this row was computed using the powered-off baseline of an adjacent run in the same round, because the powered-off baseline recorded in this run itself (−1.71 W) deviates markedly from the other runs in the same round (−1.12 to −1.20 W) 〔SMC〕. The energy per element was computed as "the full-load reading minus the powered-on idle reading of the same run." The full-load reading and the powered-on idle reading share the same baseline, so this anomaly does not affect the result. The energy per element is about 53 pJ for elementwise addition and about 61 pJ for elementwise multiplication. Each element reads two operands from L2 and writes back one result, so this energy comes mainly from on-chip data movement. Relative to the 0.58 pJ per FP16 MAC after shared overhead is subtracted, it corresponds to about 90–105 FP16 MACs. The PE workload also increases the memory power rail by about 1.1 W, whereas IOReport shows that the DRAM read bandwidth is almost zero in this case; the reason is unclear. Combined with the observation in Section 11.4 that the PE workload raises the voltage of the SoC interconnect, this portion of power may come from traffic in the interconnect or the system-level cache 〔inferred〕.

The values of the input data also affect power. When all PE inputs are zero, the ANE power decreases by about 0.42–0.45 W (about 17%). This value agrees between the two alternating measurements in [power_parts2/](../data/11_power/power_parts2/) and in power_parts4, and the memory power rail decreases by about 40% at the same time. This is consistent with the rule that "dynamic power decreases when data do not toggle" 〔SMC〕.

When measuring the effect of zero activations, the bias of the model must be zero. With a nonzero bias and ReLU between layers, the activations converge after several tens of layers to a set of values determined by the bias, so an all-zero input affects only the first few layers. We therefore used a 1×1 convolution chain with zero bias (512 channels, 1×128, 128 layers; when the input is all zero, the input of every layer is zero). We alternated between random inputs, all-zero inputs, and inputs with "one nonzero constant per channel," with two rounds for each, and computed the power as in Table 13-2 (data: [power_zero/](../data/11_power/power_zero/)) 〔SMC, IOReport〕. With random inputs, each call took about 690 µs and the ANE net power was 5.11 W. With all-zero inputs, the latency was unchanged and the power decreased to 2.15–2.18 W, a reduction of about 58%. This corresponds to a decrease in energy per MAC from about 0.82 pJ to about 0.35 pJ. With one nonzero constant per channel, the power was 5.0–5.2 W, the same as with random inputs. These results show that the dynamic power of the ANE decreases substantially when it encounters zero operands, but it does not skip these MAC operations (Section 7.5). In other words, zero activations save power but not time. The saving comes from the zero values themselves, rather than from activations being spatially constant 〔SMC, timing〕.

We repeated this measurement on the M4 (data: [power_gate_m4/](../data/11_power/power_gate_m4/)). The SMC readings on the M4 are updated about once per second and are instantaneous, so they fluctuate widely under steady load. Each segment was therefore extended to 60 s, two passes were run in alternating reverse order, the machine was prevented from sleeping during the measurements, and the call latency was checked segment by segment to exclude ramp-down. Two segments with anomalous powered-off baselines were computed using the normal values of the same round 〔SMC, timing〕. The results agree with those of the M6. With random inputs, the ANE net power was 5.08–5.32 W, and with all-zero inputs it was 2.14–2.18 W, a reduction of about 58%. With inputs of all −0 (bit pattern 0x8000), the reduction was similarly about 58%. With one nonzero constant per channel, the power was the same as with random inputs 〔SMC〕.

When only part of the activations are zero, the ANE power generally decreases as the fraction of zeros increases. We used the masking model of Section 7.5, with the fraction of zeros in each layer's input and their spatial arrangement fixed (data: [power_gate_m4/](../data/11_power/power_gate_m4/), [power_pair_m4/](../data/11_power/power_pair_m4/)). Because the masking operation adds one multiplication per layer, the absolute power of this set of models is higher than in the all-zero experiments described above. However, the models share exactly the same graph structure and weights, and the single-call time of each segment is 718–738 µs, so the power at different zero fractions can be compared directly. For randomly distributed zeros, when the fraction of zeros in each layer's input is 0%, 50%, 75%, and 90%, the ANE net power is about 8.24 W, 5.99 W, 4.33 W, and 3.03 W, respectively. Extrapolated to 100% zeros, it is about 2.0 W. If the power difference between 0% and all-zero is taken as the saveable portion, then the zero fractions of 50%, 75%, and 90% realize about 36%, 63%, and 83% of the saveable power, respectively. These values are lower than the values expected from linear scaling with the zero fraction (50%, 75%, and 90%). The measured energy saving is about 7–14 percentage points below the linear expectation, and this deviation is more pronounced at lower zero fractions 〔SMC〕.

To test whether the power saving requires adjacent zeros to occur in pairs, we rearranged the zeros at a fixed 50% fraction in each layer. Zeros were paired along the channel or width direction, arranged either aligned (2k, 2k+1) or offset by one (2k+1, 2k+2). If the hardware gates in units of two elements, the aligned pairs should save about 1 W more than the offset pairs. If the circuit does not toggle only when the operands of two adjacent cycles are both zero, the paired zeros should save noticeably more power than randomly distributed zeros. The measured difference between aligned and offset arrangements is only 0.04–0.14 W, and the paired arrangement (5.78–5.92 W) is only 1–4% lower than the random arrangement (5.99 W). Both mechanisms can therefore be ruled out 〔SMC, inferred〕. Consequently, the power saving from zero activations is largely elementwise: each zero saves its own share, independently of neighboring elements. Additional savings appear only when zeros occur in large blocks. When half of the channels are entirely zero, the power is 5.07 W, about 0.9 W lower than with randomly distributed zeros at the same fraction. We speculate that the upstream data path or the L2 reads also save power in this case 〔inferred〕. The specific mechanism of the elementwise saving may be that the hardware turns off the clock of that lane upon detecting a zero, or that the multiplier rarely toggles internally when one multiplicand is zero. Power measurements alone cannot distinguish these possibilities. The compiler has a dedicated zero-weight detection mechanism on the weight side (Section 7.5), but on the activation side we have not found a corresponding field 〔compiler, inferred〕.

Finally, we consider the case where the ANE is powered on with no task. After the workload ends and before the firmware processor is powered off (Section 12.5), the ANE is powered on but has no task. In this state, the readings of the ANE net power lie between 0 and 0.6 W, which is within the measurement error. In the direct-read PP0b measurements, this reading does not exceed 0.05 W (data: [power_parts2/](../data/11_power/power_parts2/)). We speculate that the compute array turns off its clock or power quickly between tasks, and that the power of the firmware processor itself is below the resolution of the SMC 〔inferred〕. Figure 13-1 shows the breakdown of the ANE power.

![Power breakdown](../figs/en/light/fig13-1_power_breakdown.png)

*Figure 13-1. Breakdown of ANE power. (a) ANE net power for each workload class after subtracting the CPU cluster; the dashed light-gray segment indicates the increase in the memory power rail over the same period, and for FP16 convolution, the difference between the dual-ANE power and twice the single-ANE power is separated as a shared overhead of about 1.6 W. (b) Energy per MAC, per byte read from DRAM, and per PE element, estimated from differences between workloads (data: [power_parts4/](../data/11_power/power_parts4/), [power_zero/](../data/11_power/power_zero/)).*

### 13.4 DVFS State and Energy per Inference

Chapter 12 shows that when the host inserts idle time between calls, the CLPC lowers the NE DVFS state of the ANE. Table 13-3 gives the power and the energy per inference of the same single-ANE model (1.29 × 10¹⁰ MACs per inference) at different sleep times. This set of data comes from direct-read PP0b measurements. In a re-measurement with the CPU cluster subtracted, we measured one point at a sleep time of 3200 µs, and the ANE net power was 0.43 W, which agrees with the value of 0.46 W in the table (data: [power_parts/](../data/11_power/power_parts/), [power_parts2/](../data/11_power/power_parts2/), [power_parts4/](../data/11_power/power_parts4/)) 〔SMC〕.

*Table 13-3. Sleep time before each call and ANE energy per inference*

| Sleep time | Per call | ANE power | Whole system | ANE energy per inference |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | 1647 µs | 6.29 W | 10.0 W | 10.4 mJ |
| 100 µs | 1539 µs | 6.17 W | 9.8 W | 10.1 mJ |
| 300 µs | 3696 µs | 1.00 W | 2.8 W | 4.0 mJ |
| 800 µs | 4790 µs | 0.71 W | 2.3 W | 4.0 mJ |
| 1600 µs | 4562 µs | 0.61 W | 2.6 W | 3.7 mJ |
| 3200 µs | 4577 µs | 0.46 W | 2.1 W | 3.5 mJ |
| 6400 µs | 4615 µs | 0.31 W | 1.8 W | 3.4 mJ |

Once an idle gap of about 300 µs or more is inserted between calls, the CLPC no longer drives the ANE to the top state. The per-call time increases by a factor of 2.2–2.9, and the ANE energy per inference falls to between 1/2.6 and 1/3.1 of its value at the top state. According to the measurements in Section 12.2, at a sleep time of 300 µs the ANE runs at about 1032 MHz, and at longer sleep times it runs at the lowest state of 852 MHz. Under the rule that dynamic power is proportional to the product of frequency and the square of voltage, the energy per inference depends mainly on voltage, and the reduction in energy at low states comes mainly from the lower voltage. The factor by which energy decreases is close to the factor by which latency increases, but this similarity is a numerical coincidence 〔inferred〕. This result also indicates that the static power of the ANE is small. If static power accounted for a large share, running at a low state for a long time would accumulate static energy and cancel the savings from lower voltage, so that "finish quickly and power off" would save more energy. The measurements show the opposite. Figure 13-2 shows the ANE energy per inference and the call interval.

![Energy per inference and DVFS state](../figs/en/light/fig13-2_energy_vs_level.png)

*Figure 13-2. ANE energy per inference and call interval. For the same single-ANE FP16 model with a sleep of 0–6400 µs before each call: (a) ANE energy per inference, computed as ANE power × (per-call time + sleep time); diamonds show the re-measurement at a sleep of 3200 µs with the CPU cluster subtracted; (b) per-call time and the corresponding NE DVFS state (data as in Table 13-3).*

### 13.5 Fixed Energy per Call

For small models called at high frequency, the distribution of energy differs entirely from that of large models. The 16-layer small model in Table 13-2 takes only 268 µs per call, or about 3700 calls per second. In this case the ANE net power is only 2.08 W, whereas the power of the `PACC0` cluster is as high as 7.29 W. In other words, the Core ML calling thread causes the CPU cluster to draw about 6 W more than when idle, which amortizes to about 1.6 mJ per call. On the other hand, using the value of about 0.79 pJ/MAC for the 384-layer model in Table 13-2, the computation of the 16-layer model requires about 0.43 mJ per call, whereas the actual ANE energy per call is about 0.56 mJ. The fixed overhead of the ANE itself per call is therefore about 0.13 mJ 〔SMC, IOReport〕. At this measurement point the CPU cluster was under high load. According to Section 13.1.2, the error of the baseline when the CPU cluster is subtracted may reach about 1 W, which amortizes to about ±0.27 mJ per call and is larger than the 0.13 mJ itself. Therefore, this value shows only that the fixed overhead of the ANE does not exceed about 0.4 mJ.

Accordingly, the reason that frequent small calls consume substantial energy lies mainly not in the ANE but in the calling overhead on the host CPU side. For each call, the CPU must prepare the request, wait for the completion notification, and wake the calling thread (Section 6.5). The energy of this work is at least about four times the fixed overhead of the ANE.

### 13.6 Power Model of the Compiler

The performance-model CSV described in Section 7.1 contains a column `power(W)`. We disassembled the function `perfmodel::EnginePerf::ComputePowerMetric` that generates this column, and found that it computes only the energy of memory accesses. The power equals the number of bytes accessed through the three paths of Tile DMA and the Kernel DMA, multiplied by the energy per byte, divided by the estimated execution time. The energy per byte takes only two values. When the data volume of a given path does not exceed the capacity of the system-level cache (16 MiB) and the cache flag is set, it is taken as 10 pJ/B (treated as a cache hit); otherwise it is taken as 50 pJ/B (treated as a DRAM access). These two constants are taken from offset 0x890 of the HAL parameter table 〔compiler, HAL〕. We checked this formula against specific rows of the CSV, and in each case the result was exactly 50.0 pJ/B. It follows that the compiler does not build power models for the MAC array, the L2, or the PE, so the `power(W)` column cannot be used to decompose the power of the ANE. On the other hand, the 50 pJ/B used by the compiler is consistent in order of magnitude with the total energy of reading DRAM measured in this report (about 42 pJ/B).

### 13.7 Comparison

This section compares the energy efficiency of the M6 ANE at three levels: comparison with the M4 ANE of the previous generation, comparison with the CPU and GPU on the same chip, and comparison with NPUs and accelerators from other vendors. The comparability decreases in this order, and attention must be paid to the measurement basis used in each comparison.

#### 13.7.1 Comparison with the M4

maderix [4] reports that the ANE of the M4 draws zero power when idle, 4.57 W at full load with FP16 (4.1 TFLOPS/W), and 12.25 W at full load with W8A8 (2.9 TOPS/W). That is, the throughput of W8A8 is 1.95 times that of FP16, while its power is 2.7 times that of FP16. The results of the M6 differ from these in two respects. First, on the M6, W8A8 is more energy-efficient than FP16 (about 1.4 times), whereas on the M4, W8A8 is less energy-efficient than FP16. Second, the FP16 energy efficiency of a single ANE on the M6 is about 2.5 TFLOPS/W, which is lower than the value for the M4 reported by maderix. Because this report found that PP0b on the M4 likewise supplies power to both the ANE and the CPU cluster, and because different studies use different power sources and subtraction methods, these values should not be compared directly. For reference, the ANE net power of the M4 ranges from about 5 to 9 W depending on the workload. The convolution models in Section 13.3 give 5.1–8.2 W, another FP16 convolution workload of a different shape gives about 8.7 W, and the ANE power reported by `powermetrics` over the same period is 9.6 W 〔SMC, IOReport〕. maderix reported a power of 2.8 W in Parts 2 and 3 of the series, which was later corrected in Part 4 to a reading taken with low-power mode enabled [2–4]. This also indicates that the power of the ANE is highly sensitive to the system state.

#### 13.7.2 Comparison with the CPU and GPU on the Same Chip

On the same chip, a comparison in which the energy of the CPU and GPU performing the same operations is measured with the same method is the most convincing, because it removes differences in process, packaging, and measurement basis. For reference, a previous study used `powermetrics` on the M1–M4 and measured an energy efficiency of 0.21–0.46 TFLOPS/W for FP32 matrix multiplication on the GPU [22], which corresponds to about 4–10 pJ per FP32 MAC. This figure differs by a factor of about 5–12 from the FP16 energy efficiency of the M6 ANE (about 2.5 TFLOPS/W). However, the precisions differ (FP32 versus FP16), and so do the measurement methods, so the comparison can indicate only the order of magnitude.

#### 13.7.3 Comparison with Other NPUs and Accelerators

Table 13-4 summarizes the energy efficiency of other NPUs and accelerators reported in the public literature.
