The data in the table all come from primary sources, including academic conference papers, vendor datasheets, and papers published by vendors. All values have been converted to TOPS/W and pJ/MAC, where one MAC is counted as two operations, so pJ/MAC = 2 ÷ TOPS/W. The "Basis" column of the table distinguishes two types of data: one is peak throughput divided by the rated power or thermal design power (TDP), and the other is measured power.

*Table 13-4. Energy efficiency of the M6 ANE and other NPUs and accelerators*

| Device | Process | Precision | Energy efficiency | pJ/MAC | Basis | Power range | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| M6 ANE, lowest state (852 MHz) | 2 nm | FP16 | 7.2 TFLOPS/W | 0.28 | Measured throughput ÷ measured power | Same as above | This report |
| M6 ANE, dual ANE | 2 nm | INT8 (W8A8) | 3.4 TOPS/W | 0.59 | Measured throughput ÷ measured power | PP0b (CPU cluster not subtracted) | This report |
| M4 ANE | — | FP16 / W8A8 | 4.1 TFLOPS/W / 2.9 TOPS/W | 0.49 / 0.69 | Measured | Subtraction method not stated | maderix [4] |
| Samsung Exynos 9820 NPU | 8 nm | INT8, exploiting sparsity | 11.5 TOPS/W | 0.17 | Chip measurement, best operating point | NPU core | Song et al. [14] |
| MediaTek 5G SoC APU | 7 nm | INT8, etc. | 3.4–13.3 TOPS/W | 0.15–0.59 | Chip measurement, varying with voltage and load | NPU core | Lin et al. [15] |
| Samsung Exynos 2100 NPU | 5 nm | INT8, exploiting sparsity | 13.6 TOPS/W | 0.15 | Chip measurement (Inception-v3) | NPU core | Jang et al. [16] |
| Google Edge TPU | — | INT8 | 2 TOPS/W | 1.0 | Peak ÷ rated power (4 TOPS, 2 W) | Module | Datasheet [17] |
| NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin 64GB | 8 nm | INT8, dense | About 2.3 TOPS/W | About 0.87 | Peak ÷ maximum power (about 138 TOPS, 60 W) | Whole module, including CPU and DRAM | Datasheet [18] |
| Google TPU v1 | 28 nm | INT8 | 2.3 TOPS/W | 0.87 | Peak ÷ measured busy power (92 TOPS, 40 W) | Chip | Jouppi et al. [12] |
| Google TPU v4 | 7 nm | BF16 | 1.6 TFLOPS/W | 1.23 | Peak ÷ measured average power (275 TFLOPS, 170 W) | Chip and HBM | Jouppi et al. [13] |
| NVIDIA A100 SXM | 7 nm | FP16 / INT8, dense | 0.78 TFLOPS/W / 1.56 TOPS/W | 2.56 / 1.28 | Peak ÷ TDP (400 W) | Whole card | Datasheet [19] |
| NVIDIA H100 SXM | 4 nm | FP16 / INT8, dense | 1.41 TFLOPS/W / 2.83 TOPS/W | 1.42 / 0.71 | Peak ÷ TDP (700 W) | Whole card | Datasheet [20] |
| Eyeriss | 65 nm | 16-bit fixed-point | — | About 12 | Chip measurement (AlexNet convolutional layers) | Chip | Chen et al. [21] |

Mainstream NPUs in laptop and mobile platforms are not included in the table. These include the Hexagon NPU of the Qualcomm Snapdragon X Elite (45 TOPS), the NPU 4 of the Intel Lunar Lake (48 TOPS), the XDNA 2 of the AMD Ryzen AI 300 (50 TOPS), and the NPU of the MediaTek Dimensity 9400. The vendors have published only the peak INT8 throughput and the energy-efficiency ratios relative to predecessors or competitors, not the absolute power of the NPUs, so these values cannot be converted to TOPS/W. Google has also not published the throughput and power of the TPU in the Tensor chip. The MLPerf Mobile benchmark does not report energy, and it is likewise unusable for this comparison.

The values in Table 13-4 differ widely in their bases, and four points must be considered when comparing them. First, the difference between peak and measured values. The M6 data in this report are the effective throughput under realistic workloads divided by the measured power-rail power. The values in the vendor datasheets are peak throughput divided by the rated power or TDP. The former overestimates the throughput, and the latter usually overestimates the power, so the biases act in opposite directions and the net effect is uncertain. Among datacenter accelerators, the bases closest to that of this report are those of TPU v1 and TPU v4, which use measured power, but whose throughput is still peak. The ISSCC chips in the table measured both throughput and power, but the measurement scope was limited to the NPU core. Second, the difference between sparse and dense values. The TOPS values given prominently in NVIDIA datasheets are usually computed for structured sparsity, and the table converts them to dense values. The TOPS/W values in the Samsung ISSCC and ISCA papers count skipped zero-valued operations and should be regarded as best-case values. Third, the difference in power boundaries. The ISSCC papers report the power of the NPU core at the best voltage point, the Jetson figure is the power of the whole module, the TPU v4 figure includes HBM, and the net power of the ANE in this report (PP0b minus the PACC0 cluster) excludes DRAM. Relative to the TPU v4 and the Jetson, the basis of this report favors the M6. Relative to the ISSCC data, which count only the core, it disfavors the M6. Fourth, the difference in precision and process. Mobile NPU vendors mostly provide only INT8 data, and comparable FP16 data are scarce. From the 65 nm Eyeriss to modern NPUs at 4–8 nm, the energy per MAC has decreased by more than an order of magnitude, and most of this reduction comes from process advances.

Within these limitations, we can draw two conclusions with reservations.

First, the FP16 energy efficiency of the M6 ANE at the top state, about 2.5–2.8 TFLOPS/W, is of the same order as that of the H100 (1.41 TFLOPS/W) and the TPU v4 (1.6 TFLOPS/W) in datacenters, and higher than that of the A100 (0.78 TFLOPS/W). However, the INT8 energy efficiencies reported in three mobile NPU chip papers are generally higher than the measured INT8 value of the M6 at the top state (about 3.4 TOPS/W). Song et al. [14] reported at ISSCC 2019 that the NPU of the Samsung Exynos 9820 reaches 11.5 TOPS/W. Lin et al. [15] reported at ISSCC 2020 that the deep-learning accelerator in a 7 nm 5G mobile SoC from MediaTek reaches 3.4–13.3 TOPS/W. Jang et al. [16] reported at ISCA 2021 that the NPU of the Samsung Exynos 2100 reaches 13.6 TOPS/W on Inception-v3. All three values count the power of the NPU core only, and two of the Samsung papers also count skipped zero-valued operations. Part of the gap between these values and the M6 therefore arises from differences in basis.

Second, the energy efficiencies in ISSCC papers are often given as a range, such as 3.4–13.3 TOPS/W for Lin et al. [15]. Such a range reflects how the energy efficiency of the same accelerator varies with operating voltage, load, and data precision. The M6 shows a similar phenomenon: at the lowest state (852 MHz), the FP16 energy efficiency of the M6 is about 7.2 TFLOPS/W, about three times that at the top state (Section 13.4). This value falls within the range reported by Lin et al. [15]. However, the M6 value is a result at FP16 precision, and the values in that range are not necessarily at the same precision, so the two cannot simply be equated. Accordingly, a comparison of energy efficiency among NPUs depends largely on which operating point and which precision are compared. Therefore, when comparing with other NPUs, the energy efficiency of the M6 at both the top state and the lowest state should be given, with the precision stated.

### 13.8 Summary

The M6 has no readable power rail dedicated to the ANE. The SMC power rail PP0b supplies power to both the ANE and the S and P cores of the CPU, so the power of the ANE must be obtained after the power of the CPU cluster is subtracted. Using this method, a single ANE draws about 6.2 W at full load, dual ANEs draw about 10.8 W at full load, the shared overhead between them is about 1.6 W, and the ANE draws 0 W when idle. After the shared overhead is subtracted, an FP16 MAC consumes about 0.58 pJ, and an INT8 MAC consumes about 70–80% of that. An elementwise PE operation consumes about 50–60 pJ per element, mainly due to on-chip data movement, and reading data from DRAM consumes about 42 pJ/B. When all activations of a convolution are zero, the ANE power decreases by about 58% on both the M6 and the M4, while the latency is unchanged. When the activations are partially zero, the power saving increases monotonically with the fraction of zeros (but is lower than proportional expectation), is independent of whether the zeros occur in pairs, and yields additional savings when zeros occur in large blocks. When the CLPC selects a low state, the energy per inference is about one-third of that at the top state, which indicates that the static power of the ANE is small. For frequent small calls, most of the energy is consumed on the host CPU side, about 1.6 mJ per call, which is at least about four times the fixed overhead of the ANE. The power model of the compiler computes only the energy of memory accesses and cannot be used to decompose the power of the ANE.

The composition of the shared overhead of about 1.6 W remains undetermined, and so does whether the power saving of zero activations comes from clock gating or from fewer multiplier toggles. Both questions require per-component power readings (Section 16.2). Comparing the energy of the CPU, GPU, and ANE in performing the same operations on the same chip is likewise deferred to future work (Section 16.3). The deployment recommendations derived from the conclusions of this chapter are summarized in Section 15.2 (Table 15-1).

## 14. Application Benchmarks

Earlier chapters used purpose-built microbenchmarks to study the individual parts of the M6 ANE, from which several inferences about real workloads can be drawn. This chapter tests those inferences against practical application workloads. The data come from a set of application benchmarks that we ran on two Mac mini systems, M6 and M4, covering three types of task: large language model (LLM) workloads, image classification, and text recognition. Speech-to-text speed data were affected by a transient system state and are not used; only the compilation observations from that run are retained. The application benchmarks recorded only per-call time, ANE busy fraction, memory read rate, and power; they did not record firmware timestamps or per-engine interrupt counts. Moreover, the comparison spans two chip generations, so differences in clock, bandwidth, and host CPU are confounded with one another. Accordingly, what this chapter can test is whether the results agree in direction and approximate magnitude with the expectations. A phenomenon consistent with a mechanism does not validate that mechanism, and we point out, item by item, every factor that was not separated.

This chapter first derives five classes of observable expectations from the conclusions of earlier chapters (Section 14.2), and then reports the results group by group. Each group answers three questions: which expectation it matches, how far it can be explained, and what remains unseparated. This chapter has four main conclusions. First, on compute-dense tasks with large work per call, the M6 ANE is 1.8–2.7 times faster than the M4, far above the ratio of about 1.1 for a single engine's peak, which is consistent with the expectation that the second engine mainly benefits compute-dense tasks. However, the 2.69× of text detection exceeds the dual-engine peak ratio (about 2.2×), and this report cannot explain the excess. Second, the speedup narrows markedly on tasks with very little work per call; when processing one image at a time, the M6 is only 1.42× faster, in the direction expected from the growing share of host overhead. Calculated from the host overhead corresponding to the task duration, this accounts for about two-thirds of the additional time on the M6, while the remaining about 0.15 ms is not separated. Third, LLM decode is 1.86× faster on the M6, but the ratio of engine counts and the ratio of weight-read bandwidth are both about 2, so this factor by itself cannot show whether decode is limited by weight reads or by calls. When converted to weight-read volume, the M6 uses only about 60% of its weight-read bound, below the about 70% of the M4; reducing the number of calls raises speed by 31%. This agrees with the expectation that decode is limited by both weight reads and the number of calls per token, but the custom model also changes the weight bit width, so the effect of the number of calls is not separated on its own. Fourth, the model that reduces the number of calls draws about 0.3 W of host CPU power, below the about 1 W of the standard model, which is consistent with the expectation that energy shifts toward the host under frequent calls. In addition, the ANE is more energy-efficient than the GPU on the same machine for every task whose power was measured.

### 14.1 Test Setup

The application benchmarks were run on two Mac mini systems, each with 16 GB of memory and 256 GB of storage, both running macOS 27.0.1 (26A434). On both machines, other applications and iCloud sync were turned off, and the same model files, verified by SHA256 checksum, were used. Speed tests were run without monitoring. For ANE busy fraction, memory read bandwidth, and power, a separate run with monitoring was performed; monitoring data were obtained from anemon [27], `powermetrics`, SMC power rails, and IORegistry. Small-model tasks were run with Core ML, with the compute units set to `CPU_AND_NE`; for power comparisons, the same model files were also run with `CPU_AND_GPU` as a reference.

Power data must be interpreted with caution. As described in Chapter 13, the M6 has no readable power rail dedicated to the ANE, so the M6 ANE power in the benchmarks was estimated from SMC power rails. PP0b also supplies the S cores and P cores of the CPU, so this value is biased upward when the host-side runner occupies many CPU cores. In addition, the M4, which is used as a daily-use machine, often had background programs running during testing, and whole-system idle power varied between 7 and 15 W. Accordingly, Section 14.5 relies mainly on "whole-system power minus idle power" when comparing energy efficiency. The benchmark data of this chapter are kept in our benchmark records; at present, only the ANE resource curves used in Figure 14-1 have been organized into the code repository (Appendix B.3).

### 14.2 Testable Expectations

Based on the conclusions of the earlier chapters, we formulate five classes of expectations for practical workloads. To provide a comparison across the two chip generations, we give, where possible, the approximate magnitude of M6 relative to M4.

Expectation 1: compute-dense tasks with large work per call benefit most from the second engine. The FP16 direct-convolution peak of a single M6 engine is 21.1 TFLOPS, and that of the M4 is about 19 TFLOPS (Table 7-14), so the M6 is only about 10% higher; the two M6 engines together provide 42.3 TFLOPS, and on compute-bound networks the scaling efficiency is about 96% (Section 10.3). Therefore, if the compiler splits this class of task across both engines, the M6 should be about 2× faster than the M4, with an upper bound of about 2.2×, the peak ratio; if no split occurs, it should be only about 1.1× faster.

Expectation 2: the smaller the work per call, the smaller the speedup. Each synchronous call incurs about 0.16–0.36 ms of host overhead outside the ANE task, which grows with task duration (Section 6.5), and a dual-ANE program adds about 30 µs more (Section 10.3). This overhead does not shrink as the ANE gets faster; the shorter the task, the larger its share.

Expectation 3: decode of a large language model is limited by weight reads and also by the number of calls per token. Every generated token requires reading all weights, and the second engine does not add usable weight-read bandwidth (Sections 9.3.4, 10.4, and 10.7); the measured M = 1 matrix multiplications are also not split across two engines (Section 5.8.4). Therefore, decode speed should be determined by weight-read bandwidth (about 67 GB/s for the M4 and about 145–150 GB/s for the M6; Section 9.6) and reduced by the host overhead per call; reducing the number of calls per token should raise the ANE busy fraction.

Expectation 4: when some operations remain on the CPU, the ANE speedup of a model is diluted. Core ML places operations that the ANE does not support on the CPU (Section 5.2), so the overall speedup is limited by the CPU portion.

Expectation 5: under frequent calls, a substantial share of energy is spent on the host CPU (Section 13.5); reducing the number of calls should lower host-side power.

It should be noted that Expectations 1 and 3 both give a ratio of about 2 in the cross-generation comparison, so the M6 to M4 ratio alone cannot distinguish compute-bound from weight-read-bound behavior; auxiliary indicators such as the ANE busy fraction and memory read rate are needed. The following sections test each group in turn.

### 14.3 Large Language Models

#### 14.3.1 Standard Model

The test model is Qwen3.5-4B converted to Core ML format, with weights stored using 4-bit and 6-bit palettization; each generated token requires reading about 2.56 GB of weights. The model is split into 8 segments of 4 layers each, and each segment has two functions: per-token decode, and prefill in batches of 128 tokens (prefill, that is, processing a batch of input tokens at a time). Table 14-1 compares the speed of the two ANE generations.

*Table 14-1. Speed of Qwen3.5-4B on the ANE (tokens/s)*

| Test | M4 ANE | M6 ANE | M6 ÷ M4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Prefill, 512 tokens (batches of 128 tokens) | 556.1 | 1016.4 | 1.83 |
| Prefill, 4096 tokens (batches of 128 tokens) | 569.5 | 1044.0 | 1.83 |
| Decode, 128 tokens (native argmax) | 18.63 | 34.72 | 1.86 |
| Decode, 128 tokens (output returned to Python for each token) | 16.07 | 27.51 | 1.71 |

Prefill processes 128 tokens at a time and is compute-dense; the M6 ANE is 1.83 times faster than the M4. In decode, one token is processed at a time; with native argmax, the M6 is 1.86 times faster. If the 248320-dimensional logits vector of every token is returned to Python for processing, both machines become slower, and the M6 speedup falls to 1.71 times, which shows that host-side processing directly affects ANE decode speed.

The ANE resource curves recorded in the application benchmarks reveal the bottleneck of the decode phase, as shown in Table 14-2.

*Table 14-2. ANE resource curves of Qwen3.5-4B (prefill 3072 tokens, decode 384 tokens)*

| Machine | Phase | Duration | ANE busy fraction | ANE memory read | ANE power |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| M4 | Prefill | 5.5 s | 81% (steady segment ≈91%) | 19.3 GB/s | 3.2 W |
| M4 | Decode | 24.9 s | 76% (fluctuating within 35–93%) | 44.1 GB/s | 1.6 W |
| M6 | Prefill | 3.1 s | 68% (steady segment ≈84%) | 21.7 GB/s | ≈5.5 W |
| M6 | Decode | 11.4 s | 85% (steady) | at least 82 GB/s | ≈3.2 W |

In the decode phase, for each generated token the runner calls the 8 segment models and the output layer in sequence, and the host writes the key-value (KV) cache back; that is, each token involves 9 ANE calls. Section 6.5 shows that each synchronous call incurs several hundred microseconds of host overhead outside the ANE task, and this overhead leaves the ANE idle between calls. During decode, the M4 ANE is idle for about a quarter of the time, and its busy fraction fluctuates widely, which may well be caused by this overhead 〔inferred〕.

Compared with the expectations in Section 14.2, the prefill phase is consistent with Expectation 1: the 1.83× speedup is far above the ratio of about 1.1× for a single engine's peak, which indicates that the second engine contributes; a memory read rate of only about 20 GB/s together with a high ANE busy fraction also indicates that this phase is compute-bound. However, the speedup is below the expected ratio of about 2×. The ANE busy fraction of the M6 in prefill in Table 14-2 (68%, steady segment ≈84%) is lower than that of the M4 (81%, steady segment ≈91%), which is compatible with the larger share of host overhead between calls after the M6 became faster (Expectation 2). The benchmarks did not record per-engine interrupt counts, so this report cannot confirm whether all segments of the prefill are split across the two engines.

The performance gain in decode cannot be explained by weight-read bandwidth alone. The 1.86× is of the same order as the weight-read bandwidth ratio (about 2.2), but it is also comparable to the ratio of engine counts, so this single ratio cannot distinguish the two explanations. More informative is the rate converted by the weight volume read. Each token reads 2.56 GB, so the M4's 18.63 tokens/s correspond to about 48 GB/s, about 70% of its weight-read bound, while the M6's 34.72 tokens/s correspond to about 89 GB/s, only about 60% of the bound. The M6 is farther from its bound, yet its ANE busy fraction (85%) is higher than that of the M4 (76%). This indicates that during decode, the M6 ANE is busy for part of the time without reading weights at full bandwidth; this part of the time may come from key-value cache reads and writes, the output layer, and the overhead of smaller layers 〔inferred〕. Therefore, the decode result is consistent with Expectation 3, but the 1.86× cannot be taken directly as evidence that decode is limited by weight reads. Figure 14-1 shows the busy fraction and memory read rate curves of the two chip generations during prefill and decode.

![Busy fraction and memory read rate](../figs/en/light/fig14-1_busy_bandwidth.png)

*Figure 14-1. Resource curves of Qwen3.5-4B running on the ANE (prefill 3072 tokens, decode 384 tokens; anemon samples about every 0.25–0.3 s). The left column shows the standard model and the right column the custom model (MTP off). The top row is the ANE busy fraction (for the M6, the average of the two ANEs), and the bottom row is the memory read rate. The color bars above mark the prefill (dark) and decode (light) periods of each machine, with the horizontal axis timed from the start of prefill. The M6 read bandwidth is derived from IOReport histograms; during decode, some samples fall in the top histogram bin, and hollow points indicate that the readings are lower bounds only. The M4 busy fraction fluctuates widely during decode, between 35% and 93%, whereas that of the M6 is steady. The memory read rate of the M6 during decode is still far below its weight-read bound (data: [ane_curve/](../data/12_apps/ane_curve/); extraction: [ane_curve_extract.py](../tools/12_apps/ane_curve_extract.py)).*

#### 14.3.2 Custom Model for M6

We also optimized a version specifically for the M6 ANE to examine the upper bound of running a large language model on the ANE. Compared with the standard model, it splits the model into 4 segments of 8 layers each, which reduces the ANE calls per token from 9 to 5; all weights are quantized to 4 bits; the prefill computation graph is segmented in 32-token units; and speculative decoding based on multi-token prediction (MTP) is added, verifying 3 candidate tokens per call. The output quality of this version is low: its agreement rate with the original BF16 model is 80.0%, and its perplexity rises by 9.6%. Table 14-3 gives its speed.

*Table 14-3. Speed of the custom model on the ANE (tokens/s)*

| Test | M4 ANE | M6 ANE | M6 ÷ M4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Prefill, 512 tokens | 530.9 | 1093.1 | 2.06 |
| Prefill, 4096 tokens | 568.6 | 1164.5 | 2.05 |
| Plain decode | 22.62 | 45.64 | 2.02 |
| MTP decode (real Chinese prompt, mean 1.76 tokens per step) | 31.54 | 60.24 | 1.91 |

Compared with the standard model, the plain decode speed of the custom model on the M6 rises from 34.72 to 45.64 tokens/s, an increase of 31%; on the M4 it rises by 21%. The resource curves show that, during custom-model decode on the M6, the ANE busy fraction is about 89% and the memory read rate is at least 95 GB/s. With MTP enabled, the duration for the same 384 tokens falls from 8.4 s to 4.1 s, roughly halved, while ANE power remains essentially unchanged. The prompt in this test differs from that of Table 14-3, in which the MTP speedup is 1.32 times; the difference comes from the fact that the hit rate of speculative decoding varies with content. These results are consistent with the deployment principle of keeping the ANE busy, described in Section 15.2: reducing the number of calls per token reduces the host overhead and idle time between calls, so the ANE utilization rises, and the CLPC also more readily keeps the clock at high DVFS states (Chapter 12). Reducing the number of bits per weight (Section 7.5) and using speculative decoding to complete the work of several tokens in a single call are two other means. It should be noted that during custom-model decode, the ANE memory read rate is at least about 95 GB/s, about 63% of the weight-read bound, which is close to the about 60% of the standard model; therefore, the bottleneck cannot be said to have shifted to weight-read bandwidth. With respect to Expectation 3, after the number of calls was reduced, the ANE busy fraction of the M6 during decode rose from about 85% to about 89% (Figure 14-1), and the speed increased by 31%; the direction is consistent with the expectation. However, the custom model also quantizes all weights to 4 bits and changes the segmentation scheme at the same time. A weaker clue is that the change in weight bit width is the same on both machines: if decode were limited only by weight reads, the speedups on the two machines should be similar, whereas in fact the M6 improves by 31% and the M4 only by 21%. The M6 is faster, so the share of call overhead is larger, and the benefit of reducing the number of calls should also be larger; this is compatible with the number of calls playing a role 〔inferred〕. The prefill and decode of the custom model on the M6 are both about 2 times faster than on the M4, slightly higher than the 1.8 times of the standard model and closer to the about 2–2.2× given by Expectations 1 and 3; this is compatible with the explanation that "the fewer the calls, the more completely the difference between the two hardware generations is revealed" 〔inferred〕.

### 14.4 Vision and Speech Models

Table 14-4 summarizes the speeds of three classes of small-model tasks on the two ANE generations.

*Table 14-4. Speed of vision and speech models on the ANE*

| Task | M4 ANE | M6 ANE | M6 ÷ M4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ResNet-50 image classification, batch of 16 (images/s) | 606.4 | 1412.2 | 2.33 |
| ResNet-50 image classification, one image per call (images/s) | 585.6 | 830 | 1.42 |
| EasyOCR text detection, CRAFT (images/s) | 22.7 | 61.0 | 2.69 |
| EasyOCR text recognition, batches of 16 lines (lines/s) | 177.8 | 251.2 | 1.41 |

The tasks in Table 14-4 fall into two classes. The first class consists of tasks with large work per call. The text detection network performs convolutions on the entire 736×1280 image; all 63 operators run on the ANE, the ANE busy fraction exceeds 90% during execution, and the M6 is 2.69 times faster than the M4. When ResNet-50 processes 16 images at a time, the M6 is 2.33 times faster than the M4. Such tasks have fixed shapes and are compute-dense, and the computation per call is large enough to mask the call overhead. In the text recognition network, two bidirectional LSTMs can run only on the CPU; the ANE busy fraction is only 13–21%, and the speedup is only 1.41 times.

Relative to Expectation 1, the speedups of text detection and of ResNet-50 at a batch of 16 are far above the ratio of about 1.1× for a single engine's peak, which is consistent with the expectation that the second engine benefits compute-dense tasks. However, both exceed the dual-engine expectation: the 2.33× of ResNet-50 is slightly above the peak ratio of 2.2×, and the 2.69× of text detection clearly exceeds it. The peak ratio already incorporates the clock increase, so the excess cannot be explained by the clock. Possible causes are that the M4 does not itself reach its peak on these shapes (for example, because of tiling or weight-read limits; Chapter 9), or that the hardware Winograd of the M6 provides additional effective throughput for 3×3 convolutions (Section 7.4.3). The text recognition result is consistent with Expectation 4.

The second class consists of tasks with very little work per call. When ResNet-50 processes one image at a time, in the repeat measurement the M6 takes about 1.2 ms per call, or 830 images per second, whereas the M4 takes about 1.7 ms, or 586 images per second. The M6 is only 1.42 times faster, far less than the 2.33 times at a batch of 16. This direction agrees with Expectation 2: the smaller the work per call, the larger the share of fixed call overhead in total time, and the harder it is for the advantages of the compute array and the second engine to appear 〔timing〕.

The magnitude, however, is only partly explained. Converting the throughput at a batch of 16, the M6 needs about 0.71 ms per image and the M4 about 1.65 ms. When processing one image at a time, the M4 incurs about 0.06 ms of extra time per call, which does not exceed the fixed overhead of 0.09–0.16 ms per call for the M4 reported in the literature (Table 6-11). The median extra time per call for the M6 is about 0.5 ms, and about 0.85 ms if calculated from the average throughput of 640 images per second; the difference comes from a few slow calls (see below). Host overhead increases with task duration (Section 6.5.3). For a call of about 1.2 ms, the time outside the task is about 0.34–0.37 ms (Table 6-4); after subtracting the share amortized per image at a batch of 16, it is about 0.34 ms. This can explain about two-thirds of the extra median time, and the remaining about 0.15 ms may come from the start-up and synchronization of the dual-ANE program (a model run one image at a time is split across two ANEs; see below), or from reduced per-layer efficiency at small batch sizes.

Another phenomenon, unrelated to the transient state described above and reproducible, is that when the M6 processes one image at a time, the top-1 classification result disagrees with the GPU result for 5.9% of images, whereas at a batch of 16 and in all cases on the M4, the results agree completely. The M6 runs the model executed one image at a time split across two ANEs 〔IOReport〕. We suspect that the split changes the execution of some layers, for example operation fusion, whether Winograd is used, or whether intermediate results pass through DRAM with type conversion, and that this changes the top-1 result for a few samples near the decision boundary 〔inferred〕. Chapter 8 shows that fixed-point accumulation within a single pass is independent of summation order; however, whether multi-pass accumulation passes through FP16 intermediates has not been tested, so an accumulation-related cause cannot be entirely excluded. Confirming this requires a layer-by-layer comparison of the ANE and GPU outputs. This phenomenon is not among the expectations in Section 14.2; the numerical conclusions of Chapter 8 can exclude summation order within a single pass but cannot give the cause.

The benchmarks also encountered the compilation problem described in Chapter 5. On the M6, each new process name requires recompiling the Whisper ANE program, which takes more than 20 minutes. The repeat test in Section 5.9 shows that almost all of this time comes from the 1.2 GB audio encoder: the global refinement of the dual-ANE spatial split writes weight copies into swap files, about 2.4 GB per encoder layer, so the full encoder requires about 78 GB of such writes, whereas the M4 compiles the same encoder in only 80 s, using about 1.3 GB (defect A3). After the program was terminated midway, the compiler service continued to run and kept writing to swap files; during the benchmarks, the system volume was filled until only 121 MB remained, and the system subsequently purged the ANE compilation cache, after which every load had to be recompiled (defect A4). Under the same process name, the compiled result could otherwise have been reused on loading; that every load was recompiled in the benchmarks is precisely the consequence of the purged cache. Large language models are likewise affected by compilation cost: the first compilation of a model with about 4B parameters takes about 80 minutes. These phenomena correspond to the superlinear cost of the dual-engine compilation path, the fact that the compiler service runs only on the E cores, and blocking after the compiler service runs out of control, as described in Sections 5.9 and 5.10 (defects A2, A3, A4, B1, and B2). They are a reproduction on practical workloads of the defects described in Chapter 5, rather than a test of the expectations above.

### 14.5 Power

Table 14-5 gives the power of several tasks running on the ANE, and uses the results of the GPU running the same model on the same machine as an energy-efficiency reference. Because the ANE's own power on the M6 can be estimated only from power rails (Section 14.1), the table relies primarily on energy efficiency computed as whole-system power minus idle power.

*Table 14-5. ANE power and energy efficiency (GPU as reference)*

| Task | Machine | ANE power | ANE whole-system efficiency (idle subtracted) | ANE efficiency ÷ GPU efficiency |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Qwen3.5-4B decode (standard model) | M4 | 1.39 W | 1.91 tokens/J | 1.5 |
| | M6 | ≈1.85 W | 2.55 tokens/J | 1.09 |
| Qwen3.5-4B decode (custom model, MTP) | M4 | 1.95 W | 4.55 tokens/J | 3.6 |
| | M6 | ≈2.65 W | 5.32 tokens/J | 2.3 |
| ResNet-50, batch of 16 | M4 | 2.4 W | 85.6 images/J | 8.3 |
| | M6 | ≈2.9 W | 82.0 images/J | 2.1 |
| EasyOCR text detection | M4 | — | 3.06 images/J | 11.8 |
| | M6 | — | 3.32 images/J | 4.3 |

Across these tasks, the whole-system energy efficiency of the ANE is higher than that of the GPU on the same machine: it is 1.5–11.8 times that of the GPU on the M4 and 1.09–4.3 times that of the GPU on the M6. The ANE power in Table 14-5 comes from a dedicated power comparison test, whereas the power in Table 14-2 comes from a resource-curve test with anemon monitoring; the load segments and measurement windows of the two differ, so the values should not be compared directly. The ANE's energy-efficiency advantage on the M6 is smaller than on the M4. This is partly because the energy efficiency of the M6 GPU has improved considerably (converted with the ratios of Table 14-5, the whole-system efficiency of the M6 GPU on these four tasks is about 1.8–3.8 times that of the M4), and partly because the M4 tests were affected by background programs, whose higher idle power also affected the "idle-subtracted" calculation. During LLM decode, the ANE power is only about 1.4–3.2 W. Because the custom model makes fewer ANE calls per token, its host-side CPU power is only about 0.3 W, compared with about 1 W for the standard model. However, the improvement in whole-system efficiency comes mainly from speed. Calculated from Table 14-5, the whole-system power after subtracting idle power during decode on the M6 is about 13.6 W for the standard model and about 11.3 W for the custom model (MTP), a reduction of only about 17%, while the speed increases by about 1.7 times. The ANE and the host CPU account for only a small part of this power, and the remainder comes from system components such as memory and the interconnect. The ANE's energy-efficiency advantage over the GPU is not among the expectations in Section 14.2; it is reported here only as an observation.

### 14.6 Comparison of Expectations with Results

Figure 14-2 first places the M6 ÷ M4 speedups of all tasks side by side, and Table 14-6 then compares the five expectations of Section 14.2 item by item with the application benchmark results.

![Speedups of application tasks](../figs/en/light/fig14-2_speedup.png)

*Figure 14-2. M6 ÷ M4 speedups of the application tasks on the ANE (standard model). Colors correspond to the expectations in Section 14.2. The two dashed lines are reference values only: the single-engine FP16 peak ratio is about 1.1, and the dual-engine peak ratio is about 2.2 (the M4 peak values are themselves approximate; Table 7-14). Compute-dense tasks fall near the dual-engine reference, and text detection goes beyond it; tasks with small work per call and with some operations on the CPU are close to the single-engine reference (data: Table 14-1, Table 14-4).*

*Table 14-6. Comparison of expectations with results*

| Expectation | Result | Degree of agreement | Unseparated factors |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Expectation 1: compute-dense tasks benefit most from the second engine (Sections 7.9 and 10.3) | Prefill 1.83×; ResNet-50 at batch of 16 2.33×; text detection 2.69× | Direction agrees; all far above the single-engine ratio of about 1.1× | The part of prefill below about 2×; the part of text detection above the peak ratio of 2.2×; whether all tasks are split across both engines |
| Expectation 2: the smaller the work per call, the smaller the speedup (Section 6.5) | One image per call only 1.42×; M6 prefill busy fraction lower than M4 | Direction agrees; the median extra ≈0.5 ms per call for the M6 at one image per call is about two-thirds explained by host overhead matching task duration (≈0.34 ms) | The remaining ≈0.15 ms: start-up and synchronization of the dual-ANE program; layer efficiency at small batch sizes |
| Expectation 3: decode is limited jointly by weight reads and call count (Sections 6.5, 9.3, and 10.4) | Standard model 1.86×; by weight volume, M6 uses about 60% of its weight-read bound and M4 about 70%; after reducing calls, M6 speeds up by 31% | Compatible; the ratio alone cannot distinguish compute-bound from weight-read-bound behavior | Separate effects of call count and weight bit width; whether decode uses only one engine; time when the ANE is busy without reaching full bandwidth |
| Expectation 4: speedup is diluted when some operations stay on the CPU (Section 5.2) | Text recognition LSTMs remain on the CPU; ANE busy 13–21%; speedup 1.41× | Direction agrees | Difference in CPU portions between the two generations |
| Expectation 5: under frequent calls, energy shifts toward the host (Section 13.5) | Custom model host CPU ≈0.3 W; standard model ≈1 W | Direction agrees | Separate effects of call count and weight bit width |
| Outside expectations | M6 at one image per call: top-1 disagrees with GPU for 5.9% | Chapter 8 excludes only summation order within a single pass | Which layer executions are changed by the split |
| Reproduction of Chapter 5 defects | First compilation of the large model takes tens of minutes; compiler service runs out of control; compilation cache purged | Consistent with Sections 5.9 and 5.10 | — |

Beyond Table 14-6, the benchmark records contain several ANE peak-performance figures measured on a basis different from that of this report, and these require explanation. First, the benchmarks, using anebench on the fastest shapes, measured the M6 ANE FP16 compute at 50.1 TFLOPS and its INT8 compute at about 90 TOPS (for the M4, 18.8 TFLOPS and 38.2 TOPS, respectively). The M6 FP16 figure exceeds the theoretical FP16 direct-convolution peak of this report (42.3 TFLOPS for the dual ANE; Section 7.2). The most likely explanation is that the hardware Winograd acceleration (Section 7.4.3) is counted, that is, the fastest shape selected in the benchmarks uses 3×3 convolutions. If the weights of the benchmark model contain many zero values, zero-weight skipping may also contribute (see below) 〔inferred〕.

The benchmarks also found that on the M6, results with all-zero input are inflated to 55.6 TFLOPS FP16 and 106 TOPS INT8, whereas the M4 is not affected. The compute array does not skip zero activations (Section 7.5), so this is not an effect of zero activations. One candidate explanation is zero-weight detection: zero weights stored densely trigger this function, which is enabled on the M6 and not on the M4 (Sections 7.5 and 15.3). If the weights of the benchmark tool are also all zero in this mode, this would explain the difference between the two machines. Zero weights stored in a sparse format cannot explain it, because the M4 also skips them 〔inferred〕.

Second, the ANE read-memory bandwidth estimated by the benchmarks from anemon's matrix-multiply-vector load is about 66 GB/s for the M4 and about 133 GB/s for the M6. The M4 value agrees with the about 67 GB/s measured in this report on the M4 (Section 7.5). The M6 value is lower than the about 150 GB/s measured in this report from firmware task duration (Section 9.3.2), because the former is computed from total call time, which includes host overhead. Third, the estimate in the benchmark records that the ANE on-chip cache is about 14 MB on the M4 and about 32 MB on the M6 differs from the value obtained in this report from the compiler, namely that the L2 of each ANE is used at 2 MiB. We consider the former to reflect the inflection point of a workload on a performance curve rather than the capacity of on-chip storage. The same situation arose with maderix, who earlier inferred SRAM capacity from a drop in performance and later corrected that inference (Section 2.1).

### 14.7 Summary

This chapter used practical application workloads to test the inferences of the earlier chapters. The benchmark results are directionally compatible with all five classes of expectation. Expectations 1 and 2 are supported by relatively strong evidence, whereas Expectations 3 to 5 cannot yet be distinguished from other explanations. Compute-dense tasks (prefill, batched image classification, and text detection) are 1.8–2.7 times faster on the M6 than on the M4, far above the single-engine figure of about 1.1×, and the most likely source is the second engine. When the work per call is very small, the speedup narrows to about 1.4×. LLM decode is limited jointly by weight reads and call count. For the model customized for the M6, which reduces both the number of calls and the weight bit width, the plain decode speed of the ANE is about 31% higher than that of the standard model (45.64 vs. 34.72 tokens/s), and with speculative decoding (MTP) added, it reaches about 1.7 times the standard model (60.24 tokens/s). When some operations remain on the CPU, the ANE speedup is diluted. Reducing the number of calls also lowers host-side power. Measured by whole-system power, the ANE is more energy-efficient than the GPU on the same machine for all of these tasks.

The benchmarks, however, can test only direction and magnitude. In the two-generation comparison, the ratio of engine counts and the ratio of weight-read bandwidth are both about 2, so the decode ratio alone cannot distinguish the two limiting factors. The 2.69× of text detection exceeds the peak ratio, and only about two-thirds of the extra time of the M6 at one image per call can be explained by host overhead; these two points are not fully explained in this report. The custom model changes the number of calls and the weight bit width at the same time, and their effects are not separated. The top-1 disagreement with the GPU at one image per call lies outside the expectations. To turn these compatible phenomena into validation of the mechanisms, one needs to record firmware timestamps and per-engine interrupt counts on practical models, and to change only one factor at a time.

## 15. Discussion

Chapters 4–13 follow the lifecycle of a single inference call and examine, in turn, the compilation, scheduling, computation, numerics, memory, dual-engine cooperation, clocking, frequency scaling, and power of the M6 ANE. Chapter 14 then tests these findings on application workloads. This chapter does not repeat the conclusions of the individual chapters. Instead, it considers three questions together: which overall characteristics of the M6 ANE design these results reveal; what these characteristics imply for deploying models on the ANE; and what the M6 changes from, and retains of, earlier generations. Finally, this chapter summarizes the defects and undocumented behaviors found in the experiments and analyzes their common root causes.

### 15.1 Design Characteristics of the M6 ANE

Taken together, the experimental results of the individual chapters support the overall judgment presented in Section 1.3. The M6 ANE can be summarized by three interrelated system characteristics. This grouping does not correspond item by item to the three aspects of Section 1.3; it is reorganized according to the experimental results. First, the static arrangements of the compiler and the dynamic management on the host side jointly determine how the ANE actually executes, so the two are discussed together as decision mechanisms outside the ANE. Second, the two ANEs are not fully independent; they share some key resources. Third, the compiler's model of hardware performance contains biases, so static scheduling itself can become a source of performance loss.

First, the ANE is a configuration engine that is statically scheduled by the compiler and dynamically managed by the host. At the execution level, the ANE makes almost no decisions of its own. A program is a sequence of register writes fixed at compile time, with no instructions and no branches (Chapter 5). The order of execution is determined by the task descriptor sequence arranged by the compiler. In the programs tested in this report, the parallelism that can be observed among functional units occurs only within the pipeline of a single task (Chapter 6; whole-graph timing cannot determine whether the hardware can overlap tasks). In dual-engine programs, the split scheme and the amount of work assigned to each engine are also fixed at compile time (Chapters 5 and 10). At the management level, decisions likewise lie outside the ANE. The priority of requests, the engine on which they execute, and the program variant are selected by the host driver. The DVFS state and the throttling ratio are determined by the CLPC in the host kernel, and the ANE firmware is responsible only for queuing and dispatch (Chapters 6 and 12). This division of labor allows the hardware to remain simple, but it also means that the practical performance of the ANE depends to a large extent on two external factors: the quality of the compiler's decisions and the manner in which the host submits requests.

Second, the two engines are independent in computation but share resources. Each ANE has its own 16 NEs, an L2 that the compiler uses at 2 MiB, and an independent NE clock performance domain. On compute-bound workloads, the two engines can reach a speedup of 1.92×, with a scaling efficiency of about 96% (Chapter 10). However, the two engines share more resources than their block diagrams suggest. The weight-read bandwidth (about 150 GB/s) is shared by both. The clock domain in which the PE resides is independent of the two NE clock schedules, and we speculate that it is the shared cluster that the CLPC treats as common to both ANEs. A difference-based estimate indicates that about 1.6 W of power is shared by both, and its source is unknown. The driver's power-off and maintenance timers also act on both engines in synchrony (Chapters 9, 11, 12, and 13). Accordingly, the "two ANEs" are closer to "two compute arrays on one memory system" than to two fully independent accelerators. For any workload limited by a shared resource, such as weight-read-bound LLM decoding, the benefit obtained from the second engine is very limited. A convolution chain with distinct weights per layer on a dual-ANE program gains about 1.4× (timing slope, including host-side effects), and rewritten kernels split by rows gain no speedup. Request-level concurrency yields about 1.4×, and nearly all of it comes from eliminating host idle time.

Third, there is a systematic gap between the compiler's cost model and the actual behavior of the hardware. The compiler assumes that the maximum NE frequency is 2508 MHz, whereas the hardware operates at 2580 MHz. It assumes that the L2 and the PE vary in frequency with the NE at a fixed ratio, whereas in measurements the PE clock is scheduled independently. It assumes that the two ANEs execute completely in parallel after splitting and do not interfere with each other, whereas in fact they share weight-read bandwidth. The performance model does not account for the hardware Winograd path or for sparse zero-skipping, so it overestimates the time of 3×3 and pruned layers by 1.4–4.5×. Its estimate of activation DMA bandwidth, by contrast, is optimistic by about 3× (Chapters 5, 7, 9, and 11; Table 7-13). Among these gaps, the assumption that "the two ANEs do not interfere with each other" has the most serious consequence: it leads the compiler to choose row splitting for weight-read-bound layers, so the second engine brings no speedup at all (defect B9). These gaps indicate that the compiler's cost model reads more like a simplified estimate than a precise model of the hardware, with deviations that do not point in a consistent direction across stages.

### 15.2 Recommendations for Model Deployment

The conclusions of the individual chapters can be condensed into three deployment principles: keep the ANE busy, keep data on chip, and keep computation on the shapes and precisions the hardware favors. Table 15-1 summarizes the concrete recommendations derived from these three principles, together with their basis.

*Table 15-1. Summary of deployment recommendations*

| Principle | Recommendation | Basis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Keep the ANE busy | Keep two or more requests in flight asynchronously, or merge multiple inferences into one call, to reduce idle time between tasks; asynchronous calls are preferable to batched submission | Sections 6.5, 10.7, and 12.2 |
|  | When throughput is the priority, use asynchronous calls so that requests are distributed across both engines, rather than making synchronous calls to the same model from multiple threads within one process. Matrix multiplications with M = 1 and small tensors with few pixels are not split within a single request, so the second engine can be used for them only in this way; for weight-read-bound networks, this approach yields only about 1.4× | Sections 5.8.4, 6.7, 9.3.4, and 10.7 |
|  | In the programs measured, independent computations placed in the same model did not execute in parallel; to obtain parallelism, use multiple programs, multiple processes, or multiple asynchronous requests in flight for the same program | Sections 6.6.1 and 6.7 |
|  | For large language models, reduce the number of segments in the model to lower the number of ANE calls per token | Sections 6.5, 12.2, and 14.3.2 |
|  | Latency-sensitive applications should avoid idle periods longer than about 5.7 s, issuing warm-up calls in advance when necessary | Section 12.5 |
|  | A model whose work per call is too small is placed entirely on the CPU by Core ML; merge multiple inferences into a single call with more work | Section 5.2 |
|  | Performance tests should warm up for at least 1 s, insert no idle time between calls, and use a sufficiently deep network; models dominated by PE work require a longer warm-up; models whose task durations are near 350 µs should be measured over multiple rounds, with ANE time distinguished from host time; when measuring ANE power, the power of the CPU cluster must be subtracted | Sections 6.5.3, 7.1, 7.7, 11.3, 12.3, and 13.1 |
| Keep data on chip | Allow adjacent layers to be fused by the compiler, and avoid inserting operations into the middle of the network that force writes back to DRAM | Sections 9.2 and 9.4 |
|  | When tiling for the dual-ANE path, make the number of tiles per layer even | Section 10.5 |
|  | Weight-read-bound workloads obtain only limited benefit from the second ANE, and no benefit under row splitting | Sections 9.3 and 10.4 |
|  | Keep elementwise operations to at most two inputs | Section 9.4 |
|  | When the ANE and the GPU concurrently execute read-bandwidth-bound workloads, they roughly split the DRAM bandwidth between them; while both are active, PE-class operations may run in a low DVFS state, so latency-sensitive applications should stagger the high-load phases of the two | Sections 9.5.2 and 11.3.2 |
| Keep computation on the shapes and precisions the hardware favors | Avoid output-channel counts just above 256 (for example, 272; observed on the measured single-ANE 1×1 chains, the mechanism has not been fully confirmed) | Section 7.3 |
|  | Keep the horizontal kernel width at 8 or less; at a width of 16, the layer is placed on the CPU, and for small networks the entire model remains on the CPU | Sections 7.4 and 5.2 |
|  | When designing a model, if a 3×3 convolution and a 1×1 convolution are comparable in compute and accuracy, prefer the 3×3 convolution to exploit the hardware Winograd path (note that Winograd is not enabled for all shapes) | Section 7.4 |
|  | When designing a model, prefer shapes with many channels and small spatial dimensions. The overhead of S2D itself is negligible, but inserting S2D into an existing model changes its semantics and amount of computation, so it cannot by itself speed the model up | Section 7.3 |
|  | Apply W8A8 quantization to compute-dense layers such as 3×3 convolutions (1×1 convolution chains are limited by the activation path, so the benefit is small or absent; defect B4). Compress weights of weight-read-bound layers with W8, palettization, or pruning, whose latency shrinks approximately in proportion to byte count. Avoid int4 blockwise quantization and vector palettization, as they cause the model to fall back to the CPU | Sections 7.5, 9.3, and 13.3 |
|  | Pruning a 1×1 layer reduces both weight-read time and the number of multiply-accumulates, and unstructured pruning is sufficient, so structured patterns such as 2:4 are unnecessary. Pruning must be written into the model in a sparse format through the pruning interface of coremltools; setting weights to zero directly does not cause the compiler to switch to a sparse format. 3×3 layers lose Winograd eligibility after pruning, so the pruning ratio should be 75% or more | Section 7.5 |
|  | Intermediate accumulated values of convolutions and matrix multiplications (including bias) must not reach ±32768; scale the activations beforehand where necessary. For layers with a large activation range, W8A8 is safer than FP16 with respect to accumulator overflow | Sections 8.2 and 8.3 |
|  | When comparing ANE and CPU results, a rounding difference of 1 FP16 ulp is allowed for multiply-accumulate and elementwise operations. The error of common activation functions is on the order of 10⁻³ to 10⁻² (silu being the largest), and accuracy-sensitive applications should evaluate its effect | Sections 8.2.4 and 8.5.2 |
|  | Very deep networks (the 1×1 convolution chains tested in this report exceed 386 layers, that is, from 387 layers onward) may crash the compiler and fall back to the CPU; check whether the model executes on the ANE and, where necessary, split it into multiple segments | Section 5.10 |
|  | Models with many elementwise operations may take several minutes to load for the first time on the M6; load them during application startup and use the compilation cache. Large models with many convolution slices need tens of gigabytes of free disk space for their first compilation, and the loading process should not be terminated midway through compilation | Section 5.9 |
|  | Avoid int32 operations, mathematical functions such as asin, and sequence operations such as lstm in the middle of a network; they split the network into multiple segments, and for small networks they also keep the entire network on the CPU | Section 5.2 |

Of the three principles, the first is the most easily overlooked and has the greatest impact. Intuitively, the speed of the ANE should be determined by the model itself. However, Section 12.2 shows that host-side idle time inherent between synchronous calls causes a model whose tasks are shorter than about 1.3 ms to struggle to hold the top state, even under consecutive calls. In other words, how fast the same model runs on the ANE depends on how the host calls it. Chapter 13 further shows that the main energy cost of frequent small calls lies on the CPU side. The conclusions of both lines point in the same direction: reducing the number of calls and increasing the work per call improves speed and reduces energy.

On the other hand, there is a clear trade-off between energy efficiency and latency. When the CLPC selects a low DVFS state, the ANE energy per inference is about one third of that at the top state, while latency increases by about 2.2–2.9× (Section 13.4). For applications with relaxed latency requirements and sensitivity to energy, such as background processing or sustained inference on battery power, allowing the ANE to run in a low DVFS state is in fact the better choice. Applications need not do anything to achieve this: as long as there is sufficient idle time between requests, the CLPC steps down automatically.

### 15.3 Comparison with Earlier Generations

Table 15-2 compares the M6 with the M1, the M4, and the M5 across six aspects: structure, compute, memory, scheduling, numerics, and power. The data for the M1 and the M5 come from Bryngelson [5]. The data for the M4 come from maderix [4] and from control experiments on the M4 in this report, and the data for the M6 come from this report. Measurement methods differ across sources, particularly in the definitions of throughput and power, so the notes should be consulted when comparing across columns.

*Table 15-2. ANE comparison of the M1, M4, M5, and M6*

| Aspect | Item | M1 | M4 | M5 | M6 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Structure | Compilation target | h13 | h16g | h17 | h18g |
|  | Number of engines | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2, on the same die |
|  | TD layout version | v7 (recorded as v10 in [5]; see Section 5.7.7) | v17 | v19 (recorded as v20 in [5]) | v24 |
|  | Compiled artifact | One program | One program | One program | Two programs: single-ANE and dual-ANE |
| Compute | NE clock | ≈1.14 GHz [5] | Maximum in compiler table 2.364 GHz | ≈1.89 GHz [5] | Measured 2.58 GHz |
|  | FP16 throughput | ≈12 TFLOPS (slope) [5] | 18.5 TFLOPS (48 layers, including overhead) [4] | — | Single ANE 20.6, dual ANE 39.5 TFLOPS (slope) |
|  | W8A8 throughput | — | 36.0 TOPS (48 layers, including overhead) [4] | — | 35.5 TOPS (48 layers, including overhead); effective 62 TOPS for 3×3 on a single ANE |
|  | Weight-only quantization | No speedup [5] | No speedup [4] | — | No speedup for compute-bound layers; native weight-read-bound layers shortened by 42% |
| Memory | On-chip tiling threshold | Timing inflection at 2.28–2.34 MB [5] | 2 MiB rule [4] | Timing inflection at 4.72 MB [5] | Tiles smaller than 2 MiB; no timing inflection |
|  | Weight-read bandwidth | 51 GB/s [5] | ≈67 GB/s | ≈145 GB/s [5] | ≈150 GB/s |
|  | Activation bandwidth | ≈24 GB/s [5] | — | ≈24 GB/s [5] | ≈35–40 GB/s per ANE |
| Scheduling | Fixed overhead per call | ≈190 µs [5] | ≈90 µs [4] | ≈70 µs (M5 Pro) [6] | Submission side ≈30 µs, completion side 130–325 µs |
|  | Concurrency of two requests | 1.04× [5] | Saves ≈16% [4] | — | Two processes 1.78–1.81×; two different programs in one process reach 86% of the sum of separate runs |
|  | Compilation depth limit for 1×1 convolution chains | — | 383 blocks [4]; at least 448 layers measured in this report | — | 386 layers (elementwise chains of 512 layers still compile) |
| Numerics | Accumulator | FP32-class [5] | Q15.16 fixed-point | FP32-class [5] | Q15.16 fixed-point |
|  | Near 32768 | Saturates [5] | Overflows to inf | Does not saturate [5] | Overflows to inf |
|  | Output rounding | Ties to even [5] | Ties away from zero | — | Ties away from zero |
| Power | Cold-start cost | ≈260 ms [5] | — | — | ≈50 ms |
|  | Frequency reduction under sustained load | No reduction in 210 s [5] | — | — | No reduction over 5 minutes |
|  | Power | — | FP16 4.57 W, W8A8 12.25 W [4] | — | Single ANE 6.2 W, dual ANE 10.8 W (CPU cluster subtracted) |

As Table 15-2 shows, the changes in the M6 are concentrated in two areas. The first is an increase in scale: a second engine was added, the NE clock was raised to 2.58 GHz, and the DRAM bandwidth was raised to 170.5 GB/s, which in turn led to compilation of separate single-ANE and dual-ANE programs. The second is the software machinery built around the second engine: the compiler gained split and synchronization steps, and the driver gained engine selection and variant selection. At the same time, the core design inside the ANE has remained essentially unchanged. The 16 NEs per engine, the 64 KiB weight buffer per NE, the 2 MiB single-operand limit, the 64 L2 banks, and the Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator are all identical to those of the M4. The activation functions likewise have the same 33-point lookup-table structure (for the M4, according to maderix [4]). Constants in the HAL parameter table related to the MAC array, the L2, and bandwidth have also not changed from H16 to H18 (Section 5.4). The changes in the parameter tables from H17 to H18 are concentrated in the flexibility of work-unit partitioning and in the supported weight formats, which are discussed separately below.

Several differences in the table require careful interpretation. The accumulators of the M1 and the M5 are described as "FP32-class," whereas this report measured the M4 and the M6 as Q15.16 fixed-point. Because the test methods differ, and because the "saturation at 32768" reported by Bryngelson corresponds exactly to the upper limit of the integer part of Q15.16, we cannot determine whether this reflects a genuine change across generations or a difference in measurement convention (Section 8.6). The fixed overhead per call cannot be compared directly across generations either, because the studies define and time the overhead differently (Section 6.8). With respect to power, this report found that on the M4, PP0b likewise supplies both the ANE and the CPU cluster. The studies use different power sources and subtraction methods, so the power values of the M4 and the M6 should likewise not be compared directly (Section 13.7).

While the compute array and the memory structure remain unchanged, the data formats and execution modes supported by the ANE have continually increased across generations, which forms another thread running through the generations. Table 15-3 lists the values, in each generation, of the HAL parameter table fields that reflect this trend (the data come from raw exports of each generation's parameter table; see Section 5.4) 〔HAL〕.

*Table 15-3. Fields of the HAL parameter table related to data formats and execution modes*

| Parameter table | Corresponding product | Native weight formats (0x8a0) | Types fusable with dequantization (0x6a8) | Vector palettization (0x72f/0x730) | Half and non-power-of-two work units (0x55c, 0x5a0) | New data-format switches (0x5ff/0x600) | Multiple palettes (0x5c7/0x5c8) | Double rate (0x5f7) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| M9, H11 | Early products | 7 | 3 | None | None | None | None | None |
| H12 | — | 7 | 4 | None | None | None | None | None |
| H13, H14, H15 | M1 (H13) and the two subsequent generations | 13 | 4 | None | None | None | None | None |
| H16 | M4 generation | 13 | 5 | None | None | None | None | None |
| H17 | M5 generation | 13 | 5 | Yes, length up to 4 | None | None | None | None |
| H18 | Single-engine h18 | 21 | 6 | Yes, length up to 4 | Yes | Yes | None | None |
| H19 | h19 (no product yet) | 21 | 6 | Yes, length up to 4 | Yes | Yes | Yes, up to 16 | Yes |
| 2026BaseLine | M6 (h18g) | 21 | 6 | Yes, length up to 4 | Yes | Yes | Yes, up to 16 | Yes |

In the table, the first two columns are fields stored as lists, and the table gives the length of each list. The list at offset 0x8a0 is read by functions such as `NeedsUpcastingFrom3bitPaletteTo4bitPalette`, and we infer that it contains identifiers of the weight formats natively supported by the hardware. The list at offset 0x6a8 is read by `HandleL2Config` and `IsFusableToDequant`, and we infer that it lists the weight types that can be fused with dequantization operations 〔HAL, inferred〕. The remaining columns are capability switches in the parameter table, whose meanings are inferred from the names of the functions that read them.

Table 15-3 reveals three regularities. First, capabilities only accumulate: the lengths of both lists never decrease across generations, and each new generation's list contains all elements of the previous one. The number of native weight formats rises from 7 in early products to 13 in H13 and then to 21 in H18, and the number of types fusable with dequantization increases from 3 to 6 step by step. The names of the reading functions reveal a concrete consequence of the added formats: before H18, 3-bit palettized weights had to be upcast to 4-bit palettes before they could execute, a step that is no longer required from H18 onward. Second, new capabilities are introduced in batches across generations. Vector palettization appears in H17; half work units, non-power-of-two work units, and the new data-format switches appear in H18; and multiple palettes and double rate appear in H19. Beyond the fields listed in the table, H19 also relaxes the size of FIFO buffers (offset 0x888) and the dimension limits for gather and texture operations (offsets 0x1a8–0x1b8). Third, the 2026BaseLine parameter table used by the M6 matches H19 in all of these fields, rather than matching H18, the single-engine target of the same generation (Section 5.4). Consequently, in compiler parameters the M6 already matches the H19 generation, with switches for its full set of data formats and execution modes. Whether all of these capabilities can be used on the hardware is discussed below.

The combined effect of these changes is to make the ANE increasingly flexible in practical use. More native weight formats mean that more compressed models can execute directly on the ANE without upcasting, thereby reducing the amount of weight storage and weight reads. Chapters 9 and 10 show that weight-read bandwidth is precisely the bottleneck for workloads such as LLM decoding; therefore, extending weight formats matters more for such workloads than an increase in compute throughput. More flexible work-unit partitioning enables tensors of irregular shape and small size to use the 16 NEs more fully. The measurements in Section 7.5 provide direct support for this judgment. On weight-read-bound layers, W8 and 6-, 4-, 3-, and 2-bit palettes are all read in their compressed formats, and the time per layer shrinks approximately in proportion to the number of bytes; a 2-bit palette requires only 16% of the FP16 time. However, not every format enabled in the parameter table can be used. With the generation parameters adopted in this report, int4 blockwise quantization and vector palettization both cannot be compiled by the ANE compiler, and the double-rate mode was not triggered (Section 5.7.3). The MX format fields appear only in the newer v36 layout, and the v24 layout of the M6 has no corresponding fields; their practical benefit on the hardware remains to be measured.

Table 15-3 compares the fields one column at a time. To obtain a complete view of the changes from the M4 to the M6, we also compared the H16g parameter table used for the M4 with the 2026BaseLine parameter table used for the M6, byte by byte. The two differ in 81 8-byte words. Excluding pointers such as table names and list addresses, the field-reading functions of 41 fields had already been identified in Section 5.4, and for another 24 no reading function has been determined 〔HAL〕. We wrote a tool that scans the entire compiler code by offset, searching for load instructions of the form "base register plus this offset" ([immscan2.c](../tools/re/immscan2.c)), and it located reading functions for 14 of these fields 〔HAL, inferred〕. Beyond the formats and execution modes already listed in Table 15-3, these differences include three batches of switches. H17 (the M5 generation) added zero-weight detection (offset 0x72c, whose reading function is `EnableOnTheFlySparseEncodingIfPossible`), multicast (0x72b), and a new event format, and it removed several hardware-erratum workarounds that the M4 carried. H18 added a double-Int8 mode (0x55d, whose reading function is `CanUseDoubleInt8Mode`). The 2026BaseLine added accelerated texture sampling (0x7a0) and a group of new cache-prefetch DMA parameters (0x668–0x688 and two new configuration lists) 〔HAL, inferred〕.

The meanings of fields can usually be inferred only from the names of the functions that read them. For zero-weight detection, we performed a causal test (Section 3.2). When compiling for h18g on the M4, we restored to their M4 values the numerical fields in the in-process parameter table that differ from the M4, and the zero-weight detection bit of the convolution TD (Section 7.5) changed from 1 to 0. After bisection by group and by byte, only the single byte at offset 0x72c determines this result (tool: [halpatch_lldb.py](../tools/re/halpatch_lldb.py)) 〔compiler〕. Accordingly, zero-weight detection is enabled from H17 onward and is not enabled on the M4, which is consistent with the compilation results for the two targets in Section 7.5. The correspondence between other fields and measured behavior cannot yet be determined. For example, the double-Int8 mode is enabled only in parameter tables from H18 onward, yet the measured W8A8 throughput of a 48-layer, 1024-channel chain on the M4 (36.0 TOPS) is likewise about 2× that of FP16 for the same shape (18.5 TFLOPS) (Table 7-12, maderix [4]). The double-Int8 mode therefore does not account for the doubling of INT8 throughput, and its actual effect was not measured in this report. The meanings of fields such as those at offsets 0x72d, 0x820, 0x830, and 0x610 also remain unclear.

The compiler also reveals what hardware beyond the M6 may support. As described in Section 5.7.7, besides the v24 layout used by the M6 and H19, this version of the compiler implements four TD layouts: v26, v28, v31, and v36. By the compiler's internal ArchCompareValue, v24 and v26 are 10, v28 is 11, v31 is 12, and v36 is 14. Thus v28, v31, and v36 are architectures newer than the M6, whereas v26 belongs to the same generation as the M6. The v26 layout corresponds to the internal compiler target M12, while v28, v31, and v36 have neither an external target name nor a corresponding HAL parameter table 〔compiler〕. This version of the compiler contains no target named H20, so we cannot determine which of these layouts corresponds to H20. Nevertheless, the compiler defines a set of field-reading functions for each layout version (`ZinGetRegisterProgramming<N>::GetXxx`, Section 5.7.3), and comparing the functions added or removed between versions shows which hardware fields each new layout adds. Table 15-4 lists the results of this comparison.

*Table 15-4. Field-reading functions of each TD layout version in the compiler*

| Layout | ArchCompareValue | Corresponding target | Number of field-reading functions | Fields added relative to the previous version |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| v20 | 9 | h18 | 53 | — |
| v24 | 10 | h18g (M6), h19 | 61 | `Tsr`, `Tde`, and `PublishBit` in the task header; dual-input PE tasks; double rate (`DoubleRate`) for L2 and Tile DMA |
| v26 | 10 | M12 | 58 | None added; excludes the three task-header fields of v24 |
| v28 | 11 | None | 61 | Output X-direction crop offset (`CropOffsetDstX`); atomic-write data type for output Tile DMA (`TileDmaDstAtomicDataType`); a field named `ENE` |
| v31 | 12 | None | 61 | Same as v24 |
| v36 | 14 | None | 67 | MX formats for the input, second input, and output (`IsMxInFmt`, `IsMxSrc2InFmt`, `IsMxOutFmt`); enable bits for the three Tile DMA paths |

Three inferences about subsequent hardware can be drawn from Table 15-4 〔compiler, inferred〕. First, the newest v36 layout contains fields for microscaling (MX) data formats, and the input, second input, and output can each specify a format independently. On this basis, we speculate that the corresponding hardware will support MX formats. MX is a family of block-scaled formats defined by the Open Compute Project (OCP), including MXFP8, MXFP6, MXFP4, and MXINT8, among others, in which each small block of elements shares one scale factor. Combined with the trend in Table 15-3 of native weight formats increasing with each generation, if this speculation holds, subsequent ANEs will be able to compute and read and write directly in floating-point formats of 8 bits or fewer, rather than only storing weights in low-bit-width formats. However, the fields in the compiler show only that code generation has been prepared for this; whether the hardware implements it, and when it will ship, cannot currently be confirmed. Second, the v28 layout adds an atomic-write data type for output Tile DMA, and we speculate that subsequent hardware may atomically accumulate results into the output tensor in DRAM. Section 5.8.2 shows that the dual-ANE program on the M6, when reducing along the height, requires ANE1 to write partial sums to a shared scratch buffer, after which ANE0 aggregates them. If outputs can be accumulated atomically, this kind of cross-engine reduction would no longer need to be performed centrally by one engine. The v28 layout also adds a crop offset for the output in the X direction, which corresponds to the capability "cropping of outputs not aligned in the X direction" added to the H18 parameter table. The meaning of the `ENE` field cannot be determined from its name. Third, the three fields added in v28 do not appear in the newer v31 and v36 layouts, and the set of field-reading functions in v31 is identical to that of v24. This shows that these layouts do not evolve strictly in sequence; v28 may belong to a separate product line, whereas v31 and v36 are more likely successors within the product line of the M6.

It should be emphasized that the field-reading functions cover only the fields that the compiler needs to read back, and they do not equal the full set of hardware registers. Table 15-4 can therefore establish only which fields the new layouts add, at a minimum, and it does not show that no other fields were added. Moreover, the HAL parameter tables corresponding to these layouts have not appeared in the compiler, so whether subsequent hardware changes its compute array, storage capacity, or bandwidth cannot currently be determined.

Taken together with Tables 15-2 to 15-4, the evolution of the ANE from the M1 to the M6 can be summarized in terms of Apple's design trade-offs. The following interpretation is based on data from this report and from the existing literature, and Apple has not publicly disclosed its design intent 〔inferred〕.

First, Apple has kept the compute core stable and placed performance improvements outside the core. Since H13 (the M1 generation), the number of NEs per engine in the HAL parameter table has remained 16, the per-NE resident-weight limit has remained 64 KiB, the on-chip limit for a single operand has remained 2 MiB, and the L2 has remained divided into 64 banks. The M4 and the M6 have identical fixed-point accumulator behavior, and their activation functions both use the 33-point lookup-table structure (for the M4, according to maderix [4]). The task-descriptor mechanism is essentially the same, with layout versions v17 and v24, respectively. During this period, performance growth came mainly from three sources: the NE clock rose from about 1.14 GHz [5] on the M1 to about 1.89 GHz [5] on the M5 and 2.58 GHz on the M6; the weight-read bandwidth rose from 51 GB/s [5] on the M1 to about 145 GB/s [5] on the M5 and about 150 GB/s on the M6; and the second engine was added in the M6. Keeping the core unchanged has two clear benefits. First, validated designs can be reused generation after generation, which reduces hardware risk. Second, most of the compiler's optimizations (tiling, bank allocation, output-channel grouping, and the performance model) can be carried over, so each generation's changes need only appear in a few registers of the TD layout, which the compiler absorbs (Section 5.7.7).

Second, Apple has chosen to expand compute by replicating engines rather than by widening the array. Instead of increasing the number of NEs in one engine from 16 to 32, the M6 places two complete 16-NE engines, adds split and synchronization steps to the compiler, and adds engine selection to the driver. This approach is consistent with Apple's practice of scaling CPUs and GPUs by replicating cores or dies. Its advantage is that the timing, power, and compiler models of an individual engine need not change. Its drawbacks, as shown in Chapter 10, are that the two engines share weight-read bandwidth and part of their power, that the benefit of the second engine depends on whether the workload is compute-bound, and that the compiler's dual-engine path is not yet mature. From the standpoint of power, the two engines each have their own power gating in the device tree (Section 4.3), so in principle only one needs to be powered on under light load, which matters for battery-powered devices. In measurements, however, when only one engine is in use, the other is also briefly powered on, and the power-off timers of the two are synchronized (Chapter 12). This potential is not fully exploited by the current software.

Third, Apple places growing emphasis on data formats rather than on peak compute. The number of natively supported weight formats has risen from 7 in early products to 13 in the M1 generation and 21 in the M6 generation. Compression methods such as palettization, vector palettization, and multiple palettes have been added generation by generation, and TD fields for MX formats have already appeared in the compiler, which suggests that such formats may be supported later (Tables 15-3 and 15-4). The measurements in Section 7.5 show that the M6 can already read 2–8-bit compressed weights directly and shorten weight-read time in proportion to the number of bytes. Meanwhile, the width of the MAC array has not changed, and on both the M4 and the M6 the INT8 peak is about twice the FP16 peak. This direction matches the requirements of on-device generative models. Chapters 9 and 10 show that workloads such as LLM decoding are limited by weight-read bandwidth, so reducing the number of bits per weight improves speed and reduces energy more effectively than adding MAC units does.

Fourth, Apple keeps the decisions on scheduling and frequency scaling on the host side. From the M1 to the M6, the ANE firmware has consistently been responsible only for queuing and dispatch, and the DVFS state is determined by the host's power management (Chapters 6, 11, and 12; Bryngelson [5] reports the same observation for the M1). With the M6, this is further reflected in the CLPC managing the clocks of the two engines and the shared cluster together, using utilization as its target. This design allows the ANE to be placed under the same power and thermal budget as the CPU and GPU, at the cost that the practical performance of the ANE is highly sensitive to how the host invokes it and to system load (Chapter 12).

Overall, the ANE from the M1 to the M6 has followed a path of "stable core, expanding periphery": while keeping the compute core and programming model unchanged, each generation raises the clock and bandwidth, extends the data formats, and, on the M6, doubles compute by replicating the engine. This path lowers the iteration cost of hardware and software, but it also shifts more of the complexity onto the compiler and host software.
