Most of the defects found in this report (Section 15.4) occur precisely in these newly added software stages.

### 15.4 Defects and Undocumented Behaviors

This report identified 14 suspected defects or contested designs in its experiments, as well as 10 behaviors that are not documented but are likely to be encountered by developers. The former are divided into two classes by severity. Class A comprises crashes, hangs, or silent errors, which are clearly not intended design. Class B comprises behaviors that function correctly but carry a high cost or cannot be controlled by the caller. Table 15-5 lists all Class A and Class B entries; Class C entries, together with the reproduction method for each item, are listed in Appendix D.

*Table 15-5. Suspected defects and contested designs*

| ID | Description | Section |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A1 | The dual-ANE compilation path crashes from a stack overflow when compiling 1×1 convolution chains from 387 layers onward | 5.10 |
| A2 | After the compiler service crashes, the result of Core ML loading is indeterminate and may hang permanently, blocking other processes | 5.10 |
| A3 | The global refinement of dual-ANE spatial splitting writes weight copies into swap files, with usage proportional to the number of layers (about 78 GB for the Whisper encoder) | 5.9 |
| A4 | After the client exits, the compiler service keeps running and fills the system disk, after which the ANE compilation cache is cleared | 5.9 |
| B1 | Compilation time of dual-ANE programs grows superlinearly with the number of elementwise layers | 5.9 |
| B2 | On first load by Core ML, compilation runs only on the E cores, about 2.7× slower | 5.9 |
| B3 | When multiple threads synchronously call the same compiled program, requests are serialized; asynchronous calls are unaffected | 6.7, 10.7 |
| B4 | In W8A8 1×1 convolution chains, activations pass through DRAM at every layer, and under dual-ANE they are on par with FP16 | 7.5 |
| B5 | When ANE task durations vary, host-side wake-up overhead increases progressively, by up to about 195 µs | 6.5 |
| B6 | The maximum NE frequency in the compiler's performance model does not match the hardware | 11.6 |
| B7 | At certain depths, Core ML silently moves the last operation to the CPU | 7.7 |
| B8 | When tiling, the work split across the dual ANEs is uneven: ANE1 carries 56–59% of the work, reducing throughput by 11–16% | 10.5 |
| B9 | A weight-read-bound layer is split by rows, and the second ANE brings no speedup | 10.4 |
| B10 | The clock of the shared cluster ramps up slowly and is repeatedly forced back to a low DVFS state while the GPU is working concurrently | 11.3 |

Although these issues are scattered across the chapters, most of them can be traced to three common root causes.

The first root cause is the immaturity of the dual-ANE compilation path. A1, A3, B1, B8, and B9 all arise in dual-ANE compilation steps that run only for the targets that generate dual-ANE programs (h18g and h19). The search for parallel branches is implemented recursively, which causes a stack overflow (A1). The global refinement of spatial splitting stores float32 weight copies newly created for merged convolutions in swap files that are allocated but never released (A3). A procedure in the dual-ANE compilation steps grows superlinearly with the number of elementwise layers, although the specific step has not been determined (B1). The work partition reuses the tiling grid of the single-engine path (B8). The cost model ignores contention between the two engines for weight-read bandwidth (B9). The single-engine target of the same generation, h18, does not execute these steps and does not exhibit these problems. This shows that dual-engine compilation support is a layer added on top of the single-engine compiler, and it has not yet been optimized to the same degree as the single-engine path.

The second root cause is a mismatch between host-side policies and the workload characteristics of the ANE. B2, B5, and B10 are not problems of the ANE hardware or the compiler. They are side effects of the host operating system's scheduling and power-management policies as applied to the ANE. The compiler service inherits the background scheduling role of a daemon and therefore runs only on E cores (B2). The CPU waiting for ANE results enters a deep idle state according to a general idle-prediction policy, which slows wake-up (B5). The clock of the shared cluster is scheduled independently by the CLPC; it ramps up slowly and is constrained by the interconnect power budget (B10). The statement in Chapter 12 that "the larger the host overhead, the lower the ANE DVFS state" also falls into this category. These policies may be reasonable for the CPU and GPU, but for ANE workloads that run only one or two milliseconds at a time and require frequent host involvement, they significantly affect performance.

The third root cause is that Core ML is insufficiently transparent to developers. A2, A4, B3, and B7, together with several Class C behaviors, share the property that the system changes its execution mode without giving any indication. A model that fails to compile silently falls back to the CPU or hangs permanently (A2). The compiler service has no timeout or disk budget, and it continues to run after its client exits, potentially filling the disk (A4). When multiple threads synchronously call the same model, requests are serialized (B3). Individual operations are placed on the CPU (B7). A layer is placed on the CPU when its convolution kernel width reaches 16 (C9). Developers can discover these situations only through indirect means such as IOReport interrupt counts, execution-plan caches, or sampling. For developers who target the ANE for deployment, this lack of transparency is harder to handle than the performance problems themselves.

### 15.5 Summary

The M6 ANE is a configuration engine that is statically scheduled by the compiler and dynamically managed by the host. Its two engines are independent in computation and share resources, and a systematic gap exists between the compiler's cost model and the hardware. The deployment principles derived from this are: keep the ANE busy, keep data on chip, and keep computation on the shapes and precisions at which the hardware performs well. Of these, the manner in which the host invokes the ANE has the greatest effect on speed and energy consumption, and it is the easiest to overlook. Keeping multiple requests in flight asynchronously both removes host idle time and allows requests for the same model to be spread across the two engines. Compared with earlier generations, the changes in the M6 are concentrated in the second engine and its software support, while the core design of the ANE remains essentially the same as that of the M4. At the same time, the compiler's parameter tables show that the number of weight formats natively supported by the ANE has risen from 7 in early products to 21 in the M6 generation, and that the partitioning of work units has become more flexible, which enables the ANE to execute a wider variety of compressed models directly in practical use. The measurements in Section 7.5 further show that compressed and pruned weights are both read at their compressed size in bytes, and that the compute array also skips multiply-accumulate operations on zero weights. These capabilities matter more for weight-read-bound workloads than dense peak throughput. Most of the defects found in the experiments stem from three sources: the dual-engine compilation path is not yet mature; host-side scheduling and power-management policies are mismatched with the workload characteristics of the ANE; and Core ML lacks transparency about changes to its execution mode.

## 16. Limitations and Open Questions

When stating their conclusions, the preceding chapters have each indicated the evidence category and the inferred component of the statement. This chapter brings these limitations together for discussion. It first states what the method of this report can and cannot fundamentally establish, then lists the hardware parameters and behaviors that remain undetermined, and finally proposes follow-up work that could answer these questions.

### 16.1 Limitations of the Method

The method of this report is subject to three kinds of constraint, which determine which conclusions can be measured directly and which can only be inferred indirectly.

First, the constraints of the research impose limits. This report executes models only through the public interfaces of Core ML, does not use private execution interfaces, and neither loads nor executes any modified compiled artifact (Chapter 3). This constraint keeps the study from depending on private interfaces that may stop working after a system update, and it avoids the system instability that could result from running modified programs, but it has three consequences. The first consequence is that the meaning of each field in a task descriptor can be inferred only by changing one property of the model, observing which words of the artifact change, and combining the result with disassembly. The fields cannot be verified on hardware by modifying them, as maderix [4] did (Chapter 5). The second is that the runtime decides whether a model executes as a single-ANE or a dual-ANE program, and we cannot make the same model execute in both ways. We can only compare models of similar shape on either side of the compiler's splitting threshold (Chapter 10). The third is that the higher MAC tiers that the compiler estimates for single-ANE programs appear only in programs that Core ML does not execute, so whether the hardware can reach these tiers cannot be verified (Section 7.2.3).

Second, the observation methods impose limits. The ANE does not expose a per-task-descriptor performance counter to user space. The TD does contain a per-component performance-tracing configuration (Section 5.7.8), but it can be written only through compilation options, and its results cannot be read through Core ML. kdebug provides only the start and end times of each task, and in some experiments the task-start events were either not recorded or largely missing (Chapter 6). Consequently, for any behavior inside a task, such as how much time the MAC array, the PE and the DMA each occupy within one task, this report can only infer it by constructing different models and comparing total durations. The system also provides no reading of the ANE clock frequency. The NE frequency is inferred backward from task durations, and the frequency of the shared cluster can only be estimated (Chapter 11). With respect to power, the M6 has no readable power rail dedicated to the ANE. The ANE power must be obtained by subtracting the power of the CPU cluster from PP0b, which also supplies the CPU. SMC readings are refreshed about once per second, and the power histogram of the CPU cluster uses 1 W bins. These factors limit the precision and temporal resolution of power measurements (Chapter 13).

Third, the sample is limited. Apart from the M4 control experiments, all measurements in this report were performed on a 16 GB M6 running macOS 27.0.1 with `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`. Machines with different memory configurations may have different DRAM channel organizations, and different compiler versions may have different splitting strategies and graph-depth limits; either may alter some of the numerical values in this report. The sustained-load test lasted only 5 minutes and was conducted at room temperature. Behavior over longer durations or at higher ambient temperatures was not measured. In the comparison with earlier generations, the M4 data come partly from control experiments on an M4 performed for this report, whereas the M1 and M5 data come entirely from the literature and were measured with methods different from those of this report (Section 15.3). The application benchmarks in Chapter 14 are a cross-generation comparison. They did not record firmware timestamps or the number of interrupts per engine, and only the resource curves have been organized into the code repository. Therefore they can test only the direction and order of magnitude of the conclusions.

In addition, static analysis has its own limitations. The analysis of the kernelcache and the ANE firmware relies mainly on strings and function names, and only a small number of key functions were disassembled. The meanings of the fields in the HAL parameter table were inferred from the names of the functions that read them. The linear scan used to locate readers does not follow branches or reads routed through memory, and it may therefore miss some readers (Section 5.4). All of these inferences are marked 〔inferred〕 in the body text.

### 16.2 Unresolved Questions

Table 16-1 summarizes the hardware parameters and behaviors that this report could not determine. The entries are grouped by object of study, and each entry states what is already known, what remains unknown, and the possible means of resolution.

*Table 16-1. Unresolved questions*

| Object | Known | Unknown | Possible approach |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NE internal organization | 256 FP16 MACs per NE per cycle; at most 16 output channels per round for FP16 (Sections 7.2, 7.3) | Whether the 256 MACs are "16 output channels × 16 input channels" or "16 output channels × 16 pixels"; the relation between the "accumulator depth 8" in the HAL and the OCG | Microbenchmarks that vary the number of input channels and the number of pixels separately, and comparison of the positions of the latency steps |
| Physical capacity of on-chip storage | The compiler uses the L2 as 2 MiB, and the resident-weight limit per NE is 64 KiB (Section 9.2) | Whether the physical SRAM is larger (for example, for double buffering); the capacity of the input line buffer inside the NE | Requires direct access to on-chip storage or execution of custom programs, which lies beyond the constraints of this report |
| Compiler's higher MAC tiers | The performance model assumes 512 FP16 and 1024 INT8 MACs in single-ANE programs with large work units (Section 7.2.3) | Whether the hardware can reach these tiers | Requires executing single-ANE programs, which lies beyond the constraints of this report |
| Clock of the shared cluster | Scheduled independently; the ratio from the lowest state to full load is 2.63× (Section 11.3) | Absolute frequency; number of elements processed by the PE per cycle (about 32, which depends on the estimated frequency) | Find a frequency table or reading for the shared cluster |
| New formats and modes | W8 and 2–6-bit palettes shorten latency on weight-read-bound layers by byte count (Section 7.5); the parameter table enables vector palettes, multiple palettes and double-rate operation (Section 15.3) | Under which parameters vector palettization and int4 block-wise quantization can be compiled; the trigger conditions for double-rate operation; the output error of compressed formats | Sweep coremltools parameters such as block size and vector dimension, check the compilation results, and compare outputs |
| L2 bank conflicts | The compiler pads strides to avoid conflicts (Section 9.2.3) | The effect of conflicts on actual latency | Requires executing programs with the optimization disabled, which lies beyond the constraints of this report |
| Data exchange in dual-ANE programs | Structure of the shared scratch buffer and sync records (Section 5.8.2) | The actual cost of exchanging data; the role of the DSID and the system-level cache in the exchange | Construct models with the same amount of work that differ only in the number of exchanges |
| Cost model for dual-ANE programs | Criterion formula; the "copy-and-merge" overhead appears only when each block after splitting does not exceed 128 pixels (Section 5.8.3) | Where this condition is located concretely in the performance model | Further disassembly of the performance model |
| Activation path | About 35–40 GB/s per ANE (Section 9.4) | Why operations that require multiple TDs drop to 14–27 GB/s with large tensors | Measure each TD separately using firmware timestamps |
| PE | Single-input operations at about 61 G elements/s (Section 7.6) | Nonlinearity of two-input operations at large sizes; why PE load increases the memory power rail by about 1.1 W | Further measurement combined with IOReport interconnect bandwidth statistics |
| CLPC | When idle does not exceed about 1 ms, the busy fraction is about 77%; deviations at longer idle intervals can be approximated by an "exponential average sampled at job end, with a time constant of about 6.5 ms"; the speed of ramping up and down depends on the deviation of utilization from its target (Sections 12.2–12.4) | The exact control algorithm and gains; residuals of the approximate model at idle above 10 ms; why ramp-up after 0.1–0.2 s of idle is about 2× slower than a step ramp-up; why the short-task dual-ANE model settles at about 2540 MHz when idle exceeds about 2.5 ms | Scan idle length and task duration more finely; further analyze the CLPC driver |
| Firmware scheduling | Six priority levels, a two-slot task queue, and a priority-switching mechanism (inferred to switch at TD boundaries; Section 6.4) | Latency threshold of context switching; actual latency of preemption | Construct concurrent loads with different priorities and measure them |
| Core ML segmentation | The segmenter's cost rules (0.125 ms per ANE segment, and 0.125 ms each for switching backends and for graph outputs on the ANE), and the operators and size limits supported by the ANE; the ahead-of-time compilation driver yields the same estimates for H18G and H16G (Section 5.2) | Content of the decision tree used for latency estimation; whether the M6 runtime dispatch matches it; speed of topk on the M6 | Repeat the dispatch experiment of Section 5.2 and the topk timing on the M6 |
| Power details | Shared overhead of about 1.6 W; when all convolution activations are exactly zero, power falls by about 58% (the same on the M6 and the M4), with latency unchanged; power savings increase monotonically with the fraction of zeros but fall short of the proportional expectation (Section 13.3) | Composition of the shared overhead; whether elementwise power savings come from clock gating after zero detection or from fewer toggles in the multiplier itself; source of the additional savings when whole blocks are zero | Requires per-component power readings |
| Sparsity | Pruned weights are read in compressed format; the compute array skips zero weights but does not skip zero activations (Section 7.5) | Why the speedup is below the fraction of nonzero weights; effect of zero-weight detection on the speed and power of dense zero weights on the M6; why the apparent compute throughput is inflated under all-zero inputs in the application benchmarks | Compare the compressed weight length and latency of each NE; measure dense-zero-weight models on the M6; re-examine the weights used in the application benchmark |
| Memory configuration | The 16 GB model has 8 channels with a single rank (Section 9.5) | Whether the per-processor bandwidth is higher on 32 GB and other models | Repeat the measurements on machines with other memory configurations |
| Numerical behavior across generations | The M4 and M6 both use Q15.16 fixed-point accumulators (Section 8.6) | Whether the M1 and M5 do the same, and whether the difference from the "FP32-class accumulators" in the literature arises from hardware or from measurement methods | Run the tests of Chapter 8 on the M1 and M5 |
| Bottleneck of rewritten kernels | Latency of rewritten kernels with kernel widths 9–15 is proportional to the number of weight elements; quantization to Int8 does not speed them up; both engines share this stage (Section 7.4.2) | What this element-counted stage is (conjectured to be the decoding of sparse weights) | Compare the latency of sparse-format and dense-format layers at the same weight count |
| Unexplained parts of application benchmarks | The benchmark results are consistent in direction and magnitude with the expectations of Section 14.2 (Section 14.6) | The portion of text detection that exceeds the ratio to peak; the approximately 0.15 ms extra on the M6 at batch of 1; the individual effects of the number of calls and the number of weight bits; the reason top-1 disagrees with the GPU at batch of 1 | On practical models, record firmware timestamps and the number of interrupts per engine, varying only one factor at a time; compare ANE and GPU outputs layer by layer |
| Events and fields | Positions of the three groups of event masks in the task header (Section 5.7.1) | Meaning of each event bit; meaning of the `ENE` field in the v28 layout | Further disassembly of the firmware and compiler |

Among these questions, three have the greatest impact on developers. The first is the practical benefit of new formats and modes. Section 7.5 has already shown that palettization can shorten latency on weight-read-bound layers by byte count, but it remains unclear whether vector palettization, int4 block-wise quantization and double-rate operation can be used on the M6, and how much benefit they bring. This directly affects how large language models are deployed on device. The second is the control details of the CLPC. Chapter 12 shows that the actual speed of the ANE depends to a large extent on how the host invokes it, and a more precise control model would help developers predict performance under different invocation patterns. The third is the cost of data exchange between the two ANEs. It determines which types of networks can benefit from dual-ANE programs.

### 16.3 Future Work

Based on Table 16-1, the following work can be completed within the constraints of this report and is expected to answer the main questions among them. First, building on Section 7.5, sweep coremltools parameters such as block size and vector dimension to determine under which conditions vector palettization and int4 block-wise quantization can be compiled for the ANE, and check the output error of each compressed format, in order to fully evaluate the practical benefit of the data formats newly added to the M6. Second, sweep task duration and idle length over a wider range, in particular the discontinuity of the dual-ANE model with a single task of about 1.4 ms at full clock near an idle length of 2.4–2.5 ms, in order to refine the approximate model of Section 12.4 and to identify the trigger conditions of the high DVFS state close to full clock. Third, run the numerical tests of Chapter 8 on the M1 and M5 to confirm whether the fixed-point accumulator is present across generations. Fourth, repeat the bandwidth measurements of Section 9.5 on a 32 GB M6, and repeat the sustained-load test of Section 12.6 for longer durations and at higher ambient temperatures. Fifth, using field tracing, continue to determine the 79 bit fields in the TD that have not yet been observed, in particular the enabling conditions of activation compression and multiple palettes, and test the conjecture that "the bottleneck of rewritten kernels is the decoding stage for sparse weights", for example by comparing the latency of sparse-format and dense-format layers at the same weight count. Sixth, on the M6, separately measure the energy consumed by the CPU cluster and by the GPU when completing an amount of computation comparable to that of Section 13.2, using matrix multiplication with Accelerate and with Metal, and compare these directly with the ANE on the same chip (Section 13.7.2). Seventh, after new releases of the system and compiler, check whether the v28, v31 and v36 layouts have obtained externally visible target names and HAL parameter tables, in order to track subsequent changes in the ANE's compute array and storage. Eighth, on the practical models of Chapter 14, supplement the measurements of firmware timestamps and the number of interrupts per engine, vary only the number of calls or only the number of weight bits at a time, and turn the phenomena that are consistent with the application benchmarks into tests of the mechanism. At the same time, compare the ANE and GPU outputs layer by layer at batch of 1 to identify the source of the top-1 disagreement.

Other questions, such as the capacity of the physical SRAM, whether the higher MAC tiers can be reached, and the practical cost of bank conflicts, can be answered only by directly executing custom programs or by accessing the hardware counters of the ANE. These lie beyond the constraints adopted in this report. The approach of maderix [4] shows that, provided that the corresponding risks are accepted, modifying compiled artifacts and executing them on the hardware can directly verify the meaning of fields. Combining this approach with the compiler analysis and firmware timestamps of this report is expected to resolve most of the questions in Table 16-1.

### 16.4 Summary

The conclusions of this report are limited in three respects: the research constraints, the observation methods and the sample. Because modified programs are not executed, the meanings of the fields can only be inferred. Because there are no per-task hardware counters, frequency readings or dedicated power rails, the behavior inside tasks, the frequency of the shared cluster and the power of the ANE can be measured only indirectly. With only one machine and one system version, some numerical values may vary with configuration and version. Under these limitations, a number of hardware parameters and behaviors remain undetermined. Among them, the practical benefit of the new data formats, the control details of the CLPC, and the cost of data exchange in dual-ANE programs have the greatest impact on developers, and all three can be further answered by follow-up experiments within the constraints of this report.

## 17. Conclusion

Under the constraint that models are executed only through the public Core ML interfaces and that no modified program is run, this report systematically characterizes the microarchitecture of the M6 Neural Engine along the lifecycle of a single inference call. It combines compiled-artifact analysis, kdebug firmware timestamps, IOReport counters, SMC power-rail measurements, and read-only analysis of the compiler, the driver, and the firmware. The results support the judgment advanced in the Introduction: the practical performance of the ANE is determined jointly by the static scheduling performed by the compiler, the shared resources of the two engines, and the dynamic management performed by the host, and not by the compute array alone.

Structurally, the M6 integrates two complete ANEs on a single die. Each engine contains 16 NE cores. The two engines each have their own power gating but are powered up and down synchronously, and they share a clock source yet each forms a separate performance domain. For each model, the compiler generates both a single-ANE program and a dual-ANE program. A program consists of a sequence of task descriptors, and each task descriptor is a sequence of register write packets that contains no instructions and no branches. From the perspective of the compiler, the hardware parameters of the M6 are identical to those of the next-generation H19.

In terms of computation and storage, each NE completes 256 FP16 multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations or 512 INT8 MAC operations per cycle. At full load the NE clock is 2.58 GHz, the peak FP16 direct-convolution throughput of a single engine is 21.1 TFLOPS, the measured marginal throughput is 20.6 TFLOPS, and with both engines cooperating the figure is 39.5 TFLOPS. The compiler sets a resident-weight limit of 64 KiB for each NE and uses the L2 of each engine at 2 MiB, whereas the physical capacity of on-chip storage is unknown. The weight-read bandwidth is about 150 GB/s and is shared by the two engines, while the bandwidth for activations is only about 35–40 GB/s per engine. FP16 MAC operations use a Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator; when the accumulated value reaches 32768, it overflows to infinity, and this behavior is identical on the M4.

In terms of weight format, the benefit of the ANE comes from reducing the number of bytes read rather than from increasing the dense peak. W8, 2–6-bit palettes, and pruned weights are all stored and read in compressed formats. On layers that are weight-read-bound, latency shortens approximately in proportion to the compressed byte count: a 4-bit palette yields 0.32 times the FP16 value, 90% pruning yields 0.16 times, and the two forms of compression can be combined. The compute array skips MAC operations involving zero weights. On 1×1 convolutions with weights resident on chip, 90% pruning reaches 53.6 TFLOPS when normalized to the dense MAC count, which is 2.5 times the direct-convolution peak, and unstructured pruning is as fast as 2:4 structured pruning; this capability is likewise present on the M4. The compute array does not skip zero activations, but when all activations of a convolution are zero, ANE power is about 58% lower (the same on the M4), and the power saving is largely per element, so zeros need not occur in pairs. Pruning a 3×3 convolution causes it to lose Winograd, and int4 block-wise quantization and vector palettization cannot be compiled with the generation parameters adopted in this report; these are cases to avoid when compressed formats are used.

In dual-engine cooperation, the compiler determines what each of the two engines does, the runtime determines which engine receives each request, and measurements quantify the benefit of these arrangements. The compiler's split cost model assumes that the two engines do not interfere with each other and that their workloads are equal. The runtime executes the ANE1 work whenever a dual-ANE program contains such work, and it distributes multiple requests that are in flight at the same time across the two engines; asynchronous calls to the same model from the same process behave likewise, whereas requests are serialized when multiple threads call the same model synchronously. On compute-bound networks these arrangements come close to expectations, with a speedup of 1.92 and a scaling efficiency of about 96%. The gap arises from two points that the cost model ignores. First, the two engines share the weight-read path, so weight-read-bound workloads gain only limited benefit: with dual-ANE programs, a convolution chain whose weights differ across layers gains about 1.4×, and a rewritten kernel under row split gains nothing, while request-level concurrency gains about 1.4×, almost all of it from eliminating host idle time. Second, tiling assigns work in whole tiles, so throughput is 11–16% lower when the number of tiles is odd.

In scheduling, frequency scaling, and power, the practical performance of the ANE is determined to a large extent by the host. Synchronous calls incur host overhead of about 0.16–0.36 ms outside ANE tasks. The CLPC selects a DVFS state according to utilization and, when the interval between calls is short, holds the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77%; consequently, the greater the host overhead, the lower the DVFS state at which the ANE runs. Keeping multiple requests in flight through asynchronous calls can circumvent this limitation at the application level. In terms of power, this report finds that the SMC power rail PP0b supplies both the ANE and the S cores and P cores of the CPU. After the CPU cluster is subtracted, a single engine at full load draws about 6.2 W and two engines draw about 10.8 W, and each FP16 MAC operation consumes about 0.58 pJ (after subtracting shared overhead; 0.72–0.79 pJ without subtraction). At low states, the energy per inference is about one third of that at the top state.

Based on these results, this report summarizes three principles for deploying models on the ANE: keep the ANE busy, keep data on chip, and place computation on the shapes and precisions at which the hardware excels. This report also compiles 14 suspected defects or contested designs and 10 behaviors that are not documented. Most of them stem from the immaturity of the dual-engine compilation path, a mismatch between host-side scheduling and power policies and the load characteristics of the ANE, and a lack of transparency in how Core ML changes the execution mode. The most consequential of these appears in a practical model. When a dual-engine program is generated for h18g, the global refinement of spatial splits writes weight copies to swap files, so the audio encoder of Whisper large-v3-turbo requires about 78 GB of disk space and takes more than 20 minutes to compile through Core ML, whereas the M4 needs only 80 s. The compiler service continues to run after the client exits and may fill the system disk.

The application benchmarks agree in direction and magnitude with the expectations derived from the above conclusions. On compute-dense tasks such as LLM prefill, batch image classification, and text detection, the ANE on the M6 is 1.8–2.7 times faster than on the M4. On tasks with very little work per call, such as processing one image at a time, it is only about 1.4 times faster. Custom models, which reduce the number of ANE calls and the weight bit width, improve ordinary decoding speed by about 31% over standard models, and reach about 1.7 times the speed of the standard model once speculative decoding is added. The application benchmarks cannot separate certain factors, such as the portion of the text-detection speedup that exceeds the peak ratio and the individual contributions of call count and weight bit width (see Section 14.6). The benchmarks also reveal that, when the M6 processes one image at a time, its top-1 classification result differs from that of the GPU in 5.9% of cases; the cause has not been determined.

From the M1 to the M6, the ANE has followed a path of a "stable core, expanding periphery": the compute core and the programming model have remained essentially unchanged, while clock rates, bandwidth, and supported data formats have increased from generation to generation, and the M6 doubles its compute throughput by replicating the engine. Beyond dense peak throughput, the ANE has been able to read compressed weights and skip zero weights since at least the M4. For on-device generative models that are weight-read-bound, these capabilities are more valuable than adding MAC units. The new layout that has already appeared in the compiler contains fields for the MX format, and on this basis we speculate that subsequent ANEs will support MX and other lower-bit-width formats. As on-device generative models place increasing demands on weight-read bandwidth and data formats, the development priorities of the ANE will very likely continue to shift from peak compute toward the coordination of storage, formats, and the system. The methods and public tools of this report can provide a foundation for studying subsequent ANE generations.

## Appendix A. Tools and Reproduction

This appendix lists the tools used in the experiments of this report, the corresponding data directories, and whether each tool requires root, and it gives the reproduction procedures for several representative experiments. All experiments were performed in the environment described in Section 3.1: macOS 27.0.1 (26A434), the ANE compiler `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`, and test models generated by coremltools 9.0 and PyTorch 2.7 (the generation script [wfmt_gen.py](../tools/05_compute/wfmt_gen.py) for the weight-format experiments does not require PyTorch). The Python version used in the experiments of this report is v3.12.9; the analysis scripts use numpy v2.0.1, and plotting the error figures of the activation functions additionally requires matplotlib. The C and Objective-C tools must be compiled with clang on the M6, and the compilation command is written at the beginning of each source file. The model generation scripts call `xcrun coremlcompiler` to compile `.mlpackage` files into `.mlmodelc` files, so the Xcode command-line tools must also be installed.

The execution of the tools observes the constraints of Section 3.2. The test models are always executed through the public Core ML interface `MLModel`. [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m) calls `ANECCompile`, which only compiles; the resulting compiled artifacts are only read and compared, and they are never loaded or executed. IOReport, SMC and IORegistry are only read. The tools in [tools/re/](../tools/re/) only read the locally installed compiler, kernelcache and firmware, and they do not modify them; among them, [halpatch_lldb.py](../tools/re/halpatch_lldb.py) rewrites individual HAL fields within this report's own compilation process (Section 3.2), and the resulting artifacts are likewise only read and not executed. The tools that require root fall into the following categories. The first category is the kdebug collection scripts of the chapters (most are named `*_ktrace.sh`, and there are also `d1_trace.sh`, `gov_step.sh` and `gov_target*.sh`). They record only the events of class 0x06 and subclass 0x1b through `ktrace trace -t -N -f S0x061b`, run the workload with reduced privileges through `sudo -u "$SUDO_USER"`, and change the owner of the result files back to the ordinary user when they finish. Among them, [d2_wbound.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d2_wbound.sh) and [wfmt.sh](../tools/05_compute/wfmt.sh) check whether the current process is root; when it is not, they still execute the workload and only omit the recording of kdebug events. The second category consists of reading the kernel log with `log stream` and terminating the `aned` process after the compiler service crashes. These two operations have no corresponding script, and the experimenter performs them manually in a terminal. In addition, [perfcsv_lldb.txt](../tools/05_compute/perfcsv_lldb.txt) is a debugging script that observes this report's own `anecc` process with `sudo lldb`; it only sets breakpoints and reads registers. [tdtrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/tdtrace_lldb.py), [swaptrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/swaptrace_lldb.py) and [halpatch_lldb.py](../tools/re/halpatch_lldb.py) also debug this report's own `anecc` process, but they require only that the process carry the `get-task-allow` signature and do not require root. [power_suite.sh](../tools/11_power/power_suite.sh) invokes `powermetrics` as root; because `powermetrics` cannot report the ANE power on the M6, this script and its data have been invalidated (Section 13.1). All other tools do not require root.

The tools in the repository are stored in directories by chapter, whereas in actual runs all tools are placed flat in a single working directory to avoid import problems, and the scripts invoke one another through relative paths such as `./bondrun`. [deploy_m6.sh](../tools/deploy_m6.sh) synchronizes the tools of the repository to the M6 in this flat layout. For Python scripts that have local dependencies, we added search paths to the scripts so that they run under both layouts. The commands below assume execution in this flat working directory.

### A.1 Shared Tools

Table A-1 lists the tools in [tools/common/](../tools/common/) and [tools/lib/](../tools/lib/). The former contains the runners, samplers and model generation scripts shared across chapters, and the latter contains the parsing library for compiled artifacts. The table does not list the six model list files under `tools/common/` (`b2.list`, `b2b.list`, `b4.list`, `b6.list`, `c1.list`, `c2.list`); these files are merely lists of model names for [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py).

Environment variables set the warm-up duration, zero-valued inputs, an interval or cache flush before each call, the per-call time sequence, and stepped call intervals | No       |
| [runall.sh](../tools/common/runall.sh)          | Runs `bondrun` on each model of a set in turn, printing one line per model: the median, p10, the interrupt count of each ANE per call, and the compute-clock on-time                                                                   | No       |
| [mtrun.m](../tools/common/mtrun.m)              | Invokes one or more models concurrently from multiple threads within the same process, and outputs the throughput and median time of each group and the interrupt counts of the two ANEs                                                                                 | No       |
| [asyncrun.m](../tools/common/asyncrun.m)        | Invokes the same `MLModel` object within a single process in a synchronous, asynchronous (keeping K requests in flight) or batched manner, and outputs the throughput, latency, the interrupt counts of the two ANEs and a histogram of read bandwidth                                                      | No       |
| [cmload.m](../tools/common/cmload.m)            | Times only the first load of Core ML (including ANE compilation); the compute units and the QoS of the initiating thread can be specified                                                                                   | No       |
| [coldwake.m](../tools/common/coldwake.m)        | After idling for a given duration, measures the time of the first call and of several subsequent calls, and reads the IOP power states and clock-on times of the two ANEs from IOReport                                                                   | No       |
| [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m)              | Calls `ANECCompile` within the process to compile the MIL in a `.mlmodelc` into HWX for a specified target; compilation options are passed in the form `key=value`                                                        | No       |

| [iorlist.c](../tools/common/iorlist.c)          | Lists the IOReport channels whose names contain a given keyword, and samples them to report non-zero values                                                                                                                                                | No       |
| [socsamp.c](../tools/common/socsamp.c)          | Samples at millisecond intervals the DVFS state of the SoC voltage domain, the SoC floor summarized by PMP, and the bandwidth vote of ANE0; its timestamps share the time base of kdebug                                                                  | No       |
| [smcpower.c](../tools/common/smcpower.c)        | Lists the power keys in SMC, or samples given keys at 100 ms intervals and outputs their average and maximum values                                                                                                                        | No       |
| [pclus.c](../tools/common/pclus.c)              | Simultaneously samples the power of the `PACC0` cluster (S-cores and P-cores) in IOReport and the SMC power rails, in order to subtract this part of the power from PP0b                                                               | No       |
| [membw.swift](../tools/common/membw.swift)      | Measures the read-only and copy DRAM bandwidth on the GPU with Metal, as a control workload that shares DRAM with the ANE                                                                         | No       |
| [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py)            | Generates a conv + bias + relu chain from a model name (kernel size, channels, spatial size and layer count; `_u` indicates that the layers have different weights) and compiles it to `.mlmodelc`                                                        | No       |
| [ioch.py](../tools/common/ioch.py)              | Generates 1×4 convolution chains with variable numbers of input and output channels, to examine the relation between the overhead of dual-ANE splitting and the input and output sizes of a subgraph                                                | No       |
| [mk.py](../tools/common/mk.py)                  | Generates the benchmark model of Chapter 5 (three layers of 64-channel 1×1 convolution followed by relu)                                                                                           | No       |
| [hwx_parse.py](../tools/lib/hwx_parse.py)       | Parses the header of an HWX and its load commands (segments, sections, symbol table, and so on)                                                                                             | No       |

| [tdpkt.py](../tools/lib/tdpkt.py)               | Parses the register-write packets in a TD according to the packet header format                                                                                                                                             | No       |
| [tdwalk.py](../tools/lib/tdwalk.py)             | Traverses the v24 instruction stream TD by TD using the length field of the TD header, and outputs the header words, flag words and address packets                                                                                          | No       |
| [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py)       | Reads from the TD stream the number of columns of each on-chip tile, and compares the ANE0 and ANE1 streams (applicable only to simple models such as 1×1 convolution chains with H = 1)                                                               | No       |
| [bonded_cases.py](../tools/lib/bonded_cases.py) | Generates a set of small MIL programs for observing how the h18g dual-ANE mode splits different operations                                                                                         | No       |

### A.2 Experiments by Chapter

Table A-2 lists, by chapter, the tools, data and root requirement of each experiment. The Identifier column gives the experiment identifiers of the research notes (Section 3.3): group A covers the static structure, group B the compute array, group C storage and bandwidth, group D scheduling, group E power, group F numerics, group G synthesis, and group H unplanned supplementary experiments; experiments without an identifier were performed as follow-up experiments outside the identifier scheme. Experiment identifiers always include the word "experiment", to distinguish them from the defects and behaviors in Appendix D, which are also labeled with letters and numbers (for example, defect B9 is unrelated to experiment B9). The tool directories correspond to chapters as follows: `03_compile` to Chapter 5, `04_schedule` to Chapter 6, `05_compute` to Chapter 7, `06_numerics` to Chapter 8, `07_memory` to Chapter 9, `08_bonded` to Chapter 10, `09_clock` to Chapter 11, `10_clpc` to Chapter 12, and `11_power` to Chapter 13. For historical reasons, a few tools are placed in the directories of other chapters; the table assigns them to the chapter that matches their actual use. "Optional" indicates that the script additionally records kdebug events when run as root, and that it still yields timing results when not run as root.

Three points about Table A-2 require explanation. First, [d1_trace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1_trace.sh) depends on an ANE monitoring program named anemon [27], which we developed separately. It is not included in this repository; it must be obtained from its GitHub repository and placed at the location that the script requires (`~/anemon-dev`) before the first-round fixed-overhead measurements of Chapter 6 can be reproduced. However, the conclusion of that round concerning the per-layer overhead outside the task has been corrected by the measurements of [d1b_ktrace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_ktrace.sh) (Appendix C.8). The task durations measured in that round are not affected by this correction, and Table 6-4 in Section 6.5.1 and Section 11.2.2 still use these data. Second, the register mapping adopted by [l2scan.py](../tools/07_memory/l2scan.py) has been shown to be erroneous (as noted at the top of the file); the script is retained only as a record. The evidence obtained from it has been withdrawn, and the conclusion on L2 capacity in Section 9.2.1 does not depend on it. Third, [ane_bw.py](../tools/07_memory/ane_bw.py) was taken from another project of ours, and its usage notes at the top of the file correspond to the directory structure of that original project. Section 9.3.2 borrows only the model constructor from it, and the timing uses `bondrun` instead.

### A.3 Typical Reproduction Procedures

This section illustrates the reproduction steps with three experiments. In the commands, `python` refers to an interpreter with coremltools and PyTorch installed (Section 3.1), and `python3` refers to an interpreter that requires only numpy. All commands are executed in the flat working directory.

The first example is the single-ANE FP16 marginal throughput of Section 7.2.1. We use [bond_sweep.py](../tools/08_bonded/bond_sweep.py) to generate a 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels, spatial size 1×128, and weights shared across layers, and we take two points, with 256 and 384 layers. The multiply-accumulate workload of each layer is 2 × 512 × 512 × 128 ≈ 0.0671 GFLOP. Multiplying this by the number of layers gives the workload per call (17.18 GFLOP for 256 layers and 25.77 GFLOP for 384 layers), which is passed to `bondrun` as its third argument to output the throughput per call. By default, `bondrun` first runs calls continuously for 1 s as a warm-up.

```bash
python bond_sweep.py sweep k1x1_512_h1w128_L256 k1x1_512_h1w128_L384
clang -fobjc-arc -O2 bondrun.m -framework Foundation -framework CoreML -o bondrun
./bondrun sweep/k1x1_512_h1w128_L256.mlmodelc 1000 17.18
./bondrun sweep/k1x1_512_h1w128_L384.mlmodelc 1000 25.77
```

The interrupt count of ANE1 in the output should be 0, to confirm that both points use only ANE0. In our data ([bond_ksweep_run.txt](../data/08_bonded/bond_ksweep_run.txt)), the median times of the two points are 1178.0 µs and 1595.8 µs, respectively. This gives a slope of (1595.8 − 1178.0) ÷ 128 ≈ 3.26 µs per layer, which corresponds to 0.0671 GFLOP ÷ 3.26 µs ≈ 20.6 TFLOPS. Changing the spatial width to 256 (`k1x1_512_h1w256_L*`) yields the corresponding result for dual ANEs. Each point should be measured in at least two rounds.

The second example is the breakdown of each call into its stages by kdebug, as in Section 6.5.2. The models are generated by [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py) from the names in [d1b_names.txt](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_names.txt), and they are divided into two groups whose per-layer workloads differ by a factor of 4. Because `d1b_ktrace.sh` records only 2 s of events, starting about 2.5 s after each model begins running, each model must first be run once so that the compilation performed during the first load does not fall into the recording window.

```bash
python chain.py d1b $(cat d1b_names.txt)
for n in $(cat d1b_names.txt); do ./bondrun d1b/$n.mlmodelc 300 > /dev/null; done
sudo ./d1b_ktrace.sh
python3 d1b_stages.py d1b_kt
```

The collection script runs `bondrun` in the background as the ordinary user, records the events of the ANE subclass with `ktrace trace -t -N -f S0x061b -T 2`, and retains only the events listed in Table 6-1. At the end, the script repeats the three models under 12 low-priority spinner processes, as a control for the case in which the CPU cannot enter deep idle. `d1b_stages.py` splits each call into "submission → task end" and the stages that follow the task end, and it outputs the median of each stage ([d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)). The procedures of the other kdebug experiments are the same, differing only in the retained events and the analysis script. For example, the kernel-shape experiment of Section 7.4.2 is collected with `sudo ./ks4_ktrace.sh` and analyzed with `python3 ks4_fit.py ks4_kt`; the list file [ks4_list.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_list.txt) gives, on each line, a model name and its number of invocations.

The third example concerns compilation and TD parsing in Chapter 5. [mk.py](../tools/common/mk.py) generates the baseline model `conv3.mlpackage`. After `coremlcompiler` compiles it to `.mlmodelc`, `anecc` reads the `model.mil` within it and compiles it to HWX for the specified target. The header of `anecc.m` does not give a compilation command; the file depends only on the Foundation framework, and the command below has been verified to compile successfully.

```bash
python mk.py
xcrun coremlcompiler compile conv3.mlpackage .
clang -fobjc-arc -O2 anecc.m -framework Foundation -o anecc
./anecc conv3.mlmodelc out_h18g h18g
python3 hwx_parse.py out_h18g/model.hwx
python3 hwx_bonded.py out_h18g/model.hwx --diff
python3 tdwalk.py out_h18g/model.hwx nonbonded
python3 tdpkt.py out_h18g/model.hwx nonbonded 3
```

Replacing the target name with `h16g`, `h17s`, `h18` or `h19` yields the artifacts of the other targets compared in Section 5.1; our artifacts are stored in [data/03_compile/hwx/](../data/03_compile/hwx/). Compilation options are appended after the target name in the form `key=value`, and target options must be written in the form `all.key=value`. To export the per-TD CSV of the compiler's performance model, [anevariant.c](../tools/common/anevariant.c) must first be compiled into a dynamic library, and the model is then compiled with `DYLD_INSERT_LIBRARIES=./anevariant.dylib ./anecc <mlmodelc> <output directory> h18g all.DebugMask=0x800`. [perfcsv.py](../tools/05_compute/perfcsv.py) wraps this procedure and aligns the CSV with the TDs row by row.

The reverse-engineering tools in [tools/re/](../tools/re/), except `halpatch_lldb.py`, all work in read-only mode. They load the compiler frameworks within this report's own process, locate functions by their symbol tables, read static parameter tables or export machine code, or perform string and disassembly analysis on the kernelcache and firmware images extracted from the local machine. The outputs of these tools are derived content of Apple binaries; they are not distributed with the repository, and readers must regenerate them on their own machines.

## Appendix B. Data Index

This appendix provides an index of the repository's [data/](../data/) directory. Like `tools/`, the subdirectories of `data/` are organized by chapter; the chapter text cites the files therein through relative links, and the contents of each subdirectory are listed in Table B-1. The data in the repository consist mainly of text, including timing output, IOReport and SMC samples, condensed kdebug event records, fitting results, and parsed results of compiled artifacts. Larger compiled artifacts and raw kdebug records are published separately as archives (Appendix B.2). Data that has been invalidated and is stored in `data/_obsolete/` is not listed in this appendix.

When using these data, note that some early data were corrected or reinterpreted in later experiments, but the original files are retained as they are. For example, the PP0b values in `power_smc.txt` and `power_parts/`–`power_parts3/` do not subtract the power of the `PACC0` cluster, and Chapter 13 adopts only the rows for the large models from these files. The files `c3c4_run.txt` and `c4_run.txt` in `07_memory/` contain host overhead, and their conclusions have been corrected by the kdebug measurements of Tile DMA. The extent to which each file is used is as indicated by the citations in the main text.
