The four attachments together total about 330 MB, which is about 98% of the current size of the `data/` directory (336 MB). The summary and fitting results in each directory (for example, `ks4_fit.txt`, `d1b_stages.txt`, and each `fit.txt` such as `gov_step/fit.txt`) are small in size, and it is recommended that they remain in the repository so that readers can verify the numbers in the main text without downloading the attachments.

### B.3 Data for Chapter 14

Only part of the data for the application benchmarks in Chapter 14 is currently in the code repository: the four ANE resource curves used in Figure 14-1 (for the standard model and the custom model with MTP disabled, one each for the M4 and the M6) are extracted from the benchmark records by [ane_curve_extract.py](../tools/12_apps/ane_curve_extract.py) and stored in [12_apps/ane_curve/](../data/12_apps/ane_curve/). The remaining data include the speed test results of each model on the M6 and M4, the busy fraction and memory-read bandwidth of the ANE while running under monitoring, and the power records of individual units and of the whole system. Of these, the busy fraction and read bandwidth were collected by anemon [27], and the power was collected by `powermetrics` and the SMC (Section 3.3). anemon is a program that we developed and released separately, and it is not included in this repository. We plan to supplement the remaining data after organizing it; until then, most conclusions of Chapter 14 cannot be reproduced from this repository and anemon alone.

## Appendix C. Corrections Log

This report states only the final conclusions in its main text. During the study, several of our own early conclusions were later overturned or revised by new evidence. These episodes reflect pitfalls that are easy to fall into in measurement methodology, and they are therefore recorded together in this appendix. The entries follow the order of the sections in the main text. Each entry states the early conclusion and its basis, the evidence that overturned it, and the subsection of the main text where the corrected conclusion appears. Discussion of conclusions drawn from the literature appears in Chapter 2 and in the comparison subsections of each chapter, and is not covered here.

### C.1 Parameter Set Used by the M6 (Chapters 4 and 5)

We had treated the compiler's H18 parameter set as the parameter set of the M6, described the change from M5 to M6 as "H17 → H18", and derived the ANE frequency from the H18 frequency table. Reverse engineering `TargetH18g` established that h18g uses `Soc2026BaseLine` and `ZinIrHal2026BaseLine`. The latter is byte-for-byte identical to the H19 parameter table, and H18 is the single-engine target of the previous generation (Sections 4.2 and 5.4).

### C.2 Attribution and Meaning of HAL Fields (Chapters 5 and 13)

Offsets 0x72a, 0x72f/0x730 (vector palettization), and 0x73b had been listed as changes from H17 to H18. After checking H16g, H17, and H17s, we established that these offsets had already changed between H16 and H17 (Section 5.4). The values 10 and 50 at offset 0x890 had been speculated to be a "50 GB/s bandwidth constant." Disassembling the performance model established that they are the energy per byte of memory access (10 pJ/B and 50 pJ/B; Section 13.6).

### C.3 Rule for the Number of TDs per Layer (Chapter 5)

Based on the compiled artifacts for 16×32 inputs, we had believed that the number of TDs per layer is generally equal to ⌈weights per NE ÷ 64 KiB⌉. The notes also misrecorded the 1×9 rewritten kernel at 8×32 as having 3 TDs. A statistical survey of 288 shapes shows that all kernels at 4×32 and all rewritten kernels at 8×32 have only one TD per layer (data: [bsdecide.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsdecide.txt), [ks4_hwx/](../data/05_compute/ks4_hwx/)). This rule holds only when the input is large (Section 5.6).

### C.4 Interpretation of TD Fields (Chapter 5)

Words such as `0x93498005` in convolution TDs had been treated as configuration words and interpreted bit by bit. After the packet format was decoded, we established that this word is a masked-write packet header (Section 5.7.2). The 0x134x register group had been believed to belong to L2. Analysis of address packets showed that it is the source register of the Tile DMA (Section 5.7.6).

### C.5 TD Layout Version (Chapter 5)

We had speculated that the M6 uses layout v31 and that H19 uses v36. Reverse engineering the compiler's dispatch jump table established that h18g and h19 are both subtype 11 and use v24. Because the relevant fields are identical in v19 through v31, the decoding obtained from the v31 functions remains applicable (data: [tdv/](../data/03_compile/tdv/); Section 5.7.7).

### C.6 CPU Cores Used for Compilation (Chapter 5)

An early note treated IOReport's `PCPU0–1` as P cores and recorded that "direct compilation runs on P cores." The M6 in fact has 2 S cores (`PCPU0–1`), 4 P cores, and 6 E cores. The busy time of each core during direct compilation was not recorded, and the claim that compilation runs on the S cores is only an inference. The conclusion that compilation runs on the E cores during Core ML loading is not affected (Section 5.9).

### C.7 Whisper Encoder Swap File and Dual-ANE Imbalance (Chapter 5)

Based on call-stack sampling, we had speculated that the swap file arises because global refinement copies and hashes the weights each time it produces a merged result. Based on the compiled artifacts, we had also concluded that in the dual-ANE program of the Whisper encoder, the TD sequence of ANE1 is about 13 times that of ANE0. Rechecking showed that the swap file is a `ZinIrFileBacking` that is only allocated and never freed. It holds float32 weight copies newly created by merged convolutions, and it has nothing to do with hash-based deduplication of weights. The "about 13 times" figure arose because a parsing tool, when reading the last TD sequence (ANE1), read through to the end of the `__TEXT` segment and therefore counted data from other sections that follow it. After the tool was corrected, the ratio computed from the sums of `exe_cycles` is 1.36–1.48 (data: [swaptrace/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/swaptrace/); tool: [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py); Sections 5.8.3 and 5.9). The column-count sums of the two engines in Table 10-3 in Section 10.5 equal the total of the single-ANE program, and their ratio agrees with the measured time ratio, so they are not affected by this problem.

### C.8 Per-Layer Overhead Outside the Task (Chapter 6)

From a line connecting two points, one at 16 layers and one at 384 layers, we had derived an overhead of "about 0.48 µs per layer" outside the task. Two groups of models whose layer counts differ by a factor of 4 but whose task durations are identical show that the overhead after the task ends varies only with task duration (data: [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt)). The value is an artifact of the line, which crossed a host-CPU deep-idle step (Section 6.5.2).

### C.9 Independent Branches and a Single TD (Chapter 6)

Because the time for independent branches equals the sum of their two times, we had inferred that the hardware executes only one TD at a time. Whole-graph timing cannot distinguish the compiler's serial scheduling from a hardware constraint, so Section 6.6.1 retains only the conclusion that "no overlap occurred in the programs measured."

### C.10 Location of Request Serialization for the Same Program (Chapter 6)

When multiple threads synchronously call the same model, requests are serialized. From this we had speculated that the unit of serialization is the combination of a process and a program: aned shares a single loaded program, and the driver maintains a request queue for each program. Experiments with asynchronous calls from a single process show that, when two or more requests are kept in flight, asynchronous requests from the same process to the same `MLModel` object are assigned to both engines (data: [d2_async.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_async.txt)). Serialization therefore occurs only on the synchronous call path, and its exact location has not yet been identified (Sections 6.7.2 and 10.7).

### C.11 The Higher MAC Tier (Chapter 7)

In the performance model, the tier of 512 FP16 MACs per NE per cycle had been attributed to the small-input mode with 64 input channels. Analysis of chain models shows that the tier depends on the size of the work unit. Kdebug re-measurement shows that all dual-ANE programs actually executed by Core ML fall in the base tier, so whether the higher MAC tier can be reached has not been verified (data: [hi_kt/](../data/05_compute/hi_kt/); Section 7.2.3).

### C.12 Kernel-Width Limit and Rewriting Rule (Chapter 7)

We had described the kernel-width limit of 8 at stride 1 as a hardware limit, recorded the rewriting rule as "padding the kernel width to an even number," and interpreted the sudden increase in time at 1×16 as an efficiency cliff. After correction, 8 and 16 are boundaries reflected in the compiler's encoding, and the hardware capability has not been verified. Kw is the kernel width plus 1, padded to an even number. The 1×16 case is in fact a compilation failure, which causes the entire model to fall back to the CPU (Section 7.4.1).

### C.13 Tap Count of Rewritten Kernels (Chapter 7)

The figure of 19.3 taps per channel pair for the 1×9 rewritten kernel had been taken as the number of taps actually stored. It is a normalized average obtained by dividing the byte count of the weight segment by (Cin × Cout × 2 B). The corresponding values for 1×10 and 1×12 (21.53 and 25.78) are clearly below 2 × Kw, which indicates that the two staggered kernel sets are not stored in full (data: [ks4_hwx/](../data/05_compute/ks4_hwx/); Section 7.4.1).

### C.14 Efficiency of Elongated Kernels (Chapter 7)

Early measurements gave an efficiency of 18–60% for elongated kernels such as 1×9. These figures were contaminated by waste from timing narrow widths row by row, and by slope errors caused by switching between single-ANE and dual-ANE operation. Re-measuring with shapes fixed by engine mode and with firmware timestamps shows that the rewritten kernel on a single ANE at 8×32 reaches about 85% (data: [ks4_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_fit.txt); Section 7.4.2).

### C.15 Bottleneck of the Rewritten Kernel (Chapter 7)

Because the back-calculated tap count agreed with the compiled artifacts, we had concluded that the time of about 17 µs per layer for the 1×9 kernel is determined by DRAM weight-read bandwidth (2.54 MB ÷ 150 GB/s). W8 reduces the weights per layer to 1.33 MB, yet the time was 17.0 µs compared with 17.8 µs, so the time was not reduced (data: [wfmt.txt](../data/05_compute/wfmt/wfmt.txt)). The bottleneck is a stage that is metered by weight elements and shared by the two engines. Its location has not yet been determined (Sections 7.4.2 and 7.5).

### C.16 Width of the PE (Chapter 7)

PE throughput had been converted, using the NE clock of 2.58 GHz, to about 23.5 elements per cycle. After we confirmed that the PE is not in the NE clock domain, we converted it using the derived frequency of about 1.8–1.9 GHz, which gives about 32 elements per cycle. This value is an inference (Section 7.6.2).

### C.17 Anomalous Latency at 32 FP16 Layers (Chapter 7)

The FP16 chain became anomalously slow at 32 layers, and we initially suspected a dependence on the weights. Breakdowns by stage and execution plans show that the cause was an increase in host wake-up overhead, together with execution plans for the 32-layer and 34-layer models that placed the final relu on the CPU (data: [b6_kt/](../data/05_compute/b6_kt/); Section 7.7).

### C.18 Two States of Weight Reads (Chapter 9, Also Chapter 6)

Core ML timing slopes indicate that the weight-read bandwidth is about 130 GB/s below 48 MiB and about 150 GB/s above 50 MiB, with a step of about 55 µs in between. We had concluded that the ANE switches its read method at about 48 MiB. Firmware timestamps show that the ANE's weight reads remain at about 152 GB/s throughout (data: [sw_ktrace_sum.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_ktrace_sum.txt)). The step lies on the host side and disappears when the CPU cannot enter deep idle (data: [sw_spinner.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_spinner.txt)). Its threshold corresponds to a task duration of about 350 µs (Sections 6.5.3 and 9.3.2).

### C.19 Early Estimate of Activation Bandwidth (Chapter 9)

The early estimates of "about 40 GB/s for elementwise operations" and "about 52 µs more for each additional 2 MB of input" had been derived from total Core ML call times, in which host overhead accounted for most of the time. Their agreement with later measured values was merely coincidental. Section 9.4.2 uses kdebug task durations instead (data: [tdma_kt/](../data/07_memory/tdma_kt/)).

### C.20 Extra Time of ANE1 (Chapters 9 and 10)

With tiling, ANE1 appeared to take about 10.6 µs more per layer. This was successively explained as a fixed overhead, then as an overhead proportional to the number of channels, and finally as misaligned row starting points. Analysis of the compiled artifacts showed that the dual-ANE program reuses the tile grid of the single-ANE program, and that when the number of tiles is odd, the extra tile is always assigned to ANE1 (data: [align_hwx.txt](../data/07_memory/align_hwx.txt); Sections 9.2.2 and 10.5).

### C.21 Full-Load Frequency of the NE (Chapter 11)

We successively derived about 1.74 GHz from the H18 frequency table, 1.3–1.7 GHz from an assumed MAC width, and then adopted the top state of the compiler's table, 2.508 GHz. Extrapolating a fit of firmware task durations on the ramp-up steps gives a full-load frequency of 2.58 GHz (data: [freq_kt/](../data/09_clock/freq_kt/); Section 11.2.2).

### C.22 PE Speed and SoC DVFS State (Chapter 11)

PE workloads push the SoC voltage to its top state, and we had inferred that PE throughput is affected by the SoC DVFS state. Time-aligned sampling shows that the SoC steps up its DVFS state only after the PE has ramped up, and that the PE remains in a low DVFS state while the SoC is in its top state (data: [socpe_kt/](../data/09_clock/socpe_kt/)). The PE is in an independently scheduled clock domain (Sections 11.3 and 11.4).

### C.23 Criterion of the CLPC (Chapter 12)

From timing results alone, we had speculated that the CLPC adjusts frequency according to whether requests were already queued when a task ended. Kdebug shows that, under synchronous calls, requests are never queued, and that the busy fraction stays at 76–77% during ramp-down. The CLPC is therefore a utilization controller (data: [gov_kt/](../data/10_clpc/gov_kt/)). Table 12-2 lacked a row for G = 3000 µs, which had originally been explained as tasks being filtered out and discarded. The actual reason is that the trace recorded only 75 s (Section 12.2).

### C.24 Utilization Target of the CLPC (Chapter 12)

We had inferred that the utilization target of about 77% is independent of task length, and had considered that a target computed from driver job intervals is exactly 80%. Three rounds of experiments show that 77% holds only when each idle period is no longer than about 1 ms. When idle periods are longer, the busy fraction decreases monotonically without the DVFS state reaching the lowest state. The driver-based measure also falls to about 57% under long idle periods (data: [gov_tg2/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg2/), [gov_tg3/](../data/10_clpc/gov_tg3/); Section 12.4).

### C.25 Ramp-Up and Ramp-Down Speed of the CLPC (Chapter 12, Also Chapter 11)

We had concluded that the CLPC "ramps up fast and ramps down slowly": from the lowest state, about 50 ms suffices to reach above 2448 MHz, whereas ramping down to the lowest state is 2–3 times slower. Chapter 11 likewise stated that ramping from 852 MHz to 2448 MHz requires only 30–40 tasks, about 50 ms. Rechecking showed that the earlier analysis script determined the sleep time before each call individually, by "subtracting 384 µs from the inter-task gap." A small fluctuation in host idle time during G = 0 segments was then misclassified as a different kind of sleep, so the ramp-up segment was split into fragments and timing restarted partway through the ramp-up. The estimate in Chapter 11 also merged the segments before and after a pause of about 28 ms that followed the warm-up calls. After the switching moments were determined by call count, of the six step ramp-ups only the first, which had shorter host idle time, takes about 55 ms; the other five take 101–202 ms. Ramp-downs take 94–167 ms, so the two speeds are comparable. A complete ramp-up requires 69–85 tasks (data: [gov_step/](../data/10_clpc/gov_step/), [freq_kt/](../data/09_clock/freq_kt/); Sections 11.2.3 and 12.3).

### C.26 Components Powered by PP0b (Chapter 13)

PP0b is 0 W when idle and 11.3 W when both ANEs are at full load. We had first treated it as a power rail dedicated to the ANE, and later corrected this to "the ANE and the P-core cluster." Per-core activity statistics established that PP0b powers the ANE and the `PACC0` cluster (S cores and P cores), and that the E cores are not on this rail. ANE power is therefore computed after subtracting the power of the `PACC0` cluster (data: [power_parts4/](../data/11_power/power_parts4/); Section 13.1).

### C.27 Fixed Energy per Call (Chapter 13)

When PP0b was used directly as the ANE power, we had derived an additional fixed energy of about 1.8 mJ per ANE call. After the `PACC0` cluster is subtracted, the fixed overhead per ANE call is only about 0.13 mJ. The remaining 1.6 mJ comes from the CPU occupied by the Core ML call thread (Section 13.5).

### C.28 Power with All-Zero Convolution Inputs (Chapter 13)

We had measured that the ANE power was about 6–7.5% higher when all convolution inputs were zero, and we had speculated that all-zero activations trigger some sparsity or bypass logic. Rechecking showed that the model in question had random bias values. Its activations converged, after a few dozen layers, to one set of values determined by the biases, so an all-zero input made only the first layer zero. After the model was changed to one with zero biases, the ANE power decreased by about 58% when the activations were truly all zero, and the runtime was unchanged. Spatially constant but nonzero inputs draw the same power as random inputs. Re-measurement on the M4 gives the same result (data: [power_zero/](../data/11_power/power_zero/), [power_gate_m4/](../data/11_power/power_gate_m4/); Section 13.3).

### C.29 Speed of the M6 with One Image per Call (Chapter 14)

The first evaluation (October 2) measured the ANE of the M6 at only 222 images per second when processing one ResNet-50 image per call, which is 0.38 times the speed of the M4. We had taken this as an example of "the M6 is slower on frequently invoked small tasks" and discussed its relation to host overhead. In re-measurement, the M6 took about 1.2 ms per call and reached 640–830 images per second, which is 1.1–1.4 times as fast as the M4. The extra 3.3 ms in the first run was a temporary latency added to every call. The speed at a batch of 16 and the GPU result disagreement rate were the same in both measurements. After the front end, input buffers, kernel tracing, and restarts were ruled out as causes, the source of the extra latency could no longer be traced. The Whisper speed data measured on the same day are therefore invalidated (Section 14.4).

## Appendix D. Defect List

This appendix summarizes the suspected defects, contested design choices, and undocumented behaviors that we identified in the experiments, and gives a reproduction method for each item. We stress again that, apart from the M4 control experiments, all experiments in this report were performed on a single M6 device: the SoC is T8152 (compilation target h18g), the memory is 16 GB, the operating system is macOS 27.0.1 (build 26A434), and the ANE compiler is `zin_ane_compiler v10.26.6`. The items fall into three classes: Class A comprises crashes, hangs, and silent errors; Class B comprises behaviors that function correctly but are costly or cannot be controlled by the caller; and Class C comprises behaviors that are not documented but that developers are likely to encounter. These items are suspected defects observed under the experimental conditions of this report, and none has been confirmed by Apple. Cause analyses marked as inferred have not been directly verified.

### A1 Dual-ANE compilation path crashes from stack overflow when compiling a 1×1 convolution chain at 387 layers

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When a 1×1 convolution chain with 64 channels and width 1024, whose layers share weights, is compiled for h18g, chains of 386 layers and fewer succeed, while chains of 387, 388, 400, and 512 layers crash with return code 138 (SIGBUS). The same model compiled for h16g and h18 still succeeds at 512 layers. The crash report shows that the exception occurs on the guard page of the thread stack. The call stack indicates that `ZinIrOpLayerGraphScheduler::Schedule` recurses once per layer and that the stack is exhausted in the parallel attention-branch search that only h18g performs (`FindIntraANEParallelism` → `FindAllAttentionBranchesInRegion` → `ZinIrNgraph::TopologicalSortImpl`). |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py) to generate the 387-layer convolution chain described above, then use [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m) to call `ANECCompile` with the h18g target. |
| Impact | The convolution chains tested on the M6 can be compiled to at most 386 layers. This limit originates in the recursive implementation of the compiler rather than in the hardware. It is not a layer bound that holds for all graphs: a chain of 512 elementwise layers compiles (Table 5-18), and the depth at which graphs of other structures crash was not measured in this report. |
| Workaround | When deploying very deep networks, check whether compilation succeeds and whether the network executes on the ANE; when a crash occurs, split the network into multiple segments. |
| Data and tools | [h9_depth_compile.txt](../data/03_compile/h9_depth_compile.txt); [chain.py](../tools/common/chain.py), [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m) |
| Report sections | Section 5.10 |

### A2 After a compiler service crash, Core ML loading is nondeterministic and may hang permanently, blocking other processes

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When the same group of models from A1 is loaded through Core ML, the 388-layer model and the 400–1024-layer models silently fall back to CPU execution, with zero ANE interrupts and 27–70 ms per call, more than 100 times slower than the ANE. Loading of the 387-layer and 392-layer models hangs permanently: the calling process waits for more than 11 minutes with zero CPU usage, the compiler service is idle, and the system neither reports an error nor falls back to the CPU. After the compiler service crashes or is terminated, aned keeps waiting for its reply, and afterward all processes are blocked from loading ANE models; only restarting aned restores operation. On the M4, the same `.mlmodelc` executes on the ANE at 387, 388, 400, and 448 layers. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Load an A1 model with 387 or more layers through Core ML, and read the interrupt counts of the two ANEs from IOReport to determine whether execution occurs on the ANE. |
| Impact | A compilation failure may appear either as a performance degradation of orders of magnitude or as an indefinite hang, and it may affect other processes on the same system. |
| Workaround | As in A1, check whether very deep networks execute on the ANE, and split them when necessary; restart aned if blocking occurs. |
| Data and tools | [d5_depth_run.txt](../data/04_schedule/d5_depth_run.txt), [d5_depth_m4.txt](../data/04_schedule/d5_depth_m4.txt) |
| Report sections | Section 5.10 |

### A3 Global refinement in the dual-ANE spatial split writes weight copies to swap files

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When the Whisper large-v3-turbo audio encoder from WhisperKit (15,849 operations after Core ML conversion, of which 5,120 are einsum) is compiled for h18g, the compiler writes `anecompiler.swap.*` files to the output directory, at about 2.4 GB per encoder layer (about 60 times the weight size of that layer). Extrapolating from truncation experiments, the complete 32-layer model would require about 78 GB. Compilation takes about 8–9 times as long as that of single-engine h18. The same model compiled for h18 or h16g takes only 41 s and 3.8 GB of disk space. The defect is reproduced by truncating the model to one layer (h18g: 12 s and 2.7 GB; h18: 5 s and no disk usage). |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [whisper_capture.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_capture.sh) to extract the model that Core ML hands to the ANE compiler, use [whisper_trunc.py](../tools/03_compile/whisper_trunc.py) to truncate it to its first N layers, and then use [whisper_anecc.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_anecc.sh) to compile it for h18 and h18g separately, recording the elapsed time and the peak disk usage. |
| Impact | This encoder cannot practically use the ANE on the M6: the first load through Core ML takes more than 20 minutes, and it may fill the system volume of machines with small storage. The swap file is written by `ZinIrFileBacking`, a file-backed allocator that only allocates and never releases; about 90% of it consists of float32 weight copies newly created when convolutions are merged during global refinement, and these copies are not reclaimed until compilation finishes. The directory entry is removed as soon as the file is created, so the file can only be observed from the remaining free disk space. The top-level option `GlobalRefinementInSpatialSplit=false` returns compilation to 8 s and zero disk usage, but the dual-ANE program then degrades to using only ANE0. |
| Workaround | Run the encoder on the GPU; alternatively, load it under a fixed process name and reserve about 80 GB or more of free system-volume space so that compilation occurs only once. |
| Data and tools | [whisper_compile/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/) (`anecc/`, `trunc/`, `swaptrace/`); [whisper_capture.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_capture.sh), [whisper_anecc.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_anecc.sh), [whisper_trunc.py](../tools/03_compile/whisper_trunc.py), [swaptrace_lldb.py](../tools/03_compile/swaptrace_lldb.py), [whisper_fakeweights.py](../tools/03_compile/whisper_fakeweights.py) |
| Report sections | Section 5.9 |

### A4 The compiler service keeps running after its client exits and fills the system volume

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When the model from A3 is loaded through Core ML, `ANECompilerService` continues to run at full load after the loading process exits, and it keeps writing swap files under `/Library/Caches/com.apple.aned/`. During our evaluation, the system volume was left with only 121 MB of free space, after which the system purged the ANE compilation cache, so the next load of every model had to be recompiled. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Run [whisper_tmp.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_tmp.sh) or [whisper_load.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_load.sh) as root to load the encoder under a new process name, then observe the compiler service and the available disk space after the loading process exits. |
| Impact | A runaway compilation can fill the disk of the entire machine and invalidate the ANE compilation caches of other applications. The compiler service has no timeout or disk budget and does not respond to the exit of its client, which places it in the same class of problem as A2. |
| Workaround | To stop a compilation when necessary, terminate `ANECompilerService` as root; the swap files are then released. |
| Data and tools | [whisper_compile/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/) (`wload_M6/`, `wtmp_Mac18_5/`); [whisper_tmp.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_tmp.sh), [whisper_load.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_load.sh) |
| Report sections | Sections 5.9 and 14.4 |

### B1 Compilation time of dual-ANE programs grows superlinearly with the number of elementwise layers

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When the elementwise chain y ← y·u + u is compiled for h18, compilation time grows linearly with the number of steps (about 1.5 ms per step; 0.39 s for 256 steps). When compiled for h18g, compilation time is approximately proportional to the 3.5–3.8th power of the number of steps; 256 steps (512 elementwise layers) take 178.6 s, which is 458 times the single-engine time. Convolution chains are almost unaffected: 256 layers take only 0.5 s longer than on a single engine. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m) to compile elementwise chains with different step counts separately for h18 and h18g, and compare the wall-clock times. |
| Impact | The first compilation of a large model dominated by elementwise operations on the M6 may take several minutes, and the first load through Core ML compounds this with B2. More severe compilation costs in actual models are described in A3. |
| Workaround | This report found no workaround at the compilation-option level. Compilation results are cached, so the cost occurs only at first load. |
| Data and tools | [h7_compile_time.txt](../data/03_compile/h7_compile_time.txt), [whisper_compile/](../data/03_compile/whisper_compile/); [anecc.m](../tools/common/anecc.m), [whisper_load.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_load.sh), [whisper_tmp.sh](../tools/03_compile/whisper_tmp.sh) |
| Report sections | Section 5.9. Which dual-ANE step has superlinear search complexity is not determined in this report. |

### B2 During first load through Core ML, compilation runs only on E cores and is about 2.7 times slower

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | A 128-step elementwise chain (h18g) takes 12.65 s when compiled directly within the process; the compilation is single-threaded, and we infer that it runs on an S core. During the first load through Core ML, the compilation time recorded in the compiler service log is 34.05 s, during which the six E cores are busy for about 30 s in total and the S cores for about 0.2 s. When the client QoS is set between default and user-interactive, the compilation takes 33.7–34.3 s in each case; when it is set to background, the time is 98.8 s. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [cmload.m](../tools/common/cmload.m) to time the first load of an `MLModel`, while recording the system log of the compiler service and the busy time of each CPU core from IOReport. |
| Impact | The ratio of 34.05 s to 12.65 s is 2.7, which is comparable to the single-thread performance ratio of S cores to E cores 〔inferred〕; adjusting the client QoS can only make compilation slower and cannot move it onto S cores. |
| Workaround | The client cannot control this. Compilation results are cached, so the overhead occurs only at first load. |
| Data and tools | [cm_load_vs_compile.txt](../data/03_compile/cm_load_vs_compile.txt); [cmload.m](../tools/common/cmload.m) |
| Report sections | Section 5.9. We infer that the mechanism is as follows: the launchd configuration of aned sets `ProcessType = Adaptive`, and the compiler service inherits this background role. Although its priority is raised (`clientQos=21 threadQos=21`), its threads are still scheduled onto E cores. |

### B3 Requests are serialized when multiple threads synchronously call the same program

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | Within one process, when two or four threads call the same model, the aggregate throughput equals that of a single thread (616–617 calls/s), regardless of whether the threads share one `MLModel` object or each load their own. The per-call time grows in proportion to the number of threads, and all requests execute on ANE0. Two different programs in the same process, or the same model in two processes, can be spread across the two ANEs and execute in parallel; the aggregate throughput of the two-process case is 1.81 times that of a single process. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [mtrun.m](../tools/common/mtrun.m) to call the same model from multiple threads within one process at the same time, and read the interrupt counts of the two ANEs. |
| Impact | The common pattern of one service process with multiple threads synchronously calling the same model can use only one ANE. Asynchronous calls to the same model within the same process are distributed across both ANEs (Section 10.7), which indicates that serialization occurs on the synchronous-call path; the exact location has not been identified. |
| Workaround | Use asynchronous calls through `predictionFromFeatures:completionHandler:` and keep two or more requests in flight; alternatively, distribute requests across different processes, or use different models. |
| Data and tools | [h15_mt.txt](../data/04_schedule/h15_mt.txt), [h15_mt2.txt](../data/04_schedule/h15_mt2.txt), [d2_concurrency.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_concurrency.txt), [d2_async.txt](../data/04_schedule/d2_async.txt); [mtrun.m](../tools/common/mtrun.m), [asyncrun.m](../tools/common/asyncrun.m) |
| Report sections | Sections 6.7 and 10.7 |

### B4 In the W8A8 1×1 convolution chain, activations pass through DRAM at every layer, so W8A8 matches FP16 under dual-ANE execution

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | On a 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels, the DRAM read bandwidth of the FP16 test is nearly zero, whereas each ANE of the W8A8 chain sustains 22–28 GB/s of reads, which corresponds per layer to about the size of one Int8 activation tensor. W8A8 is only about 1.3–1.5 times as fast as FP16 on a single ANE and 0.98 times as fast on dual ANEs, whereas on the compute-dense 3×3 convolution it is exactly 2 times as fast. In the compiled artifact, each W8A8 layer is split into three kinds of TD, namely quantized input (PE), convolution, and dequantized output. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [w8a8.py](../tools/05_compute/w8a8.py) to generate FP16 and W8A8 versions, measure the marginal throughput, and record the DRAM read bandwidth with IOReport. |
| Impact | 1×1 convolutions are limited by the bandwidth of the activation tensors rather than by the MAC array, so W8A8 yields little or no benefit on such layers. We infer that the cause is that per-layer quantization and dequantization route the activations through DRAM. |
| Workaround | This report found no workaround. The benefit of W8A8 is concentrated in compute-dense layers. |
| Data and tools | [h10_w8a8_slope.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_w8a8_slope.txt), [h10_w8a8_k3.txt](../data/05_compute/h10_w8a8_k3.txt); [w8a8.py](../tools/05_compute/w8a8.py) |
| Report sections | Section 7.5 |

### B5 As ANE task duration increases, host-side wake-up overhead rises in steps to about 195 µs

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When task duration does not exceed about 350 µs, the overhead after task end is about 127–183 µs. As task duration increases further, the overhead rises and stabilizes at about 320 µs once the duration reaches about 660 µs or more. All of the increase occurs on the host side: driver completion handling rises from about 21–32 µs to about 106 µs, and user-thread return rises from about 50 µs to about 157 µs, while firmware timestamps show that the ANE itself does not slow down. When 12 spinner processes at nice 20 are run in the background, this portion of the overhead for the 384-layer model falls from 324 µs to 215 µs. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [d1b_ktrace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_ktrace.sh) to record two convolution-chain groups with the same total compute and layer counts differing by a factor of four, then use [d1b_stages.py](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_stages.py) to break down the stages after task end. |
| Impact | The cause is that the CPU waiting for the result enters a deeper idle state. This effect contaminates the timing slope and was once misread as a "48 MiB step" in weight reads (Appendix C.18). |
| Workaround | Submit the next request early with an asynchronous call, or merge multiple inferences into one call. |
| Data and tools | [d1b_stages.txt](../data/04_schedule/d1b_stages.txt), [d1b_kt/](../data/04_schedule/d1b_kt/), [sw_spinner.txt](../data/07_memory/sw_spinner.txt); [d1b_ktrace.sh](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_ktrace.sh), [d1b_stages.py](../tools/04_schedule/d1b_stages.py) |
| Report sections | Section 6.5 |

### B6 The maximum NE frequency in the compiler's performance model does not match the hardware

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | The frequency table `Soc2026BaseLine`, which the compiler uses for h18g, has 32 DVFS states. The first 24 states are identical to `voltage-states8/29` in the device tree, while the last 8 differ: for those 8 states the compiler uses 2244–2508 MHz, whereas the device tree uses 2280–2580 MHz. At full load the hardware runs at 2580 MHz, so the compiler's latency estimates are about 3% too slow. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Compare the compiler's frequency table with the device-tree frequency table, and use kdebug to measure task durations after the clock has ramped to full, in order to back-calculate the full-load frequency. |
| Impact | Split decisions compare the latency of the same model before and after splitting. Since both are biased by the same proportion, the impact should be very small. The discrepancy may reflect a conservative setting in the performance model or an input error. |
| Workaround | No workaround is needed. |
| Data and tools | [freq_kt/](../data/09_clock/freq_kt/); [freq_ktrace.sh](../tools/09_clock/freq_ktrace.sh), [freq_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/freq_fit.py) |
| Report sections | Sections 11.2 and 11.6 |

### B7 At certain depths, Core ML silently places the last operation on the CPU

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | On a 1×1 convolution chain with 512 channels and a 32×32 spatial size (with different weights in each layer), among 28 models only the FP16 models with 32 and 34 layers have execution plans that include a CPU segment, namely `AneInference → Cast → CpuInference → Cast`. The final relu layer runs on the CPU, and these two models are about 100 µs slower than the linear fit. Compiling the complete 32-layer model directly with `ANECCompile` causes no problem; the 32-layer W8 and W8A8 models are not affected. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Following the settings in [b6x_sweep.txt](../data/05_compute/b6x_sweep.txt), sweep the layer count between 16 and 64, inspect the execution plans in the Core ML cache, and sample the calling process. |
| Impact | The split yields no benefit, and it occurs silently. Under the segmenter's cost rules (Section 5.2), placing the final relu on the ANE or on the CPU may have the same total cost, making the two a tie; this phenomenon may be related to that tie. However, a tie does not explain why the behavior appears only at these two depths 〔inferred〕. |
| Workaround | This report found no workaround. The phenomenon can be detected from CPU segments in the execution-plan cache or through sampling. |
| Data and tools | [b6x_sweep.txt](../data/05_compute/b6x_sweep.txt), [b6_sample_top.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_sample_top.txt), [b6_pcpu.txt](../data/05_compute/b6_pcpu.txt) |
| Report sections | Sections 7.7 and 5.2 |

### B8 Work is unevenly divided between the two ANEs under tiling

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | A 1×1 convolution chain with 256 channels and height 1 requires tiling once its width reaches 8192. ANE1 carries 56–59% of the work (56.25% at width 12288 and 59.4% at width 8192), so the per-call time is determined by ANE1, which is 12.5–19% longer than an even split, and throughput falls by 11–16%. The dual-ANE program inherits the tiling grid of the single-ANE program and only divides the tile list into two groups at tile boundaries; when the number of tiles is odd, the extra tile is always assigned to ANE1. At width 14336 (8 tiles), the amount of compute is 17% higher than at width 12288 (7 tiles), yet the time per layer is 2% lower. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [asym_ktrace.sh](../tools/08_bonded/asym_ktrace.sh) to measure the task duration of each ANE, and use [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py) to count the number of TDs and columns per engine. The compiler options `DisableNetworkImbalanceAnalysis` and `EnableForcedMaximalBondedSplit` do not change this division. |
| Impact | A more extreme case occurs in the W8A8 1×1 convolution chain, where work is divided 1 : 2 between the two ANEs, and the speedup of W8A8 over FP16 falls from 1.76–1.87 times to 1.21 times. The dual-ANE program of the Whisper encoder is also unbalanced, owing to the same rule and 128-column alignment, with the estimated workload of ANE1 1.36–1.48 times that of ANE0 (Section 5.9). The assignment rule in the compiler assigns tile i to the ANE numbered (i ≥ T/2). The cost model considers only the sum of per-tile latencies and therefore cannot detect this imbalance (Section 5.8.3). |
| Workaround | Make the number of tiles per layer even. |
| Data and tools | [asym_kt/](../data/08_bonded/asym_kt/), [align_hwx.txt](../data/07_memory/align_hwx.txt), [bsplit_even.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsplit_even.txt), [bsplit_flags.txt](../data/08_bonded/bsplit_flags.txt), [mac3_run.txt](../data/05_compute/mac3_run.txt); [asym_ktrace.sh](../tools/08_bonded/asym_ktrace.sh), [asym_split.py](../tools/08_bonded/asym_split.py), [td_widths.py](../tools/lib/td_widths.py) |
| Report sections | Sections 10.5 and 7.2.2 |

### B9 Weight-read-bound layers are row-split, and the second ANE yields no speedup

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | The 1×9, 1×10, 1×11, and 1×15 convolution chains with 256 channels on a 16×32 grid are row-split: each ANE handles 8 rows and all output channels, and each reads a complete copy of the weights. The two ANEs start and finish simultaneously, but for 1×9 each layer takes 33.0 µs, exactly twice the time a single ANE needs to process the same number of rows, so the parallel efficiency is only 42–43%. The 1×12 to 1×14 convolutions of the same size are split by output channel, so that each ANE reads only half of the weights; each layer takes 24.9–30.4 µs, with an efficiency of 73–77%. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [ks4_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_fit.py) and [ks4_engines.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_engines.py) to process the kdebug records of elongated-kernel chains, and read the split mode from the compiled artifacts. |
| Impact | For rewritten kernels such as 1×9, the two engines share a bottleneck measured in weight elements (Section 7.4.2); for native kernels, the sharing arises from the upper bound imposed by DRAM bandwidth (Section 9.3.4). The compiler's cost model (`LatencyInfo::ComputeSplitLatencyBonded`) multiplies the split latency by 0.5 and does not account for this kind of sharing. |
| Workaround | A weight-read-bound workload gains only a limited benefit, about 1.4 times, from a second ANE, and row split yields no benefit at all. A better choice is to split by output channel, or to use only one ANE. |
| Data and tools | [ks4_fit.txt](../data/05_compute/ks4_fit.txt), [ks4_hwx/](../data/05_compute/ks4_hwx/); [ks4_fit.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_fit.py), [ks4_engines.py](../tools/05_compute/ks4_engines.py) |
| Report sections | Sections 10.4, 7.4.2, 5.8.3, and 9.3.4 |

### B10 The shared cluster ramps up slowly and is repeatedly pushed back to a low DVFS state while the GPU is active

| Item | Details |
| --- | --- |
| Symptom | When only a PE model is executed, after a cold start the PE task remains at the duration of the lowest state (about 1193 µs) for about 0.4–0.5 s before it begins to speed up. During the ramp-up it oscillates between 496 and 1012 µs, and only after about 0.8 s does it settle at 466 µs at full clock. Under the same conditions, the NE clock ramps to full in about 150–200 ms. When the GPU reads memory at the same time, the PE clock is repeatedly pushed back to a low state after it has sped up, so the entire measurement round (about 1 s) never settles at full clock, while the SoC voltage domain remains at its top state, VOVD2, throughout. |
| Trigger and reproduction | Use [socsamp.c](../tools/common/socsamp.c) to sample the states and votes of the SoC voltage domain, record PE task durations with [socpe_ktrace.sh](../tools/09_clock/socpe_ktrace.sh), and align them against the system time base with [socpe_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/socpe_fit.py). |
| Impact | For inferences lasting less than about 0.5 s, the PE portion runs largely in a low state. When the ANE and the GPU operate simultaneously, the PE portion is slowed by up to about 2.6 times in this report's measurements, whereas convolutions and matrix multiplications on the NE are unaffected. We infer that the cause is the independent scheduling of the shared cluster by the CLPC, together with an interconnect power limiter. |
| Workaround | Models dominated by PE operations require a longer warm-up. |
| Data and tools | [socpe_kt/](../data/09_clock/socpe_kt/), [pefreq_kt3/](../data/09_clock/pefreq_kt3/); [socsamp.c](../tools/common/socsamp.c), [socpe_ktrace.sh](../tools/09_clock/socpe_ktrace.sh), [socpe_fit.py](../tools/09_clock/socpe_fit.py) |
| Report sections | Section 11.3 |

### Class C: Undocumented Behaviors

Table D-1 lists 10 Class C behaviors. They are not necessarily defects, but none appears in the public documentation, and each directly affects the correctness or performance of models.

*Table D-1. Undocumented behaviors*

| ID | Behavior | Implication for developers | Section |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| C1 | Core ML silently executes small models, standalone softmax or relu operations, and some sampling-type operations on the CPU, without any indication | Confirmation that the model actually executes on the ANE requires means such as IOReport interrupt counts | Sections 3.4 and 5.2 |
| C2 | FP16 convolutions and matrix multiplications use a Q15.16 fixed-point accumulator: when the absolute value of an intermediate value reaches 32768, it becomes inf, and it remains inf even if a smaller value is subtracted afterward. The bias is the initial value of the accumulator and is subject to the same range limit as the products | Intermediate accumulated values, including the bias, must not reach ±32768; if necessary, scale the activations beforehand | Sections 8.2.1 and 8.2.3 |
| C3 | FP16 inputs whose exponent field is all ones (inf and NaN) are decoded as ordinary numbers; for example, NaN 0x7E00 decodes to 1.5 × 2¹⁶, which causes the accumulator to overflow to inf | NaN does not propagate as NaN but becomes inf | Section 8.2.4 |
| C4 | Whether a tiny product whose actual value lies within 2 LSB is retained is determined by the sum of the exponents of its two factors: when that sum is not greater than −17, the entire product is discarded, even if its actual value is close to 2 LSB | The accumulated result of tiny products differs from the expected value obtained by rounding the actual values | Section 8.2.2 |
| C5 | Fixed-point results converted to FP16 use "round half away from zero" rather than the IEEE 754 default "round half to even" | Systematic differences appear when results are compared bit by bit with CPU results | Section 8.2.4 |
| C6 | The EXACT and TANH approximation modes of gelu use the same lookup table on the ANE and produce bit-identical output; on the CPU, the two modes differ at 78% of points | The choice of gelu mode on the ANE does not affect the result | Section 8.5.2 |
| C7 | After the two ANEs have been idle for 5.68 s, both are powered off, and the next call takes ≈50 ms longer; after 10.20 s of idleness, a maintenance timer causes the ANE to be powered on once more and removes idle programs | Latency-sensitive applications should avoid idle periods longer than ≈5.7 s and issue warm-up calls in advance when necessary | Section 12.5 |
| C8 | When generating a model, coremltools loads the model once by default, and the compilation cache from this load is not reused by subsequent loads | Large models are compiled twice; the default loading step should be turned off at generation time | Section 3.4 |
| C9 | The ANE compiler does not support horizontal convolution kernel widths of 16 or greater; Core ML's segmenter places such convolutions on the CPU without reporting an error at load time, and a model composed of such convolutions takes 4–7 times as long per layer as 1×15 convolutions. Kernel height is not subject to this limit | Horizontal kernel widths should not exceed 8; at 16, the layer is placed on the CPU, and for small networks the entire model remains on the CPU | Sections 7.4.1 and 5.2 |
| C10 | The accumulator of INT8 (W8A8) convolutions is a 32-bit signed integer; when the integer accumulated sum reaches 2³¹, the output becomes inf rather than saturating. The FP16 path overflows at ±32768, so the two paths have different limits | The overflow conditions of the two precisions must be considered separately; overflow on the INT8 path almost never occurs in practical networks | Section 8.3 |

## Appendix E. Glossary

This appendix summarizes in Table E-1 the main terms and abbreviations used in this report. The meaning column follows the definitions or explanations given in the main text, and the "First defined" column gives the section of the main text where that definition or explanation is first given. Where a meaning is only inferred in the main text, the entry is marked "inferred."

*Table E-1. Terms and abbreviations*

| Term or abbreviation | Full name | Meaning | First defined |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2026BaseLine | — | The parameter set used by the compiler for h18g; its hardware parameter table has the same content as that of H19. | Section 4.2 |
| Address packet | — | A packet located at the end of a TD that writes an offset within a buffer into a base address register, and identifies the buffer by its BAR number. | Section 5.7.2 |
| AFPP | — | An ANE network used exclusively by the image signal processor; the firmware provides it with one separate job queue. | Section 6.4 |
| AMCC | — | The memory controller of the M6, which manages 8 DCS channels. | Section 9.5.1 |
| ANE | Apple Neural Engine | Neural Engine. The M6 integrates two complete ANEs (ANE0 and ANE1) on the same die. | Section 1.1 |
| aned | — | The daemon of the AppleNeuralEngine framework; it must be terminated after the compiler service crashes. | Section 2.2.2 |
| ArchCompareValue | ArchCompareValue | Internal compiler value used to compare layout generations; it is 10 for v24 and v26, and 14 for v36. | Section 5.7.7 |
| BAR | base address register | Base address register slot. An address packet uses its number to indicate the buffer to which an offset belongs. | Section 1.5 |
| BNNS | Basic Neural Network Subroutines | The CPU neural network implementation in the Accelerate framework, and one of the CPU backends of the Core ML segmenter. | Section 5.2 |
| bonded | — | Dual-ANE program. Subgraphs are split between the two ANEs according to the cost model, or all remain on ANE0. | Section 4.5 |
| Busy fraction | — | The proportion of total time occupied by ANE task time, computed from the task events of kdebug. | Section 12.2 |
| CLPC | closed-loop performance controller | Closed-loop performance controller. It resides in the host kernel and selects the DVFS state of the ANE according to utilization. | Section 1.5 |
| Convolution configuration word | — | The 32-bit configuration word (register 10) that centrally encodes the geometric parameters of a convolution. | Section 5.7.4 |
| DART | — | The address translation unit (DART) node `dart-ane`, shared by the two ANEs, with 9 mappers beneath it. | Section 4.3 |
| DCS | — | The name of a DRAM channel in the device tree; the M6 has 8 DCS channels, all of which are enabled. | Section 4.4 |
| DSID | — | The system-level cache data-flow tag for Src1, Src2, and Dst in a TD, which occupies BAR slot 20. | Section 5.7.6 |
| E5 | — | The new-generation compilation and execution module of Espresso, the Core ML runtime framework. Its platform names are written as H16G, H18G, and so on, which correspond to the ANE compilation targets h16g and h18g. | Section 5.2 |
| Evidence category | — | The source of a conclusion, marked at the end of the sentence with 〔〕 brackets, such as timing, kdebug, HWX, HAL, and inferred. | Section 3.5 |
| exe_cycles | — | TD task-header field, written by `CalculateExeCycles`, in units of about 1 µs. Dense layers agree with the estimates of the performance model, whereas pruned layers do not. | Section 5.7.1 |
| Exponential moving average (EWMA) | — | The approximate model assumes that the CLPC reads an exponential moving average of the busy state at the end of a job and compares it with the target. | Section 12.4 |
| Fabric DVFM | Fabric DVFM | Fabric frequency-scaling module; its five DVFS states are identical to the compiler's "DMA frequency table." | Section 4.4 |
| fence | fence | Inter-request dependency handled by the driver; it has two modes, `LocalFast` and `LocalStrict`. | Section 6.3.2 |
| Fixed overhead | — | Overhead per call that is independent of the number of layers, about 160–355 µs (submission side plus completion side), which is removed by the slope method. | Section 3.4 |
| Grouped-channel palettization | — | A compressed weight format in which a palette is built separately for each group of channels; the 4-bit version can be executed by the ANE on the M6. | Section 7.5 |
| h18g | — | The architecture name of the M6 ANE, which is also the compilation target name; it corresponds to TD layout v24. | Section 4.2 |
| HAL | — | The hardware parameter table built into the compiler, for example `ZinIrHal2026BaseLine`; it is also an evidence category. | Section 3.5 |
| halo | halo | Halo rows: adjacent rows that a convolution kernel with a vertical size greater than 1 must read additionally when the layer is split by row. | Section 5.8.3 |
| HWX | — | The compiled artifact of the ANE, in Mach-O format; the new M6 format contains a `__RUNTIME` segment and three TD sequences. | Section 3.5 |
| Idle depth | — | The depth of sleep the host CPU enters while waiting, which determines the completion-side overhead of each call. | Section 6.5.3 |
| IOP | — | The ANE firmware processor. IOReport can record its power state. | Section 12.5 |
| IORegistry | IORegistry | The device tree. It provides properties of the ANE device nodes, frequency tables, power domains, memory channels, and so on. | Section 3.3 |
| IOReport | IOReport | System counter interface. It provides interrupt counts, DRAM bandwidth, power histograms, and frequency and voltage DVFS states. | Section 3.3 |
| kdebug | kdebug | Kernel event tracing, which records firmware and driver events with a 24 MHz timer and requires root privileges. | Section 3.3 |
| Kernel DMA | Kernel DMA | The DMA that transfers weights. Each NE has one group, and the weight-read bandwidth is about 150 GB/s (145–159 GB/s across experiments). | Section 4.5 |
| kernelcache | kernelcache | The kernel binary containing the ANE driver and the CLPC driver; this report performs only read-only static analysis of it. | Section 1.4 |
| L2 | — | The on-chip buffer in each ANE that holds intermediate results between layers. The compiler uses 2 MiB of it, divided into 64 banks. | Section 4.5 |
| LocalFast / LocalStrict | — | Two fence modes. The former can be submitted while the preceding request is executing, whereas the latter must wait for that request to complete. | Section 6.3.2 |
| LSB | least significant bit | Least significant bit. The LSB of the Q15.16 accumulator is 2⁻¹⁶. | Section 8.2.1 |
| Marginal throughput | — | Throughput obtained, for a fixed shape with varying layer counts, from the slope of time against layer count; it excludes the fixed overhead per call. | Section 4.1 |
| MCache | — | The compiler's name for the system-level cache (SLC), which has a capacity of 16 MiB. | Section 4.4 |
| MIL | Model Intermediate Language | The intermediate representation of Core ML, in typed static single-assignment (SSA) form; it is the input to the compiler. | Section 5.3 |
| MTP | — | Multi-token prediction. In custom large language models it is used for speculative decoding, and each call verifies 3 candidates. | Section 14.3.2 |
| MX | microscaling | Microscaling format. A family of block-scaled formats defined by the OCP; the v36 layout contains fields for it (inferred to be supported). | Section 15.3 |
| NE | neural engine core | Neural engine core. Each ANE has 16 of them, performing 256 FP16 or 512 INT8 multiply-accumulates per cycle. | Section 1.5 |
| nonbonded | — | Single-ANE program, which uses only ANE0. | Section 4.5 |
| OCG | output channel group | Output channel group. The number of output channels computed by each NE in each pass, at most 16 for FP16. | Section 7.3.1 |
| OCP | Open Compute Project | Open Compute Project, which defined the MX format family. | Section 15.3 |
| PACC0 | — | The CPU cluster containing the S cores and P cores, which is powered by the PP0b power rail together with the ANE. | Section 13.1.1 |
| Palettization | — | n-bit palettized compressed weights generated by coremltools, which the ANE reads in compressed form. | Section 7.5 |
| PE | planar engine | Planar engine. It executes elementwise operations, and its clock is not in the NE clock domain. | Section 1.2 |
| Performance domain | — | A unit whose frequency is scaled independently in the CLPC; ANE0 is 8 and ANE1 is 29. | Section 4.3 |
| PMP | Power Measurement Processor | A coprocessor on the SoC (full name as given in the Asahi Linux documentation [28]). Its firmware contains the DRAM frequency table and manages three voltage domains: DRAM, SoC, and display. | Section 9.5.1 |
| PP0b | — | SMC power rail that supplies both the ANE and the `PACC0` cluster of the CPU. | Section 3.3 |
| Q15.16 | — | 32-bit fixed-point format (1 sign bit, 15 integer bits, and 16 fractional bits), used as the accumulator format of the FP16 multiply-accumulate path. | Section 4.1 |
| Resident-weight limit | — | The 64 KiB weight limit that the compiler sets for each NE. Beyond this limit, a layer is split into multiple TDs, or weights are read continuously within one TD; in either case, the weights must be read again for every layer. | Section 4.5 |
| Row split | — | One of the split modes of a dual-ANE program. The two ANEs each compute half of the rows and each read a complete copy of the weights. | Section 5.8.3 |
| RTGraph | — | Runtime operation graph, stored in the `__RUNTIME` segment, describing the operations needed to execute each program. | Section 5.5 |
| RTKit | RTKit | The firmware run by the ANE firmware coprocessor; the job queues are managed by it. | Section 4.3 |
| S2D | space_to_depth | A transformation that rearranges spatial dimensions into the channel dimension, which can improve the utilization of layers with few channels and large spatial extent. | Section 7.3.3 |
| Scaling efficiency | — | The speedup of the dual-engine configuration divided by the number of engines; it is about 96% when compute-bound. | Section 10.3 |
| SDPA | — | Scaled dot-product attention, one of the operations covered by the compiler's validation functions. | Section 5.3 |
| Segmenter | segmenter | The step in the E5 compiler of Espresso that selects a backend for each operation (ANE, BNNS, or classic CPU) and divides the graph into segments. Each ANE segment carries a fixed cost, so small segments remain on the CPU. | Section 5.2 |
| Shared cluster | ANE Shared Cluster | A cluster shared by the two ANEs in the CLPC, whose frequency is controlled independently; we infer that the PE and L2 are located in it. | Section 11.3.1 |
| Shared scratch buffer | — | A buffer in a dual-ANE program that both ANEs map jointly and use to exchange partial results. | Section 5.8.2 |
| SLC | system-level cache | System-level cache, shared by the CPU, GPU, and ANE; weight reads do not pass through it. | Section 4.5 |
| Slope method | — | A method in which the layer shape is held fixed and only the number of layers varies; the slope of time against the number of layers is taken as the marginal time per layer. | Section 3.4 |
| SMC | — | Interface that provides power readings for power rails, refreshed about once per second. | Section 3.3 |
| Submission-side / completion-side overhead | — | Host overhead of about 30 µs before an ANE task and of 130–325 µs after it, for synchronous calls. | Section 6.5 |
| Sync record | — | A record in a dual-ANE sequence that coordinates the data exchange between the two ANEs (word `f0003281`). | Section 5.8.2 |
| T8152 | — | The SoC model of the M6. | Section 3.1 |
| Task header | — | The 12-word header at the beginning of each TD, containing fields such as `exe_cycles` and the event mask. | Section 5.7.1 |
| TD | task descriptor | Task descriptor: a sequence of register writes with a packet header. The compiler translates each layer into one or more TDs. | Section 1.2 |
| TDP | — | Thermal design power, used as one of the rated-power bases when comparing energy efficiency. | Section 13.7.3 |
| Throttling | throttle | A power or temperature limit whose ratio is determined by the CLPC and which the driver executes within a duty-cycle window. | Section 6.3.3 |
| Tile DMA | Tile DMA | The DMA that transfers activations. Each TD has at most two input paths and one output path. | Section 4.5 |
| Tiling | — | The compiler tiles intermediate tensors that exceed 2 MiB (in a two-layer model each tile is strictly smaller than 2 MiB, and in longer chains the tiles are finer), and each tile executes the entire chain in succession. | Section 9.2.2 |
| TQ | task queue | Hardware task queue. Each TQ has two slots. The firmware includes a priority-switching mechanism, and we infer that a high-priority task can displace a low-priority task at a TD boundary. | Section 6.4 |
| ulp | unit in the last place | The distance between two adjacent FP16-representable numbers within the exponent interval containing the exact value, used as the unit of error for activation functions. | Section 8.5.2 |
| Utilization target | — | When each idle period is no longer than about 1 ms, the CLPC holds the busy fraction of the ANE at about 77%. | Section 12.2 |
| Vector palettization | — | Palettization in which the unit is a vector of length at most 4. With the generation parameters used in this report, compilation fails on the M6 and the entire model falls back to the CPU. | Section 5.4 |
| VMIN / VOVD2 | — | The lowest state (VMIN) and the top state (VOVD2) among the five voltage levels of the SoC voltage domain. | Section 11.3.2 |
| W8 | — | Quantizes only the weights to 8 bits. It does not raise the peak multiply-accumulate throughput, but it reduces the number of bytes read for the weights. | Section 7.2.2 |
| W8A8 | — | Both weights and activations are quantized to 8 bits. On compute-dense 3×3 convolutions, it is exactly 2 times FP16. | Section 2.1.4 |
| Weight-read bound | — | A condition in which time depends mainly on weight reads: native layers limited by DRAM bandwidth, and rewritten kernels limited by a stage shared between the two ANEs. Splitting across two ANEs gives limited speedup, and row splitting gives none. | Section 10.4 |
| Winograd | Winograd | Fast convolution in which the transform is performed by hardware. It is used for 3×3 and 1×3 convolutions, and its effective throughput can exceed the peak of direct convolution. | Section 7.4.3 |
| Write packet | — | A register-write packet in a TD, encoded either as a contiguous write or as a masked write. | Section 5.7.2 |
| WU | work unit | Work unit. The number of multiply-accumulates per NE per cycle is about twice the number of pixels per WU. | Section 7.2.3 |
| Zero-weight detection | KernelDetectZeros | Bit 28 of the convolution TD weight configuration word 0x1240, which indicates that zero values in dense weights are detected at run time. It is enabled by HAL offset 0x72c and is enabled from H17 onward; it is not enabled on the M4, and its effect on the M6 has not been measured. | Section 7.5 |
